# STONEVOW

### The Rime Wards, Book One

*Iona Rask*

---

*For everyone who has been someone else's warmth, and wanted, once, to be kept.*

---

**Content notes:** This book contains fantasy violence and injury, magical body horror (cold-sickness), on-page sexual content, grief and bereavement, and off-page character deaths. Reader discretion advised.

---

The wards hold because someone pays for them. That is the whole of the
arrangement: a wardwright to shape the stone, a hearth to feed it, and a
name read aloud at the binding so the village knows whose funeral to
expect.

I have set forty-one wards. I buried the man who taught me and the
woman who kept him warm, and I have never once asked for a hearth of
my own.

Then the rite named him.

An orchard-keeper who volunteered. Who traded his own body for his
sister's exemption. Who treats the cold like a rumour, and me like a
person, rather than an office.

Three months of winter. Forty miles of failing cairns. And a bond
that lets me feel, precisely, exactly how much of him the stone is
taking.

I did not choose this. I am no longer certain I would undo it if I
could.


## Chapter One — The Assembly (Isla)

I have worn the same coat to every binding since my mentor's, and I have
never once had it cleaned properly, on the theory — untested, unproven,
mine alone — that a coat that has already survived one funeral is luckier
than a coat that hasn't, and that luck of this particular kind is not
something a person ought to risk washing away.

It has not, so far, been especially lucky. It has simply been present,
which I have come to understand, over six years of sole practice on the
northern line, is most of what luck actually is.

I stand before the assembly at Highstone Cairn in that coat, in the
rite-hall's particular cold — a cold with weight to it, unlike the cold
outside, which merely arrives — and do not let my face do anything at
all. I have had a great deal of practice not letting my face do anything
at all. It is, along with the coat, one of the two pieces of equipment I
can be certain will still be working by the end of the evening.

Keeper Bram Ossory reads the rite in the old cadence, the one that has
not changed in longer than Highstone's own foundations, and I have heard
it enough times now — mine, my mentor's, the three before his that I
learned only as names in the record-room's ledger — that I could recite
it backward without once losing the rhythm.

A wardwright to shape the stone. A hearth to feed it. A name read aloud
at the binding, so the village knows, in advance, precisely whose
funeral to expect.

It is not a comfortable sentence to have memorized. I have made my peace
with that the way I have made my peace with most uncomfortable things:
by refusing to examine the peace too closely, in case it turns out, on
inspection, not to be peace at all.

The name Ossory reads is Aldric Marrow's.

I do not know him. I have seen him twice, both times at the Highstone
market, both times buying grafting wax in the specific quantity of a man
who intends to use every last measure of it himself rather than send a
boy to fetch it cheaper elsewhere. I know, from the ledger Sela Marrow
keeps for the crown's own tax rolls, that he is twenty-eight, that he
runs the last living orchard above the line, that he has a sister three
years his junior whose name does not, as of eighteen months ago, appear
on any exemption roll it should by rights still appear on.

I do the arithmetic on that absence in the half-second between hearing
his name and watching him step forward, the way I do arithmetic on
everything, out of a habit older than my apprenticeship and considerably
more reliable than hope.

He steps forward before Ossory has finished the sentence.

That is the detail I will turn over longest, afterward, lying awake in a
cottage that has not yet learned to hold two people's weight instead of
one. Not dragged. Not summoned by a name he had no say in. He simply
walks forward, unhurried, into the space the rite has left for whichever
hearth the crown's ledgers deemed suitable, and stands there with the
specific stillness of a man who decided this outcome for himself some
considerable time before the rite ever got around to confirming it.

I understand, watching his face — sun-marked despite the season, broad
through the shoulders in the particular way of men who have spent their
whole lives lifting things heavier than themselves and never once
complaining about the weight — that he volunteered. Not for the binding
generally. For me, specifically, sight unseen, on the strength of
whatever the crown's assessors told him about the northern line's
casualty rate and his own arithmetic about what his sister's name was
worth against it.

I have spent six years being the only wardwright standing between forty
miles of cairns and the Rime's own patient, directionless advance. I
have never once, in all that time, had anyone volunteer to stand beside
me in it. I do not know what to do with a man who has, except distrust
the timing of it entirely.

Ossory calls us both to the rite-stone at the hall's center — a slab of
the same grey, frost-veined granite as every standing cairn on the line,
smaller, worn smooth by six generations of hands exactly like the two
about to be placed on it — and binds our wrists together with the
rite-cord, an unremarkable length of grey wool that has, by the old
accounts, held through worse winters than this one intends to be.

The cord itself does nothing. I have been told this since I was
nineteen and believed it, on the evidence available, entirely.

What arrives instead, the moment the knot closes, is not touch and is
not quite anything I have a proper word for — a warmth, borrowed rather
than felt, arriving in my chest from a direction that has nothing to do
with the hall's own hearth, settling there with the specific unwelcome
permanence of a guest who has already decided he isn't leaving before
morning.

I do not let my face do anything about that either. It costs
considerably more than the usual stillness costs, and I pay it anyway,
because the alternative is letting an assembly of people I have spent
six years keeping at a careful distance watch me discover, in real time,
exactly how thoroughly the rite has just rearranged the furniture of my
own chest.

Aldric's hand, bound against mine, is warm in the specific way of a man
who has spent his whole life outdoors and never once resented the cold
for costing him that warmth back. Mine, against his, is cold enough that
I feel him notice it — a small, involuntary flinch, quickly stilled,
the reflex of someone raised to be careful with things he suspects might
be fragile.

I am not fragile. I have buried a mentor and kept forty-one cairns
standing with nothing but my own two hands and an apprentice too young
to be trusted with the worst of the work, and I have never once, in any
of that, been fragile.

I do not say any of this. I let Ossory finish the rite, and I let the
cord come away from both our wrists at the appointed line in the old
cadence, and I do the thing I am best at, which is standing perfectly
still while an entire hall of people who have just watched my life
change completely fail to notice that anything about my face has moved
at all.

·

Afterward, in the corridor outside the rite-hall, where the cold is
merely the ordinary kind and considerably easier to bear, Aldric finds
me before I have finished deciding whether to let myself be found.

"I know what I did," he says. Not an apology. A fact, offered plainly,
the way a man states the weight of a thing before handing it over so
the other person isn't caught off guard by the carrying of it. "I'd
rather you heard it from me than from the ledger."

"The ledger already told me," I say. "Sela Marrow's name. The exemption
roll it should still be on and isn't."

Something moves behind his face at that — not surprise, exactly.
Recognition, perhaps, that the woman he has just been bound to for the
rest of whatever this winter turns out to cost either of them reads
ledgers the way other people read weather, and draws the same plain,
unavoidable conclusions from both.

"She'd have died on the exemption roll instead of off it," he says.
"Two winters back, before the crown tightened the terms. I had a name
to spend and she didn't. It wasn't a large decision, in the end. It
just looked like one, from outside."

I do not tell him that I do not believe decisions of that particular
size are ever small from any angle, inside or out. I file the sentence
instead, the way I file everything I am not yet ready to spend, in the
part of my chest that has, until roughly four minutes ago, belonged
entirely to me.

"I run cold," I say, instead, because it is true, and because it is the
only warning I currently have the nerve to give a stranger the rite has
just made considerably less of a stranger than either of us planned
for. "Not as a manner of speaking. As an actual, professional fact.
I've spent six years learning not to need anyone to warm me, because
needing that from a hearth I'm also responsible for spending is a debt
I was never going to be able to pay back evenly."

"I know how to be patient with a debt," Aldric says. "It's the one
skill this whole business has actually taught me properly."

I do not have an answer to that which doesn't risk giving away
considerably more than an hour-old binding has any right to extract
from me, so I do not attempt one. I simply nod, once, and let him
understand, from the nod, that the conversation is over for tonight,
and walk back out into a cold that has, for the first time in six
years, an actual second weight standing somewhere inside it alongside
my own.

·

I do not sleep, that first night, though not for any of the reasons I
had braced myself to lie awake over.

I lie instead running the same cold arithmetic I have run every night
since my mentor walked into the cold and left me the only wardwright
standing on forty miles of failing line — the cairns, the record book,
the specific number of scenes I have left before the season's demands
outstrip whatever I currently have left to give it — except tonight
there is a new variable sitting in the sum that refuses, however many
times I run the numbers, to behave like the rest of the variables I am
used to managing.

He decided this. Before he knew my name, before he knew what the
binding would actually feel like arriving, he decided that whatever it
cost him was worth less to him than what it would buy his sister.

I have spent six years believing, professionally, that the rite's whole
architecture runs on cost taken from one hearth and paid, silently, into
stone. I have never once, until an hour ago, felt what that cost
actually resembles arriving in a body I can put a face to.

I do not sleep. I lie in the specific dark of a cottage that has, as of
this evening, quietly and irreversibly stopped being only mine, and I
let myself, for exactly as long as it takes the fire to burn down to
its last coals, wonder what it might cost me to let that be something
other than a debt.

·

I had not expected, walking back from the rite-hall alone, to be
stopped at Highstone's own gate by Reny Ashe, who has run the winter
market stall nearest the gatehouse for as long as I have been
wardwright and considerably longer than that besides, and who has
never once, in all my years passing that stall, addressed a single
word to me that wasn't strictly transactional.

"They bound you to the Marrow boy," she says, not quite a question,
wrapping a twist of dried herbs I have not asked for and setting it in
my hand regardless.

"They did."

"Good," Reny says, with the specific finality of a woman who has
already decided the matter and is not currently interested in
discussing the reasoning behind it. "He's spent six years driving that
cart of his down from the last orchard in every kind of weather,
without once complaining to a soul about the cold it costs him to do
it. I'd rather the rite bind you to a man who already knows how to
carry a burden without setting it down on someone else's account."

"You know him better than I expected."

"Everyone at this gate knows him," Reny says. "You're the one who's
kept yourself too occupied holding up forty miles of stone to notice
the people walking past it." She says this without unkindness, though
it lands, regardless, considerably harder than she likely intends it
to. "That's not a criticism, Wardwright. It's an observation. You've
earned the right to be occupied. I only mean to say — you might find,
now that the rite's gone and noticed for you, that there was rather
more standing around you than you'd accounted for."

I do not have a ready answer to that, and Reny, apparently satisfied
with having said her piece, turns back to her stall without waiting for
one, leaving me standing at the gate with a twist of herbs I did not
ask for and a sentence I turn over the whole rest of the walk home,
unable to decide whether it unsettles me more for what it says about
Aldric or for what it says about the six years I have spent, by Reny's
own accounting, failing to notice anyone at all.

The herbs, I discover later, are for sleep. I do not use them that
first night. I lie awake instead with the fire burning down and Reny's
observation keeping company with everything else I am not yet ready to
examine, and find, somewhere before the coals go entirely cold, that I
am considerably less alone in this village than six years of careful,
solitary practice had ever let me believe.

·

Highstone Cairn itself, in the days before the binding, is not a place
I have ever properly described to anyone, in the sense of setting down
whole what it actually looks like rather than simply moving through it
the way you move through your own hands — familiar past the point of
noticing.

It sits at the northern edge of the last properly warm valley before
the ground begins its long climb toward the line, a squat, grey
fortification of the same frost-veined granite as every standing cairn
on the whole forty miles, built, according to the earliest records I
have ever managed to find, by wardwrights considerably more numerous
than the single practitioner it currently houses. There were, once, a
dozen of us working this line together, the record shows, back before
the crown's own arithmetic decided a smaller staff cost less to fund,
regardless of what the smaller staff actually cost the line itself.

I think about that history often, walking the keep's own corridors —
stone worn smooth by considerably more feet than currently walk it,
rooms clearly built for staff the crown no longer sees fit to maintain,
a whole institution slowly, quietly, shrinking around the single
wardwright it insists is sufficient. I have never once, until this
particular week, properly considered what it might mean to be handed,
by the same crown that shrank this institution down to one, a second
hearth to share the weight of it.

The rite-hall itself, where the binding took place, was clearly built
for considerably larger assemblies than the modest gathering that
witnessed mine — a vaulted ceremonial space with room, easily, for
several hundred, currently hosting perhaps forty on any given binding,
the crown's own patronage having thinned alongside its funding. I stood
at that rite-stone, the night of my own binding, keenly aware of all
the empty space around the small crowd that had gathered, a physical
reminder of exactly how much this whole institution has shrunk in the
years since it was first built to hold considerably more than it
currently does.

I fall asleep, eventually, that first night, still turning over the
whole shape of Highstone's own diminished grandeur, and dream, briefly,
of a rite-hall properly full again, though I could not, waking, say
whether the dream was memory, hope, or simply the particular kind of
wishful thinking that arrives easiest in the last hour before dawn.

·

I wake at my usual hour regardless, the specific discipline of six
years refusing to yield even to a night this strange, and go through
the whole of my ordinary morning routine with the deliberate care of a
woman trying to prove to herself that nothing has actually changed —
checking the previous night's inspection log, laying out the day's
priorities, making tea I mostly forget to drink.

It is only standing at the washbasin, catching my own reflection in
the small mirror I've owned since apprenticeship, that the whole of it
properly arrives. I look, if I am honest, exactly as I did yesterday —
the same weathered face, the same hair bound back hard against the
cold, the same permanent half-pallor that six years of underfed winters
has left on me. And yet I find myself examining the reflection for
some visible sign of the change, as though a binding this significant
ought to have left a mark I could actually point to, rather than simply
sitting, invisible, somewhere behind my own ribs.

I think, briefly, of what my mother might have said, seeing this
particular morning — a wardwright bound, at last, to someone, standing
at a washbasin looking for evidence of a change no mirror could ever
properly show. I decide, drying my face, that she would likely have
told me to stop looking in mirrors for answers that only time is
actually going to provide, and go down to the record-room to begin the
day's work, carrying the whole unresolved shape of last night's binding
quietly alongside me, the way I carry most things I haven't yet learned
what to do with.

·

Tomas is already at the record-room table when I arrive, considerably
earlier than his usual hour, and I understand, from the specific
careful way he doesn't quite look at me directly, that he has spent at
least part of the night composing whatever question he's currently
working up the nerve to ask.

"You don't have to say anything," he says, finally, "if you'd rather
not. I only wanted you to know I'm glad it's him. Whoever the rite
could have named, I mean. I've delivered enough reports up to the
Marrow orchard, these past three years, to know he's decent in a way
that isn't performed for an audience."

"Thank you, Tomas," I say, surprised by how much the plain sentiment
actually settles something in my chest that I hadn't realized was
still unsettled.

"I also wanted to say," he continues, working up to something else
entirely, "that whatever this means for the apprenticeship — for me,
specifically — I'd rather you tell me plainly, whenever there's
anything to tell, than have me work it out secondhand from whatever
the village happens to be saying at market."

"There's nothing to tell yet," I say. "The rite bound us last night.
I don't know myself, this morning, what any of it actually means
beyond the binding's own bare requirements."

"That's fair," Tomas says, accepting this with more grace than his
nineteen years would suggest he owes anyone. "I only wanted it said
plainly, since you've spent three years teaching me that plain things
said early cost considerably less than the same things discovered
late."

I look at him properly, understanding, not for the first time, exactly
how much of my own careful discipline he has already absorbed simply
through years of proximity, and find myself, this particular morning,
grateful for an apprentice sharp enough to ask the honest question
rather than simply speculate quietly the way the rest of the village
undoubtedly already is.

"I'll tell you," I say, "as things become clear. I promise you that
much, at least."

"That's all I'm asking," Tomas says, and goes back to the morning's
reports, and I find myself, watching him work, considerably steadied
by the specific, ordinary comfort of a routine that has, at least this
morning, remained exactly as it was the day before.


## Chapter Two — Cairns and Debts (Isla)

Down into the cold, quiet record-room, early, well before the sun's even properly up, where the ledger of every cairn on the
northern line hangs on the wall in a dead man's handwriting I have never
once had copied over into my own, on the theory that copying it would
mean admitting, formally, finally, that his hand is not going to write in it
again.

Forty-one cairns standing. Forty miles of line. Ossian Thale's
handwriting listing every stone by name — the Long Reach cairn, the
Hollow Wall cairn, the seven small waystones between Highstone and the
first proper marker — in the specific cramped, economical hand of a man
who never once wasted ink on a word that didn't need spending.

I have kept that arithmetic since I was twenty-six years old, the year
he walked into the cold and left me the only wardwright the crown was
willing to fund for a line that size, and it has never once, in five
years of keeping it, come out even.

Tomas Renn has the kettle on already, and the day's reports sorted by
urgency in three neat stacks on the workbench, the way he always does,
whether or not anyone has actually asked him to. He is nineteen, all
elbows and unfinished height, with the specific gravity of a young man
who has decided, somewhere in his own private arithmetic, that being
useful before he's asked is the safest available version of himself to
be.

"They bound you to the orchard-keeper," he says, careful, watching my
face for whatever shape my feelings about that have decided to take.
He has learned, over three years of apprenticeship, to read my face for
information it isn't otherwise offering, which is either the best or
the worst thing I have taught him, and I have not yet decided which.

"They bound me to a name and a hearth," I say. "The rest is paperwork."

I do not believe this even as I say it. I have never once, in six years
of running this exact defense against every uncomfortable fact that
arrives in my life, managed to make it sound convincing to Tomas
specifically, who has watched me deploy it against considerably smaller
facts than a rite-binding and clock every single failure.

"You don't have to pretend it's nothing, with me," he says, pouring the
tea without being asked, milk first, the way I've always preferred it
and never once explained why. "I do the ledgers. I know what a hearth
costs, structurally. I'd just like to know how you're actually doing
about it, separately from the structure."

"I'm doing the arithmetic," I say, which is, for me, the closest
available approximation of an honest answer.

Tomas doesn't push further. He has learned that too, over three years —
which questions I will actually answer if given room, and which ones
simply need to be asked once, gently, and then left alone to do their
work in private. It is, I think, watching him settle into his own chair
with his own tea and his own stack of reports, the single most valuable
thing I have ever taught anyone, and I did not teach it to him on
purpose. He simply watched me long enough to learn the shape of what I
actually needed, the way apprentices learn most of what actually
matters — sideways, from observation, rather than from anything I ever
said outright.

"The Long Reach cairn's reading low again," he says, changing the
subject to the one that will always, reliably, get my full attention.
"Third time this month. I checked the charge myself yesterday, before
you ask — it's not a fault in the reading. The stone's actually
weakening."

I take the report from him and read it twice, the numbers doing the
thing numbers do when they've already told you something you didn't
want confirmed a second time: confirming it anyway, patiently, without
apology.

"How much of the line is reading low," I ask.

"Six cairns, properly low. Another nine trending that way, if the
pattern holds through Coldwake the way it held through the last two
winters." He hesitates, the specific hesitation of a young man about to
say something he suspects will land badly and has decided to say
anyway, because saying it is his job. "You've been feeding some of them
yourself, haven't you. Outside the rite proper."

I do not answer that directly. I have not answered it directly to
anyone in two years, and I am not going to start with a nineteen-year-old
who has enough of his own arithmetic to do without adding mine to the
pile.

"The rite provides what the rite provides," I say, which is not a lie,
technically, in the specific way that most of the things I say to Tomas
about my own condition are not lies, technically, while being
considerably less than the whole truth.

He lets it go. He always lets it go, eventually, once he's registered
that I've heard the question and chosen, deliberately, not to answer
it — which is, I understand, its own kind of answer, the loudest one
available to a man who never once, in three years, has managed to get a
straight admission of weakness out of the woman training him to run
this line after her.

"What was he like," Tomas asks instead, meaning Aldric, in the careful
sideways way he asks most of the questions he actually wants answered.
"The name they read."

"Warm," I say, and am surprised, saying it, by how much the single word
costs me to admit out loud, in a record-room built entirely out of a
dead man's careful economy with ink.

"That's not an answer about what he's like. That's an answer about a
fact."

"It's the only fact I currently have," I say, which is, this once,
entirely true, and Tomas, wisely, chooses not to push any further into
territory neither of us has the proper equipment to survey yet.

·

The morning's business runs its ordinary course after that — three
reports from the outer waystones, a request from the crown's grain
office that I sign without reading closely, because I have learned,
over the years, exactly which of the crown's requests are worth the ink
it would cost me to actually argue with them — and it is nearly midday
before I let myself think about the man bound to my wrist for a second
consecutive hour.

I go, eventually, to the workshop window that looks out over the road
below Highstone, on the pretext of checking the weather, which is not,
strictly, a pretext, since the weather genuinely does need checking
before any cairn inspection this afternoon. I tell myself this several
times, in several different orders, while I stand at the glass longer
than any weather report has ever required of me.

He is not on the road. Of course he is not on the road; he has his own
orchard to run, his own sister's grain-count to keep straight, his own
entire life that existed in perfectly good order before the rite
decided it needed a second occupant.

I go back to my reports. I do not, for the rest of the morning, check
the window again, on the grounds that checking it a second time would
require admitting to myself that the first check was never actually
about the weather at all.

·

## A Stranger's Weight

Aldric finds me on the road below Highstone in the early afternoon,
which means either he has come looking or the errand that brought him
this direction was, itself, some manner of pretext I am not currently in
a position to accuse anyone else of running, given the morning I have
just had at my own window.

He has grafting wax in a satchel over one shoulder and the specific
unhurried gait of a man who does not need to arrive anywhere faster than
his own legs are willing to carry him, which I notice, watching him
approach, is already unlike most of the men I have dealt with
professionally, all of whom arrive at everything as though being late
were a personal failing rather than an occasional accident of weather.

"I didn't expect you on this road," I say, by way of greeting, which is
not quite an accusation and not quite not one either.

"I didn't expect to be on it," he says, entirely unbothered by the
half-accusation. "Sela needed wax fetched from the Highstone market.
I offered, mostly because I wanted to see whether you'd still be
standing here, doing whatever it is wardwrights do to a perfectly
ordinary road at midday."

"Reading weather," I say.

"Is that what you were doing at the workshop window an hour ago too?"

I do not have a good answer to that, mostly because I do not have any
answer to it that doesn't involve admitting he clocked me watching for
him before I had fully admitted it to myself, so I change the subject
with the specific bluntness I reserve for facts I intend to establish
early and permanently.

"I run the list on everyone the rite could plausibly have bound me to,"
I say. "I ran it before the assembly, the way I run every list before
a decision gets made without my input. You weren't on it. I didn't know
your name until Ossory read it."

"I know," Aldric says. "I made sure of that, actually. I asked the
assessors not to tell either of us who the other one was, ahead of
time. I didn't want you meeting a name in a ledger before you met the
person the name belonged to."

I consider this for longer than the sentence, on its face, seems to
warrant. It is, I realize, turning it over, the first genuinely
considerate thing anyone connected to the crown's rite-office has done
in the whole five years I've been dealing with them, and it arrived
from the one person in the entire arrangement who had no professional
obligation to consider my comfort at all.

"That's not how the assessors usually operate," I say.

"No," Aldric agrees. "I asked for a favour. I don't ask for many. It
made this one easier to grant, apparently."

We walk together for a while without any particular destination, the
way two people walk when neither has decided yet whether the
conversation is over, past the last standing waystone before the road
properly climbs toward the orchard line, and I find myself, against
every professional instinct I have cultivated for six years, doing the
thing I do to every person I am trying to understand: running the cold
list, item by item, out loud this time rather than silently.

"You're twenty-eight," I say. "You run the last orchard above the line.
Your sister keeps the grain record and should, by the exemption rolls
as they stood two winters ago, have had her own name protected without
your binding needing to cover it at all."

"The crown tightened the terms," Aldric says, exactly as he said it the
night before, in exactly the same flat register, which tells me it is
not a story he tells differently depending on the audience — a small,
useful fact, the kind I collect about people the way other wardwrights
collect cairn measurements. "Sela would have died on the roll instead
of off it. I had a name available to spend instead. It wasn't
complicated, from where I was standing."

"Most decisions that size are complicated from somewhere," I say.

"Maybe," he allows. "I've found it's usually simpler once you've
decided which direction you're willing to be uncomplicated in." He
glances at me, sideways, in the specific way of a man testing whether a
sentence has landed the way he intended it to. "You run cold on
purpose, you told me last night. I've been turning that over since.
I don't think I fully believe it's only a professional habit."

"It's a professional habit," I say, which is true, and also, I
suspect, listening to my own voice say it, considerably less than the
whole of what's actually true.

"I didn't say it wasn't a habit," Aldric says. "I said I don't think
it's only that." He does not push further. He simply lets the sentence
sit on the road between us, the way he let his own admission about
Sela sit last night, offered plainly and then handed over entirely,
without any further attempt to manage how I receive it.

I do not have an answer for that which doesn't risk telling him
considerably more than a single day's acquaintance has earned him, so I
do not attempt one. I run the rest of my cold list instead, silently
this time — what he can be trusted with, what he is likely to cost me,
whether I can afford, on any of the ledgers I actually keep, to let a
stranger become a person rather than merely an office the rite has
assigned me — and reach, by the time the road forks toward Highstone's
gate, no answer that holds up under its own weight.

"I should get back," I say. "The Long Reach cairn wants checking before
the light goes."

"I'll walk you as far as the fork," Aldric says, which is not quite an
offer to accompany me the rest of the way and not quite not one either,
and I let him, mostly because I have not yet worked out a version of
myself that knows how to say no to small, ordinary kindnesses without
it costing more than simply accepting them would.

We part at the fork with nothing resolved and nothing, strictly,
needing to be resolved yet. I walk the rest of the way to the cairn
alone, in the particular quiet that arrives once a conversation has
ended and the mind keeps right on having it anyway, turning each
sentence over for a seam I haven't found yet, and do not find one,
the whole cold walk back.

·

The Long Reach cairn reads within tolerance, which is its own small
mercy after a day spent doing arithmetic on considerably less
manageable things, and I take longer over the inspection than the
reading strictly requires, mostly because the cairn field at dusk has
always been the one place I can properly think without anyone's
expectations pressing in on the thinking.

I have stood at this exact stone the evening after every significant
change in my adult life — the evening after my apprenticeship began,
the evening after Ossian's funeral, the evening I finally accepted that
no wardwright before me had ever managed to hold this line without
losing at least one cairn a season to the Rime's patient advance. It
seems fitting, standing here now, that the evening of my binding should
join that particular list.

I think about Reny Ashe's herbs, still folded in my coat pocket. I
think about the specific weight of Aldric's hand catching mine as I
nearly went down on a patch of ice three days before any of this began,
an ordinary catch that meant nothing at the time and has, somehow, in
retrospect, taken on the shape of the first evidence of a pattern I
did not yet know I was watching for.

Tomas finds me there, eventually, having correctly guessed that I would
linger longer than the inspection required.

"You've been standing here an hour," he says, not quite a question.

"The cairn needed watching," I say, which is not, strictly, true, and
Tomas, wisely, does not challenge the lie directly.

"How are you finding him," Tomas asks instead, careful, in the specific
sideways way he asks most of his real questions. "Properly. Not the
professional answer."

I consider the question for longer than it probably warrants, watching
the last of the light go out of the sky over the standing stones.

"Considerate," I say, finally. "In a way I didn't expect a stranger to
be, this early. He mended my glove without being asked and hasn't once
mentioned it since, as though mentioning it would somehow diminish the
mending."

"That sounds like a good sign," Tomas says.

"It sounds like a great many things I haven't finished sorting through
yet," I say, which is, I suspect, considerably closer to honest than
the tidy version I'd have given him a fortnight ago.

We walk back to Highstone together in the last of the light, Tomas
peppering me with the ordinary questions of an apprentice eager for
the day's remaining lessons, and I find myself grateful, walking beside
him, for the particular steadiness of a routine that has not, despite
everything shifting underneath it, changed in any way that matters.

·

Tomas's own history, I am reminded that evening, watching him settle
into his usual chair with his usual careful economy, is considerably
more complicated than the steady young man currently sorting reports
would suggest to anyone meeting him fresh.

He came to Highstone at sixteen, an orphan of the outer waystones —
his own parents lost to a cold-sickness outbreak the winter before,
one of the smaller tragedies the line exacts regularly enough that the
crown's own records barely note it beyond a single administrative line.
Ossian took him in as an apprentice not out of any particular need for
a second hand, at the time, but because the alternative, Ossian told
me once, was watching a capable boy go to whatever thin charity the
crown's outer settlements could otherwise offer him.

"Do you remember much of them," I ask him, this particular evening,
apropos of nothing beyond the general softness the day's events have
left in the room. "Your parents."

"Some," Tomas says, without the particular flinch such questions
sometimes provoke in people who'd rather not examine an old grief in
company. "My mother kept bees, of all things, on the last stretch of
land before the cold made keeping them impossible. My father worked
the waystones same as everyone out there, minor maintenance mostly,
nothing as significant as what you do."

"That's not a small thing, maintaining a waystone."

"No," Tomas agrees, "I don't suppose it is. I didn't understand that,
at sixteen. I understand it rather better now, doing the work myself."

"Do you miss them?"

"Every day," Tomas says, plainly, "though it's gotten easier to carry,
these past three years. Ossian was kind to me, in his own quiet way. You
have been too, though I don't think either of you would call it
kindness outright — you'd both call it simply doing the job properly."

"Perhaps that's what kindness actually is," I say, "most of the time.
Simply doing the job properly, with someone else's actual wellbeing
folded quietly into the doing of it."

Tomas considers that for a long moment, and then nods, slowly,
accepting the observation the way he accepts most of what I offer him
that isn't strictly instructional — carefully, and with considerably
more gravity than his age would suggest he ought to be capable of.

·

We stop, on the way back into Highstone proper, at the small kitchen
that serves the keep's own remaining staff, where the cook — a
broad, weathered woman named Ida who has fed three generations of
wardwrights without once, as far as I know, missing a single meal's
proper timing — greets Tomas with the specific affection she reserves
for anyone she considers, functionally, one of her own children.

"You've heard, I take it," Ida says to me, setting down two bowls of
the evening's stew without being asked. "The whole keep's been talking
of nothing else."

"I'd assumed as much," I say.

"He's a good one," Ida says, plainly, meaning Aldric, with the same
directness Reny Ashe showed at the gate. "I've fed him at market twice
a year for a decade. Always thanks the stall properly. Always pays
even when the trader tries to wave the debt off out of respect for the
orchard's hard luck. That's not nothing, Wardwright, in a man."

"I'm gathering that," I say, "from rather a lot of people, rather
quickly."

"Good," Ida says, entirely unbothered by my dry tone, and goes back to
her kitchen, leaving Tomas and me to our stew.

"Everyone in this village seems to have an opinion about my binding,"
I say, once she's gone.

"Everyone in this village has been watching you run yourself thin for
six years," Tomas says, with a bluntness that surprises me slightly,
coming from him. "I think they're simply relieved someone's finally
going to make you slow down, whether or not you intend to let them."

I don't have a ready response to that, so I simply eat my stew, and let
the specific, considerable warmth of an entire village's quiet approval
settle somewhere I haven't previously made room for it.

·

Ida returns partway through the meal with a second helping neither of
us asked for, setting it down in front of Tomas with the particular
brisk affection of a woman who has decided a growing apprentice's
appetite is not a matter open to negotiation.

"Eat properly," she says, "you've been thin all winter, and I won't
have it said I let Ossian's boy go hungry on my watch." She turns to
me before I can protest on his behalf. "And you, Wardwright — I want it
understood that my kitchen's always open, whatever this binding turns
out to require of you. A body that spends itself the way yours does
needs feeding properly, regardless of how little time you generally
allow yourself to sit down and do it."

"I manage," I say, which is, I understand even saying it, precisely
the kind of answer Ida has clearly heard from me a hundred times
before and stopped believing considerably before tonight.

"You manage," Ida agrees, with the specific dry patience of a woman old
enough to have outlasted several wardwrights' particular brand of
stubbornness, "the same way a roof manages, right up until the one
winter it doesn't. I'm not asking you to stop managing. I'm simply
telling you there's a kitchen here that'll feed you properly, whenever
you're finally ready to let it."

I don't have a ready argument against that kind of plain, practical
kindness, so I simply thank her, and she goes back to her stove
satisfied, and Tomas, watching the whole exchange with poorly disguised
amusement, waits until she's properly out of earshot before speaking.

"She's right, you know," he says. "About the managing."

"I'm aware," I say. "I don't need it confirmed by my own apprentice on
top of the cook."

"I'm simply reporting the data," Tomas says, echoing, though he
doesn't yet know it, a phrase he'll use on me again in a few weeks'
time about considerably more embarrassing evidence, and we finish the
meal together in the kitchen's warm, ordinary bustle, and I find
myself, walking back out into the evening cold afterward, considerably
more fed — in every sense the word can properly carry — than I've
allowed myself to be in longer than I care to examine.


## Chapter Three — Ground Rules (Isla)

Aldric comes to the cottage that evening, in the fading winter light, because the rite requires it —
proximity, some minimum measure of it, though the old texts are
maddeningly vague about exactly how much — and because neither of us
has anywhere else the binding will hold properly, given that it was
performed, deliberately, at Highstone rather than at either of our own
doors.

I lay out terms the way I lay out a ledger, because it is the only
register I currently trust myself to speak in without saying something
I haven't fully decided I'm willing to have said.

"Separate rooms," I say. "The cottage has two; I've had the second
aired since this morning. Separate meals, unless the rite calls us
together for some specific purpose the record requires documented. And
no pretending, between the two of us, at anything the rite hasn't
actually made true. I'd rather we both know exactly where the line sits
than have either of us guess wrong about it later and pay for the
guess."

Aldric listens to the whole of it without once interrupting, which is
itself a kind of information — a man giving a woman the room to finish
saying an uncomfortable thing rather than filling the silence early to
manage his own discomfort with hearing it.

"I'm not asking you to want this," he says, once I've finished. "Any of
it. I'm asking you to let me be useful in it, since I'm going to be
here either way, whether either of us wanted a stranger in the second
room or not."

"Useful how," I say, because it is, genuinely, the practical question,
and because I have found, over six years, that vague offers of
usefulness tend to cost considerably more than specific ones once
they're actually called upon.

"However you'll let me be. I can chop wood. I can carry water further
than you should be carrying it yourself after a full day at the cairns.
I know how to graft a wound closed on a tree that's taken damage, which
I'm told is not entirely unlike some of what a wardwright does to
stone, structurally, though I'll grant the materials are different."
He pauses, considering. "I'd also like to know the actual shape of your
work, if you're willing to teach any of it. Not to take it over. To
understand what I've agreed to be bound to."

I had not expected that particular request, and I turn it over for
longer than the pause between us comfortably allows, before answering.

"Most people bound to a wardwright want to understand as little as
possible," I say. "It's easier to be spent by something you haven't
looked at closely."

"I've never once found not-looking to make a cost easier to bear," Aldric
says. "Only easier to ignore until it's too large to manage. I'd rather
look at it properly, from the start, and know exactly what I'm carrying."

I write the terms down, in the end, on a single sheet from the record
book — separate rooms, separate meals except by the rite's own
requirement, no performance of anything not actually true between us —
and I notice, writing them, that I have drawn up rules for a stranger
with considerably more care than I have drawn up anything for my own
comfort in the six years since my mentor died and left me the sole
practitioner of an arithmetic that has never once come out even.

"Sign it if you agree," I say, sliding the sheet across the table.

He reads it twice, signs it without argument, and adds a single line of
his own beneath my signature, in a hand considerably less economical
than Ossian Thale's ever was, though no less careful for the
difference: *I agree to all of the above, and to being taught, if she's
willing to teach me.*

"You added a term," I say.

"I did," Aldric agrees. "I thought it only fair, since you'd written
all of yours first."

I do not have a ready response to a stranger correcting the exact
imbalance I hadn't yet noticed I'd built into my own document, so I
simply fold the sheet and file it, and go to see about the second
room's fire, which needs building regardless of whatever else either of
us has just quietly agreed to.

## Someone Else's Warmth

My glove has been split at the seam for a fortnight. I have not
mentioned it to anyone, because mentioning small cold things has never
once, in my adult life, been a habit I let myself keep — a torn seam is
a torn seam, survivable, and survivable things do not, in my
experience, warrant the specific vulnerability of asking someone else
to fix them.

I find it mended on the cottage step before dawn, stitched along the
whole seam in a neat, close hand that has clearly done considerably more
delicate work than splitting firewood, still warm from whatever fire it
was mended beside, with no note attached and no comment left waiting to
be thanked for.

Aldric is already at the woodpile when I come out, not looking at me,
splitting logs with the specific steady rhythm of a man giving the
moment nowhere convenient to become a scene about itself.

"You didn't have to," I say, because it is the sentence available to
me, and because I have not yet worked out a better one for a situation
I did not know, until this exact moment, that I needed a sentence
prepared for.

"I know," Aldric says, and keeps splitting wood, and does not turn
around, and it is — I realize, standing on the step with a mended glove
in both hands — the first sentence anyone has said to me in longer than
I can accurately place that asks for absolutely nothing back in
exchange for having been said.

I go back inside, eventually, once the cold has done what it always
does and made the choice for me, and sit at the table with the glove
still in both hands for considerably longer than the glove itself
requires. I turn it over, looking at the stitches, small and even and
plainly practiced, and think about a man who mends a stranger's glove
before dawn and says nothing about it afterward, on the theory —
untested by me until this exact morning, and apparently entirely proven
by him — that some kindnesses are only real if nobody's kept a ledger
of them.

I have kept a ledger of nearly everything in my life since I was
nineteen years old. It occurs to me, sitting at my own table with a
stranger's careful stitching warm against my palms, that I have just
been handed the first entry I have absolutely no intention of ever
writing down.

Tomas arrives an hour later to collect the morning's reports and finds
me still at the table, glove on, flexing the mended fingers experimentally
the way you test a fact you're not yet sure you believe.

"New glove?" he asks, entirely too innocently for a boy who has clearly
already worked out that it is not, in fact, a new glove at all.

"Mended," I say.

"By whom?"

"You know by whom, Tomas."

"I do," he agrees, with the specific satisfaction of a nineteen-year-old
who has just confirmed something he already suspected, and goes to put
the kettle on without further comment, which is, from him, its own kind
of mercy.

·

The terms sheet sits on the record-room table for three full days
before I finally file it properly, mostly because I keep finding
reasons to reread it — checking the wording, I tell myself, though I
know every clause of it by heart within the first hour of writing it
down.

Aldric comes to the cottage each of those three evenings with the same
unhurried regularity he brought to the road below Highstone, chopping
wood without being asked, carrying water further than I'd have carried
it myself, precisely as promised, and asking, each evening, a single
question about the actual shape of my work that I find myself,
somewhat to my own surprise, genuinely wanting to answer.

"How do you know which cairns to inspect first," he asks, the second
evening, watching me sort the day's reports by urgency the way I have
sorted them every evening for six years.

"Age, mostly," I say. "The older stones hold their charge longer, but
they also crack more easily once they finally start to go, so I check
them more often even though they need it less frequently, on the
theory that catching a crack early costs less than repairing one
that's already spread."

"That's not so different from grafting," Aldric says. "An old tree
holds a graft better, mostly, but it also splits worse if you cut it
wrong. You learn to read which trees can bear which kind of cut."

"I hadn't thought of it that way," I say, "but I suppose it's the same
principle, dressed in different materials."

"Most principles are," Aldric says, "once you look past what they're
made of."

I find myself, listening to him, doing the thing I rarely do with
anyone — actually enjoying an evening's ordinary conversation, rather
than simply enduring it until I can retreat to my own thoughts. It
unsettles me slightly, the enjoying, in the specific way that anything
unfamiliar tends to unsettle me, though I notice, by the third evening,
that the unsettlement itself has started to feel less like a warning
and more like the ordinary discomfort of learning something new.

"You're different than I expected," I tell him, on the third evening,
because I have decided that the observation deserves saying rather than
simply filing away unspoken.

"Different how?"

"I expected someone who volunteered for a binding sight unseen to be
either reckless or performing nobility for an audience. You're neither.
You're simply — steady. About all of it. The binding, the cost, the
stranger you've been bound to. I don't know what to do with steady. I'm
not certain I've encountered much of it, in my adult life, from anyone
who wasn't being paid to provide it."

"I'm not steady because it's easy," Aldric says. "I'm steady because
the alternative costs more, in the long run, than the steadiness does.
I learned that young, watching my father manage a bad harvest without
once panicking in front of Sela and me, even when I know, now, looking
back, that he must have been considerably more frightened than he ever
let either of us see."

"That's not the same as not being frightened."

"No," Aldric agrees. "It's a different skill entirely. I'm not sure
it's always the healthier one. I'm only sure it's the one I have."

I think about that sentence for a long while after he's gone home for
the evening, turning it over the way I turn over most of what he says
that lands harder than its plainness would suggest, and find myself,
filing the terms sheet away at last, considerably less certain than I
was three days ago that steadiness and honesty are, in fact, the same
thing, or that either of us has yet worked out how to properly tell the
two apart.

·

I dream, that night, for the first time in longer than I can properly
place, of my mother — not Ossian, my actual mother, dead since I was
seven, a woman I remember mostly in fragments: the particular blue of
a shawl she wore constantly, the smell of the bread she baked every
third day without fail, a voice I can no longer properly recall the
pitch of, however hard I try.

I have never told anyone at Highstone that I had a mother at all,
beyond the bare fact recorded in whatever administrative document
brought me into Ossian's care at nineteen. It has always felt, telling
that particular story, like handing someone a piece of myself I am not
certain I can afford to have handled carelessly.

I tell Aldric the following morning, over the kettle's own slow boil,
mostly because the dream has left me unsettled enough that keeping it
entirely private feels, for the first time, like more effort than it's
worth.

"I dreamed about my mother last night," I say. "I don't talk about her,
generally. I'm not certain why I'm telling you now."

"You don't have to have a reason," Aldric says, simply. "I'd like to
hear about her, if you're willing to tell me. Whatever you remember."

I tell him the fragments — the shawl, the bread, the voice I can't
quite place — and watch him listen properly, without once trying to
fill in the gaps with reassurance or comment, simply receiving what I
have to offer exactly as it is.

"Thank you," he says, when I've finished, "for trusting me with that."

"I don't know why it felt important to say," I admit. "It's such a
small thing, in the scheme of everything else currently happening."

"I don't think it's small," Aldric says. "I think it's the opposite of
small. I think you've just handed me the first piece of yourself that
existed entirely before any of this — before the rite, before Ossian,
before the whole architecture of duty you've built your adult life
around. That's not nothing. That's rather everything, I'd say."

I don't have a ready response to being understood quite that
precisely, so I simply let the kettle finish its boil, and pour us both
tea, and carry the specific warmth of having been properly heard
through the rest of that morning's ordinary business.

·

Tomas, arriving partway through the morning to collect the day's
reports, finds the two of us still sitting over the tea, considerably
more relaxed than our usual professional posture, and clocks it
immediately, the way he clocks most things he isn't technically meant
to notice.

"Am I interrupting something," he asks, with the specific innocence of
a young man who already knows the answer and is asking purely for the
pleasure of watching us both scramble slightly to compose ourselves.

"You're not interrupting anything, Tomas."

"I only ask because you're both sitting considerably closer together
than the tea service strictly requires."

"The tea service requires exactly this much proximity," I say, with as
much dignity as I can currently muster. "It's a small table."

"Of course it is," Tomas says, entirely unconvinced, and sets the
morning's reports down with exaggerated care, as though handling
something considerably more fragile than paperwork. "I'll just leave
these here, then, and let the two of you get back to your very
proximity-requiring tea."

He goes, whistling something tuneless and entirely too pleased with
himself, and Aldric watches him leave with a small, amused shake of his
head.

"He's going to be insufferable about this for weeks," Aldric observes.

"He's already insufferable about it," I say. "I don't think there's a
version of this arrangement that doesn't involve considerable teasing
from a nineteen-year-old who's appointed himself its official chronicler."

"I find I don't mind it as much as I probably should," Aldric admits,
and I find, turning the observation over, that I don't either — a
small, unexpected comfort, having someone else's genuine delight
folded into whatever this is becoming, rather than simply the two of
us managing it alone, in careful privacy.

·

The terms sheet resurfaces a week later, when Tomas, sorting through
the record-room's own filing on some unrelated errand, comes across it
by accident and reads it before either of us thinks to stop him.

"Separate rooms," he reads aloud, entirely too delighted with himself.
"Separate meals unless the rite requires otherwise. No pretending at
anything the rite hasn't made true." He looks up at me, eyebrows
raised. "This reads like a legal document. Did you actually make him
sign this?"

"It was a reasonable precaution," I say, "for two strangers suddenly
required to share a household."

"It's the least romantic document I've ever read," Tomas says,
"and I include the crown's actual grain-exemption contracts in that
assessment."

"It wasn't meant to be romantic. It was meant to be clear."

"Well, it's certainly clear," Tomas says, examining Aldric's added
line at the bottom with obvious relish. "He added a term. That's rather
sweet, actually, now that I look at it properly. Practically poetry,
by the standard the rest of the document sets."

"Give it back, Tomas."

"I'm simply appreciating the historical record," he says, though he
does hand it over, still grinning. "I'd like it noted, for that same
record, that I predicted this arrangement would outgrow its own terms
sheet within the month, and I fully intend to remind both of you of
that prediction once it happens."

"Nothing has outgrown anything," I say, with as much composure as I
can currently manage, filing the sheet properly away at last, out of
his reach.

"Of course not," Tomas says, entirely unconvinced, and goes back to
his own work, whistling, and I find myself, once he's gone, rereading
the sheet one final time before setting it in the drawer for good,
newly aware of exactly how much has already shifted since the evening
I wrote it, without either of us formally amending a single clause.


## Chapter Four — First Frost, First Crack (Isla)

A routine inspection, nothing more, the sort of errand I have run
perhaps four hundred times in the six years since I became the
northern line's sole practitioner: walk to the nearest standing cairn,
lay a hand against the stone, read the charge it's holding, note the
reading in the ledger, walk home, exactly as unremarkable as every one
of the four hundred inspections that came before it.

The morning holds the particular stillness of first frost properly
arriving, the whole valley gone quiet and white overnight, and I find
myself grateful, walking out to the waystone, for an errand ordinary
enough to require no thought at all beyond simply placing one foot
after the other.

Aldric comes with me because the rite pulls at him when I go too far
from the line without him, an ache he describes badly and does not
complain about, in the specific tone of a man reporting a fact about
the weather rather than a grievance about his own body.

"It's not pain, exactly," he says, on the walk out, working the
description the way he works most things that cost him — carefully,
without asking anyone to make it smaller than it is. "More like a
room you've left, and can't stop feeling the shape of, from a distance."

"That's a good description," I say, meaning it, and surprised, hearing
myself say it, by how rarely I offer that particular compliment to
anyone.

"I've had a fortnight to work on it," Aldric says. "You've had six
years to get used to reading stone. I've had a fortnight to get used
to being read by one, secondhand, through you. I thought the least I
could do was describe it properly, since you're the one who has to
make sense of whatever I hand you."

We reach the nearest standing cairn — not the Long Reach, a smaller
waystone closer to Highstone, unremarkable except for the fact that it
has stood, uninterrupted, for longer than either of our own lifetimes
combined — and I lay my palm flat against the frost-veined granite, the
way I have laid a hand against stone perhaps four hundred times, to
read what it's holding and how much longer it intends to hold it.

What arrives, this time, is not the stone's charge at all.

It arrives sideways, uninvited, the way the warmth arrived at the
binding — except larger now, specific in a way that terrifies me before
I've even fully identified what I'm feeling: a cold ache, precise,
located in the smallest two fingers of a hand that is not, currently,
touching any stone at all. Aldric's hand. His left. A cold that has
nothing to do with the winter air around either of us and everything to
do with whatever the rite has quietly begun doing to him, day by day,
simply for standing near enough to a wardwright doing her ordinary work.

I take my hand off the stone as though it has burned me, though nothing
about the granite has changed temperature at all.

"You felt that," Aldric says. Not a question. He has gone very still,
in the specific way of a man who has just watched someone else confirm
a fact he had hoped, however faintly, might not be a fact at all.

"I felt something," I say, which is the closest to honest I have
managed with anyone in longer than I can accurately place, and
considerably less than the whole truth, which is that I felt his cold
hand arrive in my own chest as plainly as if it had been my own hand
going numb.

"How much," he asks. "Of what I feel. How much of it actually reaches
you."

I do not have a precise answer. I have never once, in six years of
professional practice, encountered a description in any text —
Ossian's own careful hand included — of a bond doing what this one has
just done, unbidden, without either of us calling for it, without any
rite requiring it of either of us at all.

"I don't know yet," I say, honestly. "More than I expected. More,
I think, than either of us bargained for, standing at that binding a
fortnight ago believing the cord did nothing once the knot closed."

Aldric flexes the hand in question, experimentally, the way you test a
fact you're not yet ready to fully believe, and I watch him do it with
the specific attention of a woman cataloguing, for the first time,
exactly how much of another person's cost she is now going to be able
to feel arriving, whether or not either of them has decided, yet, what
to do with that particular unasked-for gift.

"Does it frighten you," he asks. "Feeling it."

"Yes," I say, because it is true, and because I have decided, somewhere
in the last fortnight, that lying to this particular man costs more
than it's worth. "I've spent six years believing, professionally, in
the price the rite extracts. I've never once, until just now, felt it
happen to someone whose face I can put a name to while it's happening."

"I'm sorry," Aldric says, "that knowing costs you something too. I
didn't account for that, volunteering. I only ever did the arithmetic
on what it would cost me."

"You couldn't have accounted for it," I say. "Nobody's bound a hearth
to a wardwright who feels this precisely in over a century, as far as
the record shows. We're both finding out what this actually is at the
same time."

We stand at the waystone a while longer, neither of us in any
particular hurry to walk back toward whatever the cottage has quietly
become in the fortnight since the binding, and I find myself doing the
thing I did on the road below Highstone — running the cold list,
silently, item by item — except this time the list has stopped being
useful in the way lists are usually useful to me. It keeps arriving,
every single time, at the same unhelpful conclusion: that whatever this
is, arrangement or accident or something the old rite never properly
accounted for, it has already, in a fortnight, stopped behaving like
paperwork.

"We should walk back," I say, eventually, because someone has to say
it, and because standing at a waystone doing arithmetic that refuses to
resolve is not, strictly, useful to either of us.

"We should," Aldric agrees, and does not move for another moment
regardless, and neither, for that same moment, do I.

Neither of us names what just happened, on the walk back, in either
direction. But I feel it settle into the shape of a fact between us —
solid, load-bearing, the kind of thing I suspect I will find out, much
later, I have been leaning my whole weight against since the exact
second his cold hand arrived in my own chest without either of us
asking it to.

The cottage, when we reach it, has two fires lit rather than one, which
neither of us discusses, and two chairs pulled to the hearth rather
than the single one that has stood there alone for six years, which
neither of us discusses either, and I go to bed that night having felt,
for the first time since my mentor died and left me the whole of the
northern line's arithmetic to carry by myself, something arrive in my
own chest that was never, strictly, mine to begin with, and finding
that I do not, on reflection, especially want to give it back.

·

I write to the crown's rite-office the following morning, a formal
inquiry regarding any prior record of a bond exhibiting involuntary
sensation transfer beyond the standard cost-accounting the rite
requires, phrased in the careful, bloodless language the office
prefers, and receive, within the week, a reply that tells me
considerably more by what it doesn't say than by what it does.

*No such record exists in the current archive,* the reply states,
in a hand I don't recognize, signed only with an official seal rather
than a name. *Should further instances arise, the office would welcome
notification for the purposes of the historical record.*

I read the reply three times, looking for the specific bureaucratic
evasion I suspect is buried somewhere in its careful phrasing, and
find, on the third read, the detail that unsettles me most: *the
current archive*. Not *no such record has ever existed*. Simply that
none exists in whatever portion of the archive the crown's office
currently maintains — a phrasing that leaves open, deliberately or
carelessly, the possibility that older records, kept elsewhere, by
different hands, might tell a considerably different story.

I do not mention the letter to Aldric immediately. I tell myself this
is because there is nothing conclusive to report, though I understand,
turning the reply over at my own kitchen table, that it is closer to
the same habit I am only beginning to recognize in myself: the
instinct to hold uncertain, potentially frightening information alone
until I have made proper sense of it, rather than sharing the
uncertainty itself and letting someone else help carry the weight of
not knowing.

Tomas finds the letter on the table that evening and reads it before
I can stop him, apprentice's privilege, three years deep.

"You're not going to tell him," Tomas says, not quite a question.

"Not yet," I say. "There's nothing conclusive in it."

"There's something in it," Tomas says. "I don't know exactly what.
But I know you well enough to see you turning it over, and I'd rather
you turned it over with someone than alone at this table at midnight,
same as always."

"I'm not turning it over alone," I say. "I'm turning it over with you,
apparently, given you've just read my private correspondence without
asking."

"That's not the same as him," Tomas says, gently, "and you know it
isn't."

I do know it isn't, and I do not have a ready defense against being
called out so precisely by a nineteen-year-old who has spent three
years learning to read exactly this kind of evasion in me, so I simply
fold the letter and set it aside, and resolve, without quite deciding
when, to mention it to Aldric properly, once I've found the words that
don't make the uncertainty sound larger than it currently is.

·

I use the delay, unproductively, to walk the northern stretch of the
line alone the following morning, checking three waystones that don't,
strictly, need checking yet, on the theory that physical activity might
settle whatever the letter has unsettled in me more efficiently than
simply sitting with it would.

It does not, particularly, settle anything. I find myself, instead,
doing the arithmetic I have avoided doing properly since the binding —
not the practical arithmetic of cairns and charges, the considerably
less comfortable arithmetic of exactly how much of my own guardedness
is genuine caution and how much is simply habit, worn so deep by six
years of solitary practice that I no longer properly distinguish
between the two.

I think about Ossian, walking the third waystone's perimeter, and the
particular way he never once, in seven years of apprenticeship, spoke
to me about anything beyond the rite's own mechanics. I understood
that, at nineteen, as simply how a wardwright's training properly
proceeded — focused, disciplined, uncluttered by anything personal. I
understand it now, considerably differently, as its own kind of
loneliness, handed down from teacher to student without either of them
ever examining whether it needed to be handed down at all.

I do not want to hand that particular inheritance to Tomas. I have
already, I realize, walking the last stretch back toward Highstone,
begun doing something rather different with him than Ossian ever did
with me — the plain questions about how he's actually doing, the
genuine interest in his own history rather than merely his technical
progress. I had not, until this exact moment, properly recognized that
shift as a deliberate choice rather than simply an accident of my own
different temperament.

I resolve, reaching the keep's own gate, to be considerably more
deliberate about it going forward — not simply training a wardwright,
the way Ossian trained me, but raising one, properly, with all the
attention and honesty that word actually implies.

·

I find Aldric at the cottage that evening considerably earlier than
usual, having apparently finished the day's orchard work faster than
planned specifically to have more time before dinner.

"You're early," I observe.

"I wanted to be," he says, simply, in the particular plain register
I've come to recognize as his most honest one. "I found myself, today,
looking forward to this part of the evening more than I've looked
forward to anything in longer than I can properly place, and I decided
there wasn't any good reason to make myself wait for it."

I don't have a ready response to being told, so plainly, that I've
become a thing someone looks forward to, so I busy myself instead with
the kettle, giving my hands something practical to do while I work out
what to say.

"I feel the same," I admit, finally, "though I don't think I'd have
found the nerve to say it first."

"I know," Aldric says, gently, no criticism in it at all. "I don't
think you need to find that nerve every time. I'm happy to go first,
as often as it takes, until saying things plainly starts feeling less
like jumping off something and more like simply talking."

"That's a generous offer."

"It's not entirely generosity," Aldric admits. "I find I rather enjoy
being the one who gets to say the true thing first, this time, after a
lifetime of being the one who never quite manages to ask for what he
actually wants."

We sit together over the kettle's slow boil, and I find myself,
watching him settle into the good chair with the specific ease of a man
who has decided, somewhere in these past days, that this cottage is
somewhere he belongs rather than merely somewhere the rite requires him
to be, considerably more certain than I was this morning that whatever
we are building together is worth every uncertain step it demands of
either of us.

·

I tell him about the rite-office letter that same evening, finally,
having decided over the kettle's slow boil that the delay has already
cost more than the telling ever would.

"I should have told you two days ago," I say, handing him the letter.
"I told myself there was nothing conclusive in it. I understand now
that wasn't the real reason I waited."

Aldric reads it carefully, the way he reads most things that matter,
and looks up once he's finished.

"What was the real reason," he asks, gently, no accusation in it at
all.

"I wanted to be the one who understood it first," I admit. "I've spent
six years being the person who understands things before anyone else
needs to worry about them. I think I wasn't ready to hand you an
uncertainty before I'd resolved it into something manageable."

"I'd rather have the uncertainty," Aldric says, echoing something he's
said to me before, though I notice he doesn't say it as a rebuke,
simply as a plain restatement of what we've already agreed to. "Shared
properly, even unresolved, rather than managed alone until it's tidy
enough to hand over."

"I know," I say. "I'm finding the agreement considerably easier to make
than to actually practice, apparently, on both our parts."

"Then we're evenly matched at failing it," Aldric says, with a small,
wry smile, "which I suppose is its own kind of progress."

"I'll try to tell you sooner, next time," I say. "The frightening
things. Before I've resolved them into something comfortable."

"I'll hold you to that," Aldric says, "the same way I hope you'll hold
me to it, whenever I inevitably do the same thing myself."

We sit together with the letter between us a while longer, and I find
myself, watching the kettle finally come to its proper boil, grateful
for a man who receives an admitted failure with patience rather than
disappointment, and considerably more willing, because of it, to keep
trying at the harder practice of sharing uncertainty properly, rather
than only the tidy conclusions it eventually resolves into.


## Chapter Five — The Ward-Commissioner's Ledger (Isla)

Commissioner Osric Feld visits four days later with a ledger of his
own, one that prices the northern line's abandonment in a column headed
PROJECTED SAVINGS, and does not once, in the whole of his visit, ask how
many cairns are actually standing, or how many of them I've personally
walked out to inspect since his last visit.

He is not, I have come to understand over five years of dealing with
him, a cruel man, merely a tired one, worn down by an office that
measures its own success in numbers rather than lives properly held. I
have watched him age visibly across those five years, the particular
weathering of a man who has spent his whole career delivering news he
does not especially enjoy delivering.
He is a man who has been handed a column of numbers
by people considerably further from the line than he is and asked to
make those numbers balance, and has decided, somewhere in his own
career, that balancing them is the whole of the job, regardless of what
the balancing costs anyone standing closer to the actual stone.

"One practitioner and an apprentice for forty miles," Feld says, in the
tone of a man reciting a fact rather than confessing to a failure. He
has a way of saying true things that makes them sound, somehow, like
accusations aimed at whoever's left holding them. "The crown will want
this winter's numbers on the record, Wardwright. Properly kept, this
time, not estimated."

"The numbers are properly kept," I say. "Forty-one cairns standing.
Six reading low. I can show you the ledger myself, if the crown doubts
my arithmetic specifically."

"I don't doubt your arithmetic," Feld says. "I doubt your resources.
There's a difference, and it isn't one I'm in a position to solve for
you, whatever sympathy I might personally have for the position you're
in."

Tomas, listening from the doorway with the day's reports still in his
hands, goes very quiet in the specific way of a nineteen-year-old doing
sums about his own future that nobody his age should have to do this
early in a career. I notice it and file it, the way I file everything
I intend to address later, once the immediate business of not handing
Feld anything useful has been properly concluded.

"The binding is a fortnight old," I say. "It's early to be discussing
contingencies."

"It's never too early to discuss contingencies, Wardwright. That's
rather the whole of my job." Feld sets a second ledger on the table
between us, already half written, in a hand considerably less careful
than Ossian Thale's ever was. "The crown wants a plan for the
apprentice, in the event the current binding doesn't outlast the
season."

Aldric, present for the first time at one of these meetings, having
come down from the orchard on some pretext about grafting supplies that
neither of us has bothered to examine too closely, says nothing at all.
I feel, through the bond, the specific cold that moves through him at
the word *doesn't* — not fear exactly, something closer to a man
hearing his own mortality discussed as a line item by someone who has
never once had to feel what that line item actually costs.

"The binding will outlast the season," I say, which is not, strictly,
a fact yet. It is a refusal, offered plainly, to hand Feld the
alternative he has come here angling for.

Feld looks at me for a long moment, the specific assessing look of a
man deciding whether a refusal is worth pushing against today or
worth simply noting down and returning to later, once circumstances
have shifted in his favour.

"I hope, for everyone's sake, that it does," he says, eventually, and
gathers his ledger without further comment, and leaves a copy of the
apprentice contingency behind on the table, which I read twice that
evening and burn once, in the cottage's own hearth, watching the ink
curl and blacken with a satisfaction that is not, strictly, professional.

·

Tomas finds me afterward in the record-room, still holding the
poker I used to feed Feld's ledger to the fire, and does not ask what
was in it, because he has already guessed, correctly, that the answer
involves him specifically.

"You're not putting me forward," he says. It is not quite a question.

"No," I say. "Not while I have any say in the matter, which I intend
to keep having for as long as I possibly can."

"But if the binding doesn't hold—"

"It's going to hold," I say, with more certainty than the arithmetic
currently supports, because Tomas is nineteen and has enough of his own
weight to carry without also carrying the specific terror of watching
the two adults responsible for his safety admit, out loud, that they
don't yet know how to guarantee it.

He accepts this the way he accepts most things I tell him with more
confidence than I actually possess — not fully believing it, but
choosing, for tonight, to let the belief stand undisturbed, because the
alternative is a version of tonight neither of us has the resources to
survive properly.

Aldric finds me later still, once Tomas has gone to his own quarters
and the fire has burned down to the specific low, steady heat that
means the cottage has settled for the night.

"He's frightened," Aldric says, meaning Tomas, though I suspect he
means himself equally, in the careful, indirect way he has of admitting
things that cost him.

"I know," I say.

"Are you?"

I consider the question properly, the way I have learned, in a
fortnight, that he asks most of his questions — plainly, expecting a
plain answer rather than a managed one.

"Yes," I say. "I'm frightened that Feld's arithmetic is going to
outpace whatever arithmetic I can produce to counter it. I'm frightened
that Tomas is going to end up spent for a line I was supposed to be
sufficient to hold alone. And I'm frightened," I add, because I have
decided, somewhere in the last fortnight, that half-truths cost more
between us than the whole ones do, "of a considerably smaller thing
than either of those, which I'm not yet ready to say out loud."

Aldric does not push for the rest of it. He simply sits with me by the
