low fire a while longer, in the specific companionable silence of two
people who have each, separately, decided that some fears are better
carried side by side than solved on the spot, and it is, I think,
watching the last of the flames settle, the first evening since my
mentor's death that fear has felt like something shared rather than
something I am solely responsible for managing.

·

I tell him about the rite-office letter two days later, once I've
found words for it that don't overstate what little it actually
proves, sitting at the same kitchen table where Tomas first caught me
turning it over.

"There's a chance," I say, carefully, "that what's happening between
us — the sensation, the involuntary transfer — has happened before.
Somewhere. To someone. The crown's current archive has no record of
it, but the phrasing of their reply leaves room for an older record
they either don't have or haven't looked for."

Aldric reads the letter himself, slowly, the way he reads most things
that matter to him, giving each sentence its proper weight before
moving to the next.

"You've been sitting with this since the letter arrived," he says,
finally. Not an accusation. A simple observation, offered plainly, in
the tone he uses for facts rather than criticisms.

"Since Tomas caught me with it, two days ago," I admit. "I wanted to
have something conclusive to tell you, rather than simply handing you
my own uncertainty."

"I'd rather have the uncertainty," Aldric says, "shared properly, than
wait for you to resolve it alone first. That's rather the whole of what
I thought we'd agreed to, at the terms table."

"I know," I say. "I'm finding the agreement considerably easier to make
than to actually practice."

"So am I," Aldric admits, "if it's any comfort. I very nearly didn't
mention how much the byre thaw cost Isla — you — cost you, the other
day, before deciding the mentioning mattered more than my own
discomfort with admitting it."

I look at him for a long moment, turning that over — a fortnight in,
and already we are each catching the other in the exact same failure,
gently, without either of us using the catching as an accusation.

"Perhaps that's simply what this is going to look like," I say,
eventually. "Not two people who've perfectly mastered honesty
overnight. Two people who keep failing at it in small ways and keep
choosing, afterward, to correct the failure rather than let it stand."

"I can live with that version," Aldric says, "considerably more easily
than I could live with either of us pretending we'd already got it
right."

We sit with the letter between us a while longer, the fire settling
into its low, steady burn, and I find myself grateful, watching him
fold the letter carefully rather than dismissing it, for a man who
takes my uncertainties as seriously as he takes my facts, and who has
never once, in the whole of our short acquaintance, made me feel
foolish for not yet having all the answers he might reasonably have
expected a wardwright of six years' standing to possess.

·

Feld's second visit, a week later, is considerably shorter than the
first, and considerably more revealing for its brevity.

"I've had a report," he says, "of unusual sensation transfer associated
with your binding. I trust you'll be forthcoming about anything
relevant to the crown's ongoing assessment, rather than letting me
learn of such things secondhand, through whatever channel apparently
finds its way to my office faster than your own correspondence does."

I understand, hearing this, that someone at Highstone has already
mentioned the involuntary bond sensations to someone connected to
Feld's office — Tomas, perhaps, in some innocent aside, or one of the
witnesses at the binding itself, speculating about the specific
stillness I failed to properly manage that evening.

"There's nothing conclusive to report," I say, which is, technically,
still true, though I feel the specific discomfort of a half-truth
offered to a man I have never fully trusted with the whole of anything.

"I'd remind you, Wardwright, that the crown's interest in this
binding's particulars isn't merely bureaucratic curiosity. Should this
bond exhibit properties beyond the standard rite's accounting, the
crown may have grounds to reassess the whole arrangement — including,
potentially, whether the current hearth remains the appropriate one,
regardless of how the binding was originally assigned."

The threat, delivered in Feld's own carefully bloodless register, lands
considerably harder than his usual bureaucratic tedium.

"The binding stands as assigned," I say, with more force than I
generally allow myself in these meetings. "Whatever unusual properties
it exhibits are a matter for the rite-keepers to assess, not the
crown's own convenience."

Feld looks at me for a long moment, and I understand, from his
expression, that he has heard something in my own tone he did not
expect from our previous meetings — a wardwright no longer entirely
willing to simply absorb whatever pressure his office applies.

"I'll note your position," he says, finally, and leaves, and I stand in
the record-room a long while afterward, considering exactly how much
more scrutiny this whole strange bond is likely to attract, the longer
either Aldric or I let its particulars remain unexplained.

·

I find Tomas waiting outside the record-room door, having clearly
overheard at least the tail end of Feld's visit, his face carrying the
specific worried stillness he's worn more often, this past week, than
I'd like to see on someone his age.

"He's threatening you," Tomas says. Not a question.

"He's doing his job," I say, "which happens to involve a certain
amount of threatening, when the crown's own arithmetic feels
threatened first."

"That's not the same as it being acceptable."

"No," I agree. "It isn't. But it is, unfortunately, the shape of the
job I've agreed to do, working within an institution that answers to
people who've never once stood at a failing cairn and felt what the
line actually costs to hold."

Tomas considers this, turning it over with the particular gravity he's
been showing more and more, these past weeks, toward anything that
touches the actual mechanics of power on this line.

"When I'm wardwright," he says, finally, "I don't want to spend my
whole career simply managing Feld's office. I want to change how it
operates, properly, so the next apprentice doesn't have to learn this
particular lesson the hard way."

"That's an ambitious goal," I say, "and I mean that as a genuine
compliment. I'd like to see you manage it. I think you might, actually,
have both the patience and the stubbornness the job requires."

"I learned both from you," Tomas says, simply, and goes back to his
own reports, leaving me standing in the record-room doorway with the
specific, considerable pride of watching an apprentice begin to imagine
a future for this line that reaches well beyond my own tenure holding
it.

·

Aldric finds me still standing there, some minutes later, and I tell
him the whole of Feld's second visit — the reported sensation transfer,
the veiled threat about reassessing the binding, the specific chill of
realizing someone at Highstone had already carried word of our own
private difficulty to the crown's office before either of us had
decided how much of it to share ourselves.

"He can't actually reassign the binding," Aldric says, though I hear,
underneath the statement, a question rather than a certainty.

"I don't know that for certain," I admit. "I know the rite itself
doesn't permit it, structurally. I don't know how much the crown's own
office might be willing to argue around that, if they decide the bond's
particular properties represent enough of a risk to justify the
argument."

"Would they actually try," Aldric asks, "separate us, over something
neither of us asked for or can control?"

"I don't know," I say, honestly, because I have decided that
half-truths cost more between us now, even the frightening ones. "I
think Feld would prefer not to. I think Feld answers to people who
might prefer it considerably more than he does, if the alternative
looks, on paper, like an unmanageable liability."

Aldric is quiet for a long moment, and I watch him do the same
arithmetic I've been running since Feld's visit — weighing, I suspect,
exactly how much of his own safety depends on an arrangement neither of
us has full control over.

"Then we make certain there's nothing left to reassess," he says,
finally. "We understand this bond properly, ourselves, before Feld's
office ever gets the chance to define it for us."

"I'd like that," I say, and mean it, grateful for a man who meets a
frightening piece of news with a plan rather than simply absorbing the
fear silently, and we sit together in the record-room a while longer,
turning over exactly how much work still lies ahead of understanding a
bond that has already, in a fortnight, upended considerably more of my
professional certainty than six years of ordinary practice ever
managed.

·

Tomas finds us there, some while later, and listens to a careful
summary of Feld's veiled threat with the particular gravity he reserves
for anything touching the household's safety.

"Can he actually do that," he asks, "reassign the binding?"

"We don't know," I admit. "We're going to find out properly, before he
gets the chance to decide unilaterally."

"I'd like to help," Tomas says, "if there's research to be done. I've
gotten rather good at digging through old records, this past year."

"I'd welcome that," I say, and the three of us spend the rest of the
evening mapping out exactly what needs investigating, a small,
determined household preparing to meet whatever the crown's office
decides to throw at us, properly, together, rather than each of us
separately bracing for the worst.


## Chapter Six — Thaw a Stranger's Byre (Aldric)

The boy who comes running to Hollow Farm's gate is barefoot despite the
bitter frost, which tells me, before he's said a single word, exactly how much
urgency his family has decided the situation warrants, and how little
time they believed they had to spare for proper boots.

"The byre's frozen through," he says, breathless, to whoever will
listen, and it happens that Isla and I are the two people closest to
listening, having stopped at Hollow Farm on the way back from the outer
waystones to trade grafting wax for a sack of the farm's good seed
potatoes. "Da says the stock won't survive the night if it doesn't
thaw."

It isn't a cairn. It isn't, by any reading of the rite I've been given
since the binding, Isla's charge to spend anything on at all — Hollow
Farm sits three miles off the line proper, well outside any ward she's
sworn to maintain, and the crown's ledgers would not, I suspect, count
a single hour of whatever she's about to do as chargeable work.

She goes anyway.

I watch her walk to the byre without a word of explanation to the
farmer, without so much as glancing back to see whether I intend to
follow, and lay both hands flat against the frozen timber wall the way
I have watched her lay hands against standing cairns a dozen times now,
except this time there is no rite requiring it, no binding compelling
it, nothing at all except a woman deciding, on her own authority, that
a stranger's stock matters enough to spend something on that she isn't
going to write down anywhere afterward.

The warmth that moves out of her and into that wall is not small. I
know this because I feel, standing close enough to catch the edge of
it, the specific cost of it arrive somewhere in my own chest — not the
sharp, located ache the bond usually hands me, something broader,
heavier, like watching someone empty a pocket they'd told you was
already spent.

She goes grey at the mouth by the time the wall's frost has properly
broken. I have seen her go pale before, at the Long Reach cairn, in the
aftermath of ordinary, sanctioned ward-work. I have never seen her go
this particular colour, the specific grey of a person who has drawn on
a reserve she wasn't planning to touch tonight.

"You're finished," I say, more firmly than I've said anything to her
yet, and put a hand under her arm before she can wave me off, which she
tries to do anyway, on reflex, the way she tries to wave off most
offers of help before she's properly considered whether she needs them.

The farmer thanks her three separate times before we're even off his
land, pressing a second sack of seed potatoes on us that neither of us
asked for, and Isla accepts the thanks the way she accepts most
compliments — with the specific discomfort of a woman being handed
something she doesn't believe she's earned, though I watched her earn
every last measure of it not ten minutes ago.

"You didn't log that," I say, once we're walking again, the farm gone
small behind us.

"It wasn't a ward," she says. "It doesn't go in the ledger."

"That's not the reason you didn't log it," I say. It isn't a question.
I have watched her long enough now, in a fortnight, to recognize the
specific shape of a woman managing a fact she doesn't want examined,
because I have spent my whole adult life doing the exact same thing,
from the inside, and I know precisely what it looks like from the
outside now that I've finally got the angle to see it properly.

She doesn't answer, which is itself an answer, and I do not push
further, because I understand — watching her walk beside me, steadier
now but still paler than the walk out warranted — that I have just seen
the exact shape of my own flaw, worn on someone else's face, doing the
exact thing I do: taking a cost silently, in the dark, and calling the
silence discipline rather than what it actually is, which is a
refusal to let anyone else help carry a weight that was never meant to
be carried alone.

"How long have you been doing that," I ask. "Spending yourself on
things the ledger doesn't require."

"I don't know what you mean," Isla says, which is the first time,
in a fortnight, that I have heard her say something I am entirely
certain is not true.

I let it go, for now, the way she lets my own evasions go when she
suspects, correctly, that pushing too hard too early will only teach me
to hide the next one better. But I watch her the rest of the walk back
with the specific, quiet attention of a man who has just recognized,
in someone else, a shape he knows intimately from the inside, and who
has started, somewhere in the last quarter mile, to wonder whether
either of us is going to survive this winter if neither of us ever
learns to stop paying alone.

·

Sela is waiting at the orchard gate when I finally make it home that
evening, later than I told her I'd be, with two sacks of seed potatoes
and considerably more on my mind than the errand accounted for.

"You look like you've seen something," she says, taking one of the
sacks without being asked, the way she takes most burdens without
being asked, a family trait I am only now, watching Isla do the same
thing at Hollow Farm, beginning to properly recognize as a pattern
rather than simply how things are done.

"I think I have," I say, and do not, for tonight, explain further,
because I am still working out, myself, exactly what it is I saw, and
what it might mean that the woman the rite bound me to seems to be
carrying precisely the same weight I've spent my whole life carrying,
alone, in the dark, and calling it something other than what it is.

·

Sela doesn't let the matter rest there, of course. She has never once,
in twenty-four years, let a matter rest simply because I've decided the
conversation is finished.

"You're going to have to tell me eventually," she says, the following
morning, over the grain-house's own modest fire, "what actually
happened at Hollow Farm. You came home looking like a man who'd
watched something rearrange his whole understanding of a person, and
you've said exactly four words about it since."

"Isla thawed a farmer's byre," I say. "At her own cost. Off the
record."

"That's the fact of it," Sela says. "I'm asking about the part that put
that look on your face."

I consider how much to tell her, weighing the specific loyalty I feel
toward Isla's own carefully guarded privacy against the equally
specific loyalty I owe my sister, who has spent her whole life reading
me more accurately than I generally want to be read.

"I think she's been doing that for longer than anyone knows," I say,
finally. "I think she's been quietly spending herself on this line for
years, the same way I spent myself on your exemption, and calling it
duty instead of what it actually is."

Sela is quiet for a long moment, turning that over with the particular
care she gives most things that touch too close to our own family's
history.

"That's not a small thing to recognize in someone else," she says,
eventually. "Especially not this fast."

"No," I agree.

"Does it frighten you? Recognizing it?"

"Yes," I admit. "Because I don't know how to talk someone out of a
habit I've never managed to talk myself out of. I don't know how to be
the person who finally interrupts that pattern in her, when I've never
once let anyone interrupt it in me."

Sela reaches over and takes my hand — the good one, still — and holds
it a moment, the way she used to when we were children and the winters
felt considerably larger than either of us.

"Maybe you don't have to talk her out of it," she says. "Maybe you
just have to keep noticing, out loud, the way you're doing now, until
noticing becomes its own kind of interruption. That's rather all I've
ever managed to do with you, and it hasn't worked yet, but I like to
think it's slowly, eventually, working."

I don't have a ready answer to that, so I simply sit with her by the
fire a while longer, turning the whole of Hollow Farm over again in my
mind, and find, walking home later that evening, that Sela's words have
settled somewhere useful, a small stone added to whatever foundation
I'm apparently building, one recognition at a time, toward whatever
this arrangement with Isla is eventually going to become.

·

The Hollow Farm family sends word, a few days later, that the byre has
held through the whole of the following week without further incident,
and includes, with the message, a small parcel of preserved fruit that
neither Isla nor I properly expected, given the specific unofficial
nature of what she did for them.

"I don't accept payment for unlogged work," Isla says, examining the
parcel with the particular discomfort of someone unaccustomed to being
thanked so plainly.

"It's not payment," I say. "It's a family telling you they noticed
what you did, and that they're grateful for it, in the only language
most farming families have available for gratitude this large. I'd
accept it, if I were you. Refusing it would cost them something they'd
rather not have to carry — the specific indignity of a kindness
declined."

Isla considers this, turning the parcel over in her hands.

"When did you get so wise about accepting things gracefully," she
asks, "for a man who mended my glove and refused to let me thank him
for it properly?"

"I'm still working on the accepting-things-myself part," I admit. "I
find it considerably easier to counsel someone else toward it than to
practice it myself."

"That seems to be a pattern with both of us," Isla says, and there's
something in the observation — dry, exact, entirely accurate — that
makes me laugh, properly, surprised out of me the way her own laugh
sometimes surprises her.

We share the preserved fruit that evening, an ordinary domestic
pleasure neither of us has properly indulged in since the binding, and
I find myself, watching her enjoy something simple without the usual
weight of arithmetic behind it, increasingly certain that whatever we
are slowly building together is worth every uncertain step it's taking
to construct.

·

Sela's own reaction to the Hollow Farm parcel, once she hears the full
story, is considerably more pointed than either Isla's or my own.

"You thawed a stranger's byre at your own cost and didn't tell anyone,"
she says to Isla, the following week, with the specific bluntness she
reserves for observations she considers too important to soften. "That's
precisely the kind of thing Aldric would do, and I've spent our whole
lives trying, mostly without success, to talk him out of doing it."

"It needed doing," Isla says, simply.

"Plenty of things need doing," Sela says. "Not all of them need doing
by the one person who's already spending herself thin on forty other
things nobody else can manage. I understand the impulse. I watched my
own father die of exactly this impulse, dressed up as duty. I'd rather
not watch it claim anyone else I've come to care about."

Isla doesn't have a ready response to being told, so plainly, that
Sela has already begun to care about her specifically, and I watch her
absorb the observation with the same careful stillness she gives most
things that land harder than their brevity suggests.

"I'm trying to do better," Isla says, finally. "I don't know that I'm
succeeding yet. But I'm trying."

"That's all any of us can ask," Sela says, gentler now, and the
particular warmth that passes between the two of them in that moment
settles something in my own chest that I hadn't realized needed
settling — the specific relief of watching my sister extend to Isla
exactly the same fierce, unmanaged care she's always extended to me.

·

Isla and I walk back to the cottage together once Sela's own visit has
properly concluded, and I find myself, partway down the track, turning
over the whole shape of the afternoon's conversation.

"She likes you," I say. "Properly. I don't think she extends that
particular warmth to many people this quickly."

"I noticed," Isla says. "I'll admit it surprised me. I didn't expect
your sister to warm to a stranger the rite handed her brother quite
this fast."

"Neither did I," I admit. "I think she recognized something in you.
The same habit she's watched me practice my whole life, worn on
someone else's face. I think it made you easier to trust, oddly,
rather than harder."

"That's a strange kind of kinship," Isla says, "bonding over a shared
flaw rather than a shared virtue."

"Most of the bonds in this family seem to work that way," I say. "I
don't think we're particularly good at bonding over our virtues. We're
considerably more practiced at recognizing each other's damage."

Isla considers this, turning it over with the particular seriousness
she gives most things I say that land harder than their brevity
suggests.

"I don't think that's necessarily worse," she says, finally. "I think
recognizing someone's damage, and choosing to stay anyway, might be a
sturdier foundation than simply admiring someone's virtues from a safe
distance."

"I hadn't thought of it that way," I say, "but I think you're right."

We walk the rest of the way home in comfortable quiet, and I find
myself, watching the light fail properly over the orchard's own bare
winter rows, grateful for a woman who keeps finding the true, steadying
shape underneath whatever difficult thing I hand her.

·

Tomas mentions Sela's visit himself the following morning, having
apparently heard some account of it already from whichever channel
keeps him reliably informed of the household's own business.

"Sela came by, I hear," he says, setting down the day's reports.

"She did," Isla says. "We had a rather frank conversation about
matching flaws."

"That sounds like her," Tomas says, with genuine affection. "She's been
kind to me too, these past weeks, whenever I've delivered reports to
the orchard. I don't think she has to be, given I'm simply the
apprentice attached to her brother's own arrangement. She's kind
anyway."

"I've noticed that about her," Isla says. "She extends her care rather
more widely than her own brisk manner would initially suggest."

"I like her considerably," Tomas admits, somewhat shyly. "I hope that's
not an odd thing to say."

"It's not odd at all," I say. "I like her considerably too, and I've
had twenty-eight years of practice."

Tomas laughs at that, properly, and the three of us settle into the
morning's ordinary business, the whole household's own accumulated
warmth for Sela Marrow settling into something none of us feel any
particular need to examine too closely, simply grateful for its
presence.

·

I find myself, later that same day, thinking about the specific way
Isla and Sela have already started trading small kindnesses without
either of them making a particular occasion of it — a borrowed tool
here, a shared observation there, the particular ease of two women who
have decided, somewhere in a single afternoon, that they're worth
each other's trust.

"You're smiling at nothing," Isla observes, catching me at it.

"I'm thinking about how quickly you and Sela found your footing with
each other," I admit. "I spent considerable time worrying, before the
binding, about how she'd receive whoever the rite named. I needn't have
worried."

"I think we simply recognized something useful in each other early,"
Isla says. "That tends to speed things along, regardless of how
carefully either of us might otherwise have proceeded."

"I'm grateful for it," I say, "however it happened," and Isla takes my
hand, and we sit together a while longer, both grateful for a family
that keeps finding its footing faster than either of us properly
expects.


## Chapter Seven — Touch-Starved (Isla)

The track above the Long Reach cairn is bad after three days of new
snow, badly enough that I have half a mind to turn back and reschedule
the inspection for a morning when the ice hasn't had time to properly
set into the rise — except turning back would mean admitting, to
myself and to the man walking a pace behind me, that I am no longer
entirely certain of my own footing on ground I have walked four hundred
times without incident.

The snow itself is deceptively bright under the morning sun, hiding
the ice beneath a deceptively soft-looking crust, and I find myself
watching my own footing more carefully than the four hundred previous
inspections have ever required.

I go down hard, in the end, exactly where the ice has set worst, one
boot skating sideways on a slope I judged wrong by a margin that would
have been unremarkable in any other week of this winter and is,
apparently, this week, precisely enough to put me on the ground.

Aldric's hand closes on my arm before I've finished falling.

It is, on its own terms, an entirely ordinary piece of assistance — the
kind of catch that happens between any two people walking a bad track
together, the kind that would mean nothing at all between people who'd
known each other considerably longer than a fortnight, and it does not,
arriving, behave like an ordinary catch in the slightest.

He does not let go once my footing is restored. I do not, for a half
second considerably longer than balance strictly requires, pull away.
Neither of us mentions the half second, though I feel him register it —
a small, deliberate stillness, the specific quality of a man choosing
not to move first.

"You're colder than you should be," he says, eventually, meaning my
hand, which he is, I notice, still technically holding, his own palm
warm enough against my knuckles that I feel the difference in
temperature the way you feel a held breath released.

"I run cold on purpose," I say, which is not, strictly, an answer to
the observation he's actually made, and he seems to know it, though he
does not push.

"I know," he says. "You've told me. I'm not sure I entirely believed
it the first time you said it, and I'm considerably less sure now."

I do not have a ready response to that, mostly because I have spent
six years cultivating the specific cold I run on purpose as armor
against exactly this kind of observation, and it has never once, until
a man with grafting scars on both thumbs started noticing things about
me I hadn't authorized anyone to notice, failed to do its job properly.

We walk the rest of the rise not quite touching, on purpose, in the
specific way that not-touching, between two people who have just
established, plainly, that touching is available to either of them,
does considerably more work than touching outright would have managed.
I am aware of the space between our hands the entire remaining climb, a
few careful inches that feel, absurdly, more charged than the moment
his hand actually closed on my arm.

"Tell me something," I say, at the top of the rise, mostly to give my
own mind somewhere else to sit besides the inches between our hands.
"You've never once, in a fortnight, asked me anything about my own
work that wasn't strictly practical. Why not?"

"I've been waiting for you to offer it," Aldric says. "You strike me as
someone who answers questions she's asked considerably worse than she
answers ones she's decided, herself, to volunteer."

I consider this, and find, turning it over, that it is entirely
accurate — a fact about myself that I have never once had articulated
back to me so plainly, by anyone, in the whole of my adult life.

"Ask me something, then," I say. "Properly. I'll tell you if I'm
willing to answer it."

He thinks about this for longer than I expect, the particular careful
consideration of a man choosing his first proper question with the
weight of knowing he may not get many more chances at one.

"What does it feel like," he asks, finally, "setting a ward. Not the
cost of it — I've felt enough of that secondhand now to have some
notion. What does it feel like to you, doing the actual work. Is there
anything in it that isn't only cost?"

It is, I realize, turning the question over, precisely the right one to
ask — the one nobody, in six years, has ever thought to ask me,
because everyone I have ever dealt with professionally has been so
occupied with the price of the rite that no one has once wondered
whether the woman paying it might also, somewhere underneath the
paying, find something in the work itself worth having.

"There's a moment," I say, slowly, working the description out as I
go, the way he worked out his own description of the bond's ache a few
days ago, "just before the stone takes anything at all, where I can
feel exactly how much charge is already sitting in it — how close to
failing, how much longer it might hold on its own, if I simply walked
away and let it fend for itself. And there's a specific satisfaction,
every single time, in knowing precisely how much to give it. Not more
than it needs. Not less. Exactly the measure that keeps it standing
without wasting anything." I look at him, surprised at my own honesty.
"I don't think I've ever said that out loud to anyone. It sounds
smaller, said aloud, than it feels doing it."

"It doesn't sound small to me," Aldric says. "It sounds like the one
part of this whole arrangement that's actually yours. Not the crown's.
Not the rite's. Yours."

I do not have an answer for that which doesn't risk saying considerably
more than the top of a frost-slicked rise has any business hearing, so
I simply nod, once, and we stand there a while longer in the cold,
not quite touching, both of us apparently in agreement, without either
having said so, that the moment is worth more left exactly as it is
than pushed toward anything further.

·

Tomas notices the change before either of us has said anything to
anyone. He notices most things before either of us says anything,
which I have come to understand is simply the particular talent of a
nineteen-year-old who has spent three years learning to read a woman
who does not volunteer information easily.

"You came back from the Long Reach inspection in a considerably better
mood than you left in," he observes, over the evening's reports,
entirely too casually for a boy who has clearly been waiting all day
for the opportunity to say so.

"The inspection went well," I say.

"I'm sure it did," Tomas says, with a specific dry precision that tells
me he has drawn his own, considerably more accurate conclusions, and
has decided, for tonight, to be merciful about voicing them directly.

I let him have his private theory. I have, after all, very little
standing left to argue against it.

·

I go, later that same evening, to the small archive Highstone keeps
of every wardwright's personal correspondence — not the official rite
records, which the crown's office maintains its own copies of, but the
considerably less formal collection of letters and notes that
successive wardwrights have left behind, on the theory that the
private observations of a career sometimes hold more use than the
official reports ever manage to.

I am looking, specifically, for anything Ossian might have written
about sensation, about the bond's own behavior beyond the standard
cost-accounting, on the chance that his six decades of practice
uncovered something the crown's current archive has since misplaced or
never properly recorded.

I find, instead, something I was not looking for at all: a letter,
tucked between two considerably drier inspection reports, written in
Ossian's own economical hand, addressed to no one in particular, dated
some eleven years before his death.

*I have started, this winter,* it reads, *to wonder whether the cost I
take from this line is entirely the line's to take. There are nights I
give more than the rite requires, quietly, because the alternative is
watching a cairn fail that I could have held with a little more of
myself spent on it. I have not told my own apprentice this. I do not
know how to tell her, without frightening her, that the arithmetic she
believes she is learning from me is considerably more generous, in
practice, than the arithmetic I officially teach.*

I sit with that letter for a long while, in the archive's particular
hush, understanding, reading it, precisely what Ossian was admitting to
eleven years before his own death — the same habit Isla has since
confessed to me, quietly, over these past weeks. He knew. He knew, and
he never told her, and he died, in the end, having taught her, by
example rather than by word, the exact same silent over-spending that
is now, I fear, quietly killing her the same way it eventually killed
him.

I copy the letter carefully into my own hand before returning the
original to its place in the archive, uncertain, folding my copy away,
whether I will ever show it to Isla, or whether some truths are better
left where a person finds them herself, in her own time, rather than
handed over before she's ready to receive them.

Tomas finds me still sitting in the archive an hour later, and asks,
gently, what I've found.

"A pattern," I say, "older than either of us knew to look for."

He doesn't press further, which I am grateful for, and I walk home that
night carrying a considerably heavier certainty than I arrived with —
that whatever Isla and I are building together, we are, in some
fundamental sense, also fighting against a habit that has already
claimed one wardwright's life without either of us fully realizing,
until this exact evening, how deep its roots actually run.

·

I mention the archive letter to Sela the following day, in careful
general terms, unwilling to share Ossian's own private confession
without more certainty about whether that's mine to share at all, and
find her considerably more troubled by the general shape of it than I
expected.

"You're describing our own father," she says, quietly, once I've laid
out the pattern — a person who spends themselves silently, dresses the
spending up as duty, never once lets the people who love them properly
see the cost. "That's exactly what he did, all those years, and none
of us ever called it what it actually was until it was too late to
matter."

"I know," I say. "I think that's rather why it unsettled me so
thoroughly, finding it in someone else's history. It's not simply a
pattern I recognize professionally. It's the whole shape of how I was
raised to handle any difficulty at all."

"Do you think Isla can actually break it?" Sela asks. "The pattern, I
mean. Not simply notice it in herself. Actually change how she
operates."

"I don't know," I admit. "I think she's trying, harder than I've ever
seen anyone try at anything. I think the trying itself might matter
more than whether either of us ever fully succeeds."

Sela considers this, turning her own cup slowly on the grain-house
table.

"I'd like you to try too," she says, finally, plainly. "Not just notice
the pattern in someone else. Actually break it, in yourself, properly.
I've watched you do this exact thing our whole lives, Aldric, and I'm
tired of pretending I haven't."

I don't have a ready defense against being called out so precisely by
my own sister, so I simply sit with the observation, letting it settle
alongside everything else this whole strange season is teaching me
about a habit I am only now, slowly, properly beginning to recognize as
worth the considerable effort of unlearning.

·

I find Isla in the record-room, later that same day, and tell her,
plainly, about Sela's own observation — the specific pattern our father
handed down, the exact shape of the habit I am only now beginning to
properly examine.

"I think I understand something now," I say, "that I didn't understand
this morning. I've been treating my own silence as a kind of gift I'm
giving people — sparing them the burden of my difficulties. Sela's
helped me see it's closer to a withholding. I'm not sparing anyone
anything. I'm simply deciding, alone, what they're allowed to help
carry."

Isla listens to the whole of it without interrupting, the particular
attentive stillness she gives anything that costs a person to say.

"That's a considerable thing to recognize about yourself," she says,
finally, "in a single afternoon."

"I had help recognizing it," I say. "Sela mostly. You too, if I'm
honest, simply by being the kind of person who insists on the whole
truth rather than the managed version."

"I'm glad," Isla says, "that you're willing to actually look at it,
rather than simply noting the observation and continuing on exactly as
before. I've watched a great many people nod along to exactly this
kind of insight and change nothing whatsoever about their actual
behavior."

"I don't want to be one of those people," I say. "Not anymore. Not with
you specifically."

She takes my hand, and we sit together a while longer in the
record-room's quiet, and I understand, holding it, that recognizing a
lifelong habit is only the first, considerably smaller half of the
work — the harder half, the part that actually matters, is choosing,
every single day going forward, to practice something different.

·

Tomas catches me on my way out of the record-room that same evening,
holding a small stack of his own notes with the particular careful
posture of someone who's been waiting for the right moment to raise
something.

"Can I ask you something," he says, "about the archive letter Isla
mentioned. The pattern you found."

"Go on."

"Do you think it's only wardwrights who inherit it," he asks, "or do
you think it's simply how most people learn to handle difficulty,
regardless of what work they eventually end up doing?"

I consider the question seriously, surprised, as I often am, by how
much weight Tomas gives to things well beyond his years.

"I think it's considerably more common than either of us would like,"
I say. "I think most people are taught, one way or another, that
managing a difficulty alone is more responsible than sharing it. I
don't think it's unique to this line, or this family. I think it's
simply more visible here, because the costs are so much larger and so
much easier to trace back to a single decision."

"I don't want to learn it," Tomas says, with a seriousness that
surprises me. "The pattern. I've watched what it's done to you, and to
Isla, and I don't want to spend my own eventual career quietly
managing everything alone because it seemed like the responsible
thing to do at nineteen."

"Then don't," I say. "I think that's rather the whole point of
watching us fail at unlearning it properly — so you have the chance to
simply learn it differently from the start, rather than spending years
undoing a habit before you can finally set it down."

Tomas nods, considering this with real gravity, and I find myself,
watching him, grateful for a young man determined to learn from our
mistakes rather than simply repeat them out of habit or admiration.

"Thank you," he says, "for being honest about it, rather than letting
me assume the two of you have this all figured out already."

"We don't," I say. "I don't think anyone does, entirely. I think the
honest answer is that we're all simply doing our best to notice the
pattern before it costs us something we can't get back."

We part for the evening on that thought, and I walk home considerably
more grateful than usual for an apprentice wise enough to want a
different inheritance than the one this line has, until now, quietly
handed down.


## Chapter Eight — What the Ledger Doesn't Say (Aldric)

I bring Isla up to the orchard for the first time nine days after the
binding, ostensibly to inspect a cairn nobody's checked since the
snow started in earnest, actually because I want her to see where I'm
from before the rite decides that for her some other way, on its own
schedule, without either of us having had any say in the introduction.

The orchard sits above the line the way it's always sat above the
line — the last living rows of grafted trees before the ground gives
over entirely to the kind of cold that doesn't grow anything anymore,
a fact I have lived with long enough that I've mostly stopped noticing
it as strange, the way you stop noticing the specific shape of your
own house once you've lived in it long enough.

I watch Isla take in the whole shape of it as we climb, cataloguing
the rows the way she catalogues everything, and understand, watching
her, that I am showing her considerably more than an orchard this
morning — the whole quiet architecture of a life I have never properly
described to anyone the rite hadn't already bound to me.

Sela is in the grain-house when we arrive, records spread across the
counting table in the particular organized chaos of a woman who knows
exactly where everything is despite appearances suggesting otherwise.
She looks up at Isla the way a sister looks at the woman her brother's
been bound to for someone else's sake — carefully, assessing, not
unkindly, but not yet willing to spend more warmth than the
introduction strictly requires.

"So you're the wardwright," Sela says, by way of greeting.

"Isla," Isla says, offering the correction plainly, without any
particular insistence behind it, in the way she offers most
corrections — a fact stated once, and then left alone to do its own
work.

"Sela." A pause, brief, assessing. "He's told me almost nothing about
you. Which is either very good manners or very bad ones, depending on
which brother I'm currently annoyed at."

"Good manners," I say. "I didn't think it my place to describe her
before she'd had the chance to describe herself."

"That's a very diplomatic answer," Sela says, "for a man who's never
once in his life been diplomatic about anything that actually mattered
to him." She turns back to Isla, considering. "Would you like to see
the grain record? I don't generally show it to visitors. But you're not
quite a visitor anymore, are you."

She shows it to Isla without further prompting, once Isla agrees, laying
out the whole ledger of what the exemption actually cost the household —
grain traded away in the specific lean years after the crown tightened
its terms, the particular silence that fell over the orchard the week
I put my own name forward instead of letting Sela's stand exposed on
the roll.

"He never told me the rest of it," Sela says, meaning the binding,
meaning whatever the two of them currently are to each other, watching
Isla read the numbers with the specific stillness of a woman doing
arithmetic she already suspects the shape of before she's finished the
sum. "He never tells anyone the rest of anything. It's his worst habit,
and I've watched him practice it since we were children."

"I've noticed," Isla says, and there is something in the way she says
it — dry, exact, not unkind — that tells me she has, in fact, noticed a
great deal more about my particular habit of managing costs quietly
than I have yet admitted to her directly.

I leave them to the ledger and go to check the cairn at the orchard's
northern edge, mostly because I suspect the two of them will speak more
freely without me standing there managing how the conversation lands,
and because I have found, over twenty-eight years of being Sela's
brother, that she tells strangers things about me she would never say
to my face, on the theory that strangers deserve accurate information
even when I'd rather they didn't have it.

The cairn reads low, same as the last three inspections, the stone's
charge thinner than it ought to be for this early in the season. I note
it in the small record book I've started keeping myself, an
apprentice's habit I picked up without meaning to simply from watching
Isla keep hers, and stand a while longer looking north, toward the line
proper, at the specific flat grey nothing that marks where the Rime has
already taken more ground than any ledger I've seen accounts for.

When I come back down, Isla and Sela have moved from the grain record
to something considerably less formal — an old story, apparently, about
a Marrow cousin's disastrous wedding, both of them laughing in the
particular unguarded way of two people who have decided, somewhere in
the last hour, that the other one is worth actually liking rather than
merely tolerating for someone else's sake.

"You've corrupted my sister," I say, mildly, coming through the door.

"She corrupted herself," Sela says. "I merely provided the material."

Isla looks, for a moment, genuinely lighter than I have seen her look
since the binding, and I file the sight away carefully, the way I file
everything about her that I don't yet have anywhere proper to put.

·

We walk back down toward Highstone in the last of the light, Isla quiet
in the particular way that means she's turning something over rather
than simply being silent.

"Your sister's grain record doesn't balance," she says, eventually.
"Not this season. Not by a margin she can afford to keep absorbing
quietly for much longer."

"I know," I say.

"You've known for a while."

"Since before the binding," I admit. "It's part of why I put my own
name forward when the crown tightened the exemption terms. The
household couldn't have absorbed both the grain shortfall and losing
Sela's protection at once."

"You never told her that's part of your reasoning."

"No," I say. "She'd have argued. She'd have insisted on some
arrangement where the cost fell more evenly between us, and I'd
already decided, by the time the terms tightened, that I'd rather carry
it myself than watch her carry any part of it at all."

Isla is quiet for a long moment, and I understand, from the specific
quality of the silence, that she has recognized something in what I've
just said — the exact shape, perhaps, of her own habit of feeding the
failing wards her own heat and calling it, silently, nothing worth
mentioning.

"That's not generosity," she says, finally. "Not entirely. I don't
mean that unkindly. I mean it's a flaw wearing generosity's coat, and
I recognize the coat because I own one exactly like it."

I do not have a ready answer to that, mostly because I suspect, hearing
it, that she is entirely correct, and I am not yet prepared to examine
how correct.

"We should get you home before the cold gets worse," I say instead, and
she lets me change the subject, this once, though I feel her filing the
observation away all the same, for whichever later evening either of us
finally runs out of room to keep managing it quietly.

·

Sela catches up with us at the orchard gate before we've fully left,
breathless, having apparently run the whole distance from the
grain-house.

"You forgot the seed count," she says, handing Isla a folded sheet,
"for the crown's grain office. I know it's not strictly your concern,
Wardwright, but Aldric mentioned you'd offered to look it over, given
you've a better head for the crown's particular arithmetic than either
of us."

Isla takes the sheet and reads it properly, right there at the gate,
the specific focused stillness she gives any column of numbers, and I
watch Sela watch her do it, a small, considering look passing over my
sister's face that I recognize, having grown up with it aimed at every
person I've ever brought home, as the beginning of genuine approval
rather than merely polite tolerance.

"This is wrong," Isla says, after a moment, tapping a line near the
bottom. "The crown's office undercounts your winter stores by a
margin that would leave you short by spring, if they went by this
figure alone. You'll want to resubmit with the actual total, properly
itemized, or they'll base next year's exemption allotment on a number
that doesn't reflect what you actually have."

"I told him it looked low," Sela says, to me, with the specific
satisfaction of a woman whose own instincts have just been vindicated
by someone she trusts to know better. "He said the crown's office
wouldn't quibble over a small discrepancy."

"The crown's office quibbles over everything," Isla says. "That's
rather the whole of what their office does. I'd resubmit properly,
if I were you, and I'd keep a copy of the correction, in case they try
to claim the original figure stands."

Sela looks between the two of us, something settling in her expression
that I don't fully have words for yet, though I suspect, watching her,
that it's the specific relief of a woman who has spent years being the
only one in the household paying close enough attention to the crown's
particular tricks, finally finding someone else willing to share that
particular vigilance.

"Thank you," Sela says, to Isla, plainly, without any of the careful
distance she kept at their first meeting. "Properly. Not just for the
correction. For noticing it was wrong at all, when neither of us
thought to look twice."

"It's what I do," Isla says, though I notice her looking, for a moment,
genuinely pleased by the thanks rather than simply accepting it as her
professional due.

We walk the rest of the way down from the orchard with the corrected
seed count folded carefully in Isla's own coat pocket, and I find
myself, watching the easy warmth that's grown between her and my
sister in the space of a single visit, considerably more hopeful about
the shape of whatever we're all building together than I have let
myself be in longer than I can properly place.

·

We stop, on the walk back, at the small shrine near the orchard's own
boundary — not a proper cairn, simply a weathered marker where the
family has, for generations, left small offerings for whatever
half-remembered spirits the old folk once believed watched over the
last living trees this far north.

"My mother used to bring us here," I say, "every first frost, without
fail. I haven't come regularly since she died. Sela does, I think, more
often than she lets on."

Isla looks at the marker properly, reading whatever weathered carving
remains legible on its surface.

"What did she believe it did," Isla asks, "the offering?"

"I don't think she believed it did anything, precisely," I say. "I
think she believed in the doing of it. The specific discipline of
stopping, once a year, to acknowledge that the orchard's survival
wasn't entirely within our own control, regardless of how carefully we
tended it."

"That's not so different from what a ward actually is," Isla says,
considering. "An acknowledgment that some things are genuinely beyond
a single person's management, dressed up in whatever ritual language a
given tradition happens to prefer."

I leave a small offering at the marker, this time, the first time I've
done so since my mother's death — a strip of grafting wax, the only
thing I currently have on me that feels properly mine to give — and
find, doing it, that the gesture costs me considerably less than I
expected, and returns, somehow, considerably more.

"Thank you," I say to Isla, "for making me want to stop and actually do
this, rather than simply walking past it the way I have for years."

"I didn't make you do anything," Isla says. "I think you'd have gotten
here eventually, regardless."

"Perhaps," I say, "but I don't think I'd have gotten here today,
without you standing beside me while I did."

·

We find Tomas waiting at the cottage when we finally return, an
unusual evening visit that tells me, before he's said anything at all,
that something's on his mind he's been saving up the nerve to raise. He
has clearly been waiting some while, judging by how thoroughly he's
already organized the evening's reports into their proper stacks.

"Can I ask you both something," he says, once the pleasantries are
managed, "about the whole arrangement. Not the practical parts. The
actual — feeling of it."

"Go on," Isla says.

"Do you ever worry," Tomas asks, careful, "that the rite made this
happen, rather than the two of you actually choosing it? I've read the
old accounts. The bond's meant to create considerable pressure toward
attachment, regardless of whether the two people involved would have
chosen each other freely."

It's a fair question, and I understand, watching Isla consider it
properly rather than dismissing it, that she's given it real thought
herself.

"I worried about exactly that, early on," she says. "I still check
myself against it, some days. I think the honest answer is that the
rite gave us proximity, and proximity gave us the chance to actually
see each other properly. But the choosing — the actual wanting, once we
knew each other — I don't think that's the rite's doing. I think that's
simply what happened, given the chance to happen at all."

"How do you know the difference," Tomas presses, "between the rite's
pull and your own actual want?"

"We asked each other," I say, "plainly, more than once, whether the
wanting was ours. We keep asking. I think that's the only honest answer
available — not a certainty, exactly, but a practice, repeated often
enough that we've both come to trust it."

Tomas considers this, nodding slowly, filing the answer away with the
particular seriousness he gives most things that matter to him.

"Thank you," he says, "for actually answering that properly, instead of
simply telling me not to worry about it."

"You're allowed to worry about it," Isla says. "I'd rather you ask hard
questions than simply assume everything's settled because it looks
settled from outside."

·

Tomas lingers after the main question's been properly answered,
turning something else over, and I recognize, watching him, the
specific hesitation of a young man who has one more thing to ask and
isn't entirely certain it's his place to ask it.

"Can I say something," he says, finally, "that isn't really a
question?"

"Go on," Isla says.

"I think watching the two of you has changed what I expect from my own
future," Tomas says. "Before this winter, I assumed being a wardwright
meant being alone, eventually, the way you were alone before Aldric
arrived. I've started thinking, watching this, that maybe it doesn't
have to. That maybe I'm allowed to want something like this for myself,
someday, rather than assuming the job requires giving it up."

"You are allowed," Isla says, gently. "I don't think I understood that
myself, until fairly recently. I'd like you to learn it considerably
sooner than I did."

"I intend to," Tomas says, with a seriousness that makes me smile,
watching him. "I've decided I'd like an ordinary life alongside the
wardwright one, eventually, rather than choosing one at the expense of
the other, the way I always assumed the job demanded."

"I think that's exactly right," I say. "I spent most of my own life
assuming duty and happiness were somehow opposed, that choosing one
meant sacrificing the other. I don't believe that anymore. I don't
think either of you should have to believe it either."

Tomas nods, absorbing this, and takes his leave for the evening,
considerably lighter, I think, for having said the thought aloud rather
than simply carrying it privately, and Isla and I sit together a while
longer, grateful for a young man determined to build a different kind
of future than the one this line has always, until now, quietly
assumed was the only one available.


## Chapter Nine — Held Ground (Aldric)

A fortnight and a half in, and the cottage has quietly, thoroughly rearranged itself
around two people instead of one, in a hundred small ways neither of us
has discussed and both of us have apparently agreed to without ever
voting on it formally: a second chair pulled permanently to the fire, a
particular mug that has become, without a single conversation about it,
mine, a boot-rack by the door that now holds two pairs instead of one
without either of us having built it larger to accommodate the
difference.

I notice the rearranging the way I notice most things about this
cottage now — sideways, in passing, the specific accumulation of small
facts that add up, eventually, to something considerably larger than
any single fact would suggest on its own.

"You've stopped flinching when I come in," I say, one evening, mild,
not making a particular thing of it, mostly because I have learned, in
a fortnight and a half, that Isla answers observations offered lightly
considerably more honestly than she answers ones offered as though
they matter.

"I've stopped expecting you to cost me something every time you do,"
she says, and I hear, in the way she says it, exactly how much she has
just admitted without quite meaning to.

Neither of us corrects the sentence. The fire holds the room's whole
attention for a while after that, which is its own kind of agreement,
the specific comfortable silence of two people who have each, privately,
decided that the thing sitting between them doesn't need naming yet to
be true.

"Can I ask you something," I say, eventually, "that isn't strictly
practical."

"You may."

"Do you regret it. The rite naming me instead of some other hearth. I
know it's early to ask. I've been wondering anyway."

Isla considers the question with the specific seriousness she gives
most things I ask her plainly, turning it over rather than answering
reflexively.

"No," she says, finally. "I was frightened of it, the night of the
binding. I'm still frightened of parts of it. But I don't regret the
name the rite read. I've caught myself, more than once this fortnight,
grateful for it, which is not a sentence I expected to have available
to me a month ago."

"That's more than I expected you to say."

"I've decided," Isla says, "that half-truths cost more between us than
whole ones do. I'm trying to act accordingly, even when the whole truth
is inconvenient to admit."

I think about that for a long moment, watching the fire do its slow,
steady work, and find myself, without quite deciding to, offering the
same honesty back.

"I decided the same thing, the night of the binding," I say. "Watching
you not let your face do anything, standing in front of the whole
assembly. I thought — there's a woman who's spent her whole life
managing what she lets people see. I'd like to know what she looks
like when she's not managing anything at all."

"And do you," Isla asks. "Know, now."

"Some of it," I say. "More than I expected to, this early. I suspect
there's considerably more you haven't shown anyone yet, including me."

"There is," she admits, quietly, and does not elaborate further, and I
let the sentence stand exactly where she's left it, because I have
learned, watching her, that some doors open slowly, on their own
schedule, and pushing on them only teaches a person to keep them
shut more carefully next time.

We sit a while longer without speaking, the specific companionable
silence that has become, in a fortnight and a half, considerably more
comfortable than either of us, I suspect, would have predicted the
night the rite-cord first closed around our wrists.

"Tomas thinks we're courting," I say, eventually, mostly to see how
she'll take the observation.

"Tomas thinks a great many things," Isla says, "most of which he keeps
to himself until he's decided the moment's ripe for maximum effect."

"Is he wrong?"

Isla doesn't answer immediately, which is, I am learning, its own kind
of answer — the specific pause of a woman deciding whether a truth is
ready to be spoken aloud or whether it needs a while longer sitting
quietly, unexamined, before it can survive being said.

"I don't know yet," she says, finally, honestly. "I know the arrangement
has stopped feeling like an arrangement. I haven't decided what that
means it's become instead."

"That's fair," I say, and mean it, and do not push for more, because I
have decided, watching her navigate this fortnight with considerably
more honesty than I suspect comes naturally to her, that the not-yet-
knowing is itself a kind of progress worth respecting on its own terms.

The fire burns down slowly, and neither of us moves to build it back
up, and the wariness that opened this whole strange arrangement has, I
notice, become something closer to ground held rather than ground
defended — neither of us naming it first, both of us, apparently,
content to let it stand exactly as it currently is for one evening
longer.

·

Isla asks me, later that same evening, once the fire's gone properly
low, about my own mother — a subject I have not, in three weeks,
volunteered, and one she has clearly been waiting for the right
moment to raise.

"Sela's mentioned her twice," Isla says, carefully, "always in passing,
always changing the subject quickly after. I've noticed you do the
same. I'm not asking to press on something painful. I'm asking because
I'd like to actually know you, and I suspect this is a part of you that
matters."

"She died when I was nine," I say. "Sela was six. A fever, nothing the
line or the rite had any hand in — an ordinary sickness, the kind that
takes people regardless of how carefully anyone's managed their own
particular arithmetic of risk. My father raised us alone after that,
and I think, looking back, that's rather where I learned the whole
habit properly — watching him manage two children and an orchard and
his own grief, all at once, without once letting either of us see the
managing cost him anything."

"That's a heavy thing to learn at nine."

"I don't think I understood it as heavy, learning it," I say. "I
understood it as simply how a person who loves someone behaves. It
took considerably longer than nine years old to understand that my
father's silence about his own grief wasn't strength, exactly. It was
simply the only tool he had, and he handed it to me and Sela both,
without meaning to, the way you hand down anything you've never
examined closely enough to know it's worth questioning."

Isla is quiet for a long moment, turning that over with the particular
gentleness she reserves for things that matter to her considerably more
than she generally lets show.

"I think I understand you rather better now," she says, finally. "The
orchard. The exemption. All of it. You didn't simply decide, as an
adult, to start carrying costs alone. You were taught it, early, by a
man who loved you and didn't know another way to show it."

"I think that's accurate," I say, surprised, as I often am with her, by
how precisely she's named something I'd never quite managed to
articulate to myself in those exact terms.

"For what it's worth," Isla says, "I think you're allowed to unlearn
it. Slowly. Imperfectly. I don't think either of us is going to manage
it in a single winter. But I'd like to try unlearning it alongside you,
if you're willing to let me."

"I'm willing," I say, and mean it more than the simple words let on,
and we sit together a while longer in the low firelight, two people
each quietly working at unlearning the exact same inherited habit,
neither of us expecting to finish the work quickly, both of us,
apparently, willing to keep at it regardless.

·

Tomas asks me, the following morning, in the careful sideways manner he
reserves for his most genuine curiosities, whether I've ever considered
that the rite itself might have been designed, originally, with
precisely this kind of shared unlearning in mind.

"What do you mean," I ask, setting aside the morning's reports.

"Well," Tomas says, working the thought out as he speaks, "the whole
point of binding a wardwright to a hearth is supposedly to give the
wardwright someone to draw cost from. But what if the actual original
point — before whatever corruption happened to it — was to give the
wardwright someone to be honest with. Someone who'd notice, properly,
if she started hiding things. Not simply a battery to draw on. A
witness."

I sit with that observation for a long moment, turning it over with
considerably more seriousness than I expect its source to warrant.

"That's a remarkably astute theory," I say, "for a nineteen-year-old
who's spent three years mostly sorting reports and drilling cadence."

"I've had considerable time to think, this whole winter," Tomas says,
with a dry precision that reminds me, not for the first time, exactly
how much of my own manner he has absorbed simply through years of
proximity. "And I've watched you change, since the binding. Not just
softened. Actually witnessed, in a way I don't think you'd ever
properly let anyone witness you before."

"You witness me constantly, Tomas. You've been doing it for three
years."

"That's different," Tomas says. "I witness the wardwright. Aldric
witnesses you. I think that's rather the distinction the old rite was
always meant to draw, before whoever corrupted it decided a witness
was a luxury the crown's arithmetic couldn't afford to fund."

I do not have a ready response to being handed, so plainly, by my own
apprentice, an observation about the rite's own possible history that I
have never once heard voiced by anyone else at Highstone, so I simply
thank him, and go back to the day's business, carrying his words
carefully alongside everything else I am slowly, privately, beginning
to wonder about a rite I had, until this exact conversation, never once
questioned the origins of.

·

I mention Tomas's observation to Isla that evening, curious what she
makes of a theory this unorthodox coming from so young a source.

"He might be onto something," Isla says, considering it properly. "I've
spent six years assuming the rite's current shape was simply how it had
always been. I'm troubled, hearing his theory, that I never once
properly examined that assumption myself, given how thoroughly I've
studied everything else about this line."

"That's rather what unsettles me too," I admit, "though from the
opposite direction — that a practice meant to protect a line would be
designed to isolate the two people most responsible for holding it,
rather than bringing them closer together, and nobody along the way
apparently thought to question it."

"Do you think there's a way to find out? Properly, I mean. Not just
Tomas's theory, however astute."

"I don't know," I say, honestly. "The crown's own archive has nothing.
Ossian's private journal only hints at doubt, not answers. I suppose
if there's a fuller record anywhere, it would have to be considerably
older than anything currently kept at Highstone."

"Then perhaps that's worth looking for," I say, "properly, when
there's time for it."

I don't know, that evening, how soon that time is actually going to
arrive, or how urgently. I only know, turning the whole conversation
over before sleep, that some part of me has already begun quietly
searching, in the back of my mind, for wherever such an older record
might be waiting to be found.

·

Sela visits the cottage the following evening, and I find myself,
watching her settle in by the fire with Isla, turning Tomas's theory
over once more, curious whether my sister might have some fragment of
family history that could shed further light on it.

"Did Father ever mention anything," I ask her, "about the rite's
history. Where it came from, originally. Whether it was always
practiced the way we know it now."

Sela considers this, surprised by the question.

"Not directly," she says, "though I remember him saying, once, that
Grandmother kept old habits about the line that he never fully
understood himself. Some mark she kept over the door. Some phrase she
used, about hearths and witnesses, that he always assumed was simply
an old woman's superstition rather than anything with real weight
behind it."

"A mark over the door," I say, feeling something settle into place
that I can't quite name yet. "Do you remember what it looked like?"

"Rings," Sela says, after a moment's thought. "Several of them,
interlocking. I haven't thought about it in years. It came down when
Father repaired the lintel, sometime after Mother died. I don't think
either of us thought to ask why it was there in the first place."

Isla goes very still, hearing this, and I understand, watching her,
that whatever Sela's just described has landed considerably harder
than a simple family memory should.

"That's worth remembering properly," Isla says, carefully. "I don't
know yet what it means. But I don't think it's nothing."

"Should I be worried," Sela asks, looking between the two of us.

"Not yet," Isla says. "Possibly not at all. I simply think there might
be more to this line's history than either of us currently understands,
and I'd rather gather every fragment of it now, while people still
remember them, than realize later we let something important go
unrecorded."

Sela nods, accepting this, though I catch her filing the whole exchange
away with the same careful attention she gives everything that might
eventually matter to this family's safety, and I find myself, watching
the two women I trust most in the world turn this small mystery over
together, considerably more certain than I was this morning that
Tomas's theory deserves rather more serious pursuit than either of us
initially gave it credit for.

·

Sela lingers after Isla has gone to fetch her own notebook, turning
something over privately before she speaks again.

"Do you think there's more of Grandmother's own history we've simply
never thought to ask about," she says, quietly, "beyond the mark over
the door?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I don't think either of us properly knew
her, growing up. She died before I was old enough to ask anything that
mattered."

"I keep thinking," Sela says, "about how much of this family's own
history has apparently just been sitting unexamined, waiting for
someone to finally ask the right question. First your hand. Now this.
I wonder what else we've simply inherited without ever properly
understanding."

"I don't know," I say again, "but I find I want to find out, properly,
alongside everything else this winter's already asking of us."

"So do I," Sela says, and we sit together a moment longer before Isla
returns, both of us turning over the specific strange comfort of
discovering that a family's history holds considerably more mystery,
and considerably more inheritance worth claiming, than either of us
ever thought to look for.

·

Isla returns with her notebook, and the three of us spend the rest of
the evening carefully reconstructing whatever fragments of family
history either Sela or I can properly recall — half-remembered
phrases, small habits neither of us thought to question at the time,
the specific shape of a grandmother neither of us knew well enough to
ask the right questions of while she was still here to answer them.

"This is considerably more than I expected to find, simply by asking,"
Isla observes, reviewing her own careful notes.

"I think that's rather the whole lesson of this entire winter," I say.
"Considerably more is usually available, once someone finally asks."

We finish the evening with a fuller, if still incomplete, picture of
whatever older tradition my grandmother once quietly kept, and I find
myself, watching Isla carefully file the notes away, grateful for a
season that keeps handing this family new inheritances to properly
claim, one honest question at a time.


## Chapter Ten — What the Stone Takes (Isla)

Every ward I have ever set, I have set alone. The rite that bound me to
Aldric requires, for the first time in six years of practice, that I
set one with an actual hearth standing at the stone beside me, and I
find, the morning of it, that I have spent considerably more of the
night rehearsing how to manage my own nerves about the fact than I have
spent, in years, rehearsing anything at all.

I have performed this exact rite structure sixty times, by my own
count, and never once, in all those sixty performances, have I had to
consider anything beyond my own steadiness at the stone. This morning,
for the first time, I find myself considering someone else's entirely.

"Nothing's going to go wrong," I tell Tomas, in the tone I use for
things I have not yet finished believing myself, when he asks, careful,
whether he should come along in case something does.

"I didn't say I thought something would," Tomas says. "I said I'd feel
better coming, in case."

"Stay and mind the reports," I say. "I need someone I trust minding the
ledger while I'm at the Long Reach cairn, in case Feld sends anything
requiring an immediate answer."

He accepts this, though not entirely, and I find Aldric at the cottage
door already dressed for the cold, having assumed, correctly, without
being told, that today was the day.

"You didn't need to be ready this early," I say.

"I've been ready since you first mentioned it, three days ago," he
says. "I wanted to give you the fewest possible reasons to postpone."

I do not have an answer to being so precisely understood, so I simply
lead the way to the Long Reach cairn, through a cold that has, this
morning, the specific bright, brittle quality of the deepest part of
Coldwake finally arriving in earnest.

·

The Long Reach cairn stands at the northern edge of the line proper, a
slab of frost-veined granite worn smooth by generations of hands, and I
have set wards at this exact stone perhaps sixty times in six years,
alone, without incident, without once needing to account for anyone's
comfort but my own.

Today there is a hearth actually standing at the stone, and I feel the
difference in the air before I've even laid my palms against the
granite — a specific charge, expectant, the way a room feels different
once a second person has properly entered it. Even the cairn itself
seems to know something has changed, the frost along its base catching
the morning light differently than I remember it catching on any of my
sixty previous visits.

"Tell me what to expect," Aldric says, "as plainly as you can. I'd
rather know than be surprised."

"I don't fully know myself," I admit. "No wardwright's set a ward with
an actual hearth present at the stone in longer than the record shows.
I know what the rite requires, structurally. I don't know exactly what
it will feel like, to either of us, doing it properly for what may be
the first time in generations."

"Then we'll find out together," he says, simply, and steps to the
stone beside me.

I lay both hands flat against the Long Reach cairn's frost-veined
surface and call the ward the way I have called perhaps sixty others at
this exact stone — the old words, the old shape of intention, the
specific focused attention that has become, over six years, as natural
to me as breathing.

What arrives, this time, is not the stone's charge alone.

The cost leaves Aldric in a way I have only ever read about in older
wardwrights' journals, in the careful, clinical language of a rite
performed properly, with an actual hearth to draw from — heat drawn out
through his palms into the standing stone, a specific, visible
draining, and the bond, unbidden, hands me the whole of what that costs
him, not as knowledge, the way it has arrived once or twice before, but
as sensation, entire, arriving in my own chest as though the cost were
happening to me as well.

I have spent six years believing, professionally, in the price the
rite extracts. I have never once, until this exact moment, felt it
happen to a person whose face I can watch, in real time, going white to
the lips beside me.

He does not make a sound. I understand, feeling what he is feeling,
exactly how much that costs him — the specific discipline of a man who
has decided, somewhere long before he ever met me, that showing pain
is a debt he isn't willing to ask anyone else to help him pay.

I very nearly break the ward mid-cast to stop it. I do not, because I
understand, in the same instant, that stopping mid-cast would cost him
considerably worse — an incomplete rite, the stone left half-fed,
demanding to be finished properly some other day, at some other cost,
rather than concluded cleanly now.

I finish it. The ward settles into the stone the way a ward is meant to
settle, solid, load-bearing, good for another season's worth of holding
back the Rime's patient advance.

Aldric sits down hard in the snow the moment it's done, not quite a
controlled descent, and I am on my knees beside him before I've fully
decided to move.

"Now you know," he says, through it, and it is not quite a question and
not quite simply a fact either. It is closer to a confession — the
specific relief of a man who has been carrying a secret weight and has
just, for the first time, had someone else feel the actual size of it
rather than merely being told about it secondhand.

"Now I know," I agree, and do not have anything further to say for a
long moment, because I have spent six years of professional practice
believing in a cost I have just, for the first time, actually felt
arrive in a body I can put a face to, and the knowing of it has
rearranged something in my chest that I do not think is going to
rearrange itself back.

·

Tomas is waiting at the record-room door when we finally make it back
to Highstone, having clearly been watching the road for the better
part of an hour, and I understand, from his face, that he's read
something in our slow, careful walk home that tells him the inspection
did not go entirely as planned.

"What happened," he says, direct, none of his usual sideways
carefulness in the asking.

"The ward went as it should," I say. "The cost was considerably more
visible than I expected."

"How visible."

I consider how much to tell him, weighing his own safety — the
specific danger of a nineteen-year-old learning too much, too soon,
about exactly what awaits him if the crown ever does force his own
binding — against his right to understand the actual stakes of the
work he is apprenticing toward.

"Visible enough that I understand, now, precisely why the record shows
so few wardwrights setting wards with their bound hearth actually
present," I say. "It isn't simply tradition keeping the two separate.
It's mercy. For both parties."

Tomas absorbs this with the particular gravity he's shown, all
evening, watching the two of us come up the track.

"Is he going to be alright?" he asks.

"Tonight, yes," I say. "I don't know about the season."

"And you? Are you alright?"

The question catches me slightly off guard, coming from him rather than
from Aldric, and I find myself, considering it properly, uncertain how
to answer.

"I don't know that either," I admit, finally. "I've spent six years
believing I understood the whole shape of this rite's cost. I
understood, apparently, only my own half of it. Learning the other
