half, this directly, has left me considerably less certain of a great
many things I thought were settled."

Tomas nods slowly, filing that away with the particular gravity of a
young man who has just been handed a piece of honesty he wasn't
entirely prepared for and has decided, regardless, to hold onto it
properly.

"Thank you for telling me the truth," he says, "instead of the managed
version."

"You're going to inherit this line eventually," I say. "I'd rather you
inherit it with a clear understanding of what it actually costs, than
with the tidy version I was handed at nineteen and spent seven years
slowly discovering was considerably incomplete."

He goes to his own quarters not long after, and I sit a while longer in
the record-room, alone, turning over the whole of what today has
taught me, before finally making my own way home to a cottage where
Aldric is already, mercifully, asleep, the specific deep, exhausted
sleep of a man whose body has spent everything it currently has to
spend and is, for tonight at least, simply grateful for the rest.

·

I do not sleep myself, that night, despite the exhaustion of the day —
the particular wired alertness that arrives after a genuine shock,
keeping the mind circling long after the body has signaled it should
properly rest.

I get up, eventually, and sit by the low fire with Ossian's own private
journal, the one I inherited alongside his official ledgers and have
never, in seven years, properly finished reading cover to cover. I find,
this particular night, a passage I do not remember reading before,
tucked between two considerably drier inspection notes, dated some
years before his death.

*I wonder, some nights,* he has written, *whether the whole discipline
of this practice — the solitary cost, the careful accounting, the
absolute refusal to let the hearth's own experience of the rite factor
into anything beyond the arithmetic — is not simply tradition, but a
specific, deliberate choice made by someone, at some point, who found it
easier to manage a line's failing cairns than to properly reckon with
what the managing costs the people actually doing it. I do not know
who made that choice. I suspect, some nights, that I have simply
inherited it without ever properly questioning whether it needed
inheriting at all.*

I read the passage three times, in the firelight, understanding, with
each read, exactly how close Ossian came, in his own private writing,
to the very discovery I am now chasing formally at Calder's Reach and
its predecessor's record. He suspected. He never had the proof, or the
nerve, or perhaps simply the time, to go looking for it properly.

I fold the journal closed, eventually, and sit a while longer with the
particular grief of understanding a dead man slightly better than I did
that morning — not simply as my teacher, but as a person who came
almost, almost close enough to the same answer I am now pursuing, and
ran out of chances to find it before the old rite finally claimed him.

·

I do not mention the journal entry to Aldric the following morning,
though I turn the decision over carefully before settling on it. It
feels, this time, different from the small managements I am trying to
unlearn — not a withholding meant to spare him something, simply a
piece of Ossian's own private grief that I am not yet certain is mine
to share with anyone, even someone I trust as completely as I have come
to trust him.

I tell Tomas instead, carefully, in general terms, over the morning's
reports.

"He knew," Tomas says, absorbing it. "Ossian. He suspected the rite
wasn't right, and he never did anything about it."

"He suspected," I say. "I don't think suspecting and knowing carry the
same weight of obligation. I think he was frightened, the way I'd
likely have been frightened, of pursuing a suspicion that could have
cost him his whole career if it came to nothing, or worse, if it came
to something the crown's own office refused to hear."

"Do you think you'll do differently?"

"I hope so," I say, honestly. "I think the difference, if there is
one, is that I'm not alone the way he was. I have you. I have Aldric.
I suspect that changes what a person's actually willing to risk,
having people alongside them rather than simply carrying a suspicion
in private, for decades, the way he apparently did."

Tomas nods, slowly, and goes back to the reports, and I sit with
Ossian's own quiet, unfinished doubt a while longer, understanding that
whatever I eventually find, wherever this suspicion leads, I owe it to
his memory to actually follow it through, properly, rather than let it
join his own journal in a drawer nobody opens again for another seven
years.

·

Aldric finds me still sitting with the journal that same evening,
considerably later than either of us usually stays at the record-room,
and settles into the chair across from me without asking permission,
which I have come, these past weeks, to recognize as his own particular
way of offering company without demanding conversation in return.

"You found something," he observes, watching my face.

"A passage," I say. "Ossian's own private doubt about the rite,
written years before either of us had any reason to think its shape
might not be the only one that ever existed. He suspected something
was wrong with it. He never followed the suspicion anywhere."

"Do you think less of him, for that?"

I consider the question properly, turning it over with more honesty
than my first instinct offers.

"No," I say, finally. "I think I understand him considerably better
for it. I think pursuing a suspicion like this, alone, with nobody to
share the risk or the eventual cost, is considerably harder than
either of us give it credit for, sitting here with the whole of Ossian's
private doubt laid out in front of us like it should have been obvious
all along."

"You're not alone, though," Aldric says. "Pursuing it."

"No," I agree. "I'm not. I think that's precisely the difference. I
don't think it's bravery on my part, chasing this further than he did.
I think it's simply that I've been handed something he never had —
people willing to chase it alongside me, rather than a solitary
suspicion I'd have to justify entirely on my own authority."

Aldric takes my hand across the table, turning it over the way he's
started doing lately, tracing the calluses six years of ward-work have
left there.

"Then we'll chase it properly," he says, "together, the way he never
got the chance to."

We sit together in the record-room's low lamplight a while longer,
Ossian's own careful hand still open on the table between us, and I
find myself, closing the journal finally for the night, considerably
more determined than I was this morning to make certain his quiet,
unfinished doubt does not go unanswered a second time.

·

Tomas asks about Ossian's journal the following morning, having
noticed it still sitting on the record-room table rather than properly
shelved.

"Did you find something in it," he asks.

"A passage," I say. "About his own private doubts regarding the rite.
I've decided to tell you about it, since you're going to inherit this
line's whole history eventually, doubts included."

I read him the relevant passage, and watch him absorb it with the same
gravity he's shown toward everything touching the line's deeper
mysteries this season.

"He suspected, and never acted," Tomas says, finally. "That's rather
sad, isn't it. Knowing something might be wrong and never having the
proper support to pursue it."

"I think it is," I say. "I think that's rather why I wanted you to
hear it directly, alongside Aldric, rather than let his doubt stay
buried in a journal nobody else properly reads."

"Thank you for trusting me with it," Tomas says, and I watch him file
the whole account away with the same careful attention he gives
everything he considers part of his proper inheritance, and I find
myself, watching him, grateful for an apprentice determined to carry
forward not simply the mechanics of this line, but its whole honest
history, doubts and all.


## Chapter Eleven — Aftermath, Underlit (Aldric)

We shelter below the Long Reach cairn, in the lee of a shepherd's stone
hut that has stood on this stretch of the line longer than either of
our own families, until I can stand without the ground doing something
unreliable underneath me.

The whole world has gone strange and underlit around the edges, the
particular hollowed clarity that follows a cost this size, and I find
myself grateful, sitting in the hut's small shelter, simply for the
solid, unremarkable fact of stone at my back and Isla's coat still
warm across my shoulders.

Isla's coat is over my shoulders, which is, absurdly, the warmer of the
two, a fact neither of us comments on directly, though I feel her
notice it and file it away, the way she files everything she isn't
ready to spend yet.

"It's always like that," I say, finally, into the quiet, because I have
decided, somewhere in the walk down from the cairn, that she has earned
the whole truth rather than the managed version I've been handing her
in pieces. "Not always that bad. But always something. Every ward,
every inspection where you've had me near enough to feel it secondhand
— there's always a cost, and it's always been considerably more than
I've let on."

"Why didn't you tell me," Isla asks, not accusing, simply asking.

"Because knowing wouldn't have changed the numbers," I say. "You still
had forty miles of cairns to hold, with or without my honesty about
what holding them costs me specifically. I didn't see the use in
handing you a fact you couldn't do anything about, except worry."

"It changes what I'm willing to ask of you," Isla says, and there is
something in her voice, saying it, that surprises me more than it
surprises her — a specific weight, the sound of a woman discovering,
in real time, that a fact she'd filed under *unchangeable* has just
become, instead, something she has actual choices about.

"How do you mean," I ask.

"I mean I've been setting wards at whatever pace the line requires,
without once asking whether that pace is sustainable for the hearth
actually feeding them. I assumed — because it was easier to assume than
to ask — that the cost was fixed, regardless of frequency. I understand
now that it isn't. That every ward I set with you standing at the stone
is a specific, countable withdrawal, and that I have some say, however
limited by the line's own demands, in how quickly I make those
withdrawals."

I don't argue with that. I don't entirely agree with it either — the
line's demands are the line's demands, regardless of what either of us
would prefer, and I have never once, in a lifetime of watching my own
family absorb costs quietly, believed that acknowledging a cost changes
its actual size. But I don't say so, tonight, because I understand,
watching her work through the realization, that this is the first time
in six years anyone has let her believe her own choices might matter to
the arithmetic at all, and I am not willing to be the one who takes
that belief away from her on its very first evening.

"Thank you," I say instead, "for finishing the ward properly, rather
than stopping when it started costing more than either of us
expected."

"I nearly didn't," Isla admits. "I want you to know that. I very
nearly broke it mid-cast, watching what it was doing to you."

"I'm glad you didn't," I say. "An unfinished ward costs worse, in the
long run, than a finished one. I'd rather pay the whole price once than
half of it twice."

She looks at me for a long moment, in the specific underlit quiet of a
shepherd's hut with no fire and only the last grey light of an early
winter afternoon finding its way through the gaps in the stone, and I
understand, from the look, that she is doing the same arithmetic I've
been doing since the byre at Hollow Farm — recognizing, in me,
precisely the shape of her own habit, and finding, in the recognition,
something considerably more complicated than simple sympathy.

"You do that," she says, eventually. "Pay the whole price rather than
half of it twice. It's what you did with Sela's exemption. It's what
you did volunteering for a binding sight unseen. I don't think you've
ever once, in your whole life, let anyone else help you carry a cost
you'd already decided was yours alone to bear."

"No," I admit. "I don't think I have."

"I know the shape of that particular weight," Isla says, quietly. "I
carry it too. I have for six years."

Neither of us says the word that's sitting in the hut with us, small
and precise and considerably larger than either of us is prepared to
name outright tonight. It doesn't need saying yet to be true. I simply
lean, slightly, into the coat and the cold and the fact of her sitting
close enough to share both, and let the moment be exactly as large as
it actually is, without either of us trying to manage it into something
smaller.

## Tomas Watches

Tomas has been keeping his own private ledger of the two of us for the
better part of three weeks, and finally, over breakfast the morning
after the Long Reach inspection, decides to present his findings.

"You made him two mugs of tea before you made yourself one," he
announces, entirely too pleased with himself for a nineteen-year-old.
"I'm simply reporting the data."

"It's efficient," Isla says, with the specific dignity of a woman who
knows exactly how unconvincing that sounds even as she says it.

"Very efficient," Tomas agrees, in the tone of someone who has decided
not to argue with a losing position out loud, purely for the pleasure
of watching her hold it anyway.

I say nothing, mostly because I have learned, in three weeks of living
alongside the two of them, that the safest position in any exchange
between Isla and her apprentice is silence, offered from a safe
distance, while the two of them work out their own particular rhythm
without requiring my input.

"How's the hand," Tomas asks me, once Isla has gone to check the
morning's post, in a considerably more serious register than the one
he used a moment ago.

"Cold," I say, which is true, and considerably less than the whole of
what's true, in the same specific way that most of what I tell people
about my own condition tends to be.

Tomas looks at me for a long moment, the particular assessing look of a
young man who has learned, watching Isla, exactly how to read a person
who's decided not to volunteer the whole truth.

"You're doing the thing she does," he says, finally. "The not-quite-
lying thing. I've watched her do it for three years. I'd know it
anywhere."

I don't have a ready answer for being caught so precisely, so I simply
nod, once, an acknowledgment rather than a confession, and Tomas,
mercifully, lets the observation stand without pushing further, though
I understand, from the specific way he goes back to his reports, that
he has filed it, carefully, for whenever the moment finally arrives
that either of us runs out of room to keep managing it quietly.

·

I spend the rest of that morning helping Tomas catalogue the winter's
accumulated correspondence — a task neither of us particularly enjoys,
sorting the crown's routine bureaucratic notices from the handful of
letters that actually matter, and one that gives us both, I suspect,
considerably more time to talk than the actual work requires.

"Can I ask you something," Tomas says, partway through the second
stack, "about before. Before the binding, I mean. What Isla was like,
when it was just the two of you running the line."

I consider the question, and find myself, answering it, surprised by
how much has already shifted in the few short weeks since the rite.

"Quieter," I say. "Not that she isn't quiet now. But there was a
particular quality to it before — a kind of bracing, I think, against
having to explain herself to anyone. I don't see that as often now."

"I've noticed that too," Tomas says. "She laughs more. Actually laughs,
not just the polite version she used to manage for visitors." He
hesitates, then adds, careful, "I'm glad. I've watched her carry this
whole line practically alone since I was sixteen. I didn't think
anything could actually reach her, underneath all that carrying."

"I don't think I've reached the whole of it," I say, honestly. "I
think I've reached some of it. I suspect there's considerably more
underneath that neither of us has found yet."

"That's probably true of most people," Tomas says, with the specific
wisdom of a nineteen-year-old who has clearly given the matter more
thought than his age would suggest. "I don't think anyone reaches the
whole of another person. I think you just keep finding new rooms, as
long as the other person keeps letting you in."

I think about that observation for the rest of the afternoon, turning
it over the way I turn over most of what Tomas says that lands harder
than his age would predict, and find, walking home that evening, that
it has settled somewhere useful — a small, steadying thought to carry
alongside everything else I am slowly learning about the woman the rite
bound me to, one room at a time, as she continues, gradually, to let me
in.

·

I find myself, that same evening, doing something I have not done
since I was a boy — sitting down with a scrap of paper and actually
writing out, plainly, the things I am afraid of, rather than simply
letting them circle unexamined in my own head the way I've always
managed fear before.

I am afraid the rite will take more of me than either of us has
properly accounted for. I am afraid Isla will spend herself trying to
prevent that, the same silent self-spending she has already admitted to
once, at Hollow Farm, and has not fully stopped since. I am afraid,
underneath both of those fears, of a considerably smaller, more
selfish one: that whatever this has become between us will not survive
whatever the winter still has left to demand of either of us.

I do not show the list to anyone. I am not certain, writing it, that
showing it would help — some fears, I think, need simply to be named,
once, properly, in a person's own hand, before they can be managed at
all, regardless of who else eventually gets to see the naming.

I fold the paper carefully and tuck it into the same drawer where I
keep the small collection of things I've decided, this winter, are
worth keeping rather than discarding — a pressed leaf from the crooked
tree, a scrap of the rite-cord from the binding itself, saved without
quite deciding why at the time. I understand, adding the list of fears
to that particular collection, that I am building, slowly, a private
archive of my own — not the formal kind Isla keeps in the record-room,
something considerably smaller and more personal, a record of exactly
what this winter has actually cost and given me, in equal, complicated
measure.

·

Sela visits the cottage the following afternoon, ostensibly to discuss
a grafting question, actually, I suspect, simply curious enough about
how things are progressing to invent an excuse for the visit.

"You look well," she observes, studying me with the particular
scrutiny only an older sister can properly deploy. "Rested, even.
I don't think I've seen you properly rested since before Father died."

"I've been sleeping better," I admit. "I don't know that I'd have
predicted that, given everything else currently happening."

"Fear and rest aren't always opposites," Sela says. "Sometimes the
right kind of company makes even a frightening season easier to
actually sleep through, properly, rather than simply enduring it."

"Is that wisdom from experience, or simply a theory?"

"A bit of both," Sela says, with a small, private smile that tells me
there's more to the observation than she's currently willing to share.
"I'll tell you properly, someday, when it's relevant. For now, I'm
simply glad to see my brother sleeping properly again."

I don't push for the rest of whatever she's not saying, understanding,
from years of being her brother, that Sela shares things on her own
schedule and no one else's, and we spend the rest of the visit on the
actual grafting question, though I find myself, watching her go, newly
curious about whatever quiet happiness she's apparently been keeping to
herself.

·

I mention Sela's odd, private smile to Isla that same evening, curious
whether she's noticed anything similar in the weeks since they've
started properly getting to know each other.

"I've noticed something," Isla admits. "She mentioned, once, in
passing, wisdom gained from experience rather than theory. I didn't
press. I assumed it was hers to raise properly, when she was ready."

"That's rather generous of you, given how curious I know you actually
are."

"I've learned patience about other people's private doors," Isla says,
"mostly by watching how carefully you guard your own, and deciding I'd
rather earn the opening than force it."

"Fair," I say, considering this. "I think I'd like to ask her
properly, myself, at some point. Not tonight. But eventually."

"I think that's wise," Isla says. "I suspect she's been waiting a long
while for someone in this family to actually ask, rather than simply
notice and let the noticing go unspoken."

We sit together a while longer, turning over the small mystery of my
sister's guarded heart, and I find myself, thinking about it, grateful
for a season that has taught this whole family, slowly and imperfectly,
that the people who love you best are usually the ones most overdue
for being properly asked what they're actually carrying.

·

Tomas mentions Sela's small mystery himself, unprompted, a few days
later, having apparently noticed the same private smile I described to
Isla.

"Has anyone asked her about it," he says, careful, "or is everyone
simply waiting for her to volunteer it?"

"We're waiting," I admit. "I think it's hers to raise, in her own
time."

"That's probably wise," Tomas says, "though I confess I'm curious. I
don't think I've ever seen her look quite that private about anything
before."

"You've noticed her more closely than I expected," I observe.

"She's become rather important to me, this winter," Tomas admits,
somewhat shyly. "Not simply as your sister. As her own person. I think
I understand her considerably better than I did in the autumn, and I
find I rather like what I've learned."

"I think she'd be glad to hear that," I say, "whenever you find the
occasion to tell her directly."

"Maybe I will," Tomas says, considering it, and I find myself,
watching him turn the possibility over, grateful for the whole small
web of genuine affection that has grown, this winter, between people
who started the season as near-strangers to one another.

·

Isla mentions, that same evening, that Tomas has apparently developed
a genuine fondness for my sister, and I find the observation lands with
more warmth than surprise.

"I noticed something similar," I admit. "He speaks of her differently
than he speaks of anyone else at Highstone. More carefully, somehow.
More like someone he's decided matters."

"Does it worry you," Isla asks, "the age difference, or simply the
complexity of feelings tangled up in an already complicated family?"

"A little," I admit. "Mostly I find myself simply glad. Sela's spent
years guarding herself carefully. If Tomas's own quiet regard is any
part of what finally coaxes her guard down properly, I don't think I'm
inclined to worry about the particulars."

"That's a generous read," Isla says.

"I've had a good season for practicing generous reads," I say, and we
sit together a while longer, both of us quietly hopeful about a small,
tender thread growing between two people we've each come to love in
our own particular ways.


## Chapter Twelve — Ask Before It Happens (Isla)

Dusk over the cairn field above Highstone, the light doing the
particular flat-gold thing it does in the last hour before the real
cold sets in properly for the night, and I have spent the better part
of the afternoon rehearsing a conversation I have never once, in my
adult life, had any practice conducting.

I chose this exact field deliberately, over the cottage's own more
comfortable fire — some part of me understanding that a conversation
this significant deserved the same weight of stone and open sky that
has witnessed every other significant thing in my professional life,
rather than the smaller, softer privacy of a kitchen table.

I have negotiated terms for a great many things. I have never negotiated
this specific one, and I find, walking out to the cairn field with
Aldric beside me, that the rehearsal has not made the actual doing of
it any easier.

"I want to ask you something plainly," I say, once we've reached the
field's edge, the standing stones grey-gold in the dying light around
us, "because I've watched what happens when nobody asks plainly first,
and I won't have that be us, whatever else we end up being to each
other."

"Go on," Aldric says, and I notice, saying it, that he has gone very
still — not guarded, exactly, simply attentive, giving the moment the
whole of his focus in the way he gives most things that matter to him.

"There's something between us," I say. "I've felt it since the binding.
I suspect you have too. I'm not willing to let the rite be the reason
it happens, if it happens — I've read enough about what the bond does,
unbidden, to know that neither of us can fully separate what's ours
from what the cord put there without either of us asking. So I'm
asking. Plainly. In words, the way words are supposed to work between
two people who actually mean them."

I make myself say the rest of it, though it costs me considerably more
than the beginning did.

"Do you want this. Not the binding. Not the rite's cost-sharing. The
actual thing sitting between us that neither of us has named yet."

Aldric doesn't answer immediately, and I let the silence stand, because
I have learned, watching him, that he takes questions like this
seriously enough to actually consider them rather than simply
performing an answer he thinks I want to hear.

"Yes," he says, finally, no hesitation once it arrives, no performance
in it at all. "Since before I knew your name, if I'm honest, which I'd
rather be with you specifically than with almost anyone else I've ever
known. I volunteered for this binding not knowing who the wardwright
would be. I'd have volunteered again, knowing it would be you, without
a single day's more consideration."

"That's not a small thing to admit."

"No," he agrees. "I don't think either of us has much use, anymore, for
the small versions of the things we actually mean."

"I need you to hear the rest of what I'm asking," I say, "because it
matters more than the first part. I'm asking whether you want this for
yourself. Not because the bond makes wanting it easier. Not because
proximity's done half the work already. I need to know the difference
matters to you as much as it matters to me, because I've watched what
happens to people who let a compelled thing stand in for a chosen one,
and I won't have that be either of us."

"It matters," Aldric says, plainly. "I've thought about the difference
myself, more than once, lying awake in the second room you built the
fire up in without being asked. I don't think what I feel is the bond
talking. I think the bond simply gave me faster access to something
that was already there, underneath, waiting for either of us to notice
it properly."

I ask him the same question back, in the same plain terms he's just
used with me, because it seems only fair that the asking run in both
directions rather than only mine.

"Do you want this," I say. "For yourself. Separate from whatever the
rite's made easier."

"Yes," Aldric says, without a moment's hesitation this time. "I've
wanted it since the road below Highstone, the first proper conversation
we had that wasn't strictly business. I didn't let myself examine it
closely, at first, because I wasn't certain it was mine to want, given
everything else the binding had already decided for both of us. I'm
certain now."

We stand at the cairn field's edge a while longer, neither of us moving
to close the distance, both of us, I think, understanding that the
asking itself is the point tonight — a vow, quoted and taken apart word
by word, the way the old texts describe examining a rite before you let
it become a single unexamined thing you simply trust without testing.

"There's one more thing," I say, because I have decided, somewhere in
the walk out here, that I am not leaving this field with anything
unsaid that might matter later. "If either of us ever needs this to
stop — for any reason, at any point, whether the reason makes sense to
the other person or not — I need us to be able to say so, and have it
actually stop, without either of us having to argue for the right to
change our mind."

"Agreed," Aldric says, immediately, entirely. "I'd want the same
thing said back to me, if the need ever runs the other direction."

"It's said," I say. "Plainly. In words. The way this whole conversation
has been."

Nothing further happens between us tonight, out at the cairn field, in
the last of the flat gold light. Nothing needs to. The asking has been
the whole of what either of us came out here for, and I walk back
toward the cottage beside him with something in my chest that feels,
for the first time in six years, considerably less like a cost and
considerably more like a choice.

·

I do not sleep properly that night, though not for any of the reasons
that have kept me awake in the weeks since the binding. I lie instead
turning the whole conversation over, checking it the way I check a
ward's charge, looking for the seam where the asking might have been
less honest than it felt in the moment, and do not find one, however
many times I run the check.

I think about my mother, briefly, the way I've found myself doing more
often since finally telling Aldric about her a few weeks ago — the
first time I'd said her name aloud to anyone at Highstone in the whole
of my adult life. Fragments, mostly, are all I have to think with: the
particular blue of a shawl she wore constantly, the smell of bread she
baked every third day without fail, a warmth I have spent considerably
more of my adult life missing than I generally let myself admit.

I wonder, lying awake, what she would have made of Aldric. I suspect,
turning the thought over, that she would have approved — she valued
steadiness over spectacle, by what little I remember of her, much the
way I find myself valuing it now, watching a man who mends gloves
before dawn and says nothing about it afterward.

I get up, eventually, unable to properly sleep, and go to the window,
and look out at the cairn field in the dark, the standing stones just
visible under a sky gone properly clear for once, and think, watching
them, that I have spent six years believing this line was the whole of
what I had to hold onto in this world. I understand now, watching those
same stones with an entirely different weight sitting in my chest, that
I was wrong about that in a way I am only beginning to properly
measure.

·

I fall asleep, eventually, at the window, and wake near dawn to find
Aldric has come looking for me, quiet, careful not to startle me out of
whatever rest I'd finally managed.

"You didn't sleep," he says, not quite a question, taking in the cold
window, the untouched bed behind me.

"I couldn't," I admit. "I kept turning the whole evening over, checking
it for a seam I never found."

"And now?"

"Now I'm considerably more tired," I say, "and considerably more
certain than I was at midnight that whatever we said to each other
tonight was entirely honest, on both sides."

Aldric sits beside me at the window, and we watch the last of the dark
give way to the particular grey of a Coldwake dawn together, neither of
us saying much of anything further, simply sharing the specific quiet
satisfaction of two people who have, for once, managed to stay awake
turning over the same good thing rather than the same frightening one.

"I should tell you something," he says, eventually, "that I've been
turning over myself, these past few nights."

"Go on."

"I wrote down a list of everything I'm afraid of, a few nights back.
I've never done that before — simply named the fears, plainly, rather
than letting them manage themselves in the back of my head."

"What was on it?"

"That the rite takes more than either of us accounts for. That you'll
spend yourself trying to stop that. And, underneath both of those, that
whatever we've built won't survive whatever's still coming." He looks
at me, plainly, in the grey dawn light. "I wanted you to know the list
exists. Not necessarily every item on it. Simply that I've started
naming my own fears properly, the way you've been teaching me to,
rather than managing them silently the way I always have before."

"Thank you," I say, "for telling me it exists," and take his hand, and
we sit together at the window a while longer, watching Coldwake's
particular grey dawn finish arriving, considerably less afraid, both of
us, for having named at least some part of what frightens us properly,
out loud, to someone who's chosen to stay and hear it.

·

Tomas finds us both still at the window when he arrives for the
morning's business, and takes one look at the state of us — clearly
awake all night, considerably more unguarded than our usual professional
composure — before wisely deciding not to comment directly.

"I've brought the overnight readings," he says instead, setting down
his reports with careful diplomacy, "and I'll just note, for my own
records, that neither of you appears to have slept, which I am choosing
to interpret generously."

"Interpret it however you like, Tomas."

"I generally do," he says, with his usual dry satisfaction, and goes to
put the kettle on, leaving the two of us to properly rouse ourselves
for the day's actual work.

I find myself, going through the morning's ordinary business —
checking readings, reviewing Tomas's own overnight notes, the whole
familiar rhythm of six years of practice — considerably more grateful
than usual for the specific steadiness of routine. Whatever fears
either of us named at the window before dawn, the line still needs
holding, the cairns still need checking, and there is, I find, a
particular comfort in discovering that fear and duty can coexist
without either one canceling out the other.

"You're smiling," Aldric observes, midway through the morning's
reports.

"I'm considering," I say, "that I might actually be alright. Not
fixed. Not certain. But alright, in the way that matters, this
particular morning," and he smiles back, and we return to the day's
work together, considerably steadier for the night's honesty than
either of us managed before it.

·

Sela stops by later that same afternoon, ostensibly to deliver a basket
of the orchard's last stored produce, and catches the specific quality
of ease between Aldric and me before either of us has said a single
word about the previous night.

"Something's different," she observes, setting down the basket,
studying us both with the particular assessing look I've come to
recognize as entirely her own. "You're both entirely too pleased with
yourselves for a Tuesday."

"We had an honest conversation," I say, "about what we actually want,
from each other, going forward."

"That's rather a large admission, delivered rather casually," Sela
says, though I catch the genuine warmth underneath her dry tone.

"I've decided casual delivery is easier than the alternative," I say.
"I'm still learning how to say large things without making an
occasion of them."

"I approve of the method," Sela says. "Occasions have a way of making
people manage their words too carefully. I'd rather hear the truth
delivered plainly, over a basket of apples, than dressed up for some
formal moment neither of you actually needs."

She stays a while, the three of us settling into the particular easy
rhythm that's become, these past weeks, one of my favorite parts of
the day, and I find myself, watching Aldric tease his sister about the
basket's suspiciously generous size, grateful for a family that has,
somehow, made room for me this quickly and this completely.

"I like watching the two of you like this," Sela says, eventually,
quieter. "I want you to know that. I wasn't certain, at first, that I
would."

"I know," I say. "I'm grateful you've let yourself get there anyway."

"So am I," Sela says, and the three of us sit together a while longer
in the cottage's afternoon light, considerably more like family than
circumstance, however this whole arrangement first began.

·

Sela lingers after the basket's been properly emptied, watching Aldric
step out to check the woodpile, and turns to me with a considering
look I haven't quite seen from her before.

"Can I ask you something," she says, "properly, before he comes back?"

"Of course."

"Are you happy," she asks, plainly, "genuinely, not the professional
composure you show the village. I've watched my brother's face,
these past weeks, and I recognize the particular lightness in it. I
haven't asked, until now, whether you're carrying the same lightness,
or simply managing his happiness alongside your own duties."

I consider the question properly, surprised by how much it means,
coming from her specifically.

"I am," I say, finally. "Genuinely. I don't think I've been properly
happy, in this particular way, since before my mentor died. I'd
forgotten what it felt like, honestly, until these past weeks reminded
me."

Sela nods, satisfied, something in her posture easing.

"Good," she says. "I needed to hear that plainly, from you, rather than
simply assume it from watching him. I've spent my whole life
protecting my brother's happiness without anyone thinking to ask
whether mine, or anyone else's, mattered equally in the equation."

"I'll ask you the same, sometime," I say, "when you're ready to answer
it."

"I'll hold you to that," Sela says, with a small, genuine smile, and
Aldric returns from the woodpile none the wiser about the conversation
that's just passed between the two women he loves most, and the three
of us settle back into the afternoon's easy quiet, considerably more
honestly connected for the small, private exchange.

·

Tomas arrives just as Sela's preparing to leave, catching her at the
door with a shy eagerness I haven't quite seen from him before.

"I wanted to walk partway back with you," he offers, "if that's
welcome. I have a report to deliver at the orchard anyway."

"I'd like the company," Sela says, and something in her voice carries
a warmth I file away carefully, the way I file most small, promising
details this winter keeps handing me.

They set off together, and I watch them go from the doorway, Aldric
coming to stand beside me.

"Do you see it too," he asks, quietly.

"I do," I say. "I think I'm going to enjoy watching that particular
story unfold, whatever shape it eventually takes."

"So am I," Aldric agrees, and we go back inside together, considerably
warmed by the small, hopeful thread we've just watched walk out the
door.


## Chapter Thirteen — The Kiss (Isla)

Full dark, the following evening, the cairn field gone silver under a
sky clear enough that every standing stone throws its own particular
shadow, longer than daylight ever lets them manage.

I am the one who closes the distance.

It costs me something, visible, to do it — a woman who has spent a
decade taking costs silently, in private, without ever once letting
anyone watch the taking, for once taking one out loud, deliberately,
where it can be seen by the one person whose seeing of it actually
matters. I feel my own pulse do something unfamiliar, crossing the
last few feet of frozen ground between us, and I let it, rather than
managing it into something calmer the way I manage most things that
frighten me.

Aldric's hand at my jaw, when it arrives, is careful in a way that has
nothing to do with the cold and everything to do with a man who has
spent his whole life being careful with things he's afraid of breaking,
handling a graft, handling a wound, handling, now, apparently, me,
with the same exact attention he gives anything he's decided is worth
the trouble of not damaging.

The kiss itself is not scripted, not negotiated moment to moment beyond
the plain yes already given the evening before at the cairn field's
edge. It simply happens, the way a held breath finally happens into an
exhale — inevitable, once you've stopped holding it, and considerably
gentler than the anticipation of it led me to expect.

I have kissed exactly two people in my adult life before tonight,
neither of them recently, neither of them with anything like the
specific attention Aldric is currently giving the moment, as though I
am not simply a woman he's decided he wants but an actual, particular
person whose particular preferences are worth learning properly rather
than assumed.

Neither of us says anything, after. There isn't a sentence built yet
that would improve on the silence, and I find, standing in the cold
with his hand still at my jaw and my own hand fisted, without my having
decided to put it there, in the front of his coat, that I do not
especially want there to be one.

"That wasn't the bond," Aldric says, eventually, quiet, not quite a
question.

"No," I agree. "That was considerably too specific to be the bond. The
bond gives me sensation. It doesn't give me opinions about the exact
shape of your mouth."

He laughs, once, low, surprised out of him, and it is the first time I
have heard him laugh properly since the binding, unguarded, entirely
without the careful management he gives most of his own reactions.

"You have opinions about the shape of my mouth," he says.

"I have several," I say, "which I am not currently prepared to
enumerate, on the grounds that doing so would require considerably more
composure than I currently have available."

"That's fair," Aldric says, and kisses me again, unhurried, and I let
myself, for the length of that second kiss and the several after it,
stop running any arithmetic at all, which is, I understand even as I'm
doing it, the single rarest thing I have allowed myself in six years of
careful, constant counting.

·

We walk back to the cottage slowly, hand in hand, which is its own
small revolution — I have not walked anywhere holding another person's
hand since before my mentor's death, and I find the specific ordinary
warmth of it considerably more disarming than the kiss itself managed
to be.

"I want to tell you something," Aldric says, partway down the track,
"and I want you to hear it exactly as plainly as I mean it."

"Go on."

"I don't know what this is going to cost either of us, in the end. The
line's still failing. The rite's still taking more from me than either
of us has fully reckoned with yet. I don't have a version of tonight
where I promise you it all works out cleanly, because I don't believe
in promises I can't actually guarantee."

"I don't want that kind of promise," I say. "I've never trusted the
clean ones. I'd rather have the true one."

"Then here's the true one," Aldric says. "Whatever this costs either of
us, I'd rather spend it choosing you than have it taken from me some
other way, without my consent, by a rite neither of us built. That's
the whole of what I can honestly offer tonight."

"It's enough," I say, and mean it, and we walk the rest of the way to
the cottage in a silence that has stopped, entirely, feeling like the
wary quiet of two strangers bound together by circumstance, and started,
instead, to feel like something considerably closer to home.

·

Tomas is, predictably, waiting up when we finally return, ostensibly
finishing the evening's reports, actually, I suspect, simply curious
enough about how the evening went to have manufactured a reason to
still be at the cottage table when we walked in.

He takes one look at both of us — at whatever expression neither of us
has quite managed to smooth back into professional neutrality — and
sets down his pen with the specific ceremony of a young man determined
not to make a scene of the moment while making, in fact, rather a
scene of it.

"I'm not going to say anything," he announces.

"Good," I say.

"I'm simply going to note, for my own records, that you both look
considerably more settled than you did this morning, and that I take
full credit for whatever arrangement of the schedule allowed for a
private evening at the cairn field."

"You had nothing to do with tonight's schedule, Tomas."

"I had everything to do with it," he says, with enormous, entirely
unearned confidence. "I finished the inspection reports early
specifically so neither of you would have an excuse to stay late at the
record-room instead."

I don't have a ready argument against that particular claim, mostly
because I suspect, examining it, that it might even be partially true —
Tomas has grown considerably more attentive, these past weeks, to
exactly which small logistical mercies might give the two of us more
unsupervised time together, without ever once commenting on it directly
until he's decided the moment has properly arrived to take his credit.

"Go to bed, Tomas," I say, without any real heat in it.

"Going," he says, gathering his reports with theatrical slowness. "I
simply wanted it on the record that I noticed, and that I approve, and
that I intend to keep noticing, loudly, until one of you actually
confirms what the whole of Highstone has already worked out for
itself."

He goes, finally, still entirely too pleased with himself, and Aldric
and I are left alone in the cottage's quiet, and I find myself,
watching Tomas's retreating back, considerably more grateful than
irritated for a nineteen-year-old who has appointed himself, without
being asked, the whole of Highstone's unofficial keeper of our
particular happiness.

·

"Can I ask you something," Aldric says, once the cottage has properly
settled into its evening quiet, "about the cairn field. Not what was
said. How it felt, saying it."

I consider the question properly, because it deserves more than a
reflexive answer.

"Frightening," I say. "Considerably more frightening than setting a
ward at a failing cairn, which I recognize is a strange comparison, but
it's the truest one available to me. A cairn's cost is at least
predictable, within a margin. This wasn't."

"What frightened you specifically?"

"That you'd say no," I admit. "Not because I doubted what I felt. Because
I've spent six years believing that wanting something plainly, out
loud, is the surest way to be told you can't have it. I'd rather never
ask than ask and be refused. I've built my whole adult life around that
particular avoidance."

"And now you've asked anyway."

"And now I've asked anyway," I agree, "twice, actually, since the
cairn field — once there, and again tonight, in a considerably smaller
way, simply by letting Tomas tease us both without immediately
deflecting it into something safer."

Aldric takes my hand, turning it over in his, tracing the specific
calluses six years of ward-work have left across my palm.

"I think that's rather the bravest thing I've watched you do," he
says, "braver than Hollow Farm, braver than anything you've spent
yourself on quietly. Asking plainly, and letting the answer actually
matter to you, in front of witnesses."

"I don't feel brave," I say. "I feel considerably more exposed than
brave usually feels, in the stories anyway."

"Perhaps that's what actual bravery generally feels like," Aldric
says, "rather than the tidy version the stories prefer," and I find,
hearing it, that I believe him entirely, and we sit together a while
longer in the cottage's low firelight, both of us considerably more
exposed, and considerably less afraid of the exposure, than either of
us was a season ago.

·

Word of the cairn field conversation reaches Sela before I've had a
proper chance to tell her myself, which does not surprise me in the
slightest, given how efficiently news apparently travels the short
distance between Highstone and the orchard.

"You asked her properly," Sela says, the next time I see her, not
quite a question, though there's a warmth in it that tells me she
already knows the answer.

"I did."

"And?"

"And she said yes," I say, "and asked me the same question back, and I
said yes to that too, and I don't think either of us has fully worked
out yet what happens next, beyond simply choosing to keep choosing each
other, one day at a time."

Sela considers this, nodding slowly.

"That's rather all any of us actually manage," she says, "if we're
honest about it. I don't think anyone knows what happens next, not
truly. I think the ones who do well are simply the ones willing to
keep asking the question, the way you two apparently have started
doing."

"When did you get so wise about relationships," I ask, "for someone
who's spent our whole adult lives claiming she has no interest in one
herself?"

Sela's expression does something complicated at that, a flicker of
something she's clearly not ready to discuss, and I recognize it,
filing it away the same careful way she filed away my own reticence for
years, understanding that some doors open on their own schedule, and
that I owe her the same patience she's always extended to me.

·

I tell Aldric about the exchange that same evening, curious what he'll
make of his sister's sudden, complicated flicker of expression.

"She gets that look sometimes," he says, "when relationships come up.
I've never pressed her on it. I assumed, for a long time, that she
simply preferred her own company, the way some people genuinely do.
I'm starting to wonder if it's something else."

"I didn't press either," I say. "I don't think it's mine to press. But
I noticed it, and I wanted you to know I noticed, in case it's
something you'd rather ask her about yourself, properly, the way
you've been practicing asking things properly lately."

"I might," Aldric says, considering it. "Though I suspect she'll tell
me in her own time, the way she's always done things, rather than being
asked into an answer before she's ready to give it."

"That's probably wise," I agree. "I've learned, this whole winter, that
asking properly doesn't always mean asking immediately. Sometimes it
means simply making it known that you're willing to hear it, whenever
the person's actually ready to say it."

"You've become considerably wiser about this than either of us
expected, six weeks ago," Aldric says, with the particular warmth that's
become, these past days, one of my favorite things about him.

"I've had an excellent teacher," I say, "in exactly the opposite
direction — watching what happens when things go unasked for years at
a time, and deciding, deliberately, not to repeat the pattern."

We sit together a while longer, turning over the small mystery of
Sela's own guarded heart, both of us agreeing, without needing to say
it plainly, that whatever she's carrying deserves the same patient,
unhurried care either of us would want extended to our own guarded
places.

·

Tomas arrives the following morning considerably more subdued than his
usual self, and I understand, watching him settle at the record-room
table, that something's weighing on him beyond the ordinary business
of reports.

"You're quiet," I observe.

"I've been thinking about what Sela said," he admits, "about wanting
something worth building a life alongside, rather than settling for
something that merely fills the space. I don't think I'd ever
articulated that distinction to myself before she said it."

"Does it worry you?"

"A little," Tomas says. "I'm nineteen. I don't know yet what I actually
want, in that particular direction. I think I'd been assuming, before
this winter, that I simply hadn't gotten around to wanting anything yet.
Watching the two of you, and now hearing Sela's own history, I'm
starting to wonder if I've been avoiding the question rather than
merely not yet arriving at it."

"You don't have to have an answer at nineteen," I say. "I certainly
didn't."

"I know," Tomas says. "I think I simply wanted to say the uncertainty
out loud, rather than let it sit unexamined the way apparently everyone
in this family used to let their own uncertainties sit."

"That's a wise instinct," I say, "regardless of when you eventually
find the answer."

We sit together a while longer over the morning's reports, and I find
myself, watching Tomas work through his own quiet uncertainty with more
honesty than most adults manage, grateful for a young man determined
to practice this whole family's hard-won lesson properly, from the
very start, rather than waiting years to unlearn the alternative.

·

Aldric arrives partway through the morning, having heard some version
of the conversation secondhand from Tomas himself on the walk over, and
settles beside me once Tomas has gone to deliver the day's inspection
notes.

"He's been thinking about the future rather a lot lately," Aldric
observes.

"I noticed," I say. "I think this whole winter's forced everyone in
this family to examine questions they'd otherwise have comfortably
postponed for years."

"Is that a bad thing," Aldric asks, "forcing the examination early?"

"I don't think so," I say, considering it properly. "I think the
questions were always going to need asking eventually. I'd rather
everyone ask them now, honestly, while there's still time to actually
live differently because of the answers, than wait until circumstance
forces the asking the way it forced ours."

"That's a fair point," Aldric agrees, "though I confess I wouldn't
have chosen quite this particular crisis as the occasion for everyone's
collective self-examination."

"Neither would I," I say, "though I find I'm grateful for the
examination regardless, whatever occasioned it," and we sit together a
while longer, both of us turning over the whole shape of a winter that
has, in its own difficult way, handed this entire small family
considerably more clarity than any of them arrived with.

·

Sela stops by later that same day, curious about Tomas's own quiet
uncertainty, having apparently heard some account of it from him
directly.

"He asked me, plainly, how I knew what I actually wanted," she tells
me. "I didn't have a tidy answer for him. I told him the truth instead
— that I'm still working it out myself, most days, and that not
knowing at nineteen, or even at my own age, isn't a failing so much as
simply the honest state of things."

"That's a generous answer," I say.

"It's the only honest one I had," Sela says. "I think he appreciated
the honesty more than he'd have appreciated a comforting fiction."

"I think you're right," I say, and we sit together a while longer,
both of us grateful for a young man wise enough to ask the question
plainly, and for a family finally practiced enough to answer him
honestly rather than with whatever reassurance felt easiest.


## Chapter Fourteen — Grafting Season (Aldric)

I take her back up to the orchard a second time, a few days after the
cairn field, in considerably less official capacity than the first
visit — no cairn to inspect this time, no grain record to review,
simply an afternoon neither of us has anywhere more urgent to spend.

I name every tree by the season I grafted it, a private calendar
nobody else has ever bothered to properly learn, and walk Isla through
the whole of it the way a man shows someone his actual handwriting for
the first time, rather than the careful, legible version he uses for
anyone he hasn't decided yet to trust.

"That one's a hard frost graft," I say, of a stunted, twisted specimen
near the eastern wall. "Season before last. I grafted it badly — too
cold, the wax set wrong, the join never quite took the way it should
have. My father would have cut it down. I kept it anyway."

"Why?"

"Because a mistake you own outright is worth more than a straight line
you never earned," I say, which is a thing my father used to say about
considerably smaller mistakes than the ones I've made since, though I
have found it holds up regardless of scale.

We reach the tree near the northern wall, the one I mentioned once
before without fully explaining, and I find myself, walking up to it,
considerably less composed than I expected to be.

"That one stopped fruiting the week the line moved," I say. "Two
winters back. The frost came further south than it had any right to,
that particular season, and took the whole northern row with it,
except this one. It survived. It just stopped bearing anything. I kept
it anyway. Didn't seem right, cutting down a thing for failing at
something the cold did to it rather than something it did to itself."

Isla looks at the tree for a long moment, and I understand, watching
her, that she has worked out exactly which tree I actually mean.

"You're not talking about the orchard," she says, quietly.

"No," I admit. "Not entirely."

"You kept yourself standing," she says, "after the line took something
from you that wasn't your fault to lose. And you've been calling it a
tree ever since, so nobody has to look at it directly and ask what it
actually cost you."

I don't have a ready answer for being so precisely understood, so I
simply stand beside her, looking at a crooked, barren tree I have never
once, in two winters, properly grieved in front of another living soul.

"You'd have liked the orchard whole," I say, eventually, mostly to give
us both somewhere else to put the moment.

"I'd have liked you whichever way I met you," Isla says, and I feel
that land on me harder than I expected a simple sentence to manage —
harder, in its own way, than the ward-cost the bond hands me every time
I stand too close to a working stone.

I put myself between her and the wind without noticing I've done it,
the same reflex that put my own name forward at the rite instead of
Sela's, and Isla, watching me do it, doesn't comment, though I catch
her filing the observation away, carefully, the way she files
everything she's learning about me that I haven't said outright.

"You do that constantly," she says, eventually. "Put yourself between
other people and whatever's coming. I don't think you've noticed how
often."

"It's not something I decide to do," I say. "It's simply where my body
goes, given the choice."

"I know," Isla says. "That's rather what worries me about it."

We walk the rest of the orchard slowly, in the particular unhurried
quiet of two people who have run out of urgent business and found,
underneath the urgency, something considerably more worth the time —
an afternoon spent simply being known, a little more thoroughly than
either of us has ever let anyone manage before, by someone who seems,
against every reasonable expectation either of us walked into this
binding with, inclined to keep the knowing carefully rather than spend
it carelessly.

·

We find Sela in the grain-house on our way back down, counting the
season's stored apples with the particular focused patience of a woman
who has done this exact count every winter of her adult life and has
never once trusted anyone else's tally over her own.

"You two look considerably more comfortable together than you did a
fortnight ago," she observes, not looking up from her count.

"We've had a productive afternoon," I say.

"I'm sure you have," Sela says, with the same dry precision I've come
to recognize as a family trait, shared between her and Aldric both,
though Aldric's version comes out considerably more guarded than hers.
"He showed you the whole orchard, I take it. Including the crooked
tree."

"Including the crooked tree," I confirm.

Sela finally looks up, something shifting in her expression — not
quite surprise, closer to a specific recalibration, the look of a woman
revising an estimate she'd made and finding the actual figure larger
than expected.

"He doesn't show that tree to people," she says, to me, plainly. "I
don't think he's shown it to anyone since our father died. I certainly
haven't seen him take anyone up to it on purpose."

I glance at Aldric, who has gone slightly still at his sister's
observation, the specific stillness of a man caught having done
something he hadn't fully registered as significant until someone else
named it as such.

"I didn't realize," I say.

"I don't think he realized either," Sela says, "until just now, watching
your face when I mentioned it." She goes back to her count, apparently
satisfied with whatever conclusion she's drawn, though I catch her
smiling, slightly, at the apples, in a way that tells me the
conclusion is a favorable one.

We walk the rest of the way down from the orchard in a companionable
quiet, and I find myself turning Sela's observation over the whole
descent — the specific weight of having been shown something Aldric has
apparently kept from everyone else in his life, without either of us
fully realizing, until his own sister named it plainly, exactly how
much that showing actually meant.

·

Tomas has news waiting when we finally reach Highstone, delivered with
the specific breathless urgency of a young man who has been sitting on
something significant for the better part of an afternoon.

"A messenger came through from the eastern waystones," he says. "There's
a second village reported past the line, considerably further east
than Calder's Reach. Nobody's checked it in years — it wasn't on any
active record until the messenger happened to mention it in passing to
one of the market traders."

I feel the specific cold settle that any mention of the line's further
losses always brings, though I notice, this time, that Aldric's hand
finds mine almost immediately, a small, steadying anchor against the
particular dread of yet another confirmed loss.

"Do we know anything else about it," I ask.

"Only the name," Tomas says. "Fenhollow. And that it's been past the
line for at least a decade, possibly longer, judging by how casually
the messenger mentioned it, as though its loss were simply an
established fact rather than news."

I add the name to my own growing ledger of the line's confirmed losses,
a considerably longer list than I'd like it to be, and feel, writing
it down, the whole weight of exactly how much ground the Rime has
already claimed before I ever properly took up this post.

"We should go, eventually," I say, "and see what it holds. There may be
something worth learning from a village that's stood past the line long
enough for its own loss to be treated as simple fact rather than news."

"Eventually," Aldric agrees. "Not this week. I don't think either of us
has room, right now, for an expedition that far past the line, on top
of everything else the line's already asking of us."

"No," I agree, "not right now," though I feel, adding Fenhollow's name
to the ledger, the specific unease of a woman beginning to understand
that the line's losses run considerably deeper and further back than
even her own six years of careful record-keeping has properly
accounted for.

·

Tomas asks, later that same evening, whether he might take on the task
of properly cataloguing every lost settlement the crown's own records
mention, however briefly, on the theory that a complete list might
eventually prove useful, whatever shape "useful" eventually takes.

"That's considerably more work than your current duties require," I
say.

"I know," Tomas says. "I want to do it anyway. I've been thinking, since
Fenhollow came up, about how little this line's own history is actually
written down properly, in one place, rather than scattered across
whichever administrative note happened to mention a loss in passing. I
think someone should be keeping that record properly, the way you keep
the cairn ledger, and I'd like it to be me."

I look at him properly, understanding, watching his face, that this
isn't simply an apprentice looking for extra work to prove his
diligence. This is a young man beginning to understand, on his own
terms, exactly what kind of wardwright he wants eventually to become.

"Do it," I say. "Properly, at your own pace, alongside your other
duties. I think it's exactly the kind of record this line has been
missing, and I think you're precisely the person who should be the
one to build it."

Tomas's face does something bright and unguarded at the permission,
and he sets to the task that same evening, pulling old administrative
notes from the archive with the specific focused energy of a young man
who has just been handed something he genuinely wants to do, rather
than merely something he's been assigned.

·

Isla finds me still in the workshop, later that night, turning
Fenhollow's name over in my own head long after everyone else has gone
to bed.

"You're still awake," she observes.

"I keep thinking about the crooked tree," I admit, "and about Fenhollow,
and about how much of this line's own history has apparently just been
allowed to quietly disappear, unrecorded, the same way I very nearly
let my own hand disappear without telling anyone."

"That's not entirely the same thing."

"Isn't it though," I say. "A loss nobody names properly eventually just
becomes background. Ordinary. The kind of thing a messenger mentions
in passing to a market trader, rather than something anyone actually
grieves. I don't want that happening to anyone else, the way it very
nearly happened to my own hand."

Isla sits beside me, considering this properly, the way she considers
most things I bring to her that matter.

"I think that's precisely why Tomas wanting to build the record matters
so much," she says. "Not simply as an administrative exercise. As a
refusal to let anything else disappear quietly, the way Fenhollow
apparently has, the way your own hand very nearly did."

"I hadn't thought of it in those terms," I say, "but I think you're
right."

"I usually am, about grief specifically," Isla says, with a dry humor
that doesn't fully mask the truth underneath it. "It's rather my
particular specialty, this winter."

We sit together in the workshop's low lamplight a while longer,
neither of us in any hurry to end the evening, and I find myself,
watching her in the particular quiet of a house finally settling
toward sleep, considerably more grateful than I know how to properly
say for a woman who has taught me, in the space of a few short weeks,
that naming a loss plainly is its own kind of mercy, however painful
the naming turns out to be.

·

"Can I tell you something I haven't told anyone," I say, before either
of us moves toward bed, "about the crooked tree specifically. Not the
grief part. Something else."

"Go on."

"I used to think keeping it alive was a kind of penance," I say. "For
surviving the frost that took the rest of the row. I've started
wondering, lately, whether it was actually something closer to hope —
that I kept it because some part of me believed, even without evidence,
that a thing that survives can eventually bear fruit again, given
enough patient tending."

Isla considers this, turning it over with the particular gravity she
gives most things I bring her that matter.

"I think both can be true at once," she says. "Penance and hope aren't
always opposites. Sometimes the tending itself is the bridge between
them — you start out doing it because you feel you owe the thing your
attention, and somewhere along the way, without deciding to, you start
doing it because you actually believe it might still become something."

"When did you get so wise about trees," I ask, "for a woman who spends
her whole career reading stone instead."

"I've had a rather thorough education in stubborn, half-alive things
that refuse to give up entirely," Isla says, with a small, dry smile, "given the company I currently keep."

I laugh, properly, surprised by it, and we finally head toward bed
together, the workshop's lamp banked low behind us, both of us carrying
a little more hope into the night than either of us arrived at the
conversation with.

·

Sela mentions the crooked tree herself, unprompted, a few days later,
catching me at the grain-house before I've properly greeted her.

"I've been thinking about that tree," she says, "since you told Isla
about it. I wonder if I might have a look at it properly, this spring,
with you. I've done considerably more reading on frost-damaged grafts
than you'd expect, these past few winters."

"I didn't know that about you."

"There's a great deal you don't know about me yet," Sela says, with
the small, private smile that's become familiar these past weeks. "I
started reading about grafting properly after Father died, on the
theory that understanding his one genuine passion better might help me
understand him better too. I don't know that it worked, precisely. But
I learned a fair amount about reviving damaged trees in the process."

"I'd welcome your look at it," I say, surprised and moved by the offer.
"I think I've been too close to it, too long, to properly assess
whether it's actually salvageable or whether I've simply been keeping
a monument."

"Perhaps it's both," Sela says. "Perhaps that's alright too." She
considers the tree's location, visible from where we stand. "I'll come
by properly, once the thaw's far enough along to examine the wood
without freezing my hands off in the attempt."

"I'd like that," I say, and find myself, watching her walk back toward
the grain-house, considerably more hopeful about the spring ahead than
I've allowed myself to be in longer than I can properly place.

·

I tell Isla about Sela's offer that same evening, still turning over
the specific surprise of my sister's private study of grafting.

"I don't think I properly appreciated," I say, "how much she's quietly
carried of our father, all these years. Reading about his one genuine
passion, alone, without ever mentioning it to me."

"People carry their grief in whatever shape actually fits them," Isla
says, "not necessarily the shape anyone else would predict."

"I think you're right," I say. "I think I've spent considerably too
long assuming I knew the whole shape of my own sister, without
properly checking that assumption against who she's actually become."

"None of us know the whole shape of anyone," Isla says, gently, "not
even the people we've loved our whole lives. I think that's rather
one of the better lessons this winter's taught me — that there's
always more to learn, about anyone, if you're willing to keep asking."

"I'm willing," I say, and we sit together a while longer, both of us
grateful for a season that keeps handing this whole family new rooms
to discover in people they thought they already knew completely.


## Chapter Fifteen — The Commissioner's Deadline (Isla)

Feld returns to Highstone with a second ledger, this one already half
written before he's even sat down, in a hand considerably less careful
than my own or Aldric's, the specific rushed economy of a man who has
already decided the outcome and is merely gathering the paperwork to
justify it.

I recognize the particular set of his shoulders before he's said a
single word, the same posture he wore delivering the first ultimatum,
and understand, watching him settle into the council room's hardest
chair, that today's visit is going to cost considerably more than the
last one did.

"One binding isn't holding forty miles, Wardwright," he says, without
preamble. "The crown wants a plan for the apprentice, in case the
current arrangement doesn't outlast the season."

Aldric is present for this meeting, for the first time, having come
down from the orchard on the pretext of grafting supplies neither of us
has bothered to examine too closely, and I feel, through the bond, the
specific cold that moves through him at the word *doesn't* — not fear,
precisely, something closer to a man hearing his own mortality
discussed as a line item by someone who has never once had to feel what
that line item actually costs a body to carry.

"The binding will outlast the season," I say, which is not, strictly, a
fact yet. It is a refusal, offered plainly, to hand Feld the
alternative he has come here angling for, the same refusal I've given
him before and intend to keep giving him for as long as I have any say
in the matter at all.

"I hope, for everyone's sake, that it does," Feld says, in the tone of
a man who does not, in fact, especially hope so, having already
budgeted for the alternative in whatever ledger he answers to further
up the crown's own chain. He gathers his papers, pauses at the door.
"You should know, Wardwright — the crown is not sentimental about
lines it's decided cost more than they return. I'd have you prepared
for that, regardless of how this particular winter resolves."

He leaves without further comment, and I stand in the council room a
long moment afterward, doing arithmetic I don't especially want to be
doing, on exactly how much time the crown's patience is likely to
extend before Feld's contingency plan for Tomas becomes, simply, the
plan.

"He's not wrong to be worried," Aldric says, once Feld's footsteps have
faded down the corridor. "Even if I don't care for how he's worried
about it."

I stand a moment longer at the council room's window, watching Feld's
retreating figure grow small against the winter light, and find myself
wondering, not for the first time, exactly what it costs a man to
spend a career treating people as figures on a ledger rather than
lives worth knowing properly.

"I know," I say. "That's rather what makes it difficult to hate him
properly. He's doing his job. His job simply happens to require
treating people like line items, and he's decided, somewhere in his own
career, that doing the job well means not examining that requirement
too closely."

"Are you frightened," Aldric asks, "of what happens if the binding
doesn't hold the way you're telling him it will?"

"Yes," I say, honestly, because I have decided that half-truths cost
more between us now than whole ones do. "I'm frightened for Tomas,
mostly. He's nineteen. He shouldn't be doing arithmetic about his own
mortality this early in a career he's barely begun."

"And for yourself?"

I consider the question properly, the way I've learned to consider most
of what he asks me plainly.

"Less than I was, before the binding," I admit. "I used to be
frightened, mostly, of running out before the line did — of the
arithmetic simply failing, one winter, with nobody left to notice until
it was too late to matter. I'm still frightened of that. But I'm
frightened now, also, of something considerably more specific, which is
losing you to an arithmetic neither of us built and neither of us fully
controls."

Aldric doesn't have an immediate answer to that, and I don't need one.
I simply let the fear sit between us, named rather than managed, the
way I am slowly, unevenly, learning to let most things sit rather than
filing them away where nobody has to look at them directly.

·

Ossory finds me alone in the record-room the next morning, before
Tomas has arrived for the day's reports, and I understand, from the
particular formality of his entrance, that this is not a casual visit.

"Feld came to see me as well," he says, without preamble. "After his
meeting with you. He wanted my assessment of whether the current
binding is likely to hold."

"What did you tell him?"

"That I don't make predictions about a rite I've watched hold and fail
in equal measure over four decades of practice," Ossory says. "Which is
true, and which also bought you rather more time than a direct answer
would have."

I look at him properly, surprised by the specific generosity of the
evasion. Ossory has never, in my six years of dealing with him, gone out
of his way to protect my standing with the crown's own office.

"Why," I ask, plainly.

He considers the question for a long moment, the particular gravity of
a man deciding how much of his own reasoning to share.

"Because I watched your mentor die completing a rite I have never
fully trusted," he says, finally. "I signed off on it, at the time,
because the record demanded a signature and there was no alternative
form on offer. I have wondered, every year since, whether there might
have been a better way, and never found the nerve to go looking for
one myself."

"You think there might be an older form," I say, carefully, testing
whether he's guessed at something I haven't yet told anyone outside
this cottage.

"I think," Ossory says, "that a rite this old, practiced this long,
rarely arrives at its current shape by accident. Corruption implies an
original. I don't know what that original looked like. I would be
considerably less surprised than Feld to learn that someone had found
evidence of it."

I do not tell him, that morning, about the record from Calder's Reach —
I have not yet made the journey, do not yet have the proof in hand —
but I file his words away carefully, understanding, walking back to the
cottage that evening, that I may have, in Keeper Bram Ossory, an ally
considerably more willing to hear an unorthodox truth than his careful,
formal manner has ever let on.

·

I tell Aldric about the conversation that evening, curious what he'll
make of Ossory's unexpected openness.

"That's not the Ossory I've encountered at village councils," Aldric
says. "The one I've met is considerably more concerned with proper
procedure than with entertaining unorthodox theories about corrupted
rites."

"I think there are two versions of him," I say, considering it
properly. "The one who performs the formal role — careful, procedural,
unwilling to be seen taking risks in front of the crown's own
representatives. And another one, underneath that, who's spent four
decades watching this line fail people and has never quite made his
peace with it."

"Which version do you think we'll get, if it ever comes to actually
proposing something unorthodox to him formally?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I hope the second one. I suspect it depends
considerably on how much proof I can ever put in front of him, if
proof of anything is even out there to be found."

"And if it's the first version instead? The procedural one?"

"Then we'll need another approach," I say, "though I'm not yet certain
what that would look like, or even what, precisely, I'd be proposing.
I don't have anything yet beyond a suspicion and a dead man's private
doubt."

Aldric is quiet for a moment, turning the whole conversation over.

"For what it's worth," he says, "I think you've already started
building the kind of trust with him that might tip the scales toward
the second version, whenever the moment actually arrives. Today's
conversation wasn't nothing. I think he's already telling you,
carefully, that he wants to be convinced of something, even if neither
of you yet knows precisely what."

I think about that for the rest of the evening, turning Aldric's
observation over alongside Ossory's own careful words, and find myself,
for the first time since Feld's last visit, cautiously hopeful that
whatever this suspicion eventually turns out to mean, I may have more
allies within the crown's own formal process than I initially believed.

·

I sleep poorly that night regardless, my mind circling the whole
conversation with Ossory, turning over every careful word for
additional meaning, and wake before dawn to find Aldric already up,
sitting by the fire with two cups of tea he's clearly made some time
ago, one now gone properly cold.

"You couldn't sleep either," I observe.

"I woke and found you gone from the bed," he says, "and came to check.
Found you'd been asleep at the kitchen table the whole time, over the
crown's own correspondence, of all things."

"I don't remember falling asleep there."

"You looked exhausted enough that I didn't have the heart to wake
you properly," Aldric says. "I simply put a blanket over your
shoulders and let you rest, until the cold woke you on its own."
