I look at him properly, understanding the specific, unglamorous
kindness of a man who chooses to let someone sleep rather than manage
their comfort more efficiently by waking them.

"Thank you," I say, "for that particular restraint."

"I'm developing quite a collection of small restraints, these days," he
says, with a dry humor that reminds me, pleasantly, of exactly how much
lighter he's become, in small ways, since the binding first brought us
together. "I intend to keep collecting them, if you'll have me."

"I'll have you," I say, and mean it entirely, and we drink our tea
together in the cold kitchen, the day's business waiting patiently for
whenever either of us feels ready to properly begin it.

·

Tomas arrives earlier than usual that same morning, catching us still
at the kitchen table, and hesitates at the door, visibly uncertain
whether he's interrupting something.

"You can come in," I say. "We're only drinking cold tea and putting
off Feld's correspondence."

"I heard he came by again," Tomas says, settling at the table with the
particular careful posture of someone bracing for bad news. "Sela
mentioned it, in passing, at the market yesterday. I didn't like to ask
her directly what he wanted."

"He wants a contingency plan," I say, honestly, because I have decided
Tomas deserves the plain account rather than a softened one, whatever
his age. "In case the current binding doesn't hold the season."

Tomas absorbs this with more steadiness than I expect, though I catch
the specific stillness underneath it, the particular effort of a young
man determined not to let his own fear show more than he can help.

"Is it going to come to that," he asks. "Me, I mean."

"I don't know," I say. "I'm doing everything I can to make certain it
doesn't. I want you to know that, plainly, rather than simply trusting
it without being told."

"I believe you," Tomas says. "I think I've believed it for a while now,
actually. I just wanted to hear it said directly, rather than gathered
secondhand from whatever Feld happens to let slip to people who aren't
even supposed to be listening."

"You'll always hear it directly from me," I say, "whatever it turns
out to be."

Tomas nods, absorbing this, and helps himself to what's left of the
now properly cold tea, and the three of us sit together a while longer
in the kitchen's early morning quiet, the day's harder business
waiting, patiently, for whenever any of us feel ready to face it.

·

Aldric walks Tomas partway to the record-room that same morning,
ostensibly to fetch a tool he's left there, actually, I suspect, to
have a private word out of my own hearing, and returns some while
later considerably more settled than he left.

"What did you say to him," I ask, once he's back.

"I told him I wasn't going to let Feld's contingency touch him," Aldric
says. "Not while I have breath left to prevent it. I know that's not
strictly my promise to make, given I'm not the wardwright. I wanted him
to hear it from me too, regardless."

"That's not nothing," I say, "hearing it from both of us."

"I don't think a nineteen-year-old should have to carry this kind of
fear on the strength of one adult's promise alone," Aldric says. "I
wanted him to have two people telling him the same true thing, in case
either one of us ever falls short of keeping it."

I take his hand, considering this, grateful for a man who has learned,
this whole difficult season, to extend to Tomas the same fierce
protectiveness he's always shown Sela, folding our young apprentice
properly into the family's own inner circle of people worth fighting
for.

"Thank you," I say, "for making sure he heard it twice."

"I'd make sure he heard it a hundred times, if that's what it took," Aldric
says, and we sit together a while longer in the kitchen's morning
quiet, both of us considerably more determined, after the conversation,
to make certain neither promise ever has cause to be tested.

·

Sela sends word that same afternoon, asking whether she might join us
for the evening meal, an unusual enough request that I understand,
reading the note, she has something specific on her mind beyond simple
company.

She arrives with a basket of the orchard's own stores and a directness
that doesn't wait for pleasantries.

"I've heard about Feld's contingency plan," she says, once we're
settled. "The village grapevine again. I wanted to hear the actual
shape of it from you both, rather than whatever version's currently
circulating at market."

I give her the plain account, and watch her absorb it with the same
gravity she's shown toward everything touching this family's safety.

"I want to help," she says, once I've finished. "Whatever this
requires. I have standing with the crown's grain office that neither
of you has cause to have built. I'd like to use it, properly, on
Tomas's behalf, if it comes to needing leverage neither of you
currently possesses."

"That's generous," I say, "and considerably more than I expected you
to offer, given you barely know him."

"I know enough," Sela says. "I know he's been folded into this family's
own accounting the same way I was, uninvited, by a rite neither of us
chose. I don't intend to let the crown treat him as a disposable
contingency while I have any standing left to prevent it."

We finish the meal together, considerably more fortified by Sela's own
fierce offer of help, and I find myself grateful, watching her, for a
family that keeps finding new ways to extend its protection outward,
rather than guarding it jealously within its own original boundaries.

·

Aldric walks Sela partway back to the orchard once the meal's finished,
and I use the quiet hour alone to properly write out, for the first
time, a formal account of Feld's contingency threat — dates, specific
language, the whole pattern of pressure he's applied since the binding
began.

"Building the record again," Aldric observes, returning.

"I've decided it's worth doing properly this time," I say, "rather
than simply reacting to each visit as it comes. If Sela's going to use
her own standing on Tomas's behalf, I want her working from an
accurate accounting, not simply secondhand impressions."

"That's good thinking," Aldric says, settling beside me to help.

We finish the account together, methodically, and I find myself,
closing the finished pages, considerably more confident facing
whatever Feld's office has waiting for us next, armed now with
something more substantial than simply hope and careful memory.


## Chapter Sixteen — What Tomas Is For (Isla)

Tomas has started drilling the binding-rite words in the evenings,
quietly, on his own initiative, in the record-room after the day's
reports are filed, in a voice pitched low enough that he clearly
believes I haven't noticed.

I notice everything in that room. It is, after all, the room I have
spent six years making certain nothing escapes my attention in, on the
theory that the cost of missing something important there is
considerably higher than the cost of noticing something I'd rather not
have seen.

"I'm not putting myself forward," Tomas says, before I can ask, the
moment he realizes I've caught him at it. "I'm just — making sure I'm
ready. If it comes to that. Someone has to be, and it's better it's
someone who's actually prepared than someone conscripted at the last
possible hour with no proper training at all."

"You're nineteen," I say, which is not, strictly, an argument, though I
mean it as one.

"You were nineteen when you started your own apprenticeship," Tomas
says, which is true, and which I do not have a ready counter for,
mostly because I have spent considerable effort, over three years,
avoiding exactly this comparison.

I cannot argue against his readiness without admitting the exact thing
I am not yet prepared to admit to him — that I have been feeding the
failing wards more of myself than the rite alone accounts for, for two
years now, and do not know, with any precision, how much longer that
particular arithmetic holds before it fails entirely, taking me with
it, regardless of whether Feld's contingency ever technically becomes
necessary.

"Keep drilling," I say, finally, because I do not have a version of
this conversation available to me that ends any other way, and because
telling him to stop would mean explaining why, and explaining why would
mean admitting a debt I have not yet found the nerve to hand anyone,
including myself, the full weight of.

It costs me more than telling him to stop would have. I feel the cost
of it settle somewhere behind my ribs, a specific, familiar ache that
has nothing to do with the bond and everything to do with six years of
practice at silently absorbing exactly this kind of weight.

Aldric finds me afterward, in the cottage, sitting with a cup of tea
gone cold in both hands, staring at nothing in particular, and does not
ask what's wrong, exactly — he has learned, in the weeks since the
binding, that I answer direct questions about my own state considerably
worse than I answer the simple offer of company.

He sits with me instead, in the specific kind of silence that isn't
asking for anything back, and I find, some while later, that the ache
behind my ribs has settled into something more manageable, simply for
having had someone else sitting near enough to notice it was there at
all.

"Tomas is drilling the rite words," I say, eventually, because I have
decided, somewhere in the silence, that this particular fact deserves
saying aloud to someone.

"I gathered as much," Aldric says. "I heard him through the record-room
door on my way in. He's got the cadence nearly right already."

"He shouldn't have to have the cadence right at all. Not at nineteen.
Not for years yet, if I have any say in how this line gets handed
forward."

"You do have a say," Aldric says. "You're the one holding it currently.
That counts for something."

"It counts for exactly as long as I can keep holding it," I say, "and I
don't know, with any precision, how long that actually is."

Aldric doesn't press for the specifics, though I feel him wanting to,
the particular restraint of a man who has learned, watching me
carefully over these past weeks, when a question would help and when
it would simply add another weight to a room already carrying more than
its fair share.

"Whatever the number turns out to be," he says, instead, "I don't
think you're carrying it alone anymore. Whether or not you've decided
to believe that yet."

I do not have an answer to that which doesn't risk unraveling
considerably more than a cup of cold tea and an evening's quiet
conversation can properly hold, so I simply let the sentence stand, and
drink the tea anyway, cold as it's gone, because finishing it feels,
tonight, like the one small thing I can still reliably manage on my
own.

·

I find Tomas the following morning already at the record-room table,
the rite-cadence book open in front of him, and instead of the usual
gentle redirection I've given him each time I've caught him at this
particular study, I sit down across from him and let him actually
practice it aloud, properly, watching his form the way Ossian once
watched mine.

"You're rushing the third verse," I say. "The cadence wants to slow
there, not speed up. It's the part where the rite asks the hearth's
name to be spoken, and a name spoken too quickly loses its proper
weight."

Tomas repeats it, slower this time, and the improvement is immediate
and considerable — the particular satisfaction of watching a student
correct something the moment it's named properly, rather than needing
the correction repeated three times before it lands.

"You're good at this," I say, surprised, slightly, by how plainly true
the observation is. "Better than I was, at your age."

"You're only saying that to make me feel better about needing to learn
it at all."

"I'm saying it because it's accurate," I say. "I fumbled the second
verse for the better part of a year. You've had it right since your
second attempt."

Tomas looks, for a moment, genuinely pleased by the compliment, in the
specific unguarded way of a young man who doesn't receive plain praise
often enough to have built up any defense against it.

"I don't want to need this," he says, after a moment, quieter. "The
cadence. I don't want a world where I actually have to use it properly,
because using it properly means either of you failed to hold the
line."

"I know," I say. "I'd rather you never need it either. But I'd rather
you have it, properly learned, and never need it, than need it one day
and not have it at all. That's rather the whole logic behind most of
what I teach you."

"That's a very grim way of framing an education."

"It's an honest way," I say, "which I've decided, this whole autumn,
matters considerably more than the comfortable version."

We work through two more verses before the day's proper business calls
either of us away, and I find myself, watching him go, considerably
prouder of the young man across that table than I have properly let
myself feel in the whole three years of his apprenticeship — a pride
that has nothing to do with contingency planning at all, and
everything to do with simply watching someone I've taught become,
slowly, precisely, exactly the wardwright this line deserves.

·

Aldric finds me still in the record-room afterward, and asks, gently,
how the lesson went.

"Better than I expected," I say. "He has real talent for it, considerably
more than I had at his age. I keep catching myself proud of him in a
way that surprises me, given how carefully I've tried not to let myself
get too attached to any single apprentice — a habit I picked up from
watching how much Ossian's own death cost me, and deciding, at the
time, that I'd rather not build that particular vulnerability twice."

"Is it too late to avoid it now?"

"Considerably too late," I admit. "I think I've been attached to Tomas
since roughly his second month here, if I'm honest with myself about
it. I've simply managed the attachment quietly, the way I manage most
things, rather than letting it show."

"What changed, that you're letting it show now?"

"You did," I say, simply. "Watching you be honest about your own
attachments — to Sela, to the orchard, to whatever this is between us
— has made it considerably harder to keep pretending mine don't exist
either."

Aldric smiles at that, the specific unguarded smile that has become,
these past weeks, one of my favorite things to notice arriving on his
face.

"I'm glad," he says, "that Tomas gets to have a wardwright who's
willing to be proud of him properly, rather than managing the pride
quietly the way you might have a season ago."

"So am I," I agree, and we sit together a while longer in the
record-room's particular evening quiet, both of us considerably more
willing, these days, to let the people we care about actually see how
much we care.

·

Aldric arrives partway through the evening, having heard, from Tomas
himself, about the day's cadence lesson, and finds the two of us still
sitting over the practice book, considerably more relaxed than the
formal instruction warrants.

"He tells me he's better at the cadence than you were at his age," he
says, settling into the spare chair.

"He is," I say, without hesitation. "I don't think there's any use
pretending otherwise, simply to preserve my own pride."

"I like that about you," Aldric says. "You don't seem to need to be
the best at everything, the way I've watched some people manage their
own authority."

"I used to," I admit. "I think I needed, for a long time, to be
unquestionably capable at everything the job required, because
questioning meant admitting I might actually need help. I've been
learning, this winter, that admitting a student's better at a
particular skill than I was at his age isn't a threat to my own
competence. It's simply a fact, and rather a good one, given that he's
going to inherit this whole line eventually."

"That's a healthier relationship with pride than most people manage,"
Aldric says.

"I'm working on it," I say, "the same as everything else this winter's
asking me to work on," and he takes my hand, and we sit together in the
record-room's quiet a while longer, three people — soon joined by
Tomas himself, returning for one final question about the fourth
verse — building, slowly, the particular kind of found family none of
us quite expected this arrangement to produce.

·

Tomas lingers after the fourth verse is properly settled, turning
something over that I can see costs him some effort to raise.

"Can I ask you something," he says, "that isn't about the cadence."

"Of course."

"Do you ever regret it," he asks. "Taking this apprenticeship, I mean.
Taking me on, specifically, when you could have simply managed the
line alone, the way Ossian apparently expected the next wardwright
eventually would."

The question surprises me, though I understand, considering it
properly, exactly why he'd want to ask it now, in the particular
vulnerable aftermath of an evening spent being praised for talents he's
clearly worked hard to develop.

"Never," I say, plainly, without needing to consider it further. "I
think taking you on was one of the better decisions I've made in six
years of this post. I don't say that to flatter you. I say it because
watching you become who you're becoming has given this whole exhausting
line a future I wasn't at all certain it had, before you arrived."

Tomas's composure does something small and unguarded at that, the
particular relief of a young man who has apparently been quietly
carrying the fear that his presence might be more burden than gift.

"I worried, sometimes," he admits, "that I was simply one more cost
you were managing, alongside the cairns and the crown's ledgers."

"You were never a cost," I say. "You've been, since roughly your
second month here, one of the few genuinely good things this line has
handed me. I should have told you that plainly, considerably sooner
than tonight."

"Better late than never," Tomas says, echoing, I notice, the exact
phrase Aldric used with Sela, and I wonder, watching him, whether he's
picked it up secondhand from overhearing one of us, or arrived at the
same wisdom independently, the way people who spend enough time in the
same household eventually start to.

"Thank you," he says, "for telling me. I think I needed to hear it
properly, rather than simply assumed."

"I'll keep telling you," I say, "as often as it takes to properly
land," and he nods, satisfied, and gathers his things for the night,
and I watch him go with the particular warm ache of a woman who has,
somewhere in these past three years, come to love a student as
fiercely and as plainly as she loves anyone else in her considerably
expanded family.

·

Aldric finds me still sitting in the record-room some while after
Tomas has gone, the cadence book still open on the table in front of
me though I've stopped properly reading it.

"You're thinking about him," he observes, settling in across from me.

"I keep thinking I should have told him considerably sooner," I say,
"how much he actually means to me, rather than letting three years pass
before saying it plainly."

"You told him tonight," Aldric says. "That's not nothing."

"I know," I say. "I think I'm simply grieving, a little, all the years
I spent managing my own affection quietly, the way I manage most
things, rather than letting him have the plain benefit of it sooner."

Aldric takes my hand across the table, considering this properly.

"I don't think it's ever too late to say a true thing," he says,
"even if it arrives later than either of us would prefer. I think the
saying itself matters considerably more than the timing."

"I hope you're right," I say.

"I know I'm right about this one," Aldric says, with a small, certain
smile, "having only recently learned the exact same lesson myself,
rather more slowly than you managed it with Tomas tonight."

We sit together in the record-room's quiet a while longer, and I find
myself, closing the cadence book at last, considerably lighter for
having finally said the true thing out loud, however many years it
took either of us to arrive at the saying.

·

I mention the whole evening to Sela the following day, curious what
she'll make of it, and watch her absorb the account with visible
warmth.

"That sounds like exactly the kind of thing Tomas needed to hear," she
says. "I've watched him, these past weeks, quietly worrying that his
place here was somehow conditional."

"I don't think I properly realized how deep that worry ran," I admit,
"until he finally said it plainly."

"Most people's deepest worries stay hidden precisely that long," Sela
says, "until someone finally makes it safe enough to say them." She
considers me for a moment. "You've gotten rather good at that,
yourself, this winter. Making things safe enough to say."

"I've had considerable practice," I say, "given how much everyone in
this family apparently needed the same lesson, delivered at roughly
the same time."

Sela laughs at that, properly, and we spend the rest of the visit
comparing notes on exactly how much this whole difficult season has
taught each of us, in our own particular ways, about the plain,
underrated power of simply saying the true thing out loud.

·

Tomas mentions, a few days later, that he's started drafting his own
version of the same lesson into his fledgling ledger — a small
collection of things he wishes someone had said to him plainly, sooner,
rather than left him to discover slowly on his own.

"May I see it, someday," I ask, "once it's further along?"

"I'd like that," Tomas says, somewhat shyly. "I think I'd value your
opinion on whether I've actually captured the lesson properly, or
whether I'm simply repeating what I think I'm supposed to have
learned."

"I trust you've captured it properly," I say. "You've had excellent
teachers, this winter, in exactly this particular subject."

"I have," Tomas agrees, with a warm, genuine smile, and goes back to
his careful drafting, and I find myself, watching him work, considerably
proud of an apprentice determined to pass this hard-won lesson forward
rather than simply keep it for himself.


## Chapter Seventeen — Held Breath (Aldric)

The cup slips before I've fully registered that my hand has stopped
answering properly.

It is an ordinary evening, dinner at the cottage table, nothing about
the moment marked out as significant until the ordinary weight of a
half-full cup proves, suddenly and without warning, more than my left
hand is currently willing to hold, and it falls, and breaks against the
hearthstone, and both of us go very still, watching the pieces settle.

"Cold," I say, before Isla can ask, flexing the hand experimentally,
testing a fact I already know and am not yet ready to say the whole of
out loud. "Just cold. It happens, in this weather."

She doesn't push, not immediately. She also doesn't look away, and I
feel, in the specific quality of her not looking away, that she has
filed the moment permanently, under things she intends to return to
properly, later, once she's decided how best to ask.

"It's nothing the rite doesn't already account for," I say, which is,
I understand even as I'm saying it, the first outright untruth I have
told her since the binding.

I have told her partial truths before. I have withheld things,
carefully, the way I withhold most things I've decided cost more to
share than to carry alone. But this is different — a specific, deliberate
falsehood, offered to protect her from an arithmetic I have not yet
finished doing myself, and I feel it land wrong even as it leaves my
mouth, a debt I have just taken on that I did not have before tonight.

Isla lets the untruth stand, for now, though I watch her decide to let
it stand rather than simply fail to notice it, which is its own kind of
mercy — the specific patience of a woman who has learned, over these
past weeks, that pushing too hard too early only teaches a person to
guard the next admission more carefully.

We clean up the broken cup together, in a silence that has gone
considerably heavier than the ordinary quiet we've built between us
these past weeks, and I lie awake that night beside the memory of a
stone taking heat out through my own palms at the Long Reach cairn,
doing arithmetic I do not like the shape of, on exactly how much longer
two fingers can go properly numb before the numbness stops being a
minor inconvenience and starts being something considerably more
difficult to hide.

I do not tell Isla the whole of it. Not tonight. I tell myself this is
mercy, sparing her a fact she cannot yet do anything useful with,
though I suspect, lying awake beside the specific unbearable weight of
my own silence, that it is closer to the same flaw she named in me at
Hollow Farm — paying a cost alone, in the dark, and calling the
aloneness discipline rather than what it actually is, which is fear,
dressed up carefully enough that even I have trouble recognizing it
some nights.

I feel her, beside me, not sleeping either, and understand, from the
particular quality of her stillness, that she has not fully believed my
untruth even as she let it stand. She is building, I suspect, a
different theory of her own, quietly, in the dark, the same careful way
I built my own theory about her silence at Hollow Farm.

Neither of us says anything further, that night. But I understand,
lying there with a hand gone half-numb and a lie sitting unaddressed
between us, that the arithmetic I've been managing alone has just
started, for the first time, to run considerably faster than either of
us has properly accounted for.

·

I go to the orchard the next morning, alone, before Isla wakes, and
find Sela already at work grafting a late frost-guard onto the
southernmost row, her breath visible in the cold, her hands moving with
the specific practiced economy of a woman who has done this exact task
every year since our father taught her.

"You look terrible," she says, by way of greeting, not looking up from
the graft.

"Thank you."

"I mean it as concern, not insult," Sela says. "What's happened?"

I show her the hand, because I have decided, walking up here, that
keeping this from her too is no longer a debt I'm willing to take on,
not after everything Isla and I have promised each other about honest
counting.

Sela sets down her grafting knife entirely, and looks at the hand for a
long moment, and I watch her do the same arithmetic Isla did, arriving,
I suspect, at roughly the same frightening conclusion.

"Does Isla know the whole of it?" she asks.

"Most of it. Not quite the whole of it. I told a partial truth last
night that I already regret."

"Aldric." My name, from Sela, in exactly the tone our father used to
use when either of us was about to make a mistake he could see coming
from considerably further away than we could.

"I know," I say. "I'm going to correct it. I wanted to see the hand
properly in daylight first, away from her, before I decided exactly how
much to say."

"That's not honesty," Sela says. "That's simply managing the timing of
the honesty, which is the same habit dressed in slightly better
clothes."

I don't have an argument against that, mostly because I understand,
hearing it from her so plainly, that she's entirely correct, and that I
have spent the whole walk up here constructing a justification for
exactly the behavior I've promised to stop.

"You have to tell her everything," Sela says, gentler now, "and you
have to do it today, not once you've found the version that costs you
least to admit. I watched our father die of a fever he never once
properly named until it was too late for anyone to help him manage it
honestly. I am not going to watch that happen to you too, over
something considerably more preventable."

"I hear you," I say, and mean it, and walk back down from the orchard
that same morning with my sister's words settling considerably more
heavily than the cold ever manages to, understanding, by the time I
reach the cottage, that Sela is right, and that whatever I say to Isla
today needs to be the whole truth, offered plainly, rather than another
carefully managed piece of it.

·

I find Isla at the kitchen table, already dressed for the day's
inspections, and I understand, standing in the doorway with the whole
truth finally gathered and ready to be handed over, exactly how much
courage this particular ordinary morning is going to require.

"I need to tell you something properly," I say, "the whole of it, not
the version I gave you last night."

She sets down her cup, giving me her full attention, the specific
stillness she reserves for things she's already guessed matter more
than their opening suggests.

"The hand's worse than I said," I tell her. "Three fingers now, not
two. It's spread since the cup fell, not stayed the same. I told you
it was nothing the rite doesn't already account for. That wasn't true.
I don't know what the rite accounts for. I only know what I'm watching
happen to my own hand, and I was frightened enough of what that meant
that I made myself believe a smaller version of it might be easier for
both of us to carry."

Isla is quiet for a long moment, absorbing the correction.

"Thank you," she says, finally, "for coming back to tell me properly,
rather than letting the smaller version stand."

"I nearly didn't," I admit. "Sela had to say it to me plainly before I
found the nerve."

"I'm glad she did," Isla says. "I'd rather have an honesty that took
your sister's insistence to arrive than none at all. But I need you to
try, going forward, to find that nerve yourself, before someone else
has to hand it to you."

"I'm trying," I say. "I don't think I'm very good at it yet."

"None of us are very good at anything, the first several times we
attempt it properly," Isla says. "I don't expect you to be perfect at
honesty overnight. I expect you to keep coming back and correcting it,
the way you just did, every single time you catch yourself managing
instead of telling."

"I can do that," I say, and mean it, and she comes around the table and
holds me for a long moment, and I understand, standing there, that this
particular correction — small, difficult, entirely necessary — has cost
me considerably less than the silence it replaced, and given back,
somehow, considerably more.

·

I go to the orchard that same afternoon, needing, I think, to properly
close the loop with Sela, having promised her I'd correct the
management before the day was out.

"I told her the whole truth," I say, finding her at the grain-house.
"Not the version I gave her first, or the smaller version I nearly gave
Isla again this morning. The actual whole of it."

"How did she take it?"

"About as well as could be expected, given the actual news. She's
frightened. So am I, if I'm honest. But she knows now, properly, rather
than managing a smaller version of the fear on my behalf."

Sela considers this, nodding slowly, and I watch her decide something,
visibly, before she speaks again.

"I want you to know," she says, "that I'm proud of you. Not for the
hand, obviously — I wish to every power that watches this line that
none of this were happening at all. I'm proud of the correcting. That's
not nothing, Aldric, undoing a lifetime of habit in the space of a
single morning, even imperfectly."

"It doesn't feel like much, from the inside."

"Most genuine progress doesn't," Sela says, "while you're in the
middle of making it. It generally only looks like progress afterward,
once you can see the whole distance covered." She reaches over and
squeezes my good hand. "Keep correcting it. Every time you catch
yourself managing instead of telling. That's the whole of what any of
us can actually do, most days."

I walk back down from the orchard considerably steadied by her words,
carrying them alongside everything else this whole strange season is
teaching me about the slow, imperfect, entirely necessary work of
becoming someone who tells the truth before being caught in a lie
rather than after.

·

Tomas notices the broken cup's absence from the shelf that evening,
having apparently catalogued the cottage's small inventory of dishware
closely enough to register a gap, and asks about it with the
particular careless directness of someone who hasn't yet learned to
read the room before speaking.

"Another cup gone," he says. "That's the second one this month. Are
you simply clumsy, or is something else breaking them?"

I catch Isla's eye across the table, and understand, in the brief,
wordless exchange that passes between us, that we've both arrived at
the same decision simultaneously: that Tomas, however young, deserves
better than a deflection.

"It's my hand," I say, plainly, and watch him absorb it, his own
careless question giving way, almost immediately, to a much more
serious attention. "It's been going numb. Two fingers, entirely.
Considerably more of it too weak to trust with anything fragile."

"Since when," Tomas asks, all trace of his earlier carelessness gone.

"A while," I admit. "Longer than I told Isla, at first. I'm working on
correcting that particular habit."

Tomas is quiet a moment, processing this with a gravity that reminds
me, not for the first time, exactly how much this crisis has already
asked of someone barely out of boyhood.

"Thank you for telling me," he says, finally. "I know you didn't have
to. I'd rather know, even if it's frightening, than keep assuming
you're simply clumsy with cups."

"You're allowed to be frightened by it," Isla says, gently. "We are."

"I know," Tomas says. "I think I'd rather be frightened properly, with
both of you, than be kept comfortably in the dark about something this
significant." He looks between the two of us. "Is there anything I can
do? Properly, I mean, not simply asking to be polite."

"Keep drilling the cadence," I say, before Isla can answer. "If it
comes to needing it, I'd rather it be needed by someone properly
prepared."

Tomas nods, absorbing the request with the same seriousness he's given
everything else tonight, and the three of us finish the evening's meal
together in a quiet that feels, for the first time, considerably more
honest than comfortable — which I am slowly learning, this whole
strange season, matters rather more than the alternative.

·

Isla finds me at the woodpile the following morning, considerably
earlier than my usual hour, working through the last of the previous
day's split logs with more force than the task strictly requires.

"You're up early," she observes.

"I couldn't sleep," I admit. "I kept thinking about Tomas's face last
night, hearing about the hand properly for the first time. I don't
think I'd fully reckoned with what it costs him, specifically, watching
two people he's come to love manage a crisis this large."

"He's stronger than either of us gives him credit for," Isla says,
"though I understand the impulse to protect him from it regardless."

"I know he's strong," I say. "I still wish, some mornings, that he
didn't have to be. Nineteen shouldn't require this particular kind of
strength."

Isla considers this, settling onto the woodpile's edge to watch me
work.

"I think that's rather the tragedy underneath all of this," she says.
"Not simply what the rite costs the wardwright and the hearth. What it
costs everyone standing close enough to love either of them properly."

"Do you think it's worth it," I ask, "all of it, given what it costs
the people around us too?"

"Yes," Isla says, without hesitation. "I don't think the alternative —
managing everything alone, in careful isolation, to spare the people
around us the cost of caring — actually spares anyone anything. I think
it simply delays the cost, and makes it lonelier when it finally
arrives."

I finish the last log, considering her words, and set the axe aside.

"I think you're right," I say, "though I suspect it'll take me longer
than one morning's conversation to fully believe it, the way you seem
to already."

"Take the time you need," Isla says. "I'm not in any hurry. I've got
considerably more mornings like this one to spend convincing you,
apparently, and I find I don't mind the work."

We walk back toward the cottage together, the day's ordinary business
waiting, and I find myself, watching the frost catch the light over
the orchard's bare rows, grateful for a woman patient enough to keep
making the same true argument until it finally, properly lands.

·

Sela finds me at the cottage later that morning, and I tell her, since
she'll hear it eventually regardless, about the hand's full progression
and Tomas's own frightened reaction the night before.

"He took it well, all things considered," I say.

"He's stronger than either of you gives him proper credit for," Sela
says, echoing, almost precisely, Isla's own words from the woodpile.
"I've watched him grow up faster than any nineteen-year-old should
have to, this winter. I think it's time both of you started trusting
that growth properly, rather than continuing to manage him as though
he were still the boy who first arrived here."

"You're right," I admit. "I think I keep protecting the memory of who
he was rather than properly seeing who he's actually become."

"That's an easy mistake to make," Sela says, gently, "with anyone you
love. I've made the same one with you, more than once." She squeezes my
good hand. "Let him be as strong as he's already proven himself to be.
He's earned that trust."

I sit with her words a while longer, grateful, once again, for a
sister who keeps handing me exactly the correction I need, plainly,
whenever I've drifted too far into managing the people I love instead
of properly trusting them.


## Chapter Eighteen — Beyond the Line (Isla)

The line has crept four miles since first frost, by my own count, and
does not care in the slightest that the count exists, or that I have
kept it, meticulously, since the morning after my mentor's funeral,
as though the keeping of it might, eventually, amount to some kind of
leverage against a thing that has never once, in living memory,
negotiated with anyone.

We go out past the last standing cairn on a rumour — a village, once
properly inside the ward-line, now sitting past it, unaccounted for in
any ledger I have ever kept or inherited. It is the kind of rumour I
would ordinarily send Tomas to verify, except Tomas is nineteen, and
whatever waits past the line deserves, I have decided, a pair of eyes
that have already survived worse.

Aldric comes because he will not, at this point, let me go anywhere
near the line's edge alone, a fact we have not formally negotiated and
both, apparently, simply accept.

The cold past the line does something specific to sound. It flattens
it, the way a held breath flattens a room, swallowing footsteps,
swallowing the ordinary creak of frozen branches, until the only sound
either of us can properly hear is our own breathing, loud in the
particular silence of a place that has stopped, entirely, behaving like
weather and started behaving like something considerably more deliberate.

Neither of us talks much on the walk in. There is not, I find, a great
deal to say, walking into ground the Rime has already claimed for good,
that would improve on simply walking it properly, attentive to
whatever the ground itself has to tell us.

Aldric's hand finds mine partway through the walk, without either of us
deciding it should, and this time neither of us lets go for reasons
that have nothing at all to do with balance on uneven ground. I am
aware, holding it, of the specific comfort of his hand against mine —
warmer than the air around us has any right to allow, a fact I have
stopped questioning and started, instead, simply relying on, the way
you rely on a fire once you've stopped doubting it'll hold through the
night.

"Tell me what you're expecting to find," I say, mostly to give the
silence somewhere to go besides the specific dread that's been building
in my own chest since we crossed the last standing cairn.

"I don't know," Aldric admits. "I've heard stories about villages past
the line, growing up. None of them agreed on the details. Some say the
people simply left, ahead of the cold, and the buildings are just —
empty. Others say something considerably worse. I've never had reason
to test either version until now."

"What does your own arithmetic say."

"That the truth's probably somewhere between the two, and considerably
less tidy than either version manages to be." He glances at me,
sideways, in the particular way he has of testing whether a sentence
has landed the way he intended. "You're frightened."

"I am," I admit, because I have decided, weeks ago now, that half-truths
between us cost more than whole ones. "Not of what we'll find,
precisely. Of what finding it might mean for the arithmetic I've been
running my whole career — that the line holds, that the cost is
survivable, that six years of careful management amounts to something
more than simply delaying an outcome that was never actually in
question."

"We'll find out together," Aldric says, which is the same thing he
said to me at the Long Reach cairn, before the first ward we set
together, and I understand, hearing it a second time, that it has
become, between us, something close to a promise — not a guarantee of
outcome, simply an assurance that whatever arrives, arrives to both of
us rather than to either one alone.

The village comes into view exactly where the rumour said it would be,
sitting in a shallow fold of ground that would once, I suspect, have
been sheltered rather than exposed, before the line moved far enough
north to leave it standing on the wrong side of everything.

It is entirely, perfectly still.

Not the ordinary stillness of an empty place in winter — the specific,
particular stillness of a place the cold has finished with entirely,
holding its silence the way a held breath holds itself, patient,
complete, waiting for nothing at all, because there is nothing left
that it needs to wait for.

I stand at the edge of it for a long moment, Aldric's hand still in
mine, and feel, for the first time since the binding, a fear that has
nothing at all to do with either of us specifically, and everything to
do with the plain, unmanageable fact of what forty miles of failing
cairns are actually holding back.

"We should go in," I say, finally, because the alternative — turning
back without answers — is no longer, given everything currently at
stake, a choice either of us can properly afford.

"Together," Aldric says, and we go in together, into the first true
silence either of us has ever properly stood inside, toward whatever
answer is waiting in a village the Rime has already, patiently,
completely, finished taking.

·

Before we reach the first house, I stop at what must once have been the
village's own boundary marker — a low, weathered stone, considerably
older than any waystone on my own line, carved with a symbol I don't
immediately recognize, three interlocking rings rather than the single
standing figure that marks every cairn I've ever inspected.

"Do you know this mark," I ask Aldric, more out of habit than genuine
expectation that he would.

He studies it for a long moment, and I watch something shift in his
face — recognition, faint, uncertain.

"My grandmother had something like it," he says, slowly, "carved into
the lintel above her own door. I never asked what it meant. I assumed
it was simply decoration. She died before I was old enough to think to
ask properly."

"Three rings," I say, tracing the shape with one finger, careful not to
disturb whatever frost has settled into the carving's grooves. "I've
never seen this on any cairn on my own line. I wonder if it's older
than the current form entirely — a mark from before the rite was
whatever it currently is."

"Do you think it means something specific?"

"I think," I say, "that everything about this village is going to mean
something specific, and that we're going to need to be considerably
more careful than usual about which conclusions we draw from which
piece of evidence."

We continue toward the village proper, and I find myself glancing back
at the boundary marker more than once, the three interlocking rings
settling into my memory alongside everything else I am gathering here,
a piece of a puzzle whose full shape I do not yet understand, though I
suspect, walking deeper into Calder's Reach's particular, complete
silence, that I am about to.

·

The village proper holds considerably more than the first house's quiet
domesticity prepared me for. Where the boundary marker suggested a
place lost gently, the deeper streets tell a more complicated story —
a scattering of tools left mid-task, a loom still threaded halfway
through a pattern nobody ever finished, a schoolroom, of all things,
with slates still propped against a wall, half-formed letters chalked
across several of them, as though the lesson had simply paused rather
than properly ended.

"They didn't know," I say, quietly, standing in the schoolroom's
particular hush. "Whatever took this place, it didn't arrive as a
warning anyone could act on. It arrived as an ordinary day that simply
never resumed."

Aldric picks up one of the slates, reading the half-formed letters with
the specific care of a man handling something considerably more fragile
than chalk and stone.

"A child was learning to write her own name," he says, quietly. "Look.
She'd gotten as far as the third letter."

I do not have a ready response to that particular detail, and we stand
together in the schoolroom a long moment, both of us absorbing the
specific, unbearable ordinariness of what the Rime actually costs — not
simply cairns and charge readings, actual children, mid-lesson, whose
names never got finished being written.

"This is what we're actually holding the line against," I say,
eventually. "Not an abstract cold. This. A whole village of ordinary
mornings that simply stopped, all at once, because nobody held the
ward that would have kept the cold at a proper distance."

"I understand the stakes considerably better, standing here," Aldric
says, "than I did reading about them in any ledger."

We leave the schoolroom as we found it, the half-finished slate
returned carefully to its place, and continue toward the record-room
proper, both of us carrying, now, a considerably more visceral
understanding of exactly what failure on this line actually costs.

·

The record-room itself, when we finally reach it, holds considerably
more than the sealed box in the third house's recess. Shelves of
ledgers, considerably older than anything Highstone's own archive
maintains, line every wall, undisturbed by the cold in a way that
suggests the room was, at some point, deliberately warded against
exactly this kind of decay — a small mercy, whoever last tended it,
that has preserved considerably more than either of us expected to
find intact.

"This alone is worth the whole journey," I say, running a hand along
the spines of ledgers considerably older than Ossian's own earliest
entries. "There's history here that Highstone's own archive doesn't
have at all."

Aldric pulls one down carefully, opening it to a page dense with the
same cramped, economical hand as the sealed record — a full accounting
of the village's own cairns, its own losses, stretching back further
than I can properly estimate without more careful study.

"Do you think we can bring all of this back," he asks, "or only what's
most urgent?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I think, realistically, we take what's most
urgent now, and I come back, properly, once this crisis has settled,
to catalogue the rest of it. This room deserves more careful attention
than I currently have time to give it."

We spend a considerable while longer in the record-room, gathering
what we can carry, and I find myself, closing the last ledger, already
planning the eventual return trip that will properly honor whatever
predecessor built this careful, considerable archive, only to watch it
sit sealed and forgotten for however many years the cold has held this
village silent.

·

Before we leave the village, Aldric asks if we might stop once more at
the schoolroom, and I understand, seeing his face, that the
half-finished slate has stayed with him considerably more than he's
let on since we first found it.

"I want to remember her properly," he says, standing again in the
schoolroom's particular hush. "Whoever she was. I don't want her to
simply be a detail I mention, later, when someone asks what we found
here. I want to actually carry her, the way you carry Ossian, the way
I'm learning to carry the names Cera's given us."

"Do you want to give her a name," I ask, "since we don't have her
actual one?"

Aldric considers the question seriously, looking at the slate's
half-formed letters.

"Wren," he says, finally. "For no particular reason except that it's
the first name that came to me, standing here. I'd rather she have
some name, even an invented one, than remain simply 'the child from
Calder's Reach' in whatever account either of us eventually gives of
today."

"Wren," I repeat, quietly, and write it down in my own notebook,
alongside the growing list of names this whole strange season keeps
handing me to properly remember. "I think that's exactly right. Every
loss deserves a name, even one we have to give it ourselves, rather
than simply a location and a date."

We leave the schoolroom a final time, considerably more moved than
either of us expected an invented name to leave us, and I understand,
walking back toward the village's edge, that this small, deliberate
act of naming — a child neither of us ever knew, given a name neither
of us can verify — has become, somehow, one of the more important
things either of us has done since arriving here.

"Thank you," I say, as we reach the boundary marker again, "for
wanting to remember her properly, rather than letting the whole visit
become simply a mission accomplished."

"I learned that from you," Aldric says. "Watching you refuse to let
Ossian's name go unwritten. I don't think I'd have thought to name her,
a season ago. I think I'd have simply catalogued the slate as evidence
and moved on to the next urgent thing."

"You've changed considerably, this winter," I observe.

"So have you," he says, and we walk the last stretch out of Calder's
Reach together, carrying, alongside the record boxes and the ledgers,
a small, invented name that neither of us intends to ever properly
forget.

·

We stop, a final time, at the shepherd's hut where we sheltered the
first night, gathering the last of our things before the true walk
home begins, and I find Aldric lingering at the doorway a moment
longer than the errand requires.

"What is it," I ask.

"I keep thinking about the fact that this hut's stood here, empty,
for however many years, waiting for exactly two people who needed it
enough to properly use it again," he says. "It feels like it should
mean something, that detail. I'm not certain what."

"Maybe it simply means that some things wait, patiently, for whoever
eventually needs them," I say, "regardless of how long the waiting
takes."

"I like that reading," Aldric says. "I think I'd like to believe most
of what's been lost, out here past the line, is waiting in some similar
fashion — not gone entirely, simply waiting for someone with the time
and the reason to come looking properly."

"I'd like to believe that too," I say, and we take a final look at the
small stone hut before setting out, carrying, alongside everything
else Calder's Reach has handed us, a small, private hope that loss and
waiting are not, in fact, the same thing as gone for good.

·

We walk in comfortable silence for a while, once the village's shape
has properly faded behind us, and I find myself turning over the whole
strange weight of what we're carrying home — proof of a rite that might
save Aldric's life, a village's worth of unrecorded grief, a small,
invented name neither of us intends to forget.

"Do you think we've changed, doing this," I ask, "or simply discovered
something that was already true about us?"

Aldric considers the question properly, the way he considers most
things I ask him seriously.

"Both, maybe," he says. "I think Calder's Reach showed us something
true about ourselves that we hadn't properly tested yet. But I don't
think either of us walks away from a place like that unchanged, either.
I think the testing itself changes a person, regardless of what it
confirms."

"That's a fair distinction," I say, and we continue on, the whole
weight of the journey settling into something we can properly carry
together, rather than either one of us bearing it alone.

·

We stop once more before the last standing cairn comes properly into
view, both of us needing, I think, a final moment before Calder's
Reach's particular grief gives way to whatever ordinary business
awaits us at Highstone.

"Thank you," Aldric says, "for bringing me. I don't think I'd have
managed any of what we found today, alone, without your particular way
of insisting a thing be examined properly rather than merely survived."

"Thank you for coming," I say. "I don't think I'd have had the nerve to
sit with any of it as fully, without you there to sit with it alongside
me."

We take each other's hand, and continue the last stretch home together,
carrying Calder's Reach's whole complicated gift — grief, proof, and a
small stubborn hope — properly, finally, between the two of us.


## Chapter Nineteen — Calder's Reach (Isla)

Calder's Reach stands exactly as the cold left it, however many winters
ago that finally was — doors shut against weather that came anyway,
shutters latched from the inside by hands that clearly believed,
right up until the very end, that latching them properly might make
some measure of difference.

A kettle still hangs over a hearth that has not drawn breath in years,
frost sealed thick across its spout, and I find myself standing in the
doorway of the first house we come to for considerably longer than the
house itself requires, simply looking at the specific, ordinary
domesticity of it — a chair pulled slightly back from a table, a
child's shoe by the door, small facts of a life that was, until the
line moved far enough to leave this whole village stranded on its wrong
side, entirely unremarkable.

"There's no sign of struggle," Aldric says, quietly, from the doorway
behind me. "No sign anyone tried to leave in a hurry, either. It looks
like they simply — stopped."

"The cold doesn't announce itself, out here," I say. "Not the way
ordinary winter does. It arrives the way a held breath arrives — all
at once, complete, before anyone's fully registered they should have
been running."

We move through the village slowly, methodically, the way I move
through any inspection, cataloguing rather than merely observing, and
it is in the third house — larger than the others, set slightly apart,
with a specific worn threshold that speaks of considerably more foot
traffic than an ordinary dwelling would see — that I find the cairn
itself, a small standing stone in what must once have been a proper
record-room, and beside it, sealed into a recess I very nearly miss
entirely, a record box.

The seal is old, older than anything in my own record-room at Highstone,
and it takes both hands and considerably more patience than I currently
have available to work it open without damaging whatever's inside.

What's inside is a predecessor's own hand — not my line, an older one,
the ink faded to a specific rust-brown that speaks of decades rather
than years, describing a rite that does not match, in a single
particular, the one I was taught at nineteen.

I read it once, uncomprehending. I read it a second time, and
understand, with a specific cold horror that has nothing to do with the
actual temperature of the room, precisely what I'm looking at. I read
it a third time, to be certain, because being wrong about this would
cost considerably more than being slow to confirm it properly.

The record describes cost taken in both directions. Shared. Not spent
one-sided into a single hearth, the way I have understood the rite my
entire career, but drawn, deliberately, evenly, from both the
wardwright and the hearth together — a mutual form, considerably older
than the version I learned, corrupted somewhere in the generations
between this predecessor's hand and my own.

I cannot say the word for what I'm reading. Not yet. Not properly, not
where it would count. Some part of me already understands that this
discovery is not mine to speak aloud until I am certain — genuinely,
provably certain, rather than merely hopeful — because being wrong
here, announcing a form of the rite that does not actually work the way
this faded record suggests, could cost more than simply continuing to
manage the arithmetic I already, reliably, know.

Aldric watches me read it twice, three times, and does not interrupt,
though I feel him understanding, from my face, that whatever I've found
has rearranged something considerably larger than a simple historical
curiosity.

"What is it," he asks, finally, when I still haven't spoken.

"I don't know yet," I say, which is not entirely true, and which he
seems to understand is not entirely true, and which he, mercifully,
allows to stand regardless, because he has learned, these past weeks,
precisely which of my silences need pushing against and which ones need
simply being given room to finish their own work.

"You'll tell me," he says, "when you're certain."

"I will," I promise, and mean it, and fold the record carefully back
into its box, to carry home to Highstone's own record-room, where I
can set it beside everything Ossian Thale ever taught me and begin the
long, careful work of determining whether what I'm holding is a genuine
answer or simply a dead woman's desperate, unproven theory, offered too
late to help anyone but whoever finds it next.

We leave Calder's Reach as the light starts to fail, the village
settling back into its particular, complete silence behind us, and I
carry the record box the whole walk back to the last standing cairn
with the specific care of a woman carrying something she does not yet
fully understand the weight of, but suspects, already, she is never
going to be able to set back down.

·

Before we go, I make myself do one more circuit of the village proper,
a habit of thoroughness Ossian drilled into me from my earliest
inspections — never leave a site until you've confirmed there's nothing
further it can tell you — and it is on this second pass that I find the
small shrine set into the village's central well-house, considerably
better preserved than the rest of Calder's Reach, as though the cold
took everything else first and left this one small structure for last.

Inside, carved into the well-house's own stone, is a longer passage
than the record box contained — a fuller account of the mutual rite,
including, I understand, reading it carefully in the failing light, the
specific words spoken at its performance, considerably different from
the cadence Ossory taught me, gentler in places, more demanding in
others, a form that asks both participants, explicitly, to name aloud
what they are choosing to risk before the stone takes anything from
either of them.

I copy it as carefully as the light allows, my hands numb with cold
before I've finished, and understand, closing my own notebook over the
transcription, that I have just found something considerably more
complete than I expected Calder's Reach to yield — not simply proof
that the mutual form existed, but the actual shape of how it was meant
to be performed, properly, by people who understood exactly what they
were agreeing to.

"You've found something else," Aldric says, watching me close the
notebook.

"The words," I say. "The actual words of the rite, not just the
record's description of its existence. I don't know yet whether they
match what Ossory would sanction, or whether the crown's own
rite-keepers would recognize them as legitimate. But I have them now,
properly, rather than merely a dead woman's account that such a thing
once existed."

"Is that good news or considerably worse news," Aldric asks, "given how
much depends on getting this right."

"I don't know yet," I say, honestly. "I think it's simply more
information, which is generally better than less, even when the
information itself is frightening to hold."

We leave the well-house as the last of the light fails entirely, and I
carry both the record box and my own careful transcription the whole
walk back, understanding that Calder's Reach has given me considerably
more to work with than I arrived expecting, and considerably more
responsibility, too, for getting the eventual attempt exactly right.

·

We do not, in the end, make the full walk back that same evening. The
light fails too completely, too far from any proper shelter, and I make
the decision, reluctantly, to return to the predecessor's own house
rather than risk the exposed stretch of track in full dark.

"Are you alright with this," I ask Aldric, "staying another night out
here, past the line, in a village that clearly cost its own wardwright
everything."

"I'm alright," he says, "though I understand if you're not. This place
carries a considerable weight, for you specifically, given what you
found in the third house."

"I think I need to stay," I say, surprising myself with the admission.
"Not despite what I found. Because of it. There's something in sitting
with a predecessor's whole unfinished story, properly, rather than
simply gathering her evidence and leaving before I've let it mean
anything."

Aldric doesn't argue with that, and we settle back into the
predecessor's house for a second night, the fire we built the evening
before still holding a faint warmth in its embers, easily coaxed back
to a proper blaze.

"Do you think she had someone," I ask, later, watching the fire catch
properly, "the predecessor who wrote this record. Someone who sat with
her the way you're sitting with me."

"I don't know," Aldric says. "I'd like to think she did, at least for
a while. The record itself has a considerable tenderness in it, in the
parts describing the mutual form specifically — the kind of care I
don't think a person writes about a stranger's theory. I think she
loved someone, and lost them, or lost the chance to try the form
properly with them, and wrote it all down anyway, hoping someone,
eventually, might find it and finish what she couldn't."

"I hope she did," I say, quietly. "Have someone, I mean. Even if it
wasn't enough, in the end, to save either of them."

"So do I," Aldric agrees, and we sit together by the fire a while
longer, in a dead woman's house, grateful, both of us, for whatever
brought us here together rather than alone.

·

I find myself, sitting by that borrowed fire, thinking about the
whole shape of what's brought us to this exact evening — a rite
neither of us chose, a binding that could have simply remained an
arrangement, managed carefully at arm's length, the way I'd have
insisted on it going, six weeks ago, if anyone had asked me to predict
my own behavior.

"Can I tell you something," I say, "that I haven't properly examined
until just now, sitting in this particular house."

"Go on."

"I think I was frightened, at the very start, that caring about you
properly would somehow diminish what I owed the line. That a
wardwright who lets herself be loved is somehow less capable than one
who doesn't. I don't think that's true anymore. I think, if anything,
I've become a better wardwright since the binding, not a worse one —
more willing to ask for help, more honest about my own limits, more
capable of actually seeing what the rite costs rather than simply
managing around it."

Aldric considers this, turning it over with his usual care.

"I think that's rather the whole lesson this predecessor's record is
trying to teach, underneath the mechanics," he says. "That the rite
was never meant to be held by someone entirely alone. That the
solitary version isn't the disciplined version. It's simply the
version that's easier to justify, institutionally, regardless of what
it actually costs the person holding it."

"I think you're right," I say, and we sit together a while longer,
both of us turning over the whole shape of what six weeks have taught
us, grateful, in the particular quiet of a dead woman's house, for
everything this strange season has already given us, however
frightening the road ahead still is.

·

I wake once more in the night, restless despite the day's exhaustion,
and find myself drawn back to the predecessor's own record, rereading
it by the fire's low embers, searching for anything I might have
missed in my first, hurried readings.

"You can't sleep either," Aldric observes, from where he's lying
nearby, clearly not asleep himself.

"I keep thinking there's something in here I haven't properly
understood yet," I say. "Some detail that matters more than I've given
it credit for."

"What kind of detail?"

"She writes about the mutual form almost tenderly," I say, "the way
you'd write about something you'd actually witnessed rather than
merely theorized. I keep wondering if she attempted it herself, at
some point, with whoever she loved, and whether it worked, even
partially, before whatever eventually took this village."

"Do you think the record would say, if she had?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I think if it worked, she'd have written
about it differently — with the specific certainty of someone
reporting a result rather than proposing a theory. I think, reading it
again tonight, that she never got the chance to try it. I think that's
rather the tragedy underneath this whole record — a woman who found
the answer and ran out of time to test it, the same way we're
currently racing to test it before we run out of our own time."

Aldric sits up properly, considering this.

"Then we owe it to her," he says, "to actually try it. Not simply as
a desperate measure for ourselves. As the completion of something she
started and never got the chance to finish."

"I think that's exactly right," I say, and find, saying it, a
considerable comfort in reframing the whole frightening prospect ahead
of us as something closer to a shared inheritance than a desperate
last resort. "I'd like to think she'd be glad, somehow, knowing her
record eventually reached someone who could actually finish what she
started."

"I think she would be," Aldric agrees, and we sit together by the
fire's low embers a while longer, both of us considerably steadied by
the thought of carrying a dead predecessor's unfinished hope forward,
properly, rather than simply grasping at her record out of pure
desperation.

·

We wake, near dawn, to a sound neither of us expects this far past the
line — a bird, somewhere in the village's own ruined eaves, singing
into the grey morning as though the whole silence around it hadn't
happened at all.

"I didn't think anything still lived out here," Aldric says, quietly,
listening.

"Neither did I," I admit. "I think I'd assumed the cold took
everything, indiscriminately, the same way it took the people."

We find the bird eventually, a small, hardy thing nesting improbably in
the eaves of the third house, entirely unbothered by two strangers
watching it from below.

"It shouldn't be here," I say. "By every account I've ever read of
what lies past the line."

"Maybe it found a way to hold on regardless," Aldric says, "the same
way this whole village's own memory has, in its own fashion, held on
past when anyone expected it to."

We watch the small bird a while longer before continuing our
preparations to leave, and I find myself, walking away from Calder's
Reach at last, carrying an unexpected, stubborn hope alongside
everything else this village has handed us — proof, however small,
that some things survive past the line's supposed edge of what's
possible, given enough reason to keep trying.

·

"I want to name it, too," Aldric says, as we make our final preparations
to leave, "the way we named the child in the schoolroom. It seems only
fair, given how much hope it's handed us this morning."

"What would you call it?"

He considers the small bird a final moment before it disappears back
into the eaves.

"Persistence," he says, finally, with a small, wry smile. "Not
especially poetic. Accurate, though."

"I think that's exactly right," I say, and we set out at last from
Calder's Reach, carrying a small, stubbornly named hope alongside
everything else this strange, grief-heavy village has handed us to
remember.

·

I think, walking the first stretch away from the village proper, about
how differently I'll describe this whole journey to Tomas once we're
finally home — not simply the record and the translation, but Wren
and Persistence too, the small, deliberate namings that have made this
grief-heavy place considerably more bearable to have properly visited.

"Do you think he'll understand," I ask Aldric, "why we bothered naming
a bird and a child neither of us actually knew?"

"I think he'll understand better than most," Aldric says. "He's spent
this whole winter learning exactly the same lesson we did today — that
naming a thing properly is its own kind of care, regardless of whether
the thing named can ever know it's been given a name at all."

"I think you're right," I say, and we continue on toward home,
carrying Calder's Reach's whole complicated inheritance a little more
lightly for having someone waiting who'll understand its full weight.


## Chapter Twenty — What the Record Doesn't Say (Isla)

The house the record came from was a wardwright's, same as my own —
I understand this properly only once we've made camp for the night in
its main room, unwilling to walk the whole distance back to Highstone
in failing light with a discovery this fragile still uncertain in my
hands, and I have had time to look, properly, at the specific shape of
a life lived in rooms very nearly identical to my own.

The wardwright's chair sits where my own chair sits, relative to the
hearth. The record shelf holds its ledgers in the same order I keep my
own — inspection logs first, then correspondence, then, at the very
back, the private notebook every wardwright I have ever heard of keeps
for the things that don't belong in an official record at all.

I find myself, sitting on the cold hearthstone of a house whose
occupant died — walked into the cold, the old euphemism runs, though I
have never once, until tonight, properly considered how literal that
phrase might actually be — considerably longer ago than I've properly
reckoned with, unable to move to anything else until I've sat with the
whole shape of the resemblance.

"You're thinking about your own mentor," Aldric says, quietly, sitting
down beside me on the same cold stone.

"Yes," I admit.

"Tell me about him," Aldric says. "If you're willing. I don't think
you've said his name to me once, in all these weeks."

"Ossian Thale," I say, and it costs me, saying it aloud, considerably
more than I expect a simple name to cost. "He bound me as his apprentice
when I was nineteen. He taught me everything I know about the line —
which cairns fail first, how to read a charge before it's fully gone,
how to hold forty miles of stone together with considerably less
support than the crown ever properly funded. He walked into the cold
seven years ago, when I was twenty-six, and I completed the rite that
spent him."

"You completed it," Aldric says, carefully. "Meaning you were the one
who—"

"I set the final ward," I say. "The one that took what was left of him
and put it into the stone properly, so his death would mean something
rather than simply being a loss with nothing gained against it. That's
the correct way to say it, in the old cadence. Nobody at the ceremony
afterward said it that plainly. They thanked me instead. For a job
well done. Nobody asked whether I wanted to be thanked for it."

Aldric doesn't offer comfort shaped like fixing, which I am, by now,
profoundly grateful for. He simply sits with me on the cold stone and
lets the grief be exactly as large as it actually is, unmanaged, which
turns out, this once, to be the only thing that actually helps.

"I completed the rite," I say, "and then I went back to Highstone, and
I did the next day's inspections, because there was nobody else to do
them, and I have not, in the seven years since, once properly stopped
to feel the whole of what that morning cost me. I've filed it. I've
managed it. I've done the arithmetic on it the way I do arithmetic on
everything, and I have never once let it be simply a grief, unmanaged,
the way it actually is."

"You've been carrying that alone for seven years," Aldric says.

"Yes."

"I know the shape of that particular weight," he says, and I
understand, hearing it, that he means it considerably more literally
than the words alone suggest — a man who has spent his own adult life
carrying costs silently, in the dark, calling the aloneness discipline
rather than what it actually is, recognizing, in me, the exact same
practiced silence.

I let myself, finally, properly, grieve Ossian Thale — not the
managed version, not the version that fits neatly into a morning's
arithmetic before the day's inspections resume, the whole unmanageable
size of it, sitting on a dead stranger's cold hearthstone with a man
who does not once try to make the grief smaller than it actually is.

It does not fix anything. It does not undo seven years of careful
filing, or make the next morning's inspections any less necessary. But
it does, for the first time since I was twenty-six years old, feel like
carrying something in company rather than entirely alone, and when I am
finally, properly finished — spent, in the particular way grief spends
a person, hollowed out rather than resolved — I find that something in
my chest has settled that has not settled properly in seven years.

"Thank you," I say, "for asking me to say his name."

"Thank you for saying it," Aldric says, and holds me, on the cold
stone, in a house that was once, very precisely, exactly like my own,
until the fire we've built catches properly and the room, slowly,
begins to hold something closer to warmth.

·

We stay the night in the predecessor's own house, unwilling to attempt
the full walk back to Highstone in failing light with a discovery this
significant still uncertain in our hands, and I find myself, once the
fire's properly caught and the worst of the cold has been pushed back
from the room, telling Aldric considerably more about Ossian than I
have told anyone since the funeral.

"He used to say the line didn't care how tired you were," I say,
"only whether the stone was properly fed. I hated that saying, at
nineteen. I understand it rather differently now, having spent six
years learning exactly how true it is, and exactly how much it costs to
keep living by it regardless."

"Did he have anyone," Aldric asks, "the way you have me now. Someone to
share the arithmetic with."

"No," I say, and the answer costs me something, saying it, a specific
grief for a man who spent decades exactly as alone as I spent my own
first six years of practice. "He had apprentices. He had the crown's
office, at a professional distance. I don't think he had anyone who
simply sat with him, the way you're sitting with me now, without any
official reason requiring it."

"That's a lonely way to have lived."

"I think it was," I say. "I think it's rather why he never questioned
the rite's own cruelty properly, until it was far too late to matter —
he'd never had anyone close enough to help him see the arithmetic
clearly, from outside his own exhaustion."

Aldric is quiet for a long moment, turning that over.

"I don't want that for you," he says, finally. "Whatever happens with
the mutual rite, whatever it ends up costing either of us — I don't
want you to end up like him. Alone with an arithmetic nobody else ever
properly checked."

"I don't think I will," I say, and find, saying it, that I actually
believe it, for the first time properly since Ossian's death — a belief
built, slowly, out of a fortnight and a half of a stranger mending my
glove before dawn and asking, plainly, whether I actually wanted the
thing sitting between us, and choosing, every single day since, to keep
asking.

We sleep, eventually, in a dead wardwright's own bed, and I wake once,
in the deepest part of the night, to find Aldric's good hand already
holding mine, unconsciously, the way you hold onto something you've
decided, even asleep, you aren't willing to let go of.

·

I wake again near dawn, properly this time, to find Aldric already up,
sitting by the cold hearth, turning something over in his own hands —
a small, worn wooden figure, clearly a child's toy, that must have
belonged to whoever last lived in this house.

"I found it in the corner, under some rubble," he says, seeing me
watch him. "I don't know why I picked it up. It felt wrong to leave it
lying there, forgotten a second time, after however many years it's
already spent forgotten once."

"May I see it," I ask, and he hands it over — a small carved bird,
crudely made, clearly whittled by an amateur hand rather than any
proper craftsman's, the specific loving imperfection of a parent's gift
rather than a purchased toy.

"Do you think it belonged to the wardwright's own child," I ask,
"or a grandchild, perhaps."

"I don't know," Aldric says. "I don't think we'll ever know, properly.
I think that's rather the whole tragedy of this place — an entire life,
reduced now to whatever fragments happen to survive the cold's own
patient erasure, with nobody left to tell us which fragments actually
mattered most."

I turn the small bird over in my own hands, considering.

"I want to bring it back with us," I say. "Not to keep, exactly. To
give it somewhere it can be properly remembered, rather than left in a
dead house nobody visits."

"Where would you put it?"

"The record-room, perhaps. Alongside Ossian's own ledger, and yours,
eventually, and mine, someday. A small reminder of exactly what we're
actually protecting, every time either of us sets a ward — not simply
stone and charge readings. Whole lives. Children who carve birds for
their own children, and never get the chance to see them grown."

Aldric doesn't say anything for a long moment, simply watching me hold
the small carved bird, and I understand, from his expression, that
I've said something that has landed considerably harder than I
intended.

"That's exactly right," he says, finally, quietly. "I think that's
precisely what we're protecting."

We pack the small bird carefully alongside the record box and my own
transcription, and begin the walk back to Highstone properly, at first
light, carrying, between the two of us, considerably more than either
of us arrived at Calder's Reach expecting to carry home.

·

We stop once more at the boundary marker on our way out, the three
interlocking rings still visible under a fresh dusting of frost, and I
find myself, this time, tracing the shape properly, committing it to
memory in a way I hadn't managed on the way in.

"I keep thinking about your grandmother's lintel," I say. "The same
mark, on a door, generations removed from whatever built this marker.
I wonder if there's a whole thread of history here neither of us has
properly traced yet — older markings, older forms, quietly surviving in
places nobody thought to look because nobody expected anything to
survive this far past the line at all."

"Do you think it's connected to the mutual form specifically," Aldric
asks, "or something else entirely?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I think it might simply be evidence that this
