village understood something about the world that got lost, the same
way the rite itself got corrupted somewhere between whoever built this
marker and whoever taught me the version I learned at nineteen. I'd
like to come back, properly, and study it further, once we've dealt
with what's currently most urgent."

"I'll come with you," Aldric says, "whenever that eventually is,"
and we continue past the marker, out of Calder's Reach's particular
silence, toward whatever the line still has waiting for us on the
other side of it.

·

We stop, partway along the walk back, at a small rise that gives the
last clear view of the village behind us, and I find myself turning
for a final look, the way you look back at a grave once more before
walking away from it properly.

"Do you think anyone will ever come back here, after us," I ask,
"properly, to study it the way it deserves, rather than simply passing
through once in a crisis?"

"I don't know," Aldric admits. "I'd like to think so. I think Tomas
would want to, if we told him what we found. He's the kind of person
who'd want to catalogue every ledger properly, rather than let it sit
another decade unstudied."

"I think you're right," I say. "I think that's rather why I want him
involved in this, eventually, once the current crisis has settled. This
village deserves more than a single desperate visit. It deserves
someone who'll return, properly, and give it the careful attention
Ossian would have given it, if he'd ever had the chance to know it
existed."

"We'll bring him," Aldric says, "once there's time. I think he'd want
that considerably more than he'd admit, if you simply told him the
option existed."

We take a final look at Calder's Reach, sitting quiet in its shallow
fold of ground, and I make myself a private promise, standing there,
that this will not be the last time either of us properly honors what
this village and its unnamed wardwright have given us, however this
current crisis eventually resolves.

·

Tomas is waiting at Highstone's gate when we finally arrive, having
clearly kept watch since the previous evening, and his relief at
seeing us both upright is visible enough that I understand, watching
him, exactly how long the wait must have felt.

"You were gone considerably longer than planned," he says, careful not
to make it an accusation, though the worry underneath is plain.

"We found more than we expected," I say, "and needed the extra time to
properly gather it. I'll tell you everything, once we've properly
rested. There's a great deal to explain."

"Is it good news," Tomas asks, "or the other kind?"

"Both, I think," I say, honestly. "I found something that might save
Aldric's life. I also found a whole village's worth of ordinary grief
that I don't think either of us is going to properly finish carrying
for some time yet."

Tomas absorbs this with the particular gravity he's shown, this whole
season, toward anything touching the line's deeper losses, and helps
us both inside, fussing over the practical business of food and rest
with the specific focused energy of a young man who has learned,
these past weeks, exactly how to be useful in the aftermath of a
crisis he wasn't present to witness firsthand.

·

Sela arrives not long after, having heard through the village's own
reliable grapevine that we'd finally returned, and takes one look at
both our faces before setting down whatever she'd brought.

"You look like you've seen the whole shape of something considerable,"
she observes.

"We have," I say. "I don't think either of us has properly finished
absorbing it yet."

"Then don't try to explain it all tonight," Sela says, with the
particular practical firmness I've come to expect from her. "Eat
something properly. Rest. The explaining can wait until morning, once
you've both actually slept."

"That's good advice," Aldric says, "though I suspect neither of us is
very good at following it."

"You're going to start being good at it," Sela says, "starting
tonight, or I'm going to sit here and make certain of it personally."

We let her fuss over us properly, grateful, both of us, for a sister
and a household determined to insist on rest even when neither of us
feels entirely capable of taking it for granted, and I find myself,
settling finally into the cottage's familiar warmth, considerably
steadied by the specific, insistent care of people who refuse to let
either of us manage this alone.

·

Sela stays until we've both properly eaten, refusing to leave until
she's satisfied neither of us intends to simply push the food aside
in favor of talking through the whole of Calder's Reach immediately.

"Tomorrow," she says, firmly, gathering her things at last. "Properly
rested, both of you. I'll want the whole account then, and I expect
it told slowly, not rushed through simply to be done with the telling."

"Agreed," I say, and mean it, grateful for a household that has
learned, this whole season, exactly when insistence serves someone
better than sympathy.

She leaves us finally to our rest, and I find myself, watching the
door close behind her, considerably calmer than I've been since we
first crossed back over the last standing cairn, held steady by a
family determined to make certain neither of us faces the aftermath of
anything difficult entirely alone.


## Chapter Twenty-One — True Cost (Isla)

We walk back from Calder's Reach the following morning, exhausted in
the particular way grief exhausts a person, hollowed rather than
merely tired, and it is on that walk — past the last standing cairn,
back onto ground the line still properly holds — that the bond does
the thing it did once before, at a smaller waystone weeks ago, except
considerably larger now, entirely uncontrolled.

The morning itself is bright and bitterly cold, the specific clear
light that Coldwake sometimes offers after a hard frost, entirely
indifferent to the particular weight either of us is carrying home
from a village the cold already finished with, long ago.

Neither of us has spoken much since leaving the village, both of us
still properly absorbing the whole weight of what we found there, and
I am grateful, walking in that particular silence, that neither of us
feels the need to fill it with anything less than the truth currently
demands.

I feel it arrive all at once, cumulative, undeniable: not a single
scene's cost, the way it has arrived before, but every ward Aldric has
stood beside me for since the binding, stacked, compounding, the whole
sum of what six weeks of careful, well-intentioned ward-setting has
actually taken from him.

It is considerably more than the record I've been keeping accounts for.

I stop walking. I do not decide to stop; my own legs simply stop
answering properly, the way Aldric's hand stopped answering properly a
few nights ago, and I stand in the middle of the track with the whole
cumulative weight of what I've been doing to him arriving in my chest
at once, undeniable, no longer manageable by any arithmetic I currently
possess.

"How long have you been like this," I say, and my voice does something
I do not authorize — cracks, slightly, on the last word, in a way I
have not let my voice do in front of another person in longer than I
can accurately place.

"Since before you'd have noticed," Aldric says, quietly, "if you
weren't looking for it specifically."

"That's not an answer. That's an admission dressed up as an answer."

"It's the honest one," he says. "I don't know precisely when it
started, Isla. I know it's been building for weeks. I know it's
considerably worse than I've let either of us properly examine. I
didn't want to hand you a fact you couldn't do anything useful with,
except worry."

"You don't get to decide that for me," I say, and hear, in my own
voice, an anger that has nothing to do with him specifically and
everything to do with an arrangement — mine, the crown's, the whole
inherited weight of a rite I did not design and have spent six years
faithfully administering regardless — that has been quietly killing
the man standing in front of me while I kept careful, insufficient
account of the cost.

"I know," Aldric says, and does not argue further, which somehow makes
the whole of it considerably worse, because I understand, hearing him
simply accept the accusation rather than defend himself against it,
that he has already done this same arithmetic himself, privately, and
arrived at a conclusion he has been quietly bracing to deliver whenever
the moment finally forced his hand.

We stand on the track a long while, neither of us moving, both of us
absorbing the specific size of what has just become undeniable between
us.

"Whatever arrangement I thought I was managing," I say, eventually,
"it was never sustainable. Not the way I've been running it. Not
without either killing you outright or finding some other way to hold
the line that doesn't require spending one hearth quietly until there's
nothing left of him to spend."

"The record," Aldric says. "The one from Calder's Reach. The mutual
form."

"I don't know yet if it actually works," I say. "I don't know if it's
survivable, attempted wrong, or by two people the old rite-keepers never
sanctioned to try it. I know only that whatever I've been doing instead
isn't survivable either, and that I have been, without properly
reckoning with it until this exact moment, killing you by inches, one
carefully managed ward at a time."

"Then we find out," Aldric says, "whether the mutual form holds.
Together. Properly, this time — not managed quietly in the dark by
either of us alone."

I take his hand, there on the track, his good hand, the one that still
answers properly, and hold it the whole rest of the walk back to
Highstone, doing arithmetic that has, for the first time in six years,
stopped being solely mine to solve, and finding, in the sharing of it,
something that frightens me considerably less than the arithmetic
itself ever managed to.

Whatever this story was, walking out to Calder's Reach two days ago, it
is no longer that story. The true cost has arrived, all at once,
undeniable, and there is no version of what comes next that does not
require both of us, together, properly, to survive it.

·

We stop at the last standing cairn before the final stretch back to
Highstone, both of us needing, I think, a moment to properly gather
ourselves before facing Tomas and the day's ordinary business again,
and it is there, in the particular quiet of a standing stone that has
held its charge faithfully for longer than either of our own lifetimes,
that Aldric asks the question I have been half-dreading since the walk
began.

"What happens to Tomas," he says, "if the mutual rite doesn't work. If
Ossory won't sanction it, or the translation proves unreliable, or we
simply run out of time before we can properly test it."

"I don't know," I say, honestly, because I have decided that
half-truths cost more between us now, and because the honest answer is
considerably more frightening than any managed version I could offer
instead. "I know Feld's contingency doesn't disappear simply because
we've found a record suggesting an alternative. I know I am not
willing, under any circumstance, to let Tomas be bound the way you were
bound, without proper preparation, simply because the crown's patience
ran out before ours did."

"That's not really an answer to what happens," Aldric says, gently,
"only to what you're not willing to allow."

"I know," I admit. "I don't have the other half of the answer yet. I
think — I hope — that having proof the mutual form exists, even
imperfect proof, buys us enough standing with Ossory and the crown both
to argue for more time, regardless of how the actual attempt eventually
goes."

"And if it doesn't buy us that time?"

I look at him for a long moment, the cairn field silent around us, and
find myself, for the first time since Calder's Reach, considering a
question I have been carefully avoiding examining too closely.

"Then I fight for more time anyway," I say, "by whatever means are
available to me, because I am not willing to let this line's
arithmetic be solved on the backs of the two people I care about most
in the world, regardless of what the crown's own preferred timeline
demands."

Aldric doesn't have an answer to that, and I don't need one. We simply
stand at the cairn a while longer, gathering what strength either of us
has left before the walk resumes, and I find myself grateful, standing
there, for a man who lets my uncertainty stand unmanaged rather than
demanding I resolve it before I'm ready.

·

We find Tomas waiting at Highstone's own gate, having clearly been
watching the road since first light, and his relief at seeing us both
upright is visible enough that I understand, watching him, exactly how
much he has been quietly worrying in our absence.

"You were gone longer than planned," he says, careful not to make it an
accusation, though the worry underneath the observation is plain.

"We found considerably more than we expected to," I say, "and needed
the extra time to properly absorb it."

I tell him the whole of it, once we're settled in the record-room
properly — Calder's Reach, the record box, the well-house's fuller
transcription, Ossian's own grief, finally faced — and watch him
absorb each piece with the particular gravity he's shown, this whole
season, toward anything that touches the line's deeper history.

"What happens now," he asks, once I've finished.

"Now I take this to Ossory," I say, "properly, with actual evidence
rather than merely a theory. And we hope his own private doubts about
the standard rite are strong enough to let him hear it fairly."

"And if he won't hear it?"

"Then I find another way," I say, with more certainty than I
currently possess, because I have decided that Tomas, of everyone at
Highstone, deserves a version of me that sounds capable of handling
whatever comes, regardless of how uncertain the actual path forward
currently feels.

Tomas nods, accepting this, and busies himself immediately with
properly cataloguing the record box's contents, the specific practical
comfort of a young man who processes his own fear best through useful
work rather than further conversation. I let him have the task, understanding
that it serves him considerably better than any further reassurance I
could currently offer.

·

Aldric rests for the remainder of that day, at Cera's own firm
insistence, and I use the quiet hours to properly organize my
thoughts, drafting a careful outline of everything Ossory will need to
properly weigh — the predecessor's record, the well-house's fuller
transcription, Ossian's own private doubt, the whole accumulated weight
of evidence I've gathered since Calder's Reach first cracked open a
question I'd never thought to ask.

Sela arrives partway through the afternoon, having heard, through
whatever channel keeps her reliably informed, that something significant
happened on the walk home.

"Tell me everything," she says, settling in without waiting to be
asked, and I do, watching her absorb the whole of it with the specific
gravity she's shown, this whole season, toward anything touching her
brother's safety.

"Do you think Ossory will actually hear it properly," she asks, "or
will the crown's own caution simply bury it in deliberation until it's
too late to matter?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I hope the former. I've seen enough of his
own private doubt, these past weeks, to believe he genuinely wants to
be convinced. But wanting to be convinced and actually acting quickly
enough aren't always the same thing, in an institution built around
careful, deliberate process."

"Then we make certain he acts quickly," Sela says, with the specific
fierce practicality I've come to recognize as entirely her own. "Whatever
it takes. I'm not interested in watching my brother die of an
institution's caution, when an actual alternative sits available and
proven, at least in theory."

"Neither am I," I agree, and the two of us spend the rest of the
afternoon refining my presentation together, Sela's own plain,
unmanaged perspective proving considerably more useful than I expected,
sharpening my careful wardwright's argument into something considerably
harder for anyone to dismiss.

·

Aldric sleeps through most of that same afternoon, at Cera's own
instruction, and I find myself, once Sela has finally gone home,
sitting beside him rather than continuing the presentation alone,
watching him rest in a way I have rarely allowed myself the stillness
to properly do.

I think, watching him, about the specific arithmetic of loving someone
whose cost you are partially responsible for extracting — not guilt,
exactly, though guilt sits somewhere nearby, closer to the plain,
unmanageable fact of having spent six years administering a rite that
has been quietly killing the person currently sleeping in front of me,
without either of us properly reckoning with the scale of it until a
walk home from Calder's Reach forced the reckoning.

He wakes, eventually, finding me still there.

"You've been watching me sleep," he observes, not quite an accusation.

"I have," I admit. "I think I needed to see you resting properly, for
once, without either of us managing some crisis around it."

"How long have I been asleep?"

"Most of the afternoon. Sela and I worked on the presentation for
Ossory while you rested. I think it's considerably stronger for her
input."

Aldric considers this, still drowsy, and reaches for my hand.

"Thank you," he says, "for letting me rest, instead of insisting I be
part of every single piece of this. I don't think I'd have managed to
ask for that myself."

"I know," I say. "That's rather why I didn't wait for you to ask."

We sit together a while longer in the cottage's quiet afternoon light,
and I find myself, watching him properly wake, grateful for a
stillness neither of us has been able to properly afford in longer
than either of us can accurately place.

·

Tomas visits that same evening, ostensibly to deliver a final report,
actually, I suspect, simply to check on Aldric properly with his own
eyes rather than trust secondhand reassurance.

"You look better," he observes, settling into the third chair.

"I feel better," Aldric says. "Rested, for once, rather than merely
upright."

"Good," Tomas says, with visible relief. "I've been worried, this past
week, in a way I haven't quite said out loud to either of you."

"You can say it now," I offer.

Tomas considers this, gathering his thoughts properly.

"I've been frightened I'd lose you both," he admits, finally, "before
I'd properly learned everything I still need to learn. Not simply the
mechanics. The whole of what you've both taught me about actually
caring for the people this line touches, rather than merely managing
the arithmetic of it."

"You've learned considerably more than you give yourself credit for,"
I say.

"Maybe," Tomas says, "but I don't think I've learned enough yet. I
don't want to find out the hard way that I wasn't ready, because
neither of you had the time left to finish teaching me properly."

"We're not going anywhere," Aldric says, with more certainty than
either of us can strictly guarantee, though I understand, hearing it,
that he means it as a promise worth making regardless of the
arithmetic's actual odds.

"I'm holding you to that," Tomas says, with a small, watery smile, and
the three of us sit together a while longer in the cottage's gathering
evening warmth, each of us privately determined to make certain that
particular promise holds.

·

Sela arrives partway through the evening, drawn, I suspect, by the same
worry that brought Tomas, and settles in without needing an invitation,
the particular easy belonging of a woman who has, these past weeks,
folded herself properly into this household's own rhythms.

"You've got the whole family gathered," she observes, taking in the
cottage's small, crowded warmth.

"Apparently everyone needed the same reassurance tonight," I say.

"That's not a bad thing," Sela says, settling by the fire. "I think
I'd rather we all needed it at once, together, than each of us
managing our private version of the same worry separately."

"I agree," Aldric says, and the four of us spend the rest of the
evening together, the ordinary business of tea and conversation
filling the cottage's small space, and I find myself, watching this
whole assembled family settle into easy company, grateful for
precisely this kind of unremarkable evening — the one none of us,
six weeks ago, would have properly believed we'd get to have.

·

Sela lingers after Tomas has finally gone home, helping me settle the
last of the evening's dishes.

"Thank you for letting me stay tonight," she says. "I wasn't certain,
knocking, whether I was intruding on something that wasn't mine to
join."

"You're always welcome," I say, meaning it entirely. "I don't think
either of us properly knows how to separate 'our family' from 'your
family' anymore. I'm not certain we should try to."

"Neither am I," Sela says, with a small, genuine smile, and gathers
her things for the walk home, and I find myself, watching her go,
grateful for a family that has grown, this difficult winter, into
something considerably larger and considerably kinder than any of us
arrived expecting.


## Chapter Twenty-Two — Sela's Ledger (Aldric)

Sela shows Isla the winter grain count without being asked a second
time, trusting her now the way she didn't quite manage at their first
meeting in the grain-house, a fortnight and a half ago, when the two of
them were still sizing each other up across a counting table neither
had yet decided to fully open.

I notice the shift in my sister's manner immediately, watching the two
of them work through the ledger together — no longer the careful,
measured distance of a woman deciding how much to reveal, simply two
people comparing honest numbers with the particular ease of people
who've already decided to trust each other's arithmetic.

The count is bad. Considerably worse than bad, if the line keeps its
current pace through the rest of Coldwake, worse still if my own
binding fails before spring properly arrives to ease the worst of the
season's demands.

"He'd never tell you it's this close," Sela says, to Isla, laying out
the full shape of it — grain traded away in lean years, a margin that
has been shrinking steadily since the crown tightened the exemption
terms, a household that has been quietly absorbing costs for
considerably longer than the two winters since I put my own name
forward at the rite. "He didn't tell me either, for months, when it
was his exemption on the line instead of yours."

I say nothing, standing near the grain-house door, because there is
nothing useful to say to an accurate accounting of my own worst habit,
laid out plainly by the one person who has known me longest and trusts
me least to manage it honestly.

Isla listens to the whole of it, and I watch her recognize, in real
time, the family shape of my own flaw — a household that has been
quietly absorbing costs and calling it management for at least two
generations, long enough that nobody currently living properly
remembers when the habit started.

"I'm not going to let this cost him what it cost your father," Isla
says, to Sela, with a specific certainty that surprises me, hearing
it — a promise made before she's fully worked out how she intends to
keep it, offered anyway, because the alternative is watching another
Marrow quietly spend himself into the ground without anyone properly
naming what's happening while there's still time to stop it.

"I'd like to believe that," Sela says, "but I've watched him make
himself indispensable to this family's survival since he was fourteen
years old, and I've never once managed to talk him out of it. What
makes you think you'll manage where I've failed for most of his life?"

"I don't think I'll talk him out of it," Isla says. "I think I'll share
it with him instead, until the habit stops being something either of
us can do quietly, alone, because there won't be an alone left to do it
in."

Sela looks at her for a long moment, assessing, and something in her
face settles — not full trust, not yet, but the specific loosening of a
woman who has been braced, for years, against watching her brother's
pattern repeat itself unchecked, finally allowing herself to consider
that this particular repetition might, this once, actually be
interrupted.

"He's lucky, then," Sela says, "that the rite happened to bind him to
someone who recognizes the pattern instead of simply admiring it."

I do not have a ready response to being discussed, quite so plainly,
by the two women currently deciding, between them, how best to manage
my own worst habit, so I simply busy myself checking the grain-house's
door hinge, which does not, in fact, need checking, until the
conversation moves on to something considerably less directly about me.

·

Walking back down from the orchard that evening, Isla is quiet in the
particular way that means she's still turning something over.

"Your sister loves you considerably more plainly than you let yourself
notice," she says, eventually.

"I notice," I say. "I've just never found a good way to let it change
how I actually behave, day to day."

"Maybe that changes now," Isla says, "given everything else that's
changing. I'd like it to, at least. I don't think either of us can
afford, this winter, to keep managing our worst habits alone simply
because we've always managed them that way before."

"Agreed," I say, and mean it, though I understand, saying it, that
agreeing to change a habit this deeply worn into a person is
considerably easier said, walking down a dark road with someone you've
decided to trust, than actually done, in the moment, when the old
instinct to carry everything alone reasserts itself the way old
instincts always do.

·

Sela finds me alone in the orchard the next afternoon, mending a
section of fence that has been slowly failing since the last hard
frost, and watches me work for a while before she says anything at all,
which I have learned, over twenty-eight years, to recognize as a sign
she's been thinking through exactly how to phrase whatever's actually
on her mind.

"I like her," she says, finally. "I want you to know that plainly,
because I don't say it about many people, and because I think you've
spent so long assuming I'll disapprove of whatever choice you make that
you might not properly hear it when I don't."

"I hear it," I say. "Thank you."

"I mean it beyond simply liking her company," Sela continues. "I mean
I've watched the two of you these past weeks, and I've watched
something in you soften that I genuinely believed, some years ago, had
gone permanently rigid. I didn't think anyone was going to manage that.
I'm glad to be wrong."

I set down the fence post, considering how to answer that properly.

"I didn't think so either," I admit. "I'd made my peace with being the
reliable one, permanently, the way Father was. I didn't think there was
room in that arrangement for anyone to actually reach me."

"Father made his peace with it too," Sela says, "and I don't think it
was ever real peace, if I'm honest. I think it was simply exhaustion,
dressed up as contentment, because the alternative — actually needing
someone, after Mother — felt like too large a risk to properly
consider."

"You think I've been doing the same thing."

"I think you've been doing exactly the same thing," Sela says, "since
you were old enough to notice how much easier it was to manage
everyone else's needs than to admit you had any of your own. I'm not
saying it to blame you for it. I'm saying it because I'd like you to
notice, properly, that whatever's happening with Isla is the first
genuine crack in that particular armor I've seen in twenty years of
watching you wear it."

I don't have a ready response to being seen quite that precisely by my
own sister, so I simply go back to the fence, and let her words settle
alongside everything else I am slowly learning to examine rather than
simply manage, grateful, in the specific quiet way I've always been
grateful for Sela, that she has never once let me get away with the
comfortable version of my own story.

·

I ask her, before I leave the orchard that afternoon, the question I've
been turning over since Isla and I discussed it on the walk home from
the boundary marker — whether there's anything Sela has needed from me,
over the years, that she never felt able to ask for directly.

Sela looks at me for a long moment, clearly surprised by the question,
and I watch her decide, visibly, whether to answer it with the polite
deflection she generally reserves for anything that might make either
of us uncomfortable, or with something considerably more honest.

"I wanted you to ask me, once, just once, whether taking your exemption
was actually what I wanted," she says, finally, quietly. "Not whether I
was grateful. I was, enormously. But nobody ever asked whether I
wanted it done that way, or whether I might have preferred to face the
crown's terms myself, on my own footing, rather than be protected from
a choice I never got to make."

I absorb that properly, understanding, hearing it, exactly how much
weight has apparently sat underneath her gratitude for considerably
longer than I realized.

"I'm sorry," I say. "I should have asked, at the time. I decided, the
way I always decide, that protecting you mattered more than consulting
you."

"I know," Sela says. "I've made my peace with it, mostly. I'm not
asking you to undo it. I'm simply answering the question you finally
got around to asking, three years late."

"Better three years late than never," I say, "though I understand if
that's not much comfort."

"It's more comfort than you'd think," Sela says, and reaches over to
squeeze my hand, briefly, before going back to her own work, and I
walk back down from the orchard that evening carrying the specific,
complicated weight of having finally asked a question I should have
asked considerably sooner, grateful, at least, that it's asked now
rather than never at all.

·

I tell Isla about the conversation that same evening, curious what
she'll make of it.

"I think that took considerable courage," she says, "asking a question
you'd spent years assuming you already knew the answer to."

"I nearly didn't ask it," I admit. "I stood at her door for a full
minute working up the nerve, the same way I stood at the cairn field
edge before I first properly asked you anything."

"What made you finally ask?"

"You did," I say, simply. "Watching you insist on the plain question,
every time, regardless of how frightening the possible answer might be.
I found I couldn't keep asking that of you without eventually asking it
of the people I've spent my whole life protecting instead of properly
consulting."

Isla takes my hand, considering this.

"I think that's rather the best compliment anyone's given me this
whole winter," she says, "that watching me ask hard questions taught
you to ask your own."

"It's not a compliment," I say. "It's simply true. You've changed how
I move through the world, in ways I don't think either of us fully
appreciated until just now, sitting here, adding up all the small ways
it's actually happened."

We sit together a while longer, and I find myself, turning the whole
conversation over, grateful for a woman who has taught me, simply by
her own example, that asking a hard question properly is its own kind
of love, considerably more honest than any quiet, well-meaning
protection could ever manage to be.

·

Tomas asks me, the following day, whether I'd be willing to help him
draft a similar question for someone in his own life — a cousin,
apparently, in a village two days south, from whom he's been quietly
estranged since his mother's death, over some old grievance neither of
them has properly named in three years.

"I don't know how to ask it without it sounding like an accusation," he
admits. "I've drafted it four times already. It keeps coming out
wrong."

"Show me," I say, and he does, and I understand, reading it, exactly
what's gone wrong with each attempt — every version manages the
cousin's likely reaction rather than simply asking the plain question
underneath it.

"You're doing what I used to do," I tell him, gently. "Managing the
asking so carefully that the actual question gets lost underneath all
the careful cushioning."

"What would you write instead?"

"Something closer to: *I've missed you these three years, and I don't
fully understand what came between us. Would you tell me, plainly,
what I did, so I can properly apologize for it rather than simply
guessing?*"

Tomas considers this, turning the plainer version over.

"That's considerably more frightening to send," he says.

"It is," I agree. "It's also considerably more likely to actually
work. The comfortable version protects you from an honest answer. I
don't think that's what you actually want, is it?"

"No," Tomas admits. "I want to know. Even if the knowing hurts."

"Then send the frightening version," I say, "and let me know if it
gets you the answer you're actually looking for."

He drafts the letter properly that same evening, considerably plainer
than his first four attempts, and I find myself, watching him seal it,
grateful for the specific way this whole difficult winter keeps
teaching everyone in its orbit the same hard lesson, over and over, in
whatever shape each person happens to need it delivered.

·

The reply arrives some ten days later, considerably faster than Tomas
expected, and I find him at the record-room table holding it unopened,
turning it over in both hands.

"You don't have to read it in front of anyone," I say, gently.

"I know," Tomas says. "I think I want to, though. I think I'd rather
have company for it, whatever it says."

He opens it carefully, and I watch his face as he reads, the specific
unguarded relief that crosses it partway through.

"She wants to see me," he says, looking up. "Properly. She says she's
sorry too, that she let the silence run this long over something that,
reading her account of it, sounds almost embarrassingly small now that
either of us has finally named it plainly."

"How does that feel," I ask, "hearing it named."

"Considerably lighter than I expected," Tomas admits. "I think I'd
built the whole estrangement up into something larger than it actually
was, simply by leaving it unexamined for three years."

"That happens," I say. "Silence has a way of making small things grow
considerably larger than they'd have stayed, properly named early."

"I'm glad I sent the frightening version," Tomas says, "rather than the
comfortable one. I don't think the comfortable version would have
gotten me here."

"Neither do I," I agree, and we sit together a while longer, both of
us grateful for a small, private reconciliation achieved through the
same plain honesty this whole family has spent the season learning to
practice.

·

I mention it to Isla that evening, curious what she'll make of the
whole outcome, once Tomas has gone home considerably lighter than he
arrived.

"It went well," I say. "Considerably better than either of them
expected, from what he told me. They spent most of the visit simply
catching up, rather than relitigating whatever caused the original
rift."

"That's rather how most reconciliations seem to go, once the hard part
of the asking is actually managed," Isla observes.

"I think that's true," I say. "The asking is almost always the
frightening part. Once the plain question's actually been put, the
answer tends to be considerably kinder than the years of avoided
silence would suggest it might be."

"I hope that holds for the rest of us too," Isla says, "whatever
hard questions any of us still have left to ask."

"I think it will," I say, "given how much practice we've all had this
winter," and we sit together a while longer, both of us grateful for a
season that keeps, in ways large and small, teaching this whole
extended family the same steadying lesson about the plain question's
particular power.

·

Tomas tells me, a few days later, that his cousin has asked to visit
Highstone properly, once the roads clear enough for the journey.

"I'd like that," I say. "I think it would do you good, having her see
where you've landed, properly, after everything."

"I think so too," Tomas says. "I want her to meet all of you. Aldric,
Sela. I think I've become someone rather different than the boy who
left home three years ago, and I'd like her to see the difference for
herself, rather than simply hear about it in a letter."

"I think she'll be proud of who you've become," I say, and mean it
entirely.

"I hope so," Tomas says, with a small, hopeful smile, and I find
myself, watching him, grateful for a reconciliation that has already
begun, in its own quiet way, to widen this family's own generous
circle even further.


## Chapter Twenty-Three — What He Hasn't Said (Isla)

I have started noticing the pattern rather than the incidents, the way
six years of reading failing stone has taught me to read failing
people — not
merely the cup that fell at dinner, but the accumulation around it: the
left hand kept in a pocket more evenings than not, a specific flexing
he does when he believes no one is watching, the careful way he has
started avoiding any task that requires fine, delicate use of that hand
at all, grafting wax measured out now with his right hand alone, a
detail I would never have caught if I hadn't spent six years training
myself to notice exactly this kind of small, deliberate compensation.

I have watched people manage a failing body before, over the years —
a handful of the crown's own aging inspectors, an old farmer near
Hollow Farm who kept working a bad knee until it finally gave out
entirely. I recognize the specific shape of the managing. I have never
before recognized it on someone I love this much.

"Show me," I say, one evening, finally, plainly, the same register I
used the night I asked him, at the cairn field, whether he actually
wanted this.

Aldric goes very still, in the specific way of a man who has been
caught at something he wasn't ready to be caught at, and for a long
moment I think he intends to manage the request the way he manages
most things — a partial admission, carefully bounded, offered to
satisfy the asking without actually answering it.

He doesn't. He holds out the hand instead, and peels back the glove,
and I see, for the first time, properly, what six years of a
practitioner's training should have let me guess considerably sooner
than tonight: two fingers gone entirely numb, colorless in a way that
has nothing to do with the evening's actual temperature, a third
finger clearly going the same way, the whole hand cold in a manner that
does not thaw, the way the rest of him thaws, simply by sitting near a
proper fire.

"It's not the whole of it," I say, quietly, because I have learned, by
now, to read the specific shape of a partial truth even when it's
offered as though it were complete.

"I know it isn't," Aldric says, and for once doesn't try to make the
admission smaller than it actually is, which costs him, visibly,
considerably more than the admission itself.

"How much further has it spread, since the cup."

"Some," he says. "I've stopped keeping precise count. I decided, a
while back, that counting wasn't going to change the number, only make
me more afraid of it."

I want, badly, to argue with that particular logic — I have spent my
own entire career believing that precise counting is the one reliable
defense against an arithmetic that would otherwise simply run away
unchecked — but I understand, looking at his face, that arguing would
simply teach him to hide the counting from me the way he's clearly been
hiding it from himself.

"I need you to let me keep the count, then," I say, "if you won't keep
it yourself. Not to frighten either of us further. So that when the
number reaches whatever threshold makes the mutual rite urgent rather
than merely theoretical, we'll actually know it's arrived, instead of
guessing."

"Agreed," Aldric says, and I understand, from the way he says it, that
this small concession — handing me the counting he's refused to do
himself — costs him something close to what the actual numbness costs,
a different kind of surrender, considerably harder for a man built the
way he's built.

Neither of us says the words HOW MUCH LONGER. They sit in the cottage
with us regardless, taking up exactly as much room as if we had spoken
them aloud, and I find myself, sitting across from him with his numb
hand still resting, uncovered, on the table between us, doing the
arithmetic anyway, silently, the way I do arithmetic on everything,
and arriving, this time, at an answer I am not yet prepared to say out
loud to either of us.

"Thank you," I say, instead, "for showing me. Even the part of it you'd
rather I hadn't seen."

"You'd have found it eventually," Aldric says. "You notice everything,
eventually. I'd rather you found it because I showed you than because
you caught me hiding it a second time."

"There won't be a second time," I say, and mean it as a promise to
both of us — that whatever this costs, going forward, it will be
counted plainly, between the two of us, rather than managed quietly by
either one alone.

·

I go to the healer at Highstone the following morning, a woman named
Cera Voss who has treated frostbite and cold-sickness on this line for
longer than I have been wardwright, and lay out the whole of what I've
seen in Aldric's hand, asking her the plain question I am not
otherwise equipped to answer.

"How fast does it usually spread," I ask, "once numbness sets in
properly. In your experience. Not the rite's theoretical accounting.
What you've actually watched happen to people."

Cera considers the question with the particular gravity of a woman who
has, I suspect, watched this exact progression more times than either
of us would like to count.

"It varies," she says, "depending on how deep the underlying cost runs.
If it's simple cold exposure, it can stabilize on its own, given proper
warmth and rest. If it's something feeding on the person continuously —
which is, I understand, precisely what you're describing — it doesn't
stabilize. It progresses, at whatever rate the underlying drain
demands, until either the drain stops or there's nothing left for it to
take."

"How long," I ask, "before someone in that condition—"

"I can't give you a precise number," Cera says, gently, understanding
exactly what I'm asking without my needing to finish the sentence. "I
can tell you that the progression you're describing, from a fortnight's
partial numbness to a whole hand failing entirely, suggests
considerably less time than a full season. Weeks, most likely. Possibly
fewer, if the underlying draw accelerates the way these things
sometimes do once they've properly taken hold."

I sit with that number — weeks, not a season — for a long moment,
feeling the whole shape of my own remaining time contract considerably
faster than I had let myself believe.

"Is there anything that slows it," I ask, "short of stopping the drain
entirely?"

"Warmth. Rest. Reducing whatever's actively feeding on him, as much as
you're able." Cera looks at me carefully. "You understand, Wardwright,
that reducing it isn't the same as stopping it. If the underlying cause
is what I suspect it is, based on what little you've told me, the only
genuine cure is removing the cause itself."

"I understand," I say, and thank her, and walk back to the cottage with
a considerably clearer, considerably more frightening timeline than I
had this morning, understanding that whatever the mutual rite is going
to require of Ossory's careful, deliberate consideration, we do not, in
fact, have the luxury of his usual pace to work within.

·

I do not tell Aldric the precise number Cera gave me, that evening,
though I tell him the general shape of it — weeks, not a season — and
watch him absorb the news with a steadiness that surprises me, given
everything else he's had to absorb these past days.

"I think I already knew," he says, quietly. "Not the precise number.
The general shape of it. I've felt the difference, this past week,
between the ordinary cold I've lived with my whole life and whatever
this is instead."

"Does knowing it plainly change anything, for you?"

"It makes the arithmetic considerably harder to avoid," Aldric admits.
"I think I've been telling myself, quietly, that there was more room
than there actually is. Hearing an actual number — even a rough one —
makes that harder to keep believing."

"I'm sorry," I say. "I wish I had better news to bring you."

"Don't be sorry for the truth," Aldric says. "I'd rather have this,
however frightening, than another comfortable guess. You've taught me
that much, at least, these past weeks — that the true thing, however
much it costs to hear, is worth more than the managed version."

We sit together with the weight of the timeline a while longer, neither
of us pretending the fear isn't considerable, both of us, at least,
sharing it properly rather than either one carrying it alone.

"What do we do with the time we have," Aldric asks, eventually.

"We spend it properly," I say. "Pressing Ossory, finishing the
translation, doing everything within our power to make the mutual rite
ready before the timeline runs out. And in between all of that, we
keep living, properly, rather than simply surviving toward whatever
comes next."

"I can do that," Aldric says, and I believe him, and we spend the rest
of that evening doing precisely that — living, properly, in the time
we have, rather than merely counting down the time we fear we don't.

·

We walk to the cairn field, that same evening, not for any inspection,
simply because it has become, these past weeks, the place we both
return to when a conversation needs the particular clarity the standing
stones seem to offer.

"I want to ask you something," Aldric says, once we've reached our
usual spot. "Not about the timeline. About after. If the mutual rite
holds, if we actually get the years the healer wouldn't quite promise
us — what do you want, for the two of us, once this crisis has
properly passed?"

I consider the question carefully, because it deserves more than a
reflexive answer, and because I have not, until this exact moment,
properly let myself imagine a future that isn't shaped entirely by the
current emergency.

"I want ordinary evenings," I say, finally. "Considerably more of them
than we've had so far. I want to watch Tomas become the wardwright I
already know he's going to be, and I want to be there, properly, for
whatever that eventually looks like. I want your hand in mine, however
marked it ends up being, for as many years as this line and the crown
between them are willing to grant us."

"That's not a small want."

"No," I agree. "I've spent six years being frightened of wanting
anything this size, in case wanting it invited the specific pain of
losing it. I've decided, this winter, that the fear isn't worth the
cost of never wanting anything at all."

"I want the same things," Aldric says, quietly, "and I want to be
around to actually build them with you, properly, rather than simply
hoping for them from a distance."

We stand together at the cairn field a while longer, the standing
stones holding their usual patient silence around us, both of us
choosing, deliberately, to want a future neither of us can yet
guarantee, rather than let fear talk either of us out of wanting it at
all.

·

Tomas finds the two of us still at the cairn field when the evening
properly closes in, having apparently come looking once neither of us
returned by the expected hour.

"I was starting to worry," he admits, slightly out of breath from the
walk.

"We're fine," I say. "Simply talking. About after — whatever after
turns out to look like."

"Can I ask what you decided," Tomas says, settling in beside us,
"or is it private?"

"It's not private from you," I say, and mean it, understanding, saying
it, exactly how thoroughly he's become part of this family's inner
accounting rather than merely its apprentice. "We decided we want
ordinary evenings. Considerably more of them. We decided we want to
watch you become the wardwright I already know you're going to be."

Tomas's face does something unguarded at that, the particular
vulnerable pleasure of a young man who still isn't fully used to being
included this plainly in the people he serves.

"I'd like that too," he says, quietly. "Ordinary evenings. I don't
think I properly understood, before this winter, how much I wanted
that for myself as well, rather than simply the crisis-to-crisis
existence I assumed being a wardwright required."

"It doesn't have to be crisis to crisis," I say. "I think that's rather
the whole lesson underneath everything this winter's taught me. The
line can be held properly, urgently when it needs to be, and gently,
ordinarily, the rest of the time. I don't think I understood that
balance was possible, myself, until fairly recently."

"I'm glad you're teaching me that too," Tomas says, "alongside the
cadence and the ledgers. I think it might turn out to be the more
important lesson."

We walk back to Highstone together, the three of us, in the particular
easy quiet of a found family that has, somewhere in this difficult
season, learned to want an ordinary future together rather than simply
survive toward whatever crisis comes next.

·

Sela catches us at the orchard gate on our way past, having apparently
been watching the road for our return, and falls into step beside
Tomas with an easy familiarity that tells me their own friendship has
grown considerably since the season began.

"You three look like you've settled something," she observes.

"We were talking about after," Tomas says. "What we want, once this is
all behind us."

"And?"

"Ordinary evenings," Tomas says, with a small, pleased smile. "Isla and
Aldric want them. I find I want them too, rather more than I expected
to, given how much of my apprenticeship has felt like one long
emergency."

"I could use a few ordinary evenings myself," Sela admits. "I don't
think I've had one in longer than I can properly place, between the
grain counts and the exemption paperwork and worrying about my brother's
various self-sacrificing habits."

"Then we'll have them together," I say. "All of us. Once the current
crisis actually resolves."

"I'll hold you to that," Sela says, "the whole lot of you," and the
four of us continue on toward Highstone together, already, in some
small private way, beginning to practice the ordinary future we've
just finished naming aloud.

·

Aldric lingers with me a moment at the cottage door that evening, once
Tomas and Sela have both gone their separate ways, watching the last
of the day's light fail over the cairn field in the distance.

"I keep thinking about what you said," he tells me, "about wanting
ordinary evenings, considerably more of them. I don't think I properly
understood, before tonight, how rare a want that actually is, for me.
I spent most of my adult life assuming ordinary was something other
people got to have, not something I was permitted to want for myself."

"You're permitted now," I say. "As many ordinary evenings as either of
us can manage to build."

"I'd like to start tonight," Aldric says, "if you're willing. Nothing
urgent. Simply an evening, properly ordinary, the two of us."

"I'd like that very much," I say, and we go inside together, into the
cottage's low firelight, choosing, deliberately, to spend the evening
on nothing more urgent than each other's company — a small, quiet
practice of the future we've just spent the whole walk home describing
aloud.

·

We spend the evening on nothing more consequential than a shared meal
and a slow, unhurried conversation about the small, ordinary things
neither of us usually has time to properly discuss — a favorite tree
from Aldric's own childhood, a half-remembered song from mine, the
particular satisfaction of a well-mended tool.

"This is nice," Aldric says, simply, some while into the evening.

"It is," I agree. "I don't think I've properly appreciated, before
tonight, how much practice ordinary conversation actually requires,
after six years of only ever discussing cairns and crises."

"I'll help you practice," Aldric says, "as often as you'll let me,"
and we spend the rest of the evening doing exactly that, two people
slowly, deliberately, learning the particular unhurried art of simply
enjoying each other's company.


## Chapter Twenty-Four — Ask Me Properly (Aldric)

Before anything further happens between us, Isla makes us both say it
in words, out loud, unbound — not the rite speaking through either of
us, not the bond's own compulsion doing the asking on our behalf, an
actual person asking another actual person, plainly, the way she has
insisted, since the cairn field, that everything between us has to be
asked.

"I need to know this is you asking," I say, before she can begin,
because I have been turning this exact concern over myself for days
now, watching what the bond can make either of us feel without either
of us meaning to feel it. "Not the bond wanting company against the
cold. I've felt what it does, unbidden — the warmth, the ache, all of
it arriving without permission from either of us. I need to know the
difference matters to you here, specifically, as much as it's mattered
everywhere else."

"It's me asking," Isla says, and I hear, in the plainness of it,
exactly the difference she means — not a performance offered to
reassure me, a simple, unguarded fact, stated once, the way she states
most of the things she actually means. "I've spent this whole
arrangement learning precisely what the bond's pull feels like,
separate from an actual choice. I know the difference now. This is the
second one."

I ask her the same question back, in the same plain terms, because it
seems only fair the asking run in both directions, the way everything
else important between us has, since the night at the cairn field.

"Is this you," I ask, "asking. Separate from whatever the bond's made
easier."

"Yes," Isla says, without a moment's hesitation, and I believe her
entirely, in the specific way I have learned, these past weeks, to
believe the things she says plainly rather than the things she manages.

Nothing happens between us yet, in this particular conversation. The
asking is the whole of it — deliberate, complete, both of us
understanding, without either having to say so directly, that this
foundation is the only one either of us is willing to build anything
further on, given everything the rite has already taken from both of
us without asking permission first.

"There's a thing I want to say," I add, once the asking itself is
settled, "that isn't strictly part of the question, but that I think
matters regardless."

"Say it."

"I've spent my whole life giving before I'm asked. Sela's exemption. The
binding itself. It's easier, that way, to control exactly what a thing
costs me, rather than risk someone asking for more than I'd have
offered on my own. I want you to know that answering your question
honestly — actually waiting to be asked, rather than simply offering
myself the way I usually do — has been considerably harder than I
expected it to be."

"Why," Isla asks.

"Because if I wait to be asked, I have to actually risk the answer
being no," I say. "I've spent my whole life avoiding that particular
risk by simply never putting myself in a position to hear it. Tonight,
I have. And I find I'd rather hear an honest no from you than a
managed yes from myself."

Isla looks at me for a long moment, in the last of the evening light,
and I understand, from the specific quality of her stillness, that
something in what I've just said has landed considerably harder than I
intended it to.

"That's the most honest thing you've ever told me," she says, finally.
"Thank you for risking it."

"Thank you for asking properly," I say, "so I had the chance to."

Nothing further happens between us tonight beyond the asking itself.
It is, both of us understand, entirely enough — the only foundation
either of us is willing to build anything further on, and considerably
more than either of us, six weeks ago, standing wary and unbound at a
rite neither of us chose, would have believed either one capable of
offering the other so plainly.

·

I think, walking back from the cottage to the orchard the next
morning, about how differently I understood consent before this
binding — as a formality, mostly, a thing observed at the edges of
larger decisions rather than the actual foundation any of it should
properly rest on. I gave Sela's exemption without asking her whether she
wanted it given that way. I volunteered for the rite without asking
myself, honestly, whether I was choosing it for the right reasons or
simply the reasons that let me avoid being asked for anything smaller,
later, by someone who might actually say no.

Isla has taught me, in the space of a few short weeks, that asking
properly — plainly, in words, with room left for an honest refusal — is
not a formality at all. It is the entire structure a real choice stands
on, and without it, even the kindest-seeming gesture is simply another
version of deciding for someone else what they're allowed to want.

I think about my father, too, on that walk, and the specific way he
managed our whole childhood without ever once asking Sela or me
directly what we needed, assuming, always, that his own careful
provision was sufficient, that the asking itself would only burden two
children already dealing with enough. I understand, now, watching Isla
insist on the plain question every single time, that he was wrong about
that, kindly, lovingly wrong, but wrong all the same — that even a
child, even someone frightened or grieving or uncertain, deserves to be
asked rather than simply provided for.

I resolve, walking the last stretch toward the orchard gate, to ask
Sela properly, that same day, whether there's anything she actually
needs from me that I have simply been assuming, all these years,
wasn't worth the vulnerability of the asking. It feels, deciding this,
like a small thing. I suspect, by the specific weight it carries in my
own chest, that it is considerably larger than it currently feels.

·

I find Isla in the cottage's small garden, later that same evening,
attempting, with more determination than success, to salvage a patch
of winter herbs that the season's cold has been steadily overtaking.

"You don't strike me as a gardener," I observe, watching her fight with
a particularly stubborn root.

"I'm not," she admits. "Ossian kept this patch for the whole of my
apprenticeship. I've let it go to ruin these past few years, mostly out
of neglect rather than any decision. I thought, today, that I might
try to save what's left of it, before the ground freezes too hard to
manage anything at all."

I kneel down beside her and take over the stubborn root, working it
free with the particular patience grafting has taught me for anything
that resists being moved.

"Why today, specifically?" I ask.

"Because Sela's grain-house made me think about what gets tended and
what gets neglected, in a life," Isla says. "I've tended this whole
line for six years, meticulously. I let this small patch of herbs go
to ruin without a second thought, because it never once occurred to me
that a person's own garden might deserve the same care as forty miles
of cairns."

"That's a very particular kind of imbalance."

"It's the same imbalance we've both been examining, in different
shapes, all winter," Isla says. "Tending everyone else's needs
meticulously while letting our own simply wither, unattended, because
attending to them felt like an indulgence neither of us had properly
earned."

We work the small garden together for the rest of the evening,
salvaging what we can of the herbs, and I find, doing it, a particular
satisfaction in tending something this small and unimportant, by any
official measure, simply because it matters to the person kneeling
beside me in the cold.

·

Tomas discovers the salvaged garden the next morning, on his way to
deliver the day's reports, and stops, genuinely surprised, to examine
the neat, if modest, rows we managed to save.

"You gardened," he says, to Isla, in a tone that suggests this ranks
among the more unexpected developments of the whole winter.

"We gardened," Isla corrects, though I notice she doesn't seem
particularly bothered by his surprise.

"I didn't know either of you had it in you," Tomas says, examining the
herbs with genuine curiosity. "I assumed, given how thoroughly this
patch had gone to ruin, that gardening simply wasn't a skill either of
you possessed."

"It wasn't," I admit. "I'm considerably better with an orchard than
with delicate herbs. Isla claims no skill at all. We managed anyway,
mostly through stubbornness rather than any particular expertise."

"I'd like to help, if you'll let me," Tomas says, surprising both of
us. "My mother kept bees, but she had a proper garden too, before —"
He stops, the old grief briefly crossing his face before he manages it
back into composure. "I remember some of what she taught me. I think
I'd like to put it to use again, if it's welcome."

"It's entirely welcome," Isla says, gently, understanding, I think,
exactly what this small offer actually costs him to make.

We spend a portion of that same afternoon with Tomas properly teaching
us both what little he remembers of his mother's own gardening
wisdom, and I find, watching the three of us kneel together over a
patch of salvaged herbs, that this small, unofficial garden has
become, in the space of two days, considerably more than the sum of
its modest rows.

·

Sela visits the garden herself, a few days later, examining the
salvaged rows with the particular critical eye of a woman who has
spent her whole life properly tending things.

"This is decent work," she says, "for three people who apparently
didn't know a root from a weed a week ago."

"High praise, from you," Isla says.

"I don't give praise I don't mean," Sela says, though the corner of
her mouth suggests she's rather more pleased than her brisk tone
lets on. "I've been meaning to ask — would you mind if I brought some
cuttings over from the orchard's own herb beds? There's a frost-hardy
variety I've had good luck with that might do well alongside what
you've already got established here."

"I'd welcome that," Isla says. "I don't have much practical knowledge
of my own to contribute. I'd rather borrow someone else's than let the
whole patch struggle along on my own limited expertise."

"I'll bring them by tomorrow," Sela says, and lingers a moment longer,
examining the small garden with an expression I can't quite place.

"What is it," I ask.

"Nothing," Sela says, then reconsiders. "It's simply — this is the
first time in longer than I can properly remember that I've watched
you tend something for its own sake, rather than because the line or
the exemption or some other obligation required it of you. I find I
like watching you do it."

"I like doing it," I admit, surprised, saying it, by how true the
words actually are. "I don't think I'd ever properly given myself
permission to tend something just because I wanted to, rather than
because someone needed me to."

"Then keep doing it," Sela says, simply, and turns to head back toward
the orchard, leaving the three of us kneeling together in the small,
salvaged garden, considerably more settled than any of us were a
handful of days ago.

·

Tomas returns that evening with a small clay pot of his own, offered
somewhat shyly to Isla once the day's work is properly finished.

"For the garden," he says. "I found it in the storehouse. I thought
it might suit the herbs better than the cracked one you've been using."

Isla accepts it with a warmth I've come to recognize as entirely
genuine rather than merely polite. "Thank you, Tomas. That's
thoughtful."

"It's a small thing," he says, ducking his head slightly.

"It's not a small thing," Isla says. "It's exactly the kind of small
thing that adds up, over a whole winter, into something considerably
larger than any single gesture accounts for on its own. I've learned
that much, at least, this season."

Tomas seems pleased by this, in the quiet, unshowy way he's pleased by
most things that matter to him, and the three of us finish settling
the garden for the evening together, a small, unofficial family
properly tending something that belongs to all of them equally.

·

I find Isla alone at the garden's edge, later that evening, once
Tomas has gone and the light's nearly failed entirely.

"You've been out here a while," I observe.

"I was thinking about what Sela said," she admits. "About tending
something for its own sake. I don't think I've properly done that,
outside of this small patch, in the whole of my adult life."

"Not even the cairns?"

"The cairns are duty," Isla says. "Necessary, meaningful, but duty all the
same. This—" she gestures at the salvaged rows, barely visible now in the
fading light "—this is simply because I wanted to see something grow
that nobody required of me."

I consider this, kneeling down beside her at the garden's edge.

"I think that's rather important," I say, "learning the difference.
I spent most of my own life treating the orchard the same way you
treated the cairns — necessary, dutiful, a thing I tended because it
required tending, not because I'd properly let myself enjoy the
tending itself."

"Has that changed?"

"I think it's changing," I say. "Slowly. I caught myself,
grafting last week, actually enjoying the specific quiet of the work,
rather than simply pushing through it toward whatever came next."

"I'm glad," Isla says, and we kneel together at the garden's edge a while
longer, in the deepening evening dark, both of us slowly learning to
let ordinary things matter simply because they're ours, rather than
because anyone requires them of either of us.

·

We go inside eventually, once the cold properly settles, and Isla
settles beside me by the fire, turning over the whole shape of the
day's small revelations.

"I want to ask you something," she says, "that I've been circling since
the garden."

"Go on."

"Do you think it's possible to want something purely for its own sake,
the way we've just described wanting the garden — without it eventually
becoming duty too, the moment enough time passes and enough people
start depending on it?"

I consider the question properly, the particular seriousness the
subject deserves.

"I don't know," I admit. "I think duty and desire aren't always as
separate as we'd like them to be. I think the orchard started as
something my father loved purely, and became duty, over years, simply
by being needed. I don't think that transformation is necessarily a
tragedy, though. I think it might simply be what happens to anything
you keep choosing long enough."

"That's a more hopeful reading than I expected."

"I've had a good teacher, this winter, in finding hope in unexpected
places," I say, and Isla leans into me by the fire, and I hold her
there a while longer, grateful for a woman who keeps finding the
courage to ask the harder questions rather than let them sit unspoken.

·

Tomas asks, the following morning, whether he might have his own small
corner of the garden to tend, separate from the shared rows.

"I'd like something that's entirely mine to fail at," he says, "rather
than always tending things properly alongside the two of you."

"That's a fair request," I say. "What would you grow?"

"Something useless," Tomas says, with a small grin. "Purely decorative.
I've spent my whole apprenticeship learning things with practical
purposes. I find I'd like one small thing that serves no purpose
beyond simply being pleasant to look at."

"I think that's exactly the right instinct," I say, and we mark out a
small corner of the garden for his own use that same afternoon, and I
find myself, watching him plan his purely decorative plot with genuine
enthusiasm, grateful for a young man learning, this early, the
considerable value of tending something simply because it pleases him.


## Chapter Twenty-Five — First Intimacy (Isla)

Nothing urgent happens, the following evening, deliberately, by an
agreement neither of us states outright but both of us seem to arrive
at simultaneously — no ledgers, no cairns, no Feld, no numb hand
mentioned once, an evening spent entirely on nothing that requires
either of us to manage anything at all.

Aldric names the fire's shape after a grafting season, an old habit
I've stopped finding strange and started finding, instead, like a
private language I am slowly, carefully, learning to properly read.

"Tell me something that isn't about cost," I say. "Any of it. Something
that's simply yours."

He tells me about the first tree he ever grafted, badly, at eleven
years old, and how his father let it grow crooked rather than cut it
down, on the theory that a mistake you own outright is worth more than
a straight line you never earned. I recognize the phrase — he's used a
version of it before, about the tree near the orchard's northern wall —
and understand, hearing the origin of it, exactly how deep the habit of
quietly absorbing cost runs in him, taught early, by a father who
meant it kindly and could not have known how thoroughly the lesson
would take root.

I fall asleep on the cottage's good chair, that night, for the first
time in longer than I can place, without running a single column of
arithmetic first — simply tired, in the ordinary way, rather than the
specific hollowed exhaustion I have carried since I was twenty-six.

·

The following night is the one neither of us names in advance, though
both of us understand, from the particular quality of the quiet between
us, exactly what it is.

Everything that happens has already been asked for, plainly, in words,
days before, at the cairn field, and again tonight, in the cottage's
own low firelight, one final confirmation offered and received before
either of us moves any closer.

"Still yes," Aldric says, quietly, at the doorway to my own room, which
has become, in these past weeks, considerably less exclusively mine
than it once was.

"Still yes," I say back, and mean it entirely, and let him close the
distance this time, since I closed it first at the cairn field, and it
seems, somehow, correct that the asking and the choosing should take
turns between us rather than always falling to the same hand.

I will not set down, in any ledger, the whole of what passes between us
that night. Some things are not meant for accounting. I will say only
that everything arrives chosen rather than compelled — nothing
borrowed from the bond, nothing bright and involuntary the way the
bond's own sensations sometimes arrive; the bond, for once, stays
entirely quiet and lets the moment belong to the two of us alone,
uncontested by anything the rite ever demanded of either of us.

Aldric is careful with me in the specific way of a man who has spent
this whole arrangement learning that care and cost are not, in fact,
the same instrument, however much his own life has taught him to treat
them as interchangeable. I am careful with him in turn, in the specific
way of a woman learning, for the first time in six years, that
tenderness offered is not automatically tenderness spent at a loss.

Afterward, his good hand finds mine in the dark, and neither of us says
anything further, because for the first time in either of our lives,
nothing needs saying to be true. It is not a cure for anything — not
his hand, not the line, not the whole unresolved arithmetic of what the
rite is still, patiently, taking from both of us. It was never meant to
be a cure. It is, simply, ours: the one thing in this entire strange
season that belongs to no ledger, no crown, no rite-keeper's record at
all.

I lie awake a while afterward, listening to him breathe, steady and
warm beside me, and think, for the first time since my mentor's death,
that I have found something in this arrangement worth considerably
more than the six-year arithmetic I have spent my whole career
performing — not a solution to the cost, but a reason, entirely apart
from any ledger, to keep paying it.

·

Tomas, the following morning, is considerably more transparent about
his own theories than tact would generally recommend.

"You're different today," he announces, over the morning reports,
watching me with the particular scrutiny of a young man conducting his
own private investigation. "Not bad different. Just — different."

"I don't know what you mean."

"I mean," Tomas says, entirely too pleased with himself, "that you
hummed something, walking down the stairs this morning. I have never,
in three years, heard you hum anything at all, and I would like it
noted, for the historical record, that today is the day it finally
happened."

"I don't hum, Tomas."

"You did this morning," he says, with enormous satisfaction. "I'm
choosing not to speculate further about the cause, out of respect for
your privacy, though I want it understood that the restraint I'm
currently exercising is considerable, and should be appreciated
accordingly."

"Your restraint is noted," I say, dryly, "and appreciated exactly as
much as it deserves to be."

"That's very little appreciation."

"It's precisely proportionate," I say, and he laughs, properly,
delighted, and goes back to his reports, and I find myself, watching
him, grateful for a young man whose particular blend of nosiness and
genuine affection has become, somewhere in these past weeks, one of the
steadier comforts of my daily life at Highstone.

Aldric arrives partway through the morning's business, and Tomas, upon
seeing him, makes an elaborate show of suddenly finding his own reports
fascinating, glancing up only once, briefly, to catch my eye with a
look of such theatrical innocence that I have to turn away to keep from
laughing outright in front of him.

"What was that about," Aldric asks, once Tomas has gone to deliver the
morning's inspection notes.

"Tomas has apparently decided I hummed on the stairs this morning," I
say, "and is choosing, with enormous visible effort, not to comment
further on the implications."

"Did you hum?"

"I don't remember doing it," I say, "which I suppose means it's
entirely possible I did."

Aldric smiles at that, the specific unguarded smile I've come to
recognize as entirely his own rather than any managed version of it,
and I find myself, watching him, considerably more certain than I was
even yesterday that whatever this arrangement has become, it is worth
every uncounted cost it has already taken from both of us, and every
one still waiting to be paid.

·

I write to Fenhollow's nearest surviving settlement, that same
afternoon, on the small chance that whoever last had contact with the
lost village before it fell past the line might have preserved some
memory or record worth pursuing, once the current crisis has properly
settled. It is not urgent. I tell myself this several times, drafting
the letter, understanding, even as I write it, that I am doing the
thing Ossian's own journal described so plainly — reaching for a
problem I can solve at a comfortable distance, rather than sitting
properly with the one currently closest to me.

Aldric finds me at the letter, later, and reads it over my shoulder
without asking, the way he's started doing with most of my
correspondence these past weeks.

"You're worrying about a village that's been lost for a decade," he
observes, gently, "the same week you're worrying about me."

"I'm aware," I admit. "I think it's easier, somehow. Fenhollow's loss
already happened. There's nothing left to lose there, only history to
recover. You're still an open question, and I find open questions
considerably harder to sit with than settled ones, however grim."

"That's honest," Aldric says, "and I appreciate the honesty. But I'd
rather have your attention on the open question, if you're able to
spare it. Fenhollow's waited a decade. It can wait a little longer."

I fold the letter, unfinished, and set it aside, understanding that
he's right, and that I have, once again, caught myself reaching for
the safer, more distant problem rather than properly sitting with the
frightening, immediate one directly in front of me.

"Thank you," I say, "for noticing."

"I'm getting good at it," Aldric says, with a small, wry smile, "since
apparently we're both determined to keep catching each other at
exactly this particular habit, all winter long."

·

Sela visits later that same week, ostensibly to check on the garden's
progress, and finds the three of us — Isla, Tomas, and myself —
properly absorbed in the day's small domestic business, considerably
more relaxed than she seems to have expected.

"You all look remarkably settled," she observes, "for a household
currently facing down an unproven rite and a crown deadline."

"We've decided," Isla says, "that facing it down doesn't require
facing it grimly, every single hour. Some hours, we simply garden."

"That's a healthier philosophy than most of what I've watched this
family practice, historically," Sela says, with a small, private smile
that suggests she's including herself in that assessment. "I approve.
I'd like to join the gardening hours, if there's room for one more."

"There's always room," I say, and mean it, and the four of us spend
the rest of that particular afternoon in the small salvaged garden,
finding, in the ordinary work of tending something small and living,
a considerable measure of peace that none of us quite expected this
particular winter to offer.

"I want to say something," Sela says, later, once Tomas has gone back
to Highstone and it's just the three of us remaining. "I've watched
this family — the two of you, and now Tomas too, in his own way —
build something out of this crisis that I don't think any of you
expected to find in the middle of it. I'm proud of all of you. I
wanted that said properly, out loud, rather than simply felt privately
and never spoken."

None of us have a ready response to that, so we simply sit together in
the garden's quiet a while longer, considerably more grateful, all of
us, for the specific found family this strange winter has assembled
around us.

·

Aldric finds me alone in the cottage that evening, once Sela and Tomas
have both gone their separate ways, and settles beside me at the
kitchen table with the particular unhurried quiet of a man who has
nowhere else he'd rather be.

"You've been thinking about something all afternoon," he observes.
"You went quiet, twice, in the garden, in a way that wasn't simply
enjoying the work."

"I've been thinking about my mother," I admit, surprised, saying it,
by how readily the admission arrives. "Watching Sela today, fussing
over the herbs the way she fusses over everyone she loves, I kept
thinking about a blue shawl I haven't properly thought about in years."

"Tell me," Aldric says, simply, and I do — the ordinary, domestic
shape of her, dead when I was seven, a woman who baked bread and wore
a blue shawl and was, by every account I've ever gathered since, no
wardwright at all, simply someone's mother, loved plainly, missed
plainly, the specific ordinary grief of losing a parent young rather
than any larger, rite-shaped tragedy.

"I don't think I've ever told anyone the shawl detail," I say, once
I've finished. "I'm not certain why I'm telling you now."

"Because you finally have someone worth telling," Aldric says, "the
same reason I finally showed you the crooked tree."
