"I think that's exactly it," I say, and let him hold me a while
longer in the kitchen's low light, grateful for an evening spent
tending nothing more urgent than a small, ordinary grief finally given
proper room to breathe.

·

Tomas finds the two of us there, later, having come by with a final
report he could easily have left until morning, and I understand,
watching him hesitate at the door, that he's noticed the particular
quality of the evening and is deciding whether to intrude on it.

"I can come back," he offers.

"Stay," I say. "We were only talking about my mother. There's room for
one more."

He settles in, cautious at first, and I find myself, without quite
deciding to, telling him the same fragments I've just given Aldric —
the shawl, the bread, the voice I can no longer properly recall.

"I didn't know you had a mother," Tomas says, then catches himself.
"That's a foolish thing to say. Of course you had a mother. I mean I
didn't know you'd lost her so young."

"I don't talk about her," I say. "I'm not entirely sure why tonight's
different."

"Maybe because you finally have people worth telling," Tomas says,
echoing, without knowing it, almost exactly what Aldric said to me
not an hour before.

"That's precisely what he said," I tell him, and Tomas looks pleased
in the specific unguarded way of a young man discovering he's arrived,
independently, at the same wisdom as someone he admires.

We sit together a while longer, the three of us, and I find myself,
watching the fire burn low, grateful for a found family willing to
hold even the smallest, oldest griefs alongside the larger, more urgent
ones this whole winter has demanded of all of us.

·

Tomas asks, before he leaves that evening, whether I'd be willing to
tell him more, someday, about the years between my mother's death and
my apprenticeship under Ossian — the gap neither of us has ever
properly discussed.

"There isn't much to tell," I admit. "A relative's household, mostly,
that took me in out of obligation rather than genuine warmth. I don't
think about those years often. I've found it easier not to."

"You don't have to tell me now," Tomas says, gently. "I only wanted
you to know I'd like to hear it, eventually, whenever you're ready.
The same way you've made room to hear mine."

"I appreciate that," I say, surprised by how much the offer settles
something in my chest. "I think I will, eventually. Not tonight. But
eventually."

"Eventually is enough," Tomas says, echoing, without quite realizing
it, the exact phrase Aldric's used with me more than once this season,
and I find myself, watching him gather his things for the walk home,
grateful for an apprentice patient enough to leave a door open rather
than push it, trusting that I'll walk through it in my own time.

·

Aldric asks, once Tomas has gone, whether the gap years are something
he might eventually hear about too.

"You already know most of it, I think," I say. "The relative's
household. The obligation rather than warmth. I don't think there's a
version of the story that surprises you, hearing it properly."

"I'd still like to hear it properly," Aldric says, "whenever you're
ready, the same as I'd like Tomas to hear it eventually. Not because I
expect it to surprise me. Because I think some things deserve to be
said aloud, regardless of whether the listener already suspects the
shape of them."

"That's a fair point," I say, and find myself, considering it, closer
to ready than I expected to be. "Perhaps not tonight. But soon."

"Soon is enough," Aldric says, and we sit together by the fire a while
longer, both of us patient with the pace of a story that's taken
considerably longer to tell than the years it actually covers.

·

I find myself, that same night, turning over exactly why the telling
feels so much less frightening than it did even a month ago — not
because the years themselves have changed, but because I've finally
let someone stand close enough to hear them without flinching.

"You're thinking loudly again," Aldric observes, watching my face in
the firelight.

"I'm thinking about how much easier this all feels, lately," I admit.
"Telling things. I used to rehearse every difficult sentence for days
before I could manage to say it aloud. I don't think I rehearsed
tonight's conversation with Tomas at all."

"That's progress," Aldric says, "considerable progress, actually,
though I understand if it doesn't feel that dramatic from the inside."

"It doesn't," I admit, "though I believe you that it is."

"I'll keep believing it for both of us," Aldric says, "until you catch
up to believing it yourself," and we sit together a while longer,
grateful for the specific quiet ease that's grown between us where
frightened rehearsal used to live.


## Chapter Twenty-Six — Morning, Unhidden (Aldric)

Morning finds us unhurried, for once — the specific rare luxury of
neither of us having anywhere urgent to be, the fire built back up
early, tea brewing, the whole cottage quiet in a way that feels,
finally, like something closer to peace than to a truce between two
people managing an arrangement neither chose, the frost outside the
window catching the very first proper light of day.

I watch Isla move through the small kitchen with an ease I've come to
treasure these past weeks — humming, faintly, something tuneless she
doesn't seem to notice she's doing, the particular unguarded quiet of
a woman who has, somewhere in this arrangement, stopped performing
competence and started simply living inside her own ordinary morning.
I think, watching her, that whatever this winter still has waiting for
either of us, I would not trade this exact quiet hour for any version
of my life that didn't include it.

I reach for the cup, and my whole left hand simply does not close.

Not the partial failure I've been managing for weeks now, two fingers
gone, a third going — the entire hand, all at once, refusing to answer
at all, and the cup falls, breaks against the hearthstone a second
time, and I look at my own hand the way you look at a stranger's,
uncomprehending, before the specific cold horror of what's just
happened properly arrives.

Isla is already moving before either of us has decided anything.

"How long has it been the whole hand," she says, and there is no room
in her voice, this time, for me to manage the answer smaller than it
actually is.

"A week," I admit, finally, all of it, the whole truth I have been
managing in careful pieces for considerably longer than either of us
has properly acknowledged. "Maybe longer. I stopped counting precisely
because counting wasn't going to change the number, and I didn't want
to watch the number get worse in real time."

"You promised me there wouldn't be a second time," Isla says, and I
understand, hearing the specific quality of hurt underneath the anger,
that I have broken something considerably more fragile than a simple
agreement about honesty.

The hurt lands harder than any anger could have, precisely because I
recognize it — the same wounded disbelief I felt, once, discovering my
own father had hidden the true state of his health from Sela and me
until there was no longer any hiding it left to do.

"I know," I say. "I'm sorry. I kept telling myself I'd say something
once I was certain how bad it actually was, and the certainty kept
arriving in worse increments than I was prepared to hand you."

Isla sits with me on the cold floor, among the second broken cup, and I
watch her do the arithmetic I've been avoiding doing plainly, in front
of her, for a full week — the actual, undeniable shape of how much time
we might genuinely have left, if the numbness has taken the whole hand
this quickly, following whatever pattern it's been following since the
Long Reach cairn cracked something in me that hasn't properly healed
since.

I watch her face do the specific thing it does when she's converted a
fear into a task — the fastest way, I've learned, that she survives
anything genuinely frightening, turning terror into an arithmetic
problem she can properly work rather than simply feel.

"I need the truth now," Isla says. "The whole of it. Not managed. Not
softened for my sake. What does this actually mean, for how much time
we have to make the mutual rite work?"

"I don't know precisely," I say, honestly, because I have decided,
watching her face, that further management costs more now than the
truth does, however frightening the truth turns out to be. "I know
it's considerably less time than either of us was budgeting for a
fortnight ago. I know the numbness is spreading faster than the record
from Calder's Reach gives us any comfortable margin to account for."

Isla doesn't cry, exactly, though something in her face does something
close to it — the specific controlled devastation of a woman who has
spent her whole life managing grief before it fully arrives, watching
this particular grief refuse, entirely, to wait for her permission.

"Whatever time we thought we had," she says, finally, "it was never as
much as I was budgeting for either."

I do not have a comforting answer to that, because there isn't one
available, honestly, and I have decided, this morning, sitting on a
cold floor with a hand that no longer properly answers to me, that
honesty is the only currency either of us can afford to keep spending,
regardless of how much it costs either one of us to hand over.

"We go to Ossory," I say. "Today. We stop managing this quietly and we
put it in front of someone who can actually help us test whether the
mutual rite holds, before either of us runs out of the time we'd need
to find out properly."

"Today," Isla agrees, and helps me up off the cold floor, and I feel,
standing beside her, the specific, undeniable understanding that
whatever time we thought we had to manage this carefully has just, this
morning, run out entirely.

I do not, standing there with the broken cup's pieces still scattered
around both our feet, feel afraid in the way I expected fear to feel,
imagining this exact moment in whatever quiet corner of my mind has
been dreading it since the numbness first began. I feel, instead,
something closer to relief — the specific, unburdening relief of a
man who has finally run out of road for managing a thing alone, and
finds, arriving at the end of that road, that the company waiting
there is worth more than the managing ever was.

·

We stop at the orchard on the way to Ossory's hall, because I am not
willing to let Sela hear about the whole hand secondhand, from
whichever version of the story reaches her through the village's own
particular grapevine, and because I find, standing at her door, that I
want my sister to hear this from me directly, the way I should have let
her hear everything else about this whole arrangement from the start.

She takes one look at my face and knows, before I've said a word,
that whatever's happened is considerably worse than anything I've told
her so far.

"Show me," she says, and I do, and she goes very still, the specific
stillness of a woman absorbing a fact she has clearly been dreading
since the first time I mentioned the numbness at all.

"How long," she asks.

"Weeks, the healer thinks. Possibly fewer."

Sela sits down hard on the grain-house step, and for a moment I think
she might simply refuse to hear any more of it, the way our father used
to refuse to hear bad news until he'd had time to properly compose
himself first. She doesn't. She looks up at me, and at Isla standing
beside me, and asks the plain question, in the specific direct register
she's clearly decided, somewhere in the last several breaths, to adopt
permanently.

"What do you need from me. Right now. Not eventually. Today."

"I need you to trust that Isla and I are handling this properly," I
say, "and I need you to let us handle it, rather than trying to solve
it yourself the way you tried to solve the grain shortfall alone, two
winters back."

Sela's mouth tightens, recognizing the echo, and I watch her decide,
visibly, to accept the correction rather than argue against it.

"Alright," she says. "I'll trust it. But I want to be told everything,
from now on, as it happens, not gathered secondhand or handed to me
only once you've already decided how much I'm allowed to worry about
it."

"Agreed," I say, and mean it, understanding that I owe her this much,
at minimum, after a lifetime of deciding, unilaterally, exactly how
much of my own difficulty she was permitted to carry alongside me.

I think, watching her absorb the news with more steadiness than I have
any right to expect from her, about every other time I've handed her a
crisis already halfway managed, already softened, already decided —
and understand, properly, for perhaps the first time, exactly how much
that particular habit has cost her, over the years, in ways I never
once thought to ask about.

She hugs me, fiercely, before we leave for Ossory's hall, the kind of
embrace we haven't properly exchanged since before our mother died, and
I walk the rest of the way to Highstone considerably steadied by it,
understanding that whatever the day ahead demands of either Isla or me,
I am, for the first time in longer than I can properly place, not
facing it with my whole family kept carefully, quietly, at arm's
length.

I think, walking, about all the other mornings I might have let her
this close, over the years, and didn't — every hard season managed
quietly, every difficulty absorbed alone rather than properly shared —
and understand, with a clarity that costs something to hold, exactly
how much unnecessary distance I built into this family simply by
believing distance was the same thing as strength.

·

Sela insists on sending word to Tomas herself, rather than leaving that
particular task to us, and catches up with us halfway down the track,
having apparently run the whole distance to say one further thing
before we reach Highstone properly.

"I want you both to know something," she says, slightly breathless,
"before whatever happens in that hall today. Whatever Ossory decides,
however this turns out — I don't regret a single day of the two winters
your exemption cost this family. I want that said plainly, in case
either of you has spent any part of today quietly believing this
crisis is somehow a debt you owe for choices made back then."

"I hadn't thought of it that way," I admit, though I understand,
hearing her say it, that some quiet part of me had, in fact, been
carrying exactly that unspoken arithmetic.

"I know you hadn't consciously," Sela says. "I know you well enough to
suspect it was there anyway, underneath. I wanted to say it before it
had the chance to take root any further."

"Thank you," I say, and mean it, understanding, not for the first
time this winter, exactly how much my sister sees in me that I fail to
see in myself.

She turns back toward the orchard, and Isla and I continue on toward
Highstone, considerably lighter, both of us, for Sela's plain,
unmanaged reassurance.

·

We walk the rest of the way in a silence that isn't quite comfortable,
both of us turning over, privately, the specific weight of what's
waiting at Highstone, and I find myself watching Isla's face for
whatever sign might tell me how frightened she actually is underneath
the composed, purposeful stride she's kept up since the cup broke this
morning.

"You've gone quiet," I say, eventually.

"I'm rehearsing," she admits. "What I'm going to say to Ossory. I've
said the actual words to you, once, in the dark, and to no one else in
six years. I find the prospect of saying them to someone with the
standing to act on them considerably more frightening than I expected
it to be."

"Would it help to say them to me again first? A rehearsal before the
rehearsal."

She considers this, walking, and I watch her decide, visibly, that it
might.

"The line's oldest form wasn't broken," she says, quietly, testing the
words the way you test ice before trusting your full weight to it.
"It was corrupted, deliberately, by rite-keepers who found it easier
to spend one hearth than share the cost between two wardwrights loudly.
I have a record. I have proof it worked, once, before it was buried.
I want to attempt it, properly, before the numbness in your hand
finishes what the corrupted form has been quietly doing to this whole
line for generations."

"That's clearer than anything I could have said myself," I tell her,
"and considerably braver." I mean it entirely, watching her walk
beside me with the words finally settled properly in her own mouth,
considerably steadier for having said them once already, out loud, to
someone who already believes them.

"It's easier to say to you," Isla admits. "You already believe it.
Ossory hasn't decided anything yet, and I don't know which way he's
going to fall once I've said it in that hall, in front of witnesses,
where the saying actually matters."

"Whatever way he falls," I say, "you'll have said it plainly, which is
already more than most people manage with something this frightening.
I'll be standing right beside you for the whole of it."

"I know," Isla says, and something in her shoulders eases, slightly,
hearing it stated so plainly. "I think that's the only reason I'm
still walking toward that hall rather than turning back toward the
cottage to rehearse it another dozen times first."

We walk the last stretch to Highstone together, her rehearsed words
settling somewhere steadier in her chest than they were a few minutes
ago, and I understand, watching her, that whatever Ossory ultimately
decides, she has already done the harder part — deciding, finally, to
say the true thing out loud, to someone who isn't already convinced of
it.

·

We pass the small market stall near Highstone's gate on the way in,
and Reny Ashe catches sight of us both, setting down whatever she's
currently wrapping to properly look at Isla's face.

"Something's different today," Reny observes, not quite a question.

"We found something," Isla says, careful not to say more than that,
"at Calder's Reach. I'm taking it to Ossory now."

"Good," Reny says, with her usual finality. "I've watched this
wardwright hold this line practically alone for six years. I'd like to
think whatever you've found finally changes that particular
arithmetic."

"So would I," Isla says, and Reny presses a fresh twist of herbs into
her hand, unbidden, the same small ritual she's performed since the
night of the binding.

"For steadying the nerves," Reny says, "whatever you're about to walk
in and say. I don't need to know what it is to recognize the specific
look of someone about to say a frightening true thing to someone who
might not want to hear it."

"Thank you," Isla says, and we continue on toward the gate, the herbs
tucked away alongside everything else this whole strange season has
handed her to carry, and I find myself, watching her steady herself
against the market stall's small kindness, considerably more hopeful
about the day ahead than I was a few minutes before.

·

Isla does not take me to Ossory's hall directly, in the end, once we
leave the orchard — she stops short at the record-room instead, and I
understand, watching her hesitate at the door, exactly what's giving
her pause.

"I need a moment before I say this to him," she says. "What I've been
circling since Calder's Reach. I haven't said it plainly to anyone but
you, not even Ossory, and I find I need to be certain of the words
before I hand them to someone with the standing to act on them."

"Take the moment," I say. "I'll wait."

She stands at the door a long while, gathering whatever nerve the
saying requires, and I watch her, understanding that whatever she's
about to walk in and tell Ossory, she has been holding it, carefully,
privately, exactly the way she holds everything she isn't yet certain
enough to spend.

"Whatever happens in there," I say, "I'm grateful you found it at all.
Regardless of what Ossory decides."

"So am I," Isla says, and finally opens the door, and I follow her in,
understanding that whatever comes next is no longer a private theory
between the two of us, but about to become, for the first time, a
question put to someone else entirely.


## Chapter Twenty-Seven — The Mutual Form (Isla)

Ossory listens to the whole of it — the numb hand, the record from
Calder's Reach, the specific arithmetic of a season we no longer have
time to run cautiously — in the record-room at Highstone, with the
particular gravity of a man who has spent considerably longer than I
have watching this line try, and sometimes fail, to hold.

"You want to attempt an unproven rite," he says, when I've finished,
"on the strength of one dead wardwright's private record, with a hearth
already this far gone. Do you understand what you're asking me to
sanction, Wardwright?"

"I understand precisely what happens if we don't attempt it," I say.
"I've watched the alternative up close, for six years. I know its
shape considerably better than I know the shape of what I'm proposing
instead."

I lay the predecessor's record beside my own training, side by side on
the table, and say, finally, out loud, the thing I have been circling
since Calder's Reach, unwilling to voice it until I had proof enough to
survive the saying.

"The rite I was taught takes from one hearth alone," I say. "There's an
older form. It shares the cost between both of us, instead of spending
only him. I believe it's genuine. I believe it's survivable, properly
attempted. I am not certain of either, which is why I haven't said this
plainly until today, and why I am saying it now, plainly, in front of
you, rather than attempting it quietly and reporting only the outcome."

Saying it changes the room. I feel it change — the specific settling
of a discovery finally spoken where it can be tested against the
world, rather than merely turned over privately, alone, in the dark.

"Why has no one used it in generations," Ossory asks, careful, already,
I suspect, guessing at an answer he does not want confirmed.

"Because sharing the cost means the wardwright pays too," I say. "And
every generation of rite-keepers since has found it considerably
easier to spend one hearth quietly than risk two wardwrights loudly."

Aldric goes very still beside me, hearing that, and I understand,
watching him, exactly what he's registering: that I am proposing to
spend myself for him precisely as visibly, precisely as completely, as
he has spent himself, silently, for everyone in his own life since he
was fourteen years old.

"You'd take that cost onto yourself," Aldric says, quietly, "knowingly.
Openly."

"Yes," I say. "That's rather the whole point of the mutual form. Cost
shared, rather than concentrated into a single hearth until it kills
him. I'm not offering you a rescue that costs me nothing. I'm offering
you the only version of this rite that has any chance, at all, of
letting both of us survive the season."

Ossory looks between the two of us for a long moment, weighing
considerably more than the record on the table.

"I'll need to see the full text properly translated," he says,
finally, "and I'll need the crown's ward-commissioner informed, whether
either of you would prefer that or not, because an attempt this
significant cannot be made entirely off the crown's record without
inviting considerably worse consequences than Feld's usual
bureaucratic tedium."

"Understood," I say.

"I am not sanctioning an attempt tonight," Ossory adds, "or this week.
I want the translation checked, and I want time to consider what
you're actually asking this line's whole rite-keeping tradition to
risk. But I will not, this time, simply dismiss it because it's
inconvenient to the crown's preferred arithmetic. That much, I think,
you've earned, bringing it to me plainly rather than attempting it in
secret."

It is not, I understand, walking out of the record-room afterward, a
victory. It is not even, strictly, permission. But it is the first
time, in the whole of my career, that I have said the true, difficult
thing out loud, to someone with the standing to act on it, rather than
managing it quietly, alone, in the dark, and finding that the saying
itself has changed something I did not expect it to change.

"Thank you," Aldric says, once we're alone in the corridor, "for
saying it plainly. I know what it cost you, offering yourself that
visibly."

"I've spent six years offering myself invisibly instead," I say. "I
find I'd rather be seen doing it, if the alternative is watching you
die quietly while I keep careful, insufficient account of the reasons
why."

·

Tomas catches up with us in the corridor outside the record-room,
having clearly been listening at the door for at least part of the
conversation, and does not bother pretending otherwise.

"You're actually going to attempt it," he says, something between awe
and terror in his voice. "The mutual form."

"If Ossory sanctions a proper attempt, once the translation's checked.
Yes."

"I want to help," Tomas says, immediately, with the specific
determination of a young man who has spent weeks feeling useless in the
face of a crisis considerably larger than his own training has
prepared him for. "Not the rite itself — I know I'm not ready for that.
But the translation, the preparation, whatever needs doing before
Ossory's satisfied it's safe to attempt."

"You can help," I say. "I'd welcome it, actually. Cross-checking my own
translation against the fragments in the record box, making certain I
haven't let my own hope color the reading in places where the ink's
gone unclear."

"I won't let hope color it," Tomas says, with the particular gravity
of someone taking an assignment considerably more seriously than the
assignment, on its surface, might warrant. "I'll be as rigorous as you
taught me to be."

"I know you will," I say, and mean it, understanding that handing him
this task is itself a kind of gift — a way of letting him participate
properly in the thing that will determine both his own future and the
future of everyone he cares about, rather than simply waiting, helpless,
on the sidelines of a decision made entirely by the adults around him.

We spend the rest of that afternoon with the record box spread across
the workshop table, the three of us — Tomas, Aldric, and I — checking
every fragment of the translation against each other, arguing, gently,
over a handful of ambiguous phrases, building, together, a version of
the rite's words that all three of us can properly stand behind before
Ossory sees a single line of it.

It is, I think, watching Tomas argue a fine point of grammar with
Aldric over a passage neither of them fully agrees on, the first time
this entire winter that the whole weight of the mutual rite has felt
like something we're building together, rather than something I am
carrying alone and simply reporting on to everyone else.

·

Ossory does not answer immediately, once I've finished laying the whole
of it out — the record, the translation, the plain admission of what
I'm proposing. He sits with the silence for a long while, turning the
predecessor's own pages over in his hands, and I understand, watching
him, that I have just handed him something he is going to need
considerably more than an afternoon to properly weigh.

"I will not pretend this is a small thing to decide," he says, finally.
"I need time to verify the translation properly, against whatever
fragmentary records I can find elsewhere. I need to consider what
sanctioning an unproven form means for every future binding on this
line, not merely the one currently in front of me."

"I understand," I say, though the words cost me, given how little time
Aldric's own condition affords for anyone's proper deliberation.

"I am not refusing you," Ossory adds, seeing my face. "I am telling you
that I intend to take this seriously, which means I cannot decide it
carelessly, however urgent your own timeline has become. Give me what
time you can afford, Wardwright. I promise you I will not waste a single
day of it."

I leave the record-room with considerably less certainty than I'd
hoped for, and considerably more than I feared — not a sanction, not
yet, but a genuine hearing, properly weighed, from a man whose careful
formality has never once, in six years, struck me as cover for
indifference.

·

Aldric is waiting outside, and I tell him the whole of the exchange,
watching him absorb it with the particular steadiness he's shown more
and more, these past weeks, toward news that would once have frightened
him into managing it silently.

"He didn't refuse," Aldric says, turning the outcome over.

"No. He didn't refuse. He also didn't sanction, which I understand,
intellectually, is the responsible position for a man in his role to
take. I find myself frustrated by it anyway."

"You're allowed to be frustrated," Aldric says, "even by a reasonable
caution. I don't think the two things cancel each other out."

"I know," I say. "I think I wanted, somewhere beneath the reasonable
part of me, for him to simply say yes, immediately, and let us begin
tomorrow. I understand why he can't. I'm frustrated regardless."

"For what it's worth," Aldric says, "I think you did exactly what
needed doing. You said it plainly, finally, to someone with the
standing to act on it. Whatever he decides, however long it takes him
to decide it, you've done the part that was actually yours to do."

I lean into him, there in the corridor outside Ossory's record-room,
and let myself, for a moment, simply be held rather than managing the
next step immediately, understanding that the waiting ahead is going
to require considerably more patience than either of us currently
feels equipped to offer.

·

Sela finds us still in the corridor, some while later, having come to
Highstone on an errand of her own and stumbling, by apparent accident,
onto the tail end of the day's much larger news.

"Something's happened," she says, reading our faces plainly. "Tell me."

I tell her, the whole of it — the numb hand's full extent, the record
from Calder's Reach, the mutual form finally spoken aloud to Ossory —
and watch her absorb each piece with the specific gravity of a woman
who has spent this entire winter bracing for exactly this scale of
news.

"You're proposing to spend yourself," she says, to me, once I've
finished, "the same way he's already spending himself. Knowingly."

"Yes," I say.

Sela is quiet a long moment, and I understand, watching her, that she
is weighing something considerably larger than simple approval or
disapproval.

"I don't know whether to thank you or be frightened of you," she says,
finally, with the particular dry honesty I've come to expect from her.
"I think, on balance, I'm going to do both. Thank you, for offering
what nobody else in this family has ever offered him. And be
frightened, because I don't think either of you fully knows yet what
it's actually going to cost."

"Neither do I," I admit. "I don't think anyone alive knows precisely
what it costs. That's rather the whole difficulty of proposing an
unproven rite."

"Then I suppose we're all simply going to have to be frightened
together," Sela says, "rather than each of us managing our own private
version of it separately, the way this family's always preferred to
do things." She looks between the two of us. "For what it's worth, I
trust you. Both of you. I don't think I'd have said that, plainly, a
month ago. I'm saying it now."

"Thank you," Aldric says, quietly, and I watch brother and sister share
a look that carries considerably more warmth than either of them
generally lets show in front of others, and I understand, standing
with them in the corridor, that whatever the mutual form eventually
costs any of us, it has already, in the asking, begun mending
something in this family that has been quietly fraying for
considerably longer than this particular winter accounts for.

·

Sela walks with us partway back to the cottage, unwilling, I think, to
let the evening end without saying one further thing that's clearly
been sitting with her since the corridor.

"I want to help properly," she says, "with whatever the mutual form
ends up requiring. Not simply moral support from the sidelines.
Something actually useful."

"What did you have in mind," I ask.

"I don't know the rite," Sela says, "but I know this family's finances,
and I know the crown's own particular weaknesses better than either of
you, having spent years managing our exemption paperwork against
exactly this kind of institutional resistance. If Ossory needs
convincing, or Feld needs managing, I'd like to be the one handling
that portion of it, so neither of you has to split your attention
between the rite itself and the crown's own bureaucracy."

"That's a considerable offer," Aldric says.

"It's not entirely selfless," Sela admits. "I've spent this whole
crisis feeling useless, watching from the orchard while the two of you
carry the actual weight of it. I'd like something to actually do,
rather than simply worry from a comfortable distance."

"I'd welcome that," I say, "more than you know. I think Ossory takes
the whole matter more seriously with a family's practical voice in the
room, alongside the wardwright's own argument."

"Then it's settled," Sela says, with the particular satisfaction of a
woman who has just been handed a genuine role rather than a comforting
platitude, and the three of us continue on toward the cottage together,
each of us now carrying a specific piece of the work ahead, rather
than one person shouldering the whole of it while the others simply
watch.

·

We stop, on the way, at the small grain-house where Tomas has taken to
waiting when he's finished his own evening reports, and find him
already asleep at the table, exhausted past the point of properly
making it back to his own quarters.

"He's been working himself considerably too hard," Sela observes,
gently.

"He wanted to be useful," I say, "in whatever way he could manage. I
don't think either of us has properly told him it's alright to rest
too, alongside all the effort."

"Then let's tell him," Sela says, and we wake him gently, and I watch
her fuss over him with the same practical, unshowy care she extends
her own brother, insisting he come back to the cottage for a proper
night's rest rather than sleep sitting upright at a grain-house table.

"I didn't mean to fall asleep here," Tomas says, embarrassed, gathering
his things.

"You're allowed to rest," I say. "Properly. You've done everything
that could reasonably be asked of you today, and considerably more
besides."

"I keep forgetting that," Tomas admits, "that rest is allowed, not
simply a failure to keep working hard enough."

"We're all still learning that lesson," Sela says, "at every age, apparently,"
and the four of us walk the rest of the way together, considerably
more settled for the small, necessary reminder that even the most
willing hands need proper rest to keep being useful.

·

Tomas walks beside Sela for the final stretch, still visibly
embarrassed about being caught asleep at his post, and I catch the two
of them talking quietly, easily, in a register that tells me their own
friendship has grown into something considerably warmer than simple
acquaintance.

"They get along well," Aldric observes, to me, walking a few paces behind.

"They do," I agree. "I've noticed it building for weeks now."

"Do you think it's more than friendship," he asks, "or am I simply
projecting my own hopeful mood onto two people who are, in fact, simply
friendly?"

"I don't know," I say, considering it properly. "I think it might
be more, eventually, if it isn't already becoming that. I think we
should let them find their own pace with it, though, rather than
speculating too loudly where either of them might overhear."

"Fair," Aldric agrees, and we walk the rest of the way in comfortable quiet,
both of us quietly hopeful about a small, tender possibility neither
of us intends to rush.


## Chapter Twenty-Eight — Matching Secrets (Aldric)

I ask her the plain question that night, turning the mutual rite over
in my head long after the cottage has gone quiet: if the wardwright
pays too, under the older form, what has Isla already been paying,
alone, that the record doesn't show.

I have been circling the question for two days now, ever since Tomas
first read the translated passage aloud in the record-room, unwilling
to ask it directly until I felt certain I could hear whatever answer
came without flinching.

It is, I realize, saying it, exactly the shape of question she asked me
at the Long Reach cairn, the night I finally showed her my hand. She is
owed the same answer she demanded of me, offered with the same
directness, in the same low firelight, at the same late hour when the
day's ordinary business has finally quieted enough to make room for it.

"Two years," Isla says, quietly, after a long silence, in the specific
tone of a woman finally setting down a weight she has carried so long
she has half forgotten it was ever meant to be shared. "I've been
feeding the failing wards my own heat, outside the rite proper, since
before Ossian's death made me the only practitioner left to do it.
It isn't in any ledger. I didn't want it to be."

I don't look surprised, hearing it, because I am not, in fact,
surprised — I have suspected some version of this since Hollow Farm,
since the byre thawed at a cost she refused to log anywhere.

"I wondered," I say. "You told me, the first week I knew you, that you
run cold on purpose. I understood it as a manner of speaking. I didn't
understand what it actually cost you until just now, hearing the whole
shape of it."

"It's not the same cost as yours," Isla says. "It's slower. Less
visible. I haven't lost feeling in anything, the way you have. But it's
been drawing on something all the same, for two years, and I don't
entirely know, any more than you know about your own hand, how much
longer I could have kept doing it before the arithmetic caught up with
me too."

"Why didn't you tell me," I ask, "once we'd agreed neither of us would
manage things quietly anymore?"

"Because I was going to," Isla says, "the same night you finally showed
me your hand. And then the hand was so much worse than either of us
expected, and I let the urgency of your secret excuse me from telling
mine, for a while longer than I should have. I'm telling you now
because I don't think either of us can afford, going forward, to let
one secret hide behind the other's urgency."

We sit with the matching shape of it a long while — two people who have
each spent themselves in silence and called it discipline, one for two
years, one considerably longer if you count the whole of his adult
life, each convinced, privately, that carrying the cost alone was the
kinder, more responsible choice.

"We're going to have to actually watch each other," I say, eventually.
"Properly. Not just feel what the bond hands us secondhand. Actually
ask, regularly, whether the other one is managing something quietly
again."

"I know," Isla says. "I'm not certain either of us is very good at
being watched that closely. I know I'm not."

"Neither am I," I admit. "But I'd rather be bad at it together than
good at hiding things separately. I've had considerable practice at the
second one. I don't think it's served either of us particularly well."

Isla laughs, once, low, surprised out of her the way my own laugh
surprised me the night of the kiss, and something in the room settles
— not resolved, not fixed, but shared, finally, in a way neither
secret managed to be on its own.

"For what it's worth," she says, "I don't think you're the only one
who's going to have to relearn how to do this. I've spent six years
believing that needing anyone was a debt I could never repay evenly. I
suspect unlearning that is going to take longer than one honest
conversation on a cold night."

"We have time," I say, and understand, even saying it, how much of a
hope that sentence currently is rather than a fact either of us can
properly guarantee — but I say it anyway, because some hopes are worth
stating plainly, even uncertain, especially to someone who has spent
her whole life refusing herself the comfort of stating them at all.

·

I go to see Cera Voss myself, the following day, curious what she might
tell me that she hasn't already told Isla, and find the healer
considerably more direct with me than I expected, given we've barely
exchanged a dozen words in the whole time I've lived at Highstone.

"You should know," Cera says, without preamble, examining my hand with
the specific brisk competence of a woman who has done this exact
examination more times than either of us would like to count, "that
what's happening to you isn't unique. I've seen it twice before, in
wardwrights' hearths, over the course of my own career. Neither
survived the season."

I absorb that plainly, without flinching, because I have decided,
these past weeks, that flinching from hard truths costs more,
eventually, than simply hearing them properly the first time.

"What happened to them," I ask. "The other two."

"One refused to say anything, the way you very nearly did, until it was
too late for the wardwright involved to do anything useful with the
knowledge. The other told his wardwright plainly, the way you've
finally started doing, but neither of them had anywhere further to
take that honesty. There was nothing left to try beyond what the
standard rite already offered, and the standard rite simply couldn't
save him in time."

"So being honest early actually matters," I say. "Even without knowing
yet what it buys us."

"It's the only thing that gives you any chance at all," Cera agrees,
"whatever that chance eventually turns out to be worth. I can't promise
you honesty solves anything on its own. I can tell you that neither of
the two men I lost ever got the chance to find out, because neither of
them said anything soon enough for it to matter."

"I understand," I say, and thank her, and walk back toward the cottage
turning her words over — the specific weight of being told, plainly,
that simply being honest early enough might be the only thing that
gives me any chance at all, whatever shape that chance eventually takes.

I do not tell Isla about this particular conversation immediately. I
tell myself it's because there's nothing new in it, strictly, beyond
what we already knew. I understand, walking home, that it is closer to
not wanting to hand her one more frightening fact before she's had time
to properly rest from the last one — a small, well-intentioned
management I catch myself doing and decide, before I reach the cottage
door, to correct before the evening's out, rather than let it become
another quiet debt.

·

I tell her about the visit that same evening, exactly as I resolved to,
and watch her absorb it with the particular stillness she reserves for
information she's already half-expecting.

"Two others," she says, quietly. "That you know of."

"That Cera knows of, in her own career. There may be more, further
back, that neither of us has any record of at all."

"I want to know their names," Isla says, "if Cera has them. Not for any
practical reason. I think they deserve to be remembered properly,
rather than simply referenced as a statistic in a healer's professional
accounting."

I find, hearing that request, that it does not surprise me in the
slightest — precisely the kind of instinct I have come to expect from
a woman who copied her own mentor's ledger into her own hand rather
than let his memory go unwritten.

"I'll ask her," I say. "I think you're right that they deserve it."

We sit together a while longer, the weight of two unnamed predecessors
settling into the cottage alongside everything else this winter has
handed us to carry, and I find myself, watching Isla's face in the
firelight, grateful, even now, even frightened, for a woman who insists
on properly naming every single cost this whole arrangement extracts,
rather than letting any of it pass unmarked and unmourned.

·

Cera provides the names within the week, delivered by Tomas, who
apparently offered to fetch them himself rather than let either of us
make the trip specifically for something this heavy.

*Corwin Vane and Petra Ash,* the note reads, in Cera's own brisk hand.
*Corwin's wardwright was his own sister, bound by circumstance rather
than the usual rite-office process — a small mercy the crown permitted,
in those days, before the current procedures tightened. Petra's
partner was named Iven Hallow, a stonemason by trade, who told her
everything, plainly, and still couldn't save him in time.*

I read the names aloud to Isla, that evening, watching her absorb each
one properly, the way she absorbs every fact she considers worth
remembering.

"Corwin Vane," she repeats, quietly. "Petra Ash. Iven Hallow." She
writes them into her own private notebook, alongside Ossian's name and
her mother's, a small, growing record of everyone this rite has cost,
properly named rather than left as statistics.

"Thank you," I say, "for insisting on this. I don't think I'd have
thought to ask, myself."

"Someone should remember them properly," Isla says. "Whatever else
happens with the mutual form, whatever it eventually costs either of
us, I want it understood that these names mattered, and that we
carried them forward, rather than simply building on their loss without
ever once looking back to see what it actually was."

·

I find myself thinking, that same night, about my own father's name,
and whether it belongs in Isla's small, growing record alongside
Corwin Vane and Petra Ash and Iven Hallow — not a wardwright's cost,
exactly, but a cost all the same, paid quietly, over years, by a man
who never once let either of his children see the toll his own
careful management was taking.

"I've been thinking about my father," I tell her, later, once the
names have been properly written down and the notebook set aside for
the evening.

"What about him?"

"Whether he belongs in your record," I say. "Not the same kind of
cost. But a cost, all the same. He spent himself managing our whole
childhood alone, the same way I've spent myself managing the exemption
and the rite and everything else. I don't think anyone ever properly
named what it cost him, doing that."

Isla considers this seriously, the way she considers most things I
bring to her that matter.

"I think he belongs somewhere," she says, "though perhaps not in the
same record as the others. Theirs is a record of the rite's own
particular cruelty. His is something else — a record of what a whole
culture of careful, silent management costs a family, generation over
generation, until someone finally decides to do it differently."

"That's a fair distinction," I say. "I think I'd like to write it down
somewhere, regardless. Properly. Not simply carry it in my own head the
way I've carried everything else."

"Then write it," Isla says. "I'll help, if you want company doing it."

I do write it, that same evening, a short account of my father's own
quiet cost, the specific weight of a man who loved his children
considerably more than he ever let himself show it, and I find,
finishing the account, a measure of the same lightness Isla described
feeling after copying Ossian's ledger into her own hand — not simply
a chore completed, something closer to a proper goodbye, offered late
but offered honestly, to a man who never got the chance to hear it
while he was still here to receive it.

"Thank you for helping me find the words," I say, once it's finished.

"Thank you for finally looking for them," Isla says, "instead of
letting the whole account go unwritten, the way I very nearly let
Ossian's go unwritten myself."

·

Sela reads the account, a few days later, at my own invitation, and
sits with it a long while in the grain-house's quiet before saying
anything at all.

"You wrote this properly," she says, finally. "Not the tidy version we
usually tell each other about him. The actual shape of it."

"I wanted it honest," I say. "I think we've spent our whole lives
telling each other a kinder, simpler version of Father than the one
either of us actually lived through. I didn't want to keep doing that,
writing this down."

"I'm glad you didn't," Sela says. "I've been carrying my own version
of him for years, and I don't think I've ever properly compared it to
anyone else's. This one's closer to what I remember, underneath the
stories we tell company."

"Do you want a copy," I ask, "for yourself?"

"I'd like that," Sela says, "very much," and I promise to make her one,
and we sit together a while longer in the grain-house's quiet, two
siblings finally examining a shared grief honestly, together, rather
than each carrying a separately managed version of the same man,
alone, the way both of us learned to do from watching him do exactly
that his whole life.

·

"Can I ask you something else," Sela says, once the account's been
properly set aside, "while we're being honest about him."

"Go on."

"Do you think he'd have approved," she asks, "of what you're doing
now. The mutual rite. Spending yourself openly, alongside a wardwright,
rather than managing everything quietly the way he taught us both to."

I consider the question properly, turning it over with more care than
my first instinct offers.

"I don't know," I admit. "Part of me thinks he'd have been frightened
for me, the way he was frightened of anything that looked like an
unnecessary risk. Another part of me thinks he might have recognized,
watching it, exactly what he never let himself have — someone to share
the weight with, rather than simply provide for from a careful
distance."

"I think the second part is more likely," Sela says, "though I
understand why the first part frightens you more."

"Why do you think that," I ask.

"Because if he'd have approved," Sela says, "it means he knew, all
along, that there was a better way to carry difficulty than the way he
chose. And I think that's a harder thing to sit with than simply
believing he did his best with what he had available to him."

I don't have a ready response to being understood quite that precisely
by my own sister, so I simply sit with her words a while longer, the
whole complicated shape of our father's legacy settling into something
I can finally examine honestly, rather than only mourn from a
respectful distance.

·

I tell Isla about the conversation that same evening, curious what
she'll make of Sela's own sharp observation.

"She's right, I think," Isla says, once I've finished. "Approval and
recognition aren't quite the same comfort. I understand why the second
one frightens you more."

"It shouldn't, logically," I say. "If he'd have approved, that's a
kinder reading of him, not a harsher one."

"Kinder readings of the dead are sometimes harder to sit with than
harsh ones," Isla says, "because they mean the person was capable of
more than they ever let themselves have. That's a grief of its own,
separate from simply missing someone."

"I hadn't thought of it that way," I admit, "but I think you're
right, again, about something I hadn't properly examined myself."

"I've had a great deal of practice this winter," Isla says, "examining
exactly this kind of grief," and we sit together a while longer,
turning over the specific complicated comfort of understanding a dead
man better than either of us understood him while he was still here to
be understood.


## Chapter Twenty-Nine — Feld's Deadline (Isla)

Feld's letter arrives three days after the meeting with Ossory, shorter
than his usual ledgers and considerably more dangerous for the
brevity: one more failed cairn, it states, and the crown will authorize
the apprentice's binding regardless of any wardwright's personal
objection to the timing.

I read it twice at the record-room table, the specific bureaucratic
economy of its phrasing somehow more chilling than any of Feld's
lengthier ultimatums have managed to be.

"You've had a season to prove the current binding sufficient,
Wardwright," Feld says, in person this time, having ridden down from
whatever crown office issues these particular ultimatums specifically
to deliver this one with his own hand. "The Rime doesn't extend
courtesy for good intentions, and neither, I'm afraid, does the crown's
patience."

I want, badly, to tell him about the mutual rite — to offer him
something other than my own bare word that the current arrangement is
worth preserving a little longer. I cannot, not without the proper
translation Ossory has requested, not without risking Feld deciding the
whole discovery is exactly the kind of destabilizing nonsense a
desperate wardwright, out of options, would simply invent to buy time.

"The binding is holding," I say instead, which is true, this week,
though the truth has never once felt more provisional than it does
saying it to Feld's carefully unreadable face.

"For how much longer," Feld asks, "is the actual question the crown
needs answered, Wardwright, and I notice you haven't given me a number,
in any of our conversations, that I could actually take back to them."

"I don't have a number I'm prepared to guarantee," I say, honestly,
because I have decided, somewhere in these past weeks, that lying to
Feld costs considerably more, eventually, than simply refusing to hand
him false comfort.

Feld's letter, once read fully, contains the part that frightens me
considerably more than the ultimatum itself: a specific date, one
month hence, by which the crown expects either proof of the current
binding's sufficiency or Tomas's own binding properly arranged,
regardless of my objection.

Tomas, told the contents of the letter afterward, goes quiet in a way
that isn't fear exactly — closer to a young man doing the specific
arithmetic of duty that nobody his age should have to perform this
early in a career he's barely begun.

"I won't let it be you," I tell him, and hear, saying it, exactly how
much that promise is going to cost me to keep, given a month is
considerably less time than either the translation or Ossory's own
careful consideration is likely to require.

"You can't promise that," Tomas says, gently, "not if the choice ends
up not being yours to make."

"I know," I say. "I'm promising it anyway, because I intend to make
certain the choice stays mine to make, one way or another, before that
date arrives."

Aldric finds me afterward, still holding Feld's letter, and reads it
over my shoulder without asking.

"A month," he says.

"A month," I agree.

"That's not much time to properly test an unproven rite."

"No," I say. "It isn't. But it's the time we have, and I'd rather
spend all of it trying to make the mutual form work than spend any of
it managing Feld's patience instead of the actual problem."

Aldric doesn't argue with that, though I feel him weighing something,
privately, in the particular stillness he gets when he's about to say
something he suspects will cost him to say.

"If it comes down to a choice," he says, finally, "between Tomas and
me—"

"It won't," I say, before he can finish the sentence, sharper than I
intend, because I understand, hearing the shape of where he's headed,
exactly what he's about to offer, and I am not, tonight, prepared to
hear it out loud.

"I need you to hear the rest of the sentence," Aldric says, gently, "even
if you don't like where it's going."

"Not tonight," I say, and he lets it go, this once, though I feel the
unfinished sentence sitting between us for the rest of the evening,
patient, waiting for whichever night either of us finally runs out of
room to keep postponing it.

·

Tomas brings the finished translation to Ossory himself, the following
morning, insisting, with a stubbornness I recognize as entirely my own
teaching bearing unexpected fruit, that he be the one to present the
portion he cross-checked, rather than simply letting me carry the whole
of it forward on his behalf.

"You did good work," I tell him, watching him gather the pages with
visibly shaking hands, nervous in a way I haven't seen from him since
his very first solo cairn inspection.

"I want him to see that I understand it too," Tomas says. "Not just
that I helped. That I actually understand what we found, properly, the
way a wardwright should."

"He'll see that," I say, and I am right — Ossory listens to Tomas's
own portion of the presentation with the particular careful attention
he generally reserves for considerably more senior voices, asking two
genuine questions about the grammar Tomas cross-checked, both of which
Tomas answers precisely, without hesitation.

"You've trained him well," Ossory says to me, afterward, once Tomas has
gone, visibly relieved, to report the meeting's success to Aldric.

"He's trained himself, mostly," I say. "I've simply tried not to get in
the way of it."

"That's rarer than you'd think, among practitioners," Ossory says.
"Most wardwrights I've known guard their own expertise rather more
jealously than you seem inclined to. I take it as a good sign, for the
line's future, that you've chosen otherwise."

I think about that observation for the rest of the day, turning it over
alongside everything else this whole strange season has taught me
about the difference between carrying a burden alone because you
believe it's noble, and handing pieces of it forward, deliberately, to
whoever's ready to carry their own share of it properly — a lesson, I
understand, watching Tomas practically glow with the specific pride of
having been taken seriously, that applies to considerably more than
simply the mutual rite's translation.

·

Feld's letter, once the shock of its deadline properly settles, prompts
a considerably more practical response from me than my first reading
allowed — I begin, that same evening, drafting a formal reply of my
own, one that does not yet reveal the mutual form's existence, but that
lays out, plainly, precisely how much the current binding has already
achieved against the line's failing arithmetic.

"You're building a case," Aldric observes, watching me work through
several drafts.

"I'm building a record," I say. "If Feld intends to force Tomas's own
binding regardless of what Ossory eventually decides, I want the
crown's own office to have, in writing, an honest account of exactly
how much this current arrangement has already held, in case anyone
further up the chain ever needs reminding that the current binding is
worth defending on its own merits, independent of whatever else Ossory
eventually sanctions."

"Do you think it'll matter? A written record, against Feld's own
timeline?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I know that Ossian never once, in his whole
career, kept this kind of formal defense of his own work. I think, if
he had, the crown's office might have thought rather harder before
funding this line down to a single practitioner in the first place. I'd
rather leave a proper record than simply hope the crown's better
judgment eventually prevails on its own."

Aldric reads the finished draft that evening, and I watch him take in
the whole of it — six years of cairns held, of the line's slow,
patient defense against a cold that never once extends courtesy for
good intentions — with something that looks, on his face, considerably
closer to awe than I expected from a man who has watched me do this
exact work, day in and day out, for the whole of our acquaintance.

"I don't think I properly understood, until reading this, quite how
much you've actually done," he says, quietly. "I've watched you work.
I don't think I've ever seen the whole of it laid out, plainly, in one
place."

"Neither had I," I admit, "until I sat down to write it."

·

Feld's own response to my formal letter, when it finally arrives,
surprises me with its brevity — a single line, considerably less
bureaucratic than his usual correspondence, acknowledging receipt and
noting only that "the record will be considered alongside all other
relevant factors in the crown's ongoing assessment."

"That's not much of a response," Aldric observes, reading it over my
shoulder.

"No," I agree, "but it's not a dismissal either, which is rather more
than I expected from him. I think — and I could be wrong — that he's
actually reading it properly, rather than filing it unread the way I
half-feared he would."

"Do you think he's capable of being convinced? Genuinely, I mean, not
simply outmaneuvered."

I consider the question properly, thinking back over every interaction
I've had with Feld across six years of dealing with his particular
brand of careful, bloodless bureaucracy.

"I think he's a man who's decided that following the crown's own
arithmetic exactly is the safest way to conduct himself professionally,"
I say. "I don't think he's cruel, precisely. I think he's simply never
been given a reason to imagine an alternative worth risking his own
career over. I'd like to think this letter might be the beginning of
that reason, though I'm not holding out excessive hope."

"That's a considerably more generous read of him than I'd have
managed," Aldric says.

"I've had six years to develop it," I say, "and I find, this particular
season, that I have rather more patience for complicated people than I
used to. I suppose that's simply one more thing this whole arrangement
has taught me."

·

Aldric raises, that same evening, the unfinished sentence from a few
nights before — the one I refused to let him complete — choosing his
moment carefully, once the day's harder business has settled and
neither of us has anything further urgent demanding attention.

"I'd like to finish what I was saying," he says, "about Tomas. If
you're willing to hear it now."

I consider refusing again, the old reflex rising immediately, and then
make myself set it aside, because I have decided, this whole season,
that avoiding a fear rarely makes it smaller.

"Go on," I say.

"If it comes down to a choice between his binding and mine," Aldric
says, carefully, "I want it to be mine. Not because I think my life is
worth more than his. Because I chose this, at the rite field, with my
eyes open, and he hasn't had the chance to choose anything yet. I don't
think it's fair to let a nineteen-year-old inherit a crisis neither of
us managed to solve in time."

"I understand why you'd want that," I say, "and I need you to
understand that I'm not going to let it come to a choice at all. I'm
not choosing between the two of you. I'm going to find a way to save
you both, or I'm going to fail trying, but I refuse to sit here and
plan which one of you I'm prepared to lose."

"That's not always a choice either of us gets to make," Aldric says,
gently. "Sometimes the arithmetic simply runs out before anyone's
ready for it to."

"I know," I say. "I've spent six years watching the arithmetic run out
on people I couldn't save in time. I'm not accepting it in advance,
this time, simply because it might eventually prove necessary. I'd
rather exhaust every possibility first."

Aldric doesn't argue further, though I can see the offer still sitting
there, unwithdrawn, a small, terrible kindness he's prepared to make if
the month's deadline ever forces the question. I choose, for tonight,
simply to hold his hand rather than argue the point any further, both
of us understanding that whatever the coming weeks demand, neither of
us is fully in control of how this particular arithmetic will
eventually resolve.

·

Tomas arrives the following morning with news of his own, considerably
more cheerful than the previous evening's difficult conversation, and I
find myself grateful for the ordinary interruption.

"I've finished the second draft of the translation," he announces,
setting a neat stack of pages on the table. "Properly this time, cross-
checked against every fragment in the record box twice over. I don't
think there's a single ambiguous phrase left unexamined."

"That's excellent work," I say, examining the pages, genuinely
impressed by the thoroughness. "This is considerably more polished than
what we brought Ossory initially."

"I wanted it to be unimpeachable," Tomas says, "given how much rides on
Ossory trusting the translation completely. I didn't want a single
loose thread anyone could use to dismiss the whole argument."

"You've more than managed that," I say. "I think this might be the
single most careful piece of work I've seen from you in three years."

Tomas's pride at the compliment is entirely unguarded, the particular
pleasure of a young man who has poured real effort into something and
finally been told, plainly, that the effort was worth it.

"I wanted to actually contribute something concrete," he admits, "rather
than simply watch the two of you navigate the harder decisions. This
felt like the one part I could genuinely help with, properly."

"You've helped considerably more than you know," I say, and mean it,
and the morning's harder business waits a little longer while the three
of us properly celebrate a piece of careful, patient work finally,
thoroughly finished.

·

Aldric takes the finished translation from Tomas that afternoon,
reading it carefully in a quiet corner of the record-room, and I find
him some while later, considerably more moved than the document's dry,
careful language would seem to warrant.

"What is it," I ask.

"This line," he says, pointing to a passage near the middle. "About
naming, before the stone, exactly what each person is choosing to risk.
I keep thinking about how differently my own life might have gone, if
someone had ever asked me to name what I was risking, properly, before
I volunteered for anything — Sela's exemption, the binding itself. I
don't think I've ever once been asked to name the risk before taking
it on."

"You're asking now," I say, "in your own way, simply by noticing what
you missed."

"I suppose I am," Aldric says. "I find I want that for Tomas,
specifically, whenever he eventually faces his own significant choices.
I don't want him inheriting the habit of volunteering blindly, the way
I always have."

"I don't think he will," I say. "I think he's already learned,
watching all of us this winter, that naming the risk properly is part
of choosing it honestly."

"I hope you're right," Aldric says, and we sit together with the
translation between us a while longer, both of us grateful for a
document that has, in its careful, ancient language, taught us
something considerably more personal than its authors likely intended.

·

Tomas returns that evening, curious what we've made of the finished
translation, and I share Aldric's own observation about naming the
risk before taking it on.

"I hadn't thought of it that way," Tomas admits, "though I think I
understand it properly now, hearing it named. I suppose that's rather
what I've been doing, drafting this translation so carefully — naming,
to myself, exactly what I was risking, getting a single detail wrong
in something this significant."

"That's a good instinct," I say, "carrying it into your own work,
even before either of us pointed it out directly."

"I think I learned it from watching both of you, this whole season,"
Tomas says, "long before either of you said it plainly enough for me
to properly notice I'd already absorbed the lesson."

We sit together a while longer, the three of us, considerably grateful
for a document that has, in its own quiet way, taught this whole
household the same steadying truth about naming a risk honestly before
choosing to carry it.


## Chapter Thirty — The Cairn at Long Reach, Again (Isla)

We set another ward at the Long Reach cairn, ten days into Feld's
month, because the line does not pause for translations or sanctioned
deliberation, and forty miles of stone do not care whether the crown
has properly authorized whichever form of the rite currently holds
them up.

I feed the ward everything I have held back before, trying to spare
Aldric even a fraction of what the stone wants, and it is not enough,
and I know, before I've even finished the old words, that it will not
be enough, because the Long Reach cairn has been reading low for
months now and a single ward, however generously fed, was never going
to reverse an entire season's accumulated debt.

I feel the specific, sinking recognition of an arithmetic I have run
before, at other failing cairns, in other winters — the moment a
wardwright understands that no amount of personal sacrifice, however
total, is actually going to be sufficient, and that the only honest
next step is admitting the current method has simply run out of road.

Aldric's hand — the numb one, useless now for anything requiring fine
control — still presses to the stone regardless, without complaint,
without hesitation, because the rite does
not care which hand a hearth has left to offer it, only that a hand is
offered at all.

Afterward, he cannot stand without my arm under his, and neither of us
says the word FAILING, though it is the only honest word currently
available to describe what just happened.

"This can't hold through the season," I say, flat, to myself as much as
to him, doing the arithmetic I have been avoiding doing plainly since
Calder's Reach.

"Then we don't hold it the old way," Aldric says, and it is the first
time either of us has said the mutual rite's name out loud as an
actual plan rather than merely a discovery still awaiting Ossory's
consideration.

"Ossory hasn't sanctioned it yet."

"Ossory doesn't have a month," Aldric says. "We have considerably less
than that, if this cairn is any indication of how quickly the others
are going to follow it. I don't think we can afford to wait for
sanction before we're certain we're going to need it regardless."

I sit with him on the cold ground beside the cracked, exhausted stone,
and feel, for the first time since Calder's Reach, a fear that has
nothing at all to do with the crown's timeline and everything to do
with the plain, unmanageable arithmetic of a rite that may simply run
out of time to be properly tested before the old form finishes what
it's already started.

"I'm frightened," I say, "that we won't have the choice of doing this
properly. That the old rite will simply take you, one cairn at a time,
before Ossory's finished weighing whether the mutual form is safe
enough to attempt."

"Then we don't wait for him to finish weighing it," Aldric says,
quietly. "We go back to him tomorrow, and we tell him the timeline has
changed, and we ask him to decide faster than he's comfortable
deciding, because comfortable isn't a luxury either of us currently
has."

I help him up, eventually, once he can stand on his own again, and we
walk back from the Long Reach cairn slower than either of us would
like, his weight leaning more heavily on my shoulder than either of us
is willing to name aloud, and I understand, walking that slow, quiet
mile, that whatever careful deliberation any of us had hoped to afford
this decision has just, this evening, run considerably shorter than any
of us accounted for.

·

Sela is waiting at the cottage when we finally arrive, having heard,
somehow — the village's grapevine again, faster than any official
report could travel — that something had gone wrong at the Long Reach
cairn, and I watch her take in the whole of Aldric's condition with the
specific controlled fear of a sister who has spent her entire life
being the one who copes, regardless of how frightened she actually is
underneath the coping.

"How bad," she asks, direct, no preamble.

"Bad enough that we can't wait for Ossory's usual pace any longer," I
say. "I'm going back to him tomorrow, to press for a faster decision.
I wanted you to know, before I go, exactly how urgent it's actually
become."

Sela sits with Aldric a long while that evening, the two of them
talking quietly in a register I don't fully intrude on, giving them the
space I suspect a lifetime of shared history has earned, and it is only
once Aldric has finally gone to sleep, exhausted past the point of
staying properly conscious, that Sela finds me alone by the fire.

"I want to help," she says. "Whatever you need tomorrow, at Ossory's
hall — testimony, evidence, simply a witness who can speak to what
this has already cost our family. I'm not willing to sit at the orchard
waiting for news any longer than I have to."

"I'd welcome that," I say, honestly. "I think Ossory takes the whole
matter more seriously with the family's own voice in the room,
alongside the wardwright's."

"Then I'll be there," Sela says, with the specific steel I've come to
recognize as entirely her own, distinct from Aldric's quieter version
of the same stubbornness. "I've spent this whole winter watching from
the outside while the two of you carried the actual weight of it. I'm
not doing that tomorrow. Whatever this costs, I want to be properly
inside it, standing with my own family, rather than simply hearing
about the outcome secondhand."

I don't have a ready answer for the specific fierceness of her
declaration, so I simply nod, grateful, and the two of us sit together
by the dying fire a while longer, two women who have each, in their own
way, learned this winter exactly how much easier a burden becomes once
it's properly shared rather than carried in careful, well-meaning
isolation.

·

Tomas joins us before the fire burns down entirely, having heard,
through whatever channel now reliably keeps him informed of every
significant development at Highstone, that the Long Reach cairn had
cracked.

"I want to come with you tomorrow," he says, to me, direct, no
hesitation in the asking. "To Ossory's hall. Not simply to wait here for
news."

"It's not your burden to carry, Tomas."

"It is, though," he says, with a firmness I haven't heard from him
before. "You've told me I'm the next wardwright of this line. That
means it's my line too, eventually, and I'd rather understand properly
how this crisis gets resolved than simply inherit whatever's left of it
once the two of you have already decided everything without me."

I look at Sela, who nods, and understand, between the two of them,
that I am considerably outnumbered in my instinct to protect Tomas from
the whole of tomorrow's uncertainty.

"Alright," I say. "You come. But you listen, properly, and you let
Aldric and me speak for ourselves in the actual hearing, whatever
Ossory decides to hold."

"Agreed," Tomas says, satisfied, and the three of us — soon to be four,
once Aldric wakes properly in the morning — sit together by the fire a
while longer, a whole small household drawn close around the same
shared fear, refusing, together, to let any single member of it face
tomorrow alone.

·

Tomas lingers after Sela has gone up to check on Aldric, turning
something over in his hands that I recognize, after a moment, as the
small carved bird Aldric started for him months ago and never quite
finished.

"He gave me this half-done, back in the autumn," Tomas says, "and told
me he'd finish it once the season slowed down enough to properly sit
with the knife again. I don't think either of us believed, saying it,
that the season was ever actually going to slow down."

"It hasn't, has it," I say. "Not once, this whole winter."

"No," Tomas agrees. "I keep it anyway. Half-finished. I think I like it
better this way, honestly, though I wouldn't have said so a few months
ago. It reminds me that some things are worth keeping even unfinished,
rather than only the tidy, completed version of them."

"That's a wardwright's instinct," I say, "whether you've fully claimed
the title yet or not. Most of what we hold on this line is exactly
that — unfinished, imperfect, kept anyway because the keeping matters
more than the completing."

Tomas turns the little bird over once more before setting it carefully
on the mantel, where I notice, for the first time, he has apparently
been keeping it in plain view rather than tucked away in some private
drawer.

"I want him to finish it properly," Tomas says, quieter now, the
bravado of the earlier conversation with Sela fully set aside, "once
all of this is actually behind us. I'm not ready for it to stay
half-done permanently. I just needed to say, out loud, that I could
bear it if it did."

"I understand," I say, and mean it more than the simple words properly
carry, understanding exactly the particular courage it takes to name a
fear plainly rather than let it manage itself quietly in the dark.

"Do you think he'll survive whatever tomorrow decides," Tomas asks,
finally, the question he has clearly been circling since he first
arrived at the cottage door tonight.

I do not have a comforting answer ready, and I have decided, this
whole difficult season, that honesty costs less in the long run than
whatever comfortable lie might be available in the moment.

"I don't know," I say. "I think our chances are better than they were
a week ago, and considerably worse than either of us would like. I
think the honest answer is that I don't know, and that not knowing is
something we're all going to have to hold together, rather than
pretend certainty either of us doesn't actually have."

Tomas nods slowly, accepting the answer's honesty even though it
plainly isn't the reassurance he'd hoped for, and I find myself,
watching him, grateful for a young man who has learned, somewhere in
these past months, to prefer a difficult truth over an easy comfort.

"Thank you," he says, "for not pretending."

"Thank you for not needing me to," I say, and the two of us sit a
while longer in the banked firelight, saying nothing further, simply
keeping each other company against a fear that neither of us can
properly resolve alone tonight.

·

I do not sleep, that night, despite the exhaustion of the previous
day's crisis, my mind circling the whole shape of what tomorrow might
demand of all of us. I find myself, near dawn, going through Ossian's
old journal one final time, searching for anything I might have missed
— some fragment of the mutual form's mechanics that his own private
doubt might have preserved, even incompletely.

I find nothing new. But I find, rereading his careful, economical
hand one more time, a measure of comfort I hadn't expected — the
specific companionship of a predecessor who wondered the same
questions I am now asking, even if he never found the nerve, or the
proof, to act on them himself.

"I'm going to finish what you started," I say, quietly, to the
journal, to the empty record-room, to whatever remains of a man who
taught me everything I know and never once let me see how much he
himself was carrying. "Whatever it costs. I promise you that."

I fold the journal closed, finally, and go to check on Aldric, finding
him asleep, properly, for the first time since the cairn cracked,
Sela keeping her own quiet vigil in the chair beside him. I settle in
alongside her, and the two of us wait out the last hours before dawn
together, two women bound by the same fierce love for the same man,
neither of us willing to face the coming day anywhere but exactly
where we currently are.

·

"Can I ask you something," Sela says, quietly, somewhere in the
deepest, stillest part of the pre-dawn hours, "that I've been meaning
to ask since the Long Reach cairn cracked."

"Go on."

"Do you regret it," she asks. "Any of it. The binding, the mutual
form, the whole arrangement the rite handed you without asking whether
you wanted it. I know what you'd say if I asked you plainly, in
daylight, with everyone listening. I'm asking now, in the dark, because
I want the answer you'd give if nobody else were listening at all."

I consider the question properly, in the particular honest quiet the
hour seems to grant.

"No," I say, finally. "I've been frightened, considerably, more than
I've let show most days. I've done arithmetic on outcomes I don't like
to examine directly. But I don't regret it. I don't think I've ever,
