in six years of careful, solitary practice, felt as thoroughly alive as
I have these past weeks, frightened and exhausted and uncertain as
they've been."

"That's not a small thing to say," Sela says, "about a rite that very
nearly killed him tonight."

"I know," I say. "I think that's rather the whole complicated shape of
it. The fear and the aliveness aren't opposites. I've learned that,
this winter, more than almost anything else."

Sela is quiet a long moment, considering this.

"I think I understand that better than I expected to," she says,
eventually. "I've spent this whole crisis frightened out of my mind,
and also, somehow, more properly connected to my own family than I've
felt in years. I don't think I'd have predicted that combination, going
into this winter."

"Neither would I," I agree, and we sit together the rest of the night,
two women holding the same complicated truth, waiting out the dark
until whatever the coming day demands finally arrives.

·

Tomas finds us there, near dawn, having risen early himself, unable to
properly sleep, and settles quietly on the floor beside our two chairs
rather than disturb the room's particular stillness.

"I couldn't sleep either," he admits, when Sela glances at him.

"None of us managed much of that, tonight," Sela says.

"I keep thinking about what I'd say to Ossory," Tomas says, "if he asks
me anything directly. I want to make sure I've got the translation's
provenance properly memorized, in case he questions any specific
detail."

"You'll be fine," I say. "You've prepared more thoroughly than anyone
could reasonably ask of you."

"I know," Tomas says. "I think I simply need to keep rehearsing it
anyway, the same way you're clearly not sleeping either, doing whatever
arithmetic keeps circling in your own head."

"That's fair," I admit, and the three of us sit together in the
cottage's pre-dawn quiet, each holding our own private version of the
same nervous anticipation, none of us alone in the particular vigil of
waiting for a morning none of us can properly predict the shape of.

·

Aldric wakes properly not long before dawn, finding the three of us
still gathered, and takes in the scene with visible tenderness.

"You've all been keeping vigil," he observes.

"None of us slept much," I admit.

"Neither did I," he says, "though I think I managed rather more rest
than the rest of you, given the circumstances." He settles in among
us, taking my hand. "Whatever today brings, I want it said properly,
before we walk into that hall: I'm grateful, more than I know how to
say, for every single person currently sitting in this room."

"We feel the same," Sela says, and Tomas nods his own fierce agreement,
and the four of us sit together in the last of the night's darkness,
considerably more ready to face whatever the coming day demands, having
spent its final hours properly together rather than each alone with
private fear.


## Chapter Thirty-One — What the Rite Would Cost Him (Isla)

No wardwright alive has performed the mutual rite. I have a dead
woman's account of it, faded and incomplete in places where damp has
gotten into the record box over however many decades it sat sealed in
Calder's Reach, and nothing else — no second source, no living
practitioner who's attempted it and survived to describe what happens
if it's tried wrong, or late, or by two people the rite-keepers never
properly sanctioned to attempt it at all.

I have read her account so many times, these past days, that I could
recite whole passages from memory, and still I find myself returning
to it nightly, searching the same faded ink for some reassurance it
was never written to provide.

"You could tell the Keepers everything, formally," Tomas offers, in the
record-room, watching me read the translation for what must be the
twentieth time, searching for a caveat I have not yet found and am
increasingly afraid does not exist to be found. "Let them decide
properly, with more than one record to weigh against the crown's own
concerns."

"The Keepers would take a season to decide properly," I say. "We don't
have a season. We barely have the one cairn Aldric can still stand at
without his knees giving out under him."

I do not tell Tomas the other fear sitting underneath the first one —
that the rite, attempted wrong, could spend us both at once, cost taken
from two hearths simultaneously with no old wardwright's careful
account of how to stop it once begun, and that I am, for the first time
in my entire life, considerably more frightened of losing Aldric than
I am of dying myself.

I have spent six years being the only wardwright standing between forty
miles of cairns and the Rime's own patient advance, and I have never
once, in all that time, been properly frightened for myself. I am
frightened now, sitting in a record-room built entirely out of other
people's endings, of a fear that has nothing to do with my own
mortality at all.

"Do you think it'll work?" Tomas asks. "Properly. Not just — do you
hope it will. Do you actually think it will."

I consider the question with more honesty than I have given myself
permission for since Calder's Reach.

"I think it's genuine," I say. "I think the record describes something
real, a form of the rite that actually existed, actually worked, before
whichever generation of rite-keepers found it easier to spend one
hearth quietly than share the cost between two wardwrights loudly. I
don't know if it survives being attempted this late, by two people who
weren't trained in it from the start, with a hearth already this
compromised."

"That's not a yes," Tomas says.

"No," I agree. "It isn't. It's the honest answer, which is
considerably less comfortable than a yes would be, but which I've
decided, this whole autumn, matters more."

Tomas sits with that for a while, doing his own private arithmetic, the
particular careful sum of a young man calculating what happens to his
own future depending on which way an unproven rite falls.

"Whatever happens," he says, finally, "I want you to know I'd rather
you attempt it and it not work than watch you keep doing what you've
been doing for two years, quietly, until it finally catches up with
you the same way it's catching up with him."

I do not have an answer for being seen, quite so plainly, by a
nineteen-year-old I have spent three years trying to protect from
exactly this kind of clear-eyed accounting, so I simply nod, once, and
let the record-room hold the fear with me a while longer, in the
specific silence of a room built entirely out of endings, waiting to
see whether this particular one turns out to be different from all the
rest.

·

Aldric finds me still there past midnight, the lamp burned low, the
translation spread across the table in the specific disarray of a
woman who has read the same uncertain passages considerably more times
than rereading them will ever resolve.

"You should sleep," he says, gently, though I notice he makes no move
to leave himself.

"I should," I agree, and do not move either.

He sits across from me, and for a while neither of us says anything at
all, simply sharing the particular exhausted quiet of two people who
have run out of easy words for the size of what's currently ahead of
them.

"I keep thinking about the crooked tree," Aldric says, eventually. "The
one that stopped bearing fruit. I kept it because cutting it down felt
like agreeing the cold had won something it hadn't actually earned. I
wonder if that's what I'm doing now. Refusing to accept an ending
simply because accepting it would mean admitting the cold's finally
caught up with me."

"I don't think that's quite the same thing," I say. "The tree stopped
fruiting through no fault of its own. You haven't stopped anything.
You're still here, still arguing with me at midnight in a record-room,
still considerably more stubborn than any reasonable cold has a right
to expect from you."

"For now," Aldric says.

"For now," I agree, because I have decided that false comfort costs
more than honest uncertainty, even at midnight, even exhausted,
"which is the only tense any of us actually get to live in, regardless
of how much either of us would prefer the certainty of a different
one."

He reaches across the table and takes my hand — the good one, still
warm, still entirely his — and we sit like that a while longer, not
solving anything, simply sharing the considerable weight of not
knowing, until the lamp finally gutters and neither of us has the
heart to relight it, choosing instead to sit together in the dark a
little longer before the day's fear properly resumes.

·

Sela finds the two of us there, still in the record-room, as the first
grey light of dawn starts finding its way through the shuttered window,
and does not comment on the fact that neither of us has properly slept.

"I've brought breakfast," she says, simply, setting down a basket that
smells of the orchard's own last stored apples, "on the theory that
neither of you thought to eat anything since yesterday, and that
whatever today requires, it's going to require it from people who've
actually fed themselves properly first."

"Thank you," Aldric says, and means it, and I watch brother and sister
share a look that carries considerably more than the simple exchange of
a meal — the specific, wordless communication of two people who have
weathered enough difficult mornings together to no longer need words
for most of it.

We eat together, the three of us, in the record-room's grey dawn light,
and I find myself, watching Sela fuss quietly over whether Aldric's
actually finished his portion, grateful for a family that has, this
whole strange winter, folded me into its own particular rhythms without
ever once making the folding feel like an imposition on either side.

"Whatever Ossory decides today," Sela says, once the meal's finished,
"I want it understood, by both of you, that this family isn't going
anywhere. Regardless of the outcome. We'll face whatever comes, together,
the way we should have been facing everything, all along, instead of
each of us managing our own private corner of it alone."

"Agreed," I say, and Aldric echoes it, and the three of us finish the
morning's preparations together, considerably more steadied by the
shared meal and the shared resolve than either of us managed alone,
lying awake in the record-room's dark the whole of the previous night.

·

Tomas arrives shortly after, dressed for what he clearly understands to
be a significant day, carrying his own carefully organized notes on the
translation's provenance, ready to argue his portion of the case
alongside the rest of us.

"I've reviewed everything once more," he says, "in case Ossory has
further questions about the verification process specifically."

"You've done everything that could reasonably be asked of you," I say,
"and considerably more besides."

"I want to do more than reasonably," Tomas says, with a seriousness
that reminds me, not for the first time, exactly how much this crisis
has already asked of a nineteen-year-old who never signed up for
quite this scale of responsibility. "This is Aldric's life. I want to
have done everything within my power, properly, rather than simply
enough to satisfy some reasonable standard."

"I understand," I say, and mean it, recognizing in his own fierce
diligence something of my own habit, carefully inherited, of believing
that enough is never quite enough when someone you love is at stake.
"Just remember, whatever happens today, that you've already done more
than anyone could rightfully ask of you. Regardless of Ossory's
decision, that much is already true."

Tomas nods, absorbing this, and we set out together for the rite-hall,
the whole small household moving as one toward whatever the day has
waiting for us.

·

We stop, briefly, at the record-room on the way, because I find I want
one final look at Ossian's own translation before handing the whole
question over to Ossory's judgment, and Aldric waits with me at the
door while I retrieve it.

"Nervous," he asks, watching me tuck the fragile pages carefully into
my satchel.

"Considerably," I admit. "I've spent six years being the person other
people bring their fears to. I'm not entirely practiced at being the
one asking someone else to take a risk on my account."

"You're allowed to be frightened," Aldric says. "I'd be more worried
if you weren't."

"I know," I say. "I think I'm more frightened of what happens if
Ossory says no than of anything the rite itself might cost either of
us. I've built this whole plan around the assumption that he'll hear
it properly. I don't have a version of today prepared for the
alternative."

"Then we'll build one," Aldric says, "together, if it comes to that.
Whatever Ossory decides, we're not facing the aftermath of it alone,
either of us."

I take some comfort in that, more than I expect to, and we continue on
toward the rite-hall together, the fragile translation carefully
tucked away, the whole weight of the morning ahead of us considerably
lighter for not facing it as two separate people making two separate
cases, but as one shared argument, offered jointly, for a future
neither of us is willing to face without the other.

"Whatever happens today," I say, as Highstone's gate comes into view,
"I want you to know that asking for this properly — in front of
witnesses, plainly, together — matters to me considerably more than
whatever Ossory ultimately decides. I'd rather lose this argument
having asked it honestly than win it by any of the old, careful,
managed methods either of us used to prefer."

"I feel exactly the same," Aldric says, and takes my hand as we
approach the gate, and I understand, walking the last stretch toward
whatever today holds, that this alone — the plain, unmanaged asking,
offered together — is already considerably more than either of us had
six weeks ago, regardless of what the day still has waiting for us.

·

Ossory receives us in the record-room rather than the rite-hall proper,
a small mercy I recognize immediately — a formal hall would have made
today's argument feel like a trial rather than a request, and I
understand, seeing the choice, exactly how deliberately he's already
begun trying to make this easier than his position strictly requires.

"Show me everything," he says, without preamble, and I do — the
predecessor's record, Tomas's own careful cross-checking, the whole
patient architecture of proof we've assembled since Calder's Reach.

He reads slowly, methodically, asking precise questions at exactly the
points I'd have asked them myself, and I find, watching him work
through the material with genuine rigor rather than performed
skepticism, a considerable relief in being taken seriously by someone
whose seriousness actually counts.

"This is thorough," he says, finally, setting the last page down. "More
thorough than I expected, given the timeline you've been working
under."

"Tomas did the bulk of the cross-checking," I say. "I want that
properly credited, whatever you decide."

Ossory looks at Tomas for a long moment, something considering in his
expression.

"You'll make a fine wardwright," he says, "assuming this line survives
long enough to need one." He turns back to the record spread across
the table. "I need more time before I can properly sanction an attempt.
I want that understood plainly. But I want equally plainly understood
that I intend to move as quickly as diligence permits, rather than at
my office's usual comfortable pace."

"Thank you," I say, and mean it, though I feel the day's earlier
nervousness settling into something more like cautious relief than
outright celebration.

"Don't thank me yet," Ossory says, echoing what has apparently become
this whole village's standard response to gratitude offered too early.
"Thank me once I've actually delivered something worth the thanks."

·

We leave the record-room together, the three of us, and I find Sela
waiting at the corridor's end, having come to Highstone specifically to
hear the outcome the moment it was available.

"Well," she says, reading our faces. "Tell me."

"He needs more time," I say, "but he's taking it seriously. Properly.
I think that's rather more than we had reason to expect, going in."

"More time," Sela repeats, turning the phrase over. "How much more?"

"I don't know," I admit. "I don't think Ossory knows precisely either.
I think the honest answer is that he's going to move as quickly as his
own diligence permits, and none of us gets to set the pace for him."

Sela absorbs this, nodding slowly, though I can see the specific effort
it costs her not to press further.

"Then we wait," she says, finally, "properly, together, rather than
each of us managing the waiting separately."

"Together," I agree, and the four of us walk out into Highstone's cold
afternoon light, considerably more united in the waiting ahead than any
of us were facing this morning's uncertain hearing alone.

·

Tomas walks beside me on the way back to the cottage, quiet in the
particular way of a young man processing something he hasn't quite
worked out how to say.

"Do you think he'll actually move quickly enough," he asks, finally.

"I don't know," I admit. "I think he means what he said. I think
'quickly enough' and 'quickly by Ossory's own standards' might turn out
to be two different measures, and I don't yet know which one we're
actually going to get."

"That frightens me," Tomas says, plainly.

"It frightens me too," I say. "I don't think there's any use pretending
otherwise, to you specifically, given how much of today you've already
witnessed firsthand."

"Thank you for not pretending," Tomas says. "I think I'd rather be
frightened honestly, alongside you, than reassured falsely and left to
discover the fear on my own later."

"I'll keep choosing honestly, then," I say, "whatever it costs either
of us in the short term," and we walk the rest of the way together, the
whole small company considerably more bound by shared, unmanaged fear
than any comforting pretense could have made them.

·

Aldric is waiting at the cottage when we finally arrive, considerably
steadier than the morning left him, and reads the whole shape of the
day's outcome from our faces before any of us says a word.

"He didn't sanction it," Aldric says.

"Not yet," I say. "He's taking it seriously, properly. I think that's
the most honest thing I can currently say about it."

"That's more than nothing," Aldric says, taking my hand. "I know it's
not everything either of us wanted walking in this morning."

"I know," I say, "and I've decided I'd rather have an honest 'not yet'
than a comfortable lie dressed up as progress."

"So would I," Aldric agrees, and the whole small household settles
into the cottage's evening quiet together, considerably more prepared
for whatever waiting still lies ahead, having faced the day's uncertain
outcome honestly, together, rather than each of them managing a
separate private disappointment alone.


## Chapter Thirty-Two — The Cairn Cracks (Aldric)

I go out to the Long Reach cairn alone, at dusk, without waking her, telling myself the whole walk there that this once, surely, is different.

The evening's gone properly cold by the time I reach the track's edge,
the specific bright, brittle cold that means the frost is setting hard
overnight, and I find myself moving faster than the errand strictly
requires, some part of me already sensing, before the reading confirms
it, exactly how much worse the stone has gotten since morning.

It is not, I want to be clear, a decision I make lightly, or one I make
without understanding exactly what it will cost her to wake and find me
gone. I make it because the cairn's reading, when I checked it that
afternoon while Isla was still in the record-room with Tomas, had
dropped considerably further than either of us accounted for, and I
understood, checking it, that it would not hold through the night
without someone feeding it, and I was not willing to wake her and ask
her to spend herself on it again, not after what the last feeding cost
her, not with the mutual rite still unsanctioned and the whole of our
remaining time uncertain.

The cairn cracks at dusk, a sound like the whole line taking a breath it
cannot let back out, granite splitting along a fault line that has
clearly been building for considerably longer than the visible reading
suggested, and I throw everything I currently have left into holding it
rather than let it fail outright, because a cairn that fails outright
takes the whole surrounding stretch of line down with it, and I am not
willing to let that happen on a night when the alternative is simply my
own cost, spent alone, the way I have spent every cost in my whole
adult life.

I feel Isla arrive through the bond before I hear her arrive on the
frozen track — a cold that isn't weather, arriving all at once in my
own chest, her fear translated, somehow, into a sensation I recognize
immediately as hers rather than mine, the bond running, for the first
time, in the opposite direction from how it usually runs.

She reaches the cairn at a dead run, having covered three miles in
considerably less time than three miles should properly take, and finds
me upright, barely, one hand — the numb one, useless for anything
requiring feeling, entirely reliable for anything requiring simple,
stubborn contact — fused white to the cracked stone, the cairn holding
by a margin so thin it is not, in any honest accounting, really holding
at all.

"I wasn't going to let it be the one that finally breaks," I say,
through it, which is not, I understand even saying it, the same as
saying I expected to survive making sure it didn't.

"You went without me," Isla says, and I hear, underneath the fury, a
terror considerably larger than the anger itself.

"I didn't want you spending yourself on this one too," I say. "Not
after the last feeding. Not with the rite still unsanctioned."

"That wasn't your choice to make alone," she says, and gets me off the
stone, physically, her own arms considerably stronger than I would have
guessed, given how slight she is against my own weight, and I go where
she pulls me, because I no longer have the strength left to argue the
point, or even, entirely, to stand without her holding me upright.

The cairn holds, for now, cracked and ugly and standing, a scar in the
granite that will never fully close, and both of us understand, in the
long, slow walk back toward Highstone, that we have run out of time to
do this the careful, properly sanctioned way any of us would have
preferred.

"I'm sorry," I say, once we're far enough from the cairn that speaking
feels survivable again. "For going without you. I thought I was
protecting you."

"You weren't," Isla says. "You were doing the exact thing you promised
me you'd stop doing. Paying alone, in the dark, and calling it
protection instead of what it actually was, which was fear, dressed up
carefully enough that even you couldn't recognize it."

I don't have an argument against that, because I understand, hearing
it, that she is entirely correct, and that whatever ground we'd gained
these past weeks — the honesty, the shared counting, the mutual watching
we'd promised each other — I have just, in a single evening's decision,
undone considerably more of it than I intended to.

"We're out of time," I say, instead of arguing. "For the careful
version. For waiting on Ossory's sanction. The cairn's proof enough of
that."

"I know," Isla says, and something in her voice, saying it, frightens
me considerably more than the crack in the stone did, because I
understand, hearing it, that she has already begun doing the
arithmetic on what happens next, and that whatever answer she's
arriving at is not one she is currently prepared to say out loud to me.

·

Tomas is waiting at the cottage door when we finally reach it, having
apparently sat up the whole evening watching for our return, and his
face, seeing the state of us, does something I have not seen it do
since the night Feld's first ultimatum arrived — a specific, total
collapse of composure that he does not bother trying to manage back
into his usual careful calm.

"What happened," he says, already moving to help me get Aldric properly
seated by the fire.

"The Long Reach cairn cracked," Isla says, flatly, exhausted past the
point of softening the report. "Aldric held it alone rather than wake
me."

Tomas looks at me with an expression considerably harder than I've
ever seen from him, closer to genuine anger than the mild disapproval
he generally reserves for my own worse habits.

"That was a terrible decision," he says, plainly, with none of his
usual deference.

"I know," I say, too exhausted to muster any defense of it.

"I don't think you do, actually," Tomas says, "or you wouldn't have
made it. Do you understand what it would have done to her, to all of
us, if that cairn had taken the whole of you instead of merely nearly
taking it? We are not simply managing your condition as an abstract
problem, Aldric. We're managing it because we'd all considerably rather
you stayed alive."

I don't have an argument against a nineteen-year-old's fury, mostly
because I understand, hearing it, that he's entirely right, and that
the whole shape of tonight's decision looks, from outside my own head,
considerably more reckless than it felt making it alone in the dark.

"I hear you," I say. "I'm sorry."

Tomas's anger softens, slightly, seeing the honesty in the apology, and
he busies himself instead with building the fire back up properly,
fetching blankets, doing the ordinary, useful work of caring for
someone in crisis that requires no further argument to accomplish.

"We go to Ossory first thing tomorrow," Isla says, to both of us, once
Tomas has settled and the worst of the immediate cold has been pushed
back from the room. "No more waiting for his usual pace. Tonight
proved we don't have the luxury of it."

Neither Tomas nor I argue with that, and the three of us sit together
by the fire a while longer, the whole small household drawn close
around the specific shared fear of a night that very nearly took
considerably more from all of us than any of us were prepared to lose.

·

Isla does not sleep, once Tomas has finally gone to his own bed and
Aldric has drifted into the particular heavy, exhausted sleep of a man
who has spent everything he currently has to spend. She sits beside
him instead, in the low firelight, doing the arithmetic she has been
avoiding doing plainly since the crack first sounded across the Long
Reach cairn.

I watch her from where I lie, not quite asleep myself, too depleted to
properly speak but not so far gone that I miss the specific stillness
of her, sitting there, working through some decision I cannot yet name.

"You're not sleeping," I manage, finally, my voice considerably
rougher than I intend it.

"Neither are you," she says, "which means you should be, rather than
watching me not do it either."

"I don't think either of us is going to sleep properly tonight," I
say. "Tell me what you're deciding. I can see it on your face, even
this exhausted."

She's quiet a long moment, and I understand, watching her, that
whatever she's about to say has cost her considerable effort to
arrive at.

"I'm deciding," she says, finally, "that we can't wait for Ossory's
comfortable pace any longer. That if the choice is between a careful,
properly sanctioned attempt that arrives too late, and an urgent,
imperfect one that arrives in time, I know which one I'm choosing,
regardless of what either of us promised each other about doing this
properly."

"That frightens me," I admit, "hearing you say it."

"It frightens me too," Isla says. "I've spent this whole crisis
insisting on the careful version, the plainly asked version, the
version with proper witness and proper sanction. I still believe in
all of that. I just don't think we have the luxury of waiting for it
to arrive at its own pace anymore. Tonight proved that, whatever else
it proved."

I don't have an argument against her reasoning, mostly because I share
it, entirely, underneath my own considerable fear of whatever urgency
might cost either of us in the attempting.

"Whatever you decide," I say, "I want it decided together. Not you
alone, working it out in the dark while I sleep. Not me, deciding
something at dawn without waking you first."

"Together," Isla agrees, and takes my good hand, holding it the rest
of the night, neither of us sleeping, both of us simply present, in
the low firelight, for whatever the coming days demand of us.

·

Sela arrives before dawn, having received word from Tomas the moment
we returned, and finds her brother sleeping, finally, exhausted past
the point of waking easily, and sits with him a long while before
turning her attention to me.

"You should have woken me," she says, quietly, meaning the cairn, "the
moment you realized he'd gone."

"I didn't know, until I was already running," I say. "The bond told me
something was wrong. It didn't tell me in time to gather anyone else
first."

Sela absorbs this, nodding slowly, and I watch her decide, visibly, not
to press the point further, understanding, I think, that I am already
carrying more guilt about last night than any further reproach could
usefully add to.

"I'm not angry at you," she says, gentler now. "I want that understood.
I'm angry at whatever in him decided going alone was preferable to
waking either of us. I know that's not a fair anger to hold against a
man who's clearly trying, imperfectly, to unlearn a lifetime of exactly
that habit. I'm holding it anyway, for tonight, because I need
somewhere to put the fear."

"I understand," I say. "I've been holding a version of the same anger
myself, since the moment I found him at that stone."

We sit together, the two of us, in the cottage's early morning quiet,
watching Aldric sleep, both of us holding our own private versions of
the same fierce, frightened love, and I find, sitting there, a
considerable comfort in not being the only person in this arrangement
who refuses to let him carry anything alone.

·

Tomas joins the two of us before the morning's properly begun,
carrying a mug of something warm for each of us, and settles quietly
into the third chair without being asked, the particular unassuming
presence of someone who has learned exactly when a room needs company
and when it needs simply to be occupied without demand.

"I've already sent word to Ossory," he says, quietly, so as not to
wake Aldric. "About last night. I told him plainly what happened,
rather than let the account arrive filtered through whatever the
village grapevine does to a story by the time it's traveled three
households."

"Thank you," Sela says. "That was well done."

"I've been thinking," Tomas continues, "about what I said to him
last night. That he wasn't managing this as an abstract problem." He
glances toward Aldric's sleeping form. "I meant it. But I want you both
to know I understand why he did it. I don't agree with it. I understand
it."

"What do you understand," I ask.

"That being the one who's always managed alone doesn't stop just
because you've decided to try differently," Tomas says. "I think old
habits reach back further than a few weeks of good intentions can
properly counter, especially under this much pressure."

Sela considers this, nodding slowly.

"That's rather more grace than I've managed to extend him this
morning," she admits.

"I'm not extending grace instead of the anger," Tomas says. "I think
both are true at once. I'm angry, and I understand, and neither one
cancels the other out."

We sit together a while longer, the three of us, in the quiet
aftermath of a night that very nearly cost all of us considerably more
than any of us were prepared to lose, and I find myself, watching Tomas
navigate his own complicated feelings with more grace than his years
would suggest he owes anyone, grateful for a young man who has learned,
this whole difficult season, to hold contradiction properly rather
than force it into something simpler than it actually is.

·

Cera Voss arrives not long after, summoned by Tomas's own early
message, and examines Aldric properly while he sleeps, her brisk,
practiced hands checking the worst of the previous night's damage
without waking him.

"He's stable," she says, finally, straightening. "Considerably more
depleted than I'd like, but stable. Whatever you did, getting him off
that stone before it finished taking everything, likely saved his
life outright."

"Will he recover," I ask, "properly, or is this simply delaying the
same outcome."

"I don't know yet," Cera admits, with the same honesty she's shown
from our first meeting. "I know delaying it bought you time you didn't
have yesterday. Whether that time is enough depends considerably on
whatever comes next — and I understand, from what Tomas has told me,
that something considerably more significant may be coming next."

"The mutual form," I say. "If Ossory sanctions it."

"Then I hope he sanctions it soon," Cera says, gathering her things.
"I've treated enough hearths on this line to know that borrowed time
has a way of running out faster than anyone budgets for."

She leaves us with careful instructions for the hours ahead, and I sit
back down beside Aldric, considerably more aware than I was an hour ago
of exactly how narrow the margin has become between the crisis we
survived last night and whatever the coming days still have waiting
for us.

·

Aldric sleeps through most of the remaining day, at Cera's own firm
instruction, and I keep watch beside him well past the point either of
us would call reasonable, exhaustion finally catching up with me
somewhere in the deep, quiet hours of the evening.

"You should rest properly," Sela tells me, gently, finding me still
upright. "He's stable. He's not going anywhere tonight."

"I know," I say, though I don't move.

"Isla." My name, from her, in the specific tone of a woman who has
learned, this whole crisis, exactly when insistence is warranted. "You
won't be any use to him tomorrow if you don't sleep tonight."

She's right, and I know it, and I let her guide me, finally, to my own
bed, and sleep finds me almost immediately, deeper and more complete
than any rest I've properly managed since the cairn first cracked —
a sleep so thorough that I do not hear, until Tomas wakes me with the
note already in his hand, that Aldric has risen before dawn and gone.


## Chapter Thirty-Three — Vow Renounced (Aldric)

I go to Keeper Ossory alone, before dawn, while Isla is still asleep,
and I want to be honest about the reasoning, because I have promised
myself, walking to Highstone in the dark, that whatever else I do this
morning, I will not lie to myself about my own motives the way I have
spent a lifetime managing everyone else's understanding of them, even
though every step of the walk down feels like an argument I am
carefully avoiding having with myself.

The frost has properly set in overnight, thick enough that my own
breath fogs visibly in the pre-dawn dark, and I find myself grateful,
walking, for the cold's specific clarity — a fact demanding attention
plainly enough that it leaves no room for the reasoning I am not yet
ready to properly examine.

I am not asking him for the mutual rite. I am asking him for the old
one, finished, on my own terms, before the crack in the Long Reach
cairn finishes the job some other way, at some other cost, that neither
of us gets to choose the shape of.

I left the note on the kitchen table, weighted under the good mug, in
the specific spot I knew she'd find it first thing, and told myself,
writing it, that this was mercy — a clean explanation rather than a
frightened absence with nothing attached to it. I understand now,
walking the last stretch to Highstone in the pre-dawn cold, that I
chose the spot as carefully as I chose the words, and that carefulness
itself should have told me something about what I was actually doing,
if I'd been willing to look at it plainly before the choosing was
already finished.

"The rite was mine to volunteer for," I tell him, in the rite-hall's
particular pre-dawn cold, considerably emptier than it was the morning
of the binding. "Let it be mine to finish. Don't let her spend herself
proving an untested form to save a hearth that's already this far
gone."

Ossory looks at me for a long moment, weighing considerably more than
my own words.

"You understand what you're asking," he says. "The old rite, completed
properly, takes the whole of what's left. There's no partial version of
it, once begun."

He does not ask, immediately, why I've come alone, though I see the
question sitting in his face, and I understand, watching him hold it
back, that he is extending me a courtesy I have not properly earned —
the space to make this decision without being made to justify, aloud,
exactly why I've chosen to make it without the one person who might
have talked me out of it.

"I understand," I say, and I do, entirely, and I have decided, walking
here in the dark, that understanding it fully and choosing it anyway is
the one genuinely brave thing available to me, rather than the
cowardice I suspect, somewhere underneath my own careful reasoning, it
might actually be.

I understand something else too, standing in that hall, that I have
not fully let myself examine until this exact moment: that this is the
exact corrupted shape of the rite Isla and I swore, together, to
rewrite. One hearth, alone, spent quietly, called noble instead of
named for what it actually is, which is a man too frightened of being
asked to simply wait to be asked.

I understand it, and I proceed anyway, which is perhaps the truest
measure of how deep this particular habit runs in me — that even full,
clear-eyed recognition of the pattern isn't, this morning, quite
enough to stop me from repeating it one final time.

I do it anyway. I ask Ossory to begin the old form, and I write a note
for Isla — because I am not, whatever else I am doing this morning,
willing to let her wake to an absence with no explanation at all,
though I understand now, considerably too late, that a note is not
the same as a presence, and that I chose the smaller, safer offering
precisely because it asked nothing further of my own nerve — and
I hand it to Tomas to deliver once the sun is fully up, and I go with
Ossory to the rite-stone, and I formally, through him, withdraw the
grant I gave Isla the night we asked each other properly at the cairn
field.

It does not feel like betrayal, doing it. It feels, in the moment,
like the single most loving thing I have ever done for anyone.

I understand, considerably later, once it is nearly too late to
understand anything at all, exactly how wrong I was about that.

Ossory's own hand does not waver, beginning the recitation, though I
catch something in his face that might, on another man, be called
reluctance — the specific weariness of a Keeper who has performed this
exact rite too many times to properly believe, any longer, in its own
claimed nobility.

The rite begins to take. I feel it pull at whatever's left of me — not
the specific, located cost of an ordinary ward, something considerably
larger, the whole final measure the old form was always designed to
extract from a single hearth, and I let it, because I have decided,
walking here in the dark, that giving before being asked is the same
thing as love, a belief I have held my entire life and am only now,
with the rite's own considerable weight bearing down on me, beginning
to properly suspect might be the exact opposite of what it's always
felt like from the inside.

I think, somewhere in the taking, of Isla's face the night she told me,
plainly, that she'd rather have the true promise than the clean one. I
think that I have just handed her, instead, the cleanest, most
carefully managed lie I have ever told anyone — a note instead of a
conversation, a decision made unilaterally instead of asked for
properly, the exact thing she made me promise, at the cairn field,
never to do to her.

I understand, as the old rite takes hold in earnest, that I have broken
that promise as thoroughly as it is possible to break one, and that I
am not, any longer, entirely certain I will live long enough to
properly apologize for it.

·

I think, in the strange, distant clarity the old rite's taking seems to
grant a person even as it empties them, about the whole of the reasoning
that brought me here — reasoning that felt, walking to Highstone in the
dark, entirely sound, and that feels, now, considerably less sound with
every measure the stone draws out of me.

I think about Sela, and the specific relief I felt, putting my own name
forward at the rite two winters ago, at finally having a cost I was
permitted to pay openly, rather than the whole accumulated weight of
providing for a household that never once asked me to carry quite this
much of it alone.

I think about my father, managing our whole childhood without ever once
asking either of us what we needed, and understand, with the particular
lucidity of a man running out of time to understand anything further,
that I have just done exactly what he did — decided, unilaterally, that
my own suffering was a private matter, to be managed rather than shared,
regardless of who else's life it was also going to reshape.

I understand, with the same terrible clarity, that he likely felt this
exact same conviction, dying — that he was sparing us something, rather
than simply denying us the chance to properly help carry it. I wonder,
distantly, whether he ever once, in his own final hour, glimpsed the
shape of what I am only now beginning to see.

I think, last of all, about Isla asking me, plainly, at the cairn field,
whether I actually wanted this — the two of us, whatever we've become —
and giving her, in return, the truest yes I have ever given anyone. I
understand, feeling the old rite pull at whatever's left, that renouncing
the grant this morning was not, in fact, an act of love at all. It was
the oldest fear I have, dressed up carefully enough to fool even myself:
the fear of being asked for more than I believed I was worth giving, and
choosing, instead, to give everything at once, alone, so that no one
would ever have the chance to find the asking insufficient.

I do not get to finish the thought properly. The rite's taking has its
own momentum now, considerably larger than my own capacity to reason
against it, and I understand, distantly, that whatever happens next is
no longer entirely mine to decide.

I think, in the strange suspended time the taking allows, about Tomas,
too — about the specific relief on his face when I first showed him
how to properly grip a graft knife, months before any of this began,
long before either of us knew the rite would eventually bind me to the
woman training him. I think about the small carved bird I never got
the chance to finish giving him, the lesson in carving I'd half-promised
and never properly delivered. I think, distantly, that there are a
great many small unfinished things a person leaves behind, dying this
way, that no formal record of the rite ever properly accounts for —
not simply a hearth's cost to a wardwright, but a brother's cost to a
sister, a teacher's cost to a student he never got to finish teaching.

I think, last, about the crooked tree above the orchard's northern
wall, and understand, with a clarity that frightens me more than the
rite's own taking does, that I have spent my whole life keeping things
alive that stopped bearing fruit, on the theory that the keeping itself
was worth something regardless of the fruit — and that I am, this
morning, in this rite-hall, doing precisely the opposite to myself,
choosing to be cut down rather than kept, on the mistaken belief that
my own barrenness would somehow spare everyone else the trouble of
tending me.

I understand, with the last clear thought the rite's taking properly
affords me, that I have gotten the whole lesson of that tree
backward from the very beginning — that keeping a thing alive was
never truly about the fruit at all, and that I am no exception to a
rule I have spent two winters believing applied to everything except
myself.

·

I think, somewhere further into the taking, about the note I left for
Isla, and understand, with a clarity that arrives too late to be of
any use, exactly how carefully I chose every word in it to make the
leaving sound like generosity rather than what it actually was — a
man too frightened to be asked, choosing instead to decide everything
alone and dress the deciding up as a gift.

I think about the specific phrase I wrote — *please don't follow me* —
and understand, only now, feeling the rite's own considerable pull,
that it was never a request at all. It was an instruction, the same
kind my father used to give, gently, lovingly, and completely without
room for refusal, dressed in language soft enough that neither Sela
nor I ever quite noticed, growing up, that we'd never actually been
asked anything at all.

I understand, with the same late clarity, that Isla would have caught
the falseness of it immediately, reading it, the exact hollow center
underneath the careful, generous-sounding words — and that whatever
frightened, desperate hope I had, writing it, that she might simply
accept the note and let me go, was never a hope grounded in who she
actually is. It was a hope grounded in who I needed her to be, this
one morning, so that my own decision could go unchallenged.

She was never going to simply accept it. I understand that now, with
the rite's own weight bearing down, in a way I should have understood
writing the note in the first place, if I had been honest with myself
about what six weeks with her have actually taught me: that she does
not let costs go unnamed, does not let unilateral decisions stand
unquestioned, does not, under any circumstance I have yet discovered,
simply accept a version of events handed to her instead of asked of
her.

I think, with something that might be the beginning of proper regret
rather than the earlier's manufactured nobility, that I owe her
considerably more than an apology, if either of us survives what's
currently taking hold of me. I owe her the plain admission that I knew,
somewhere underneath my own careful reasoning, exactly how she would
respond to that note — and wrote it anyway, hoping, against everything
six weeks should have taught me, that this once, she might simply let
me have my own quiet ending without a fight.

I think, too, about Tomas, and the promise I half-made him without
quite meaning to — that he would inherit a line properly held, rather
than one still shaped by the same corrupted arithmetic that killed
Ossian Thale. I understand, feeling the old rite's considerable pull,
that dying here, quietly, alone, having renounced the very mutual form
that might have spared him the same eventual choice, would teach him
precisely the wrong lesson at precisely the moment he's most vulnerable
to learning it. I would be handing him, as my last act, the exact
inheritance I always meant to spare him — a wardwright's line held by
solitary sacrifice, dressed up as nobility, precisely because nobody
before either of us found the nerve to try it any other way.

I understand, with the rite's own weight bearing down harder now, that
this is not, in fact, only my ending to choose. It never was. Not
Isla's alone to grieve, not Tomas's alone to inherit lessons from, not
Sela's alone to explain to whatever remains of our shrinking family. I
have spent this whole morning believing I was making a private decision
about my own private cost, and I understand now, far too late to
properly act on the understanding, that there has never been a private
version of this available to me at all.

·

There is a strange mercy, I discover, in the particular clarity the old
rite grants even as it empties a person — a mercy I would not have
believed possible, reading about it secondhand in whatever accounts of
the standard rite I've encountered over the years. I find myself, in
the taking's own peculiar suspended moment, remembering things I had
not thought about in years: my mother's specific laugh, a sound I'd
believed entirely lost to memory; the particular satisfaction of a
graft finally taking root after a difficult season; Isla's face, the
first time she properly smiled at something I'd said, unguarded, before
either of us had admitted what was growing between us.

I think, distantly, that if this is how it ends — not tragically, not
with the specific violence I'd always imagined the old rite's taking
must involve, simply this quiet accounting of a life properly lived,
however briefly, however incompletely — then perhaps I have not,
after all, wasted the whole of what I was given.

I do not believe this thought fully. Some part of me, even now, even
emptying, understands that ending here, quietly, alone, having broken
the one promise that mattered most to the person who taught me what
honesty actually costs, would not be a good ending at all. It would
simply be the same old failure, dressed up in a peaceful enough
feeling that I very nearly let myself mistake the dressing for the
substance underneath.

I hear, distantly, a voice raised over Ossory's own recitation, and
understand, even as the old rite's taking briefly falters, that Isla
has arrived, exactly as I feared and precisely as I should have known
she would, refusing, as she has refused everything else this winter,
to let this particular cost be paid without her.


## Chapter Thirty-Four — What She Won't Let Him Pay Alone (Isla)

Tomas wakes me with the note in hand, his face already telling me the
whole of what I'm about to read before I've properly opened it. He has
run the whole distance from the record-room, I understand later, the
moment he found the cottage door standing open and Aldric nowhere in
it, and his own breathing hasn't properly settled even now, standing
at my bedside in the grey pre-dawn light, his knuckles white around
the folded page.

*I renounced the grant,* it says, in Aldric's considerably less
economical hand. *I've gone to Ossory to finish the old rite, on my own
terms, before the crack in the cairn finishes it some other way. I
don't want you spending yourself proving an untested form for a hearth
that's already this far gone. I'm sorry. I love you. Please don't
follow me.*

I read it twice, standing barefoot on the cold floor, my mind doing the
same fast, cold arithmetic it's done every crisis this whole winter —
how long the old rite typically takes, how far the rite-hall sits from
the cottage, how much of that distance I can cover if I stop wasting
time reading a note a third time instead of moving.

I am dressed and running before I've finished reading the last line.

The road to Highstone has never once, in six years of walking it,
seemed this long, and I find myself, running it now, doing the same
arithmetic I did the morning after the byre thawed at Hollow Farm — not
whether the cost is worth paying, simply whether I can possibly move
fast enough to make sure I'm the one who gets to help pay it.

I reach the rite-hall to find Ossory mid-ceremony, the old cadence
already well underway, Aldric standing at the rite-stone with the
specific stillness of a man who has decided, entirely, to let this
particular ending happen to him rather than fight it, and I feel,
through the bond, the whole of what the old rite is currently taking —
not a single scene's cost, the entire final measure, arriving all at
once, and I understand, feeling it, that we have considerably less time
than the note suggested to stop this before it finishes what it's
started.

"Stop," I say, loud enough that it carries over Ossory's own recitation,
loud enough that the whole hall turns to look at a wardwright who has
never once, in six years, raised her voice in this particular room.

Ossory stops. Aldric does not turn, does not look at me, the specific
deliberate refusal of a man who knows, if he looks, he will not be able
to finish what he's started.

The whole hall holds its breath around us, witnesses to something none
of them properly understand yet, and I find I do not care, in this
exact moment, who's watching or what it costs my own professional
standing to be seen shouting down a rite mid-ceremony. Some things
matter considerably more than composure.

"You don't get to decide this alone," I say, walking toward the
rite-stone, saying the thing I have spent this whole arrangement
learning to say instead of silently absorbing whatever cost the world
hands me. "Not the cost. Not the ending. Not what I'm allowed to be
spent on, or protected from, or asked to simply accept without a say in
the matter."

"I renounced the grant," Aldric says, hollow, still not looking at me,
meaning to make it simple, meaning, I understand, to make it
irreversible before I could argue him out of it. "You don't have to—"

"I don't have to," I agree. "I'm choosing to. That's the whole of what
the mutual rite actually means, Aldric — not a debt either of us is
compelled to pay, a choice each of us makes, openly, in front of
witnesses, precisely because it costs something and we've decided,
together, that the cost is worth sharing rather than avoiding. You
don't get to protect me out of choosing it. That's not protection.
That's simply making the choice for both of us, alone, the exact thing
you promised me you'd stop doing."

He turns, finally, to look at me, and I see, in his face, the specific
devastation of a man realizing, in real time, that the thing he
believed was the single bravest act of his life has just been named,
plainly, as its opposite, the whole hall's silence pressing in around
the two of us as though even the old rite-stones themselves were
waiting to hear what he'd say next.

"I'm sorry," he says. "I thought—"

"I know what you thought," I say. "You thought giving before being
asked was love. I understand where that comes from. I understand it
better than almost anyone could, given how long I've practiced the
exact same habit myself. I need you to understand, right now, in front
of Ossory and whoever else is in this hall, that it isn't. It's fear,
wearing love's coat, the same coat I've worn myself for six years."

He does not argue the point further, and I understand, watching the
fight visibly go out of him, that some part of him has known it all
along, underneath the careful reasoning — that the note, the silence,
the whole quiet architecture of this morning's decision was never
protection at all, simply the oldest habit he owns, dressed up one
final time in its most convincing coat.

Ossory, watching two wardwrights argue in his rite-hall over which one
gets to be spent, does something neither of us expects: he calls the
old rite's progression to a halt, formally, and sends for the mutual
form's translation himself.

"I'll hear the mutual form argued properly," he says, "tonight, rather
than let either of you decide anything further alone, in a hall built
for exactly the kind of decision neither of you should be making without
proper witness."

It is not, yet, a rescue. The old rite has already taken considerably
more than either of us would have chosen to spend this morning, and I
do not know, watching Aldric sway slightly where he stands, whether we
have caught this in time. But it is, for the first time since the
crack in the Long Reach cairn, a fight we are both actually in
together, rather than one either of us is attempting to survive alone.

I go to him, finally, now that Ossory's halted the rite properly, and
take his good hand in both of mine, and he lets me, entirely, without
the reflexive flinch of a man still trying to manage how much of his
own weakness I'm allowed to see. It is, I think, the first time all
morning that he has let himself simply be held rather than continuing,
somewhere underneath the surrender, to argue for his own right to be
spent alone.

·

Sela arrives at the rite-hall within the hour, summoned by Tomas the
moment the halt was called, and I watch her cross the whole distance
from orchard to hall faster than I have ever seen her move, arriving
breathless, her face white with a fear she does not bother hiding from
anyone in the room, still in her working clothes, hands still marked
with the morning's grafting wax, having clearly dropped everything the
instant word reached her.

"How bad," she says, to me, direct, the same question she asked the
night the cairn cracked.

"Bad," I say, honestly. "He's stable, for now. Ossory's halted the old
rite before it finished taking everything. But he's spent, considerably,
and we don't yet know if the mutual form can be readied fast enough to
matter."

Sela's composure cracks, briefly, entirely, and I watch her allow
herself the full weight of the fear for exactly as long as it takes to
properly feel it before she gathers herself back into the fierce,
practical woman this whole family has come to depend on.

Sela goes to Aldric's side, kneeling beside where he sits, propped
against the rite-stone's own base, and takes his good hand in both of
hers.

"You absolute fool," she says, with a specific, fierce tenderness that
tells me exactly how much love is folded into the insult. "You could
have told me. Any of it. I'd have come to Highstone myself and sat on
you bodily before I let you walk in here alone."

"I know," Aldric says, weakly, something almost like a smile crossing
his face despite everything. "I think that's rather why I didn't tell
you."

"That's not funny."

"No," Aldric agrees. "It isn't. I'm sorry, Sela. For all of it. For
letting you find out this way, again, the same as every other time
I've decided a cost was mine alone to carry."

Sela doesn't answer immediately, simply holding his hand, her own
composure visibly costing her considerable effort to maintain. When she
finally speaks, her voice is steadier, though I can see what the
steadiness costs her.

"We're doing this properly," she says, "the mutual rite, whatever it
requires of any of us. I don't care what Ossory's usual timeline
prefers. I want my brother alive considerably more than I want this
handled according to anyone's comfortable schedule."

"Agreed," I say, and mean it entirely, and the three of us — Sela,
Aldric, and I — wait together at the rite-stone's base for Ossory's
final word on how quickly the mutual form might actually be attempted,
a small, frightened, determined company, refusing, all of us, to let
this particular cost be paid alone by anyone.

·

Tomas keeps watch at the rite-hall door while Sela goes to find Ossory,
and Aldric and I are left, briefly, with something almost like privacy,
his head resting against the rite-stone's cold base, my hand in his
good one, neither of us saying anything for a long moment because there
does not seem to be anything left worth managing between us.

"I need you to promise me something," I say, finally, "and I need you
to actually mean it, this time, rather than simply telling me what
you think will let me stop worrying long enough to leave you alone
again."

"Anything," he says, and I hear, underneath the single word, exactly
how much he means it, chastened in a way no lecture of mine could have
produced as thoroughly as this morning's near-catastrophe already has.

"If we survive the mutual rite today," I say, "you don't get to decide,
ever again, that a cost is yours alone to manage quietly. Not the small
ones. Not the large ones. Not even the ones you're certain I'd tell you
not to worry about. I would rather be woken at any hour, for any reason,
than find another note in Tomas's hand telling me you've already decided
something on my behalf."

"I promise," Aldric says. "Properly, this time. Not the version of
myself that believes his own reasoning right up until the exact moment
it very nearly kills him." He turns his head, slightly, to look at me
properly, the effort of it visibly costing him something. "I understand
now what you meant, that first week, about the difference between being
protected and being managed. I thought I understood it. I didn't, not
really, until I was the one lying against a rite-stone realizing I'd
done to you exactly what I promised I'd never do."

"I forgive you," I say, "conditionally, on the promise actually holding
past today's fear."

"That's fair," he says. "I'd forgive me conditionally too."

I laugh, despite everything, a short, startled sound that surprises me
more than it seems to surprise him, and he manages something like a
smile in return, weak but genuine, and for a moment, in the cold
rite-hall with the whole outcome of the day still entirely unresolved,
it feels almost bearable, sitting here together with nothing left
between us to manage.

"Whatever happens at the cairn," I say, "I want you to know I'd rather
have had these six weeks with you, exactly as difficult and exactly as
frightening as they've actually been, than the whole quiet, careful,
unbound life I had before you arrived on that road."

"I feel exactly the same," Aldric says, "though I'd have preferred
arriving at saying so without quite this much blood loss involved."

"Next time," I say, "we'll manage the confession part without the
near-fatal old rite attached to it."

"I'll hold you to that," he says, and closes his eyes, not sleeping,
simply resting, and I sit with him, holding his hand, until Sela's
returning footsteps announce that Ossory is on his way back with
whatever answer he's spent the last hour deciding.

·

Tomas slips back inside briefly, checking on us before returning to his
post at the door, and I catch him looking at Aldric with an expression
that isn't quite anger anymore, though it isn't quite forgiveness
either.

"I'm not ready to say I've forgiven you," he tells Aldric, plainly,
"for the note. I want that understood before whatever happens next at
the cairn."

"That's fair," Aldric says, weakly. "I don't think I've forgiven myself
yet either."

"I'm frightened," Tomas admits, "of what happens if the mutual form
doesn't work, after everything. I wanted to say that properly, rather
than manage it silently the way apparently everyone in this hall has
spent their whole lives doing."

"You're allowed to be frightened," I say. "We all are. I think that's
rather the only honest way to walk into what's coming."

"Is there anything I can actually do," Tomas asks, before turning back
to the door, "besides simply keeping watch?"

"Keep believing this is going to work," I say. "I know that's not
practical, in the way you'd probably prefer. It's what I actually need
from you right now."

"I can do that," Tomas says, with more conviction than his frightened
face quite manages, and I understand, watching him steady himself
against his own fear for my benefit, exactly how much this whole
crisis has already asked of someone barely out of boyhood.

Tomas nods, absorbing this, and returns to his watch at the door, and
I find myself, in the brief quiet that follows, grateful for a young
man young enough to say his fear plainly, rather than manage it the
way the rest of us have spent a lifetime learning not to.

·

Ossory returns within the hour, considerably more decisive than his
own usual careful pace generally allows, and I understand, watching
his face, that whatever internal deliberation he's just concluded, it
has cost him something to move this quickly.

"I have spent every spare hour since you first brought me this record
verifying what I could of its provenance," he says, "and I find myself
unable, in good conscience, to let procedural caution cost a man his
life when the alternative has already very nearly killed him once this
morning. I will sanction the attempt. Today. At the Long Reach cairn,
where the crack already stands as its own kind of evidence that the
standard rite has run out of road."

"Thank you," I say, and the words feel entirely insufficient for the
weight of what he's just decided to risk on our behalf.

"Don't thank me yet," Ossory says, echoing, almost word for word, the
exact sentiment I have heard offered, in one form or another, by
everyone who has ever handed me something significant this winter
without full certainty of the outcome. "I am sanctioning an attempt,
not guaranteeing a result. I
have no proof this form is survivable, only a dead woman's careful
account and my own considerable hope that hope is not, in this
instance, foolish."

"That's more than we had an hour ago," Aldric says, from where Sela is
helping him to his feet, considerably steadier than he was, though
still visibly diminished by the morning's near-catastrophe.

"Then let's not waste the daylight we have left to attempt it properly,"
Ossory says, and the whole small company — Ossory, Tomas, Sela, Aldric,
and I — set out together for the Long Reach cairn, carrying between us
considerably more hope than any of us arrived at the rite-hall with
this morning.


## Chapter Thirty-Five — The Great Northern Cairn (Isla)

Ossory sanctions the attempt before midday, once the translation has
been checked against every fragment of the old record I can produce and
found, in every particular that matters, sound — not proven, not
certain, but sound enough, given the alternative currently standing
in front of him barely able to hold himself upright, to warrant the
risk, and I feel the whole morning's fear finally begin to loosen its
grip, if only slightly.

We go to the Long Reach cairn together, all of us — Ossory, Tomas, Sela
sent for and arrived breathless from the orchard, the whole small
company of people who have, across these past weeks, become
considerably more than witnesses to whatever this arrangement has
turned into.

The cairn stands cracked, exactly as we left it, granite split along
the fault line that nearly took the whole northern stretch of line down
with it three nights ago, and I understand, looking at it, that this is
precisely the right stone for what we're about to attempt — not a
clean, whole cairn, a wounded one, considerably more honest about what
the old rite has already cost than any undamaged stone could manage.

"Ask me again," Aldric says, at the stone, his voice considerably
weaker than it was even yesterday, but steady, entirely certain. "Ask
me properly. Unbound. So there's no version of tonight where either of
us claims the other one didn't choose it."

I ask him. I ask him the same question I asked at the cairn field,
weeks ago, in the same plain words, in front of witnesses this time
instead of only each other, and he answers, plainly, entirely, the same
yes he gave me then, considerably more costly to give now, with so
little of himself left to spend on the giving.

He asks me back, the same question, and I give him the same answer,
and both grants are renewed there, at the stone, chosen and mutual,
witnessed properly by the people who matter most to both of us, rather
than negotiated privately where no one else could confirm the asking
actually happened.

We lay both hands on the cracked stone together — his good hand and
mine, since the numb one no longer answers reliably enough to trust
with anything this significant — and I speak the predecessor's mutual
rite whole, for the first time in generations, the old words
considerably stranger in my mouth than the familiar cadence I've spoken
sixty times at this exact stone, alone, over six years.

The stone takes from both of us, evenly, for the first time in the
northern line's long, corrupted history.

I feel it the moment the balance shifts — not the one-sided draining
I've watched happen to Aldric a dozen times now, a genuine sharing,
cost halving rather than concentrating, drawn from both hearths
together the way the predecessor's faded record always insisted it
should be. It is not painless. I feel my own reserves pull, properly,
for the first time in two years of quiet, unlogged self-spending, a
cost that is entirely, finally, mine to feel rather than mine to
silently absorb and call nothing.

Aldric goes pale, but not the specific grey of a hearth being emptied
entirely. Considerably less, this time, than the old rite would have
taken, spread across two people instead of concentrated into one.

The cairn holds.

Not perfectly — the crack remains, a permanent scar in the granite that
will never fully close, a visible record of what the old rite very
nearly finished before we stopped it. But it holds, standing, on terms
neither the Rime nor the old corrupted form ever offered either of us
before: a cost shared rather than spent, survivable rather than merely
delayed.

Ossory examines the stone for a long moment, running his own
practiced hand over the crack, over the specific quality of the charge
now settled properly into the granite.

"It's genuine," he says, finally, something in his voice that sounds,
to my ear, almost like relief. "The mutual form. It's genuine, and it
holds."

Tomas is crying, I notice, unashamed, and Sela has both hands pressed
to her mouth, and I understand, looking around at the small company
gathered at this cracked, ordinary, extraordinary stone, that we have
just done something considerably larger than save one man's life. We
have handed the whole northern line a future it did not have this
morning.

Aldric sits down, finally, not the hard, uncontrolled collapse of the
first ward we set together, a deliberate, careful descent, and I sit
beside him, and neither of us says anything for a long while, simply
holding each other up, on new terms, entirely chosen, in front of
everyone who matters, exactly the way it was always meant to be done.

·

Sela is the first to properly break the silence, kneeling beside her
brother, both hands pressed to his face, checking, the way she must
have checked him for fever a hundred times in childhood, for whatever
signs might tell her, faster than any healer's assessment, exactly how
much of him has actually survived the whole ordeal.

"You're warm," she says, something between a question and a
declaration. "Properly warm. Not the fever-warmth. The actual kind."

"I feel warm," Aldric says, testing the observation the way you test a
fact you're not yet ready to fully believe. "I haven't felt properly
warm in weeks."

Ossory examines the cracked stone a while longer, running careful hands
over the whole of the fault line, murmuring what sounds like a private
verification of his own, before turning back to the small company
gathered at the cairn.

"I will need to write this properly into the crown's record," he says,
"and I suspect Feld is going to have a great deal to say about a rite
performed without his office's prior sanction. I find, having watched
what I've just watched, that I am considerably less concerned with
Feld's opinion than I have been in forty years of practice."

"Thank you," I say, "for hearing it properly, when you could have
simply dismissed the whole idea as a desperate wardwright's last
theory."

"I've buried too many hearths on this line," Ossory says, "to dismiss
anything that might actually change the arithmetic. I only wish I'd had
the nerve to go looking for an alternative decades sooner, before your
own mentor paid the old rite's full price without ever getting the
chance you've just given each other."

Tomas, standing slightly apart with the translated record still
clutched in both hands, looks, in this moment, considerably more like a
wardwright than an apprentice — steady, watchful, already, I suspect,
turning over everything he's just witnessed for whatever lesson it
holds for whichever winter, eventually, becomes his own to hold alone,
or, I hope now, considerably less alone than mine has been.

We stay at the Long Reach cairn a while longer, the small company
gathered there in the cold, none of us in any particular hurry to
return to the ordinary business of the day, all of us simply grateful,
for this one suspended moment, that the stone held, that the cost
shared, that whatever comes next for the northern line, it arrives with
two hearths standing to meet it instead of one.

·

The walk back to Highstone, when we finally make it, is slower than
any of us intends, everyone reluctant, I think, to let the particular
suspended peace of the cairn field give way too quickly to the
ordinary business of a life resuming.

Tomas walks beside me for most of it, quiet in the specific way of a
young man processing something considerably larger than his years have
previously required of him.

"I want to ask you something," he says, eventually, "and I want an
honest answer, not a reassuring one."

"Go on."

"Was I any use, today? Genuinely. Not simply present, taking up space
while the actual work happened around me."

I stop walking, and turn to look at him properly, understanding that
this question deserves my full attention rather than a distracted
answer offered mid-stride.

"You were considerably more than useful," I say. "Your translation work
gave Ossory the confidence to sanction an attempt he'd never have
risked on my word alone. Your steadiness, these past weeks, gave both
Aldric and me somewhere solid to stand while everything else felt
uncertain. I don't think today happens, not the way it happened,
without you."

Tomas's composure cracks slightly at that, the particular unguarded
relief of a young man who has been quietly terrified, this whole
crisis, that his own contribution amounted to nothing more than
watching from the sidelines.

"Thank you," he says, "for saying that properly, rather than simply
letting me assume."

"I'll keep saying it," I say, "as often as you need to hear it, until
you believe it as thoroughly as I do."

·

Sela falls into step beside me a little further on, once Tomas has
drifted ahead to walk with Ossory, and says nothing at all for a long
stretch, simply matching my pace, the two of us watching Aldric walk a
few yards ahead, steadier with every step than he's been in weeks.

"I owe you an apology," she says, eventually.

"For what?"

"For the first weeks," Sela says. "For treating you like a stranger
who'd been handed my brother by accident, rather than someone who
might actually turn out to be exactly what he needed. I was frightened
for him, and I let the fear make me unkind to you, in ways I don't
think I've properly acknowledged."

"You weren't unkind," I say. "You were protective. I understood it,
even at the time."

"I was unkind," Sela says, firmly, "and I'd like to actually say so,
rather than let you extend me the same generous reading you extend
everyone else in this family. I judged you before I knew you, on the
theory that anyone the rite handed him was better treated as a threat
until proven otherwise. I was wrong to do that. I'm glad, more than I
can properly say, that you turned out to be exactly the opposite of a
threat."

I consider her words for a moment, watching Aldric's steady progress
ahead of us, the whole shape of these past weeks settling into
something I can finally look back on without the specific dread that
accompanied most of it as it happened.

"Thank you for saying so," I say. "I'd rather have the honest
apology than the comfortable pretense that there was never anything
to apologize for."

"That seems to be the whole family motto now," Sela says, with the
first real lightness I've heard in her voice all day. "Honesty over
comfort. I'm still getting used to it, but I find I prefer it,
considerably, to how carefully we all used to manage each other
instead."

"So do I," I say, and the two of us walk the rest of the way to
Highstone together, easier with each other than we've been since the
very first night I arrived in this village, two women who have each,
in their own way, learned this winter exactly how much a family can
hold when it finally stops managing itself in careful, well-meaning
silence.

·

Ossory catches up with the two of us partway to the gate, having
lingered behind at the cairn a while longer to properly record the
day's events for the crown's own archive.

"I want to say something, Wardwright," he says, "that I couldn't
properly say back at the stone, with everyone still absorbing what
we'd just witnessed."

"Go on."

"I've spent forty years administering a rite I privately doubted,"
he says, "and I told myself, every single time, that doubting quietly
was the responsible position for a man in my role to hold. I understand
now that it wasn't responsible at all. It was simply comfortable,
dressed up as caution." He looks at me directly. "I want you to know
that watching what happened at that cairn today has cost me something
too — not the same cost as either of yours, but a genuine reckoning,
regardless. I don't think I'll administer this office the same way
again."

"Thank you," I say, "for telling me that, and for being willing to
change, this late in a long career."

"Don't thank me yet," Ossory says, with a small, wry acknowledgment of
his own frequent refrain. "I have considerable institutional damage
still to properly undo. But I intend to start trying, and I wanted you
to hear that intention directly from me, rather than simply watch it
unfold, eventually, in whatever new precedents I manage to establish."

We walk the rest of the way to Highstone together, the three of us,
and I find myself, listening to Ossory turn over his own reckoning
aloud, considerably more hopeful about the whole institution's future
than I've allowed myself to be in the whole of my six years serving it.

·

Cera Voss meets us at Highstone's gate, having heard, through whatever
channel keeps the whole village reliably informed, that something
significant had happened at the Long Reach cairn, and takes one look at
Aldric's face before speaking.

"You're steadier than I expected," she observes, already moving to
examine him properly.

"The mutual form held," I say. "Properly. Cost shared, rather than
concentrated."

Cera's examination is thorough, brisk, exactly as I've come to expect
from her, and she straightens, finally, with something like genuine
relief crossing her usually composed features.

"This is remarkable," she says. "I've treated hearths on this line for
twenty years. I've never once seen one recover color this quickly after
a rite's taking. Whatever you did today, it's changed the whole shape
of what's medically possible for this line's future."

"Will you help us document it properly," I ask, "for whoever comes
after us?"

"I'd be honored to," Cera says, with a warmth I haven't heard from her
before. "I've spent considerably too many years treating the aftermath
of the standard rite's cruelty. I'd like to spend the years I have left
treating the aftermath of something considerably kinder instead."

We continue on toward the cottage together, Cera's own genuine
enthusiasm adding one more voice to the small, growing chorus of people
determined to make certain today's discovery outlasts the crisis that
produced it.

·

Reny Ashe stops us at the gate, having heard, already, some early
account of the day's events, and studies Aldric's face with her usual
plain directness before speaking.

"You look considerably more alive than you did this morning," she
observes, to him.

"I feel considerably more alive," Aldric says.

"Good," Reny says, with her usual finality, and presses a fresh twist
of herbs into my hand, unbidden, the same small ritual she's
performed since the very first night. "For celebrating properly,
rather than simply collapsing from the relief of it."

"Thank you, Reny," I say, "for every single one of these, since the
beginning."

"Someone had to keep steadying your nerves," Reny says, "seeing as you
were too occupied holding up forty miles of stone to properly steady
them yourself." She waves us on before either of us can properly
