thank her further, and I find myself, walking the last stretch home,
grateful for a village that has, in its own small, unglamorous ways,
been holding us both up considerably longer than either of us properly
noticed.

·

Ida catches us near the keep's own small kitchen, drawn out, I suspect,
by whatever word has already traveled through Highstone's particular
grapevine, and presses a still-warm loaf into my hands before either
of us can properly protest.

"Eat something proper," she says, "both of you, before you collapse
from all the relief you're currently running on instead of an actual
meal."

"Thank you, Ida," I say, accepting the loaf gratefully.

"Don't thank me," Ida says, echoing the whole village's apparent
consensus on gratitude offered too plainly. "Just keep doing whatever
you did today. I've fed wardwrights on this line for three generations.
I'd like to feed considerably more of them, going forward, than I've
managed to feed in the past."

We continue home with the warm loaf between us, grateful, once again,
for the specific, unglamorous care of a village that has been quietly
sustaining us both since long before either of us properly noticed it
happening.


## Chapter Thirty-Six — Cost Shared (Aldric)

I wake with feeling returned to three fingers I had, privately, written
off for good, and a cold in the other two that the healer says will
likely never fully thaw — a mark, not a cure, and neither Isla nor I
pretends, examining the hand together in the grey morning light, that
it's anything other than exactly that.

The morning light itself seems different, somehow, filtering through
the cottage's small window — not changed, I understand, turning the
thought over, simply properly noticed, for the first time in longer
than I can accurately place, by a man who has stopped bracing for
whatever the day intends to cost him.

"You're not fixed," Isla says, meaning the hand, meaning the whole of
what the old rite very nearly finished before we stopped it.

"I know," I say. "I wasn't asking to be fixed. I was asking to still be
here, which is a considerably lower bar, and one we've actually
cleared."

CE-01 and CE-03 close together, in the quiet accounting either of us
does without needing to say it aloud: the debt I took on binding myself
for Sela's exemption, discharged now mutually rather than paid alone,
the threat of my being spent entirely to death answered, finally, by a
rite that no longer asks one hearth to carry what two can properly
share.

Isla feels her own cost too, this morning, properly, for the first time
in two years of quietly spending herself uncounted — tired in a way
that has real weight attached to it rather than the specific shame
she's carried, silently, since long before I ever knew her.

"How do you feel," I ask, "honestly. Not managed."

"Tired," she says. "Properly tired, the way I haven't let myself be
tired in longer than I can accurately place. It's not an unpleasant
feeling, oddly. It feels like a debt finally being called in, rather
than left to compound indefinitely in the dark."

We sit with the shared ache of it for a long while, in a cottage that
has, I notice, fully and finally rearranged itself around two people
rather than one — not simply the second chair, the second mug, the
small accumulated evidence of these past weeks, but something
considerably deeper, a settling that has nothing left to hide behind
and no reason, any longer, to try.

"I need to apologize properly," I say, "for the note. For going to
Ossory alone. I told myself I was protecting you. I understand now that
I was simply doing the exact thing I promised you I'd stopped doing,
dressed up carefully enough that I nearly convinced myself it was
something else."

"I know," Isla says. "I forgave you for it already, standing in that
rite-hall, watching you realize what you'd actually done. I don't think
forgiving you means pretending it didn't happen, though. I think it
means we both understand, now, exactly how easily either of us could
slide back into the old habit, given enough fear and not enough
practice at the new one."

"Agreed," I say. "I'd like considerably more practice at the new one,
if you're willing to keep teaching me."

"I'm willing," Isla says, "on the condition that you extend me the same
patience. I suspect I'll need considerably more practice at being
watched than you'll need at being asked."

We sit together a while longer, in a cottage full of morning light and
the specific ordinary sound of a kettle beginning, slowly, to properly
boil, and I understand, watching her lean into the warmth of the fire
rather than merely tolerating it the way she used to, that whatever
this arrangement was six weeks ago — a rite neither of us chose, a
binding neither of us asked for — it has become, entirely, something
considerably more deliberate: two hearths, marked by the same winter,
choosing, openly, on purpose, to keep sharing whatever it costs to
hold the line a little longer.

·

Ossory visits the cottage himself, a few days later, an occurrence rare
enough that I am considerably more startled by his knock than by
whatever news he might be bringing.

"I wanted to bring this to you directly," he says, producing a
formally sealed document, "rather than let it reach you through
Feld's office first, filtered through whatever spin his particular
ledger prefers to put on things."

The document is the crown's own formal recognition of the mutual rite,
properly registered, properly precedented, with a clause I read twice
to be certain I've understood it correctly: any future binding on the
northern line may, at the wardwright's own discretion, be performed
under either form, with the mutual form no longer treated as an
irregular exception requiring special sanction each time it's
attempted.

"You did this," I say, understanding the specific weight of what he's
just handed me. "Argued for this, against however many objections the
crown's usual process would have raised."

"I argued for what I watched with my own eyes," Ossory says. "A rite
that works, properly, without costing one hearth everything to spare
the crown's own convenient arithmetic. I'm an old man, Wardwright. I've
buried enough hearths under the old form to have earned the right, at
this point in my career, to argue loudly for something better."

"Thank you," I say, and mean it more than the two words properly carry.

"Don't thank me yet," Ossory says, with the first genuine trace of
humor I've heard from him in six years of dealing with his careful,
formal manner. "Feld is going to be exceedingly tedious about the
paperwork this generates. I expect you'll both need considerable
patience for several months of bureaucratic complaint before the whole
matter properly settles."

"I think we can manage tedious," I say, still recovering but steadier every day, "considerably better
than we managed the alternative."

·

Cera Voss visits the following week, at my own invitation, to examine
Aldric's hand properly and give us both her honest assessment of how
much of it might eventually recover.

"The three fingers that regained feeling first are healing well," she
says, working through her examination with the same brisk competence
she's shown from our first meeting. "The two that took longest to
number, I suspect, will stay marked permanently. Cold-sensitive, likely,
for the rest of his life. Not useless. Simply changed."

"I can live with changed," I say.

"I'd expect you to," Cera says, with a dry humor I haven't heard from
her before. "I've treated enough hearths on this line to know that
'changed but alive' is considerably more than most of them ever get
to say." She looks between the two of us. "I want it on record, since
apparently everyone's keeping records these days, that I'll be
recommending the mutual form to the crown's healing office as standard
practice for any future binding on lines like this one. What you've
done isn't only a wardwright's victory. It's a considerably better
outcome for the whole practice of medicine on the northern frontier."

"Thank you," I say, "for saying so, and for treating him properly
through the whole of it."

"Don't thank me," Cera says, echoing the whole village's apparent
consensus on the correct response to gratitude this large. "Just keep
doing what you're doing. I'd like to retire, eventually, having treated
considerably fewer hearths dying of quiet, unnecessary sacrifice than
I did in my first twenty years of practice."

Ossory allows himself something almost like a smile at that, and takes
his leave, and I sit for a long while afterward with the formal
document in hand, understanding that this small, unglamorous piece of
crown paperwork represents, in its own quiet way, the whole of what
this winter's terrible cost has actually purchased: not simply two
lives spared, but a future, properly precedented, for every wardwright
and hearth who comes after us on this line.

·

Sela comes by that same evening, ostensibly to return a borrowed pot
but plainly, once she's settled by the fire, for something else
entirely.

"I've been thinking about what you said," she tells me, "about sliding
back into old habits, given enough fear. I want to say something to
both of you, while it's fresh, rather than let it go unsaid the way
this family tends to let important things go unsaid."

"Say it," Isla says, from the other chair, mending something small by
the firelight.

"I spent this whole autumn managing the household's arithmetic alone,"
Sela says, "the same way Aldric's spent his whole life managing his
own cost alone, and I told myself it was simply competence, not fear
wearing competence's coat. I watched the two of you nearly die of the
exact same habit, dressed up more dramatically than my own version of
it, and I don't want to wait for my own version to cost me something
this large before I learn the lesson properly."

"What would learning it properly look like," I ask, "for you
specifically?"

Sela considers the question with real seriousness, the same gravity
she's brought to every hard conversation this whole difficult season.

"Telling you when the grain stores are lower than I've let on," she
says. "Asking Tomas to help with the ledgers instead of pretending I
don't need it. Letting someone else carry a piece of the household
before I'm already buckling under the whole of it." She looks between
the two of us. "Small things. I don't have a rite to rewrite. I just
have the same old habit, and I'd like some practice unlearning it
while the stakes are still small enough to survive getting it wrong."

"We'd be glad to help," I say, "the same way you've helped us."

"I know," Sela says. "That's rather why I came to say it out loud,
instead of simply resolving it privately and hoping the resolution
held on its own."

We sit together a while longer, the three of us, the pot returned and
long forgotten on the table, and I understand, watching Sela finally
let herself be simply tired in front of us rather than composed, that
whatever this winter has cost the whole household, it has also handed
back something none of us quite had before it: the plain, practiced
habit of saying the difficult thing out loud, to the people equipped
to help carry it.

·

Tomas arrives partway through the evening, having finished the day's
reports, and finds the three of us still settled by the fire, Sela's
own admission still hanging warmly in the room's particular quiet.

"Am I interrupting something," he asks, hesitating at the door.

"Come in," Isla says. "We're simply practicing honesty. Sela's been
teaching us all a lesson in it tonight."

"I could use more practice at that myself," Tomas admits, settling in.
"I've been meaning to say something, actually, and I keep finding
reasons to put it off."

"Say it now," Sela says, "since apparently that's the whole theme of
the evening."

Tomas takes a breath, considering his words properly.

"I've been frightened," he says, finally, "that once the crisis
passed — once the two of you were properly safe — I'd become less
necessary. That whatever purpose I had here was tied specifically to
the emergency, rather than to anything more permanent."

"That's not true at all," I say, immediately, understanding exactly
how much courage that admission cost him.

"I know that, intellectually," Tomas says. "I still feel it, some
evenings. I wanted to say it plainly, rather than let it manage itself
quietly, the way apparently none of us are supposed to do anymore."

"You're not less necessary," Isla says. "If anything, you're more. The
crisis is passing. The work of actually building something worth
protecting — that's only just beginning, and I don't intend to do it
without you."

Tomas's composure cracks slightly at that, the particular relief of a
young man who has been quietly carrying a fear he'd convinced himself
was unreasonable to voice.

"Thank you," he says, "for saying it plainly."

"Thank you for asking," Sela says, "instead of simply assuming the
worst and managing the fear silently. I think that's rather the whole
lesson this family's finally learning, all of us, together."

We sit a while longer, the whole small household settled around the
fire, considerably more certain, all of us, that whatever comes next
will be built in company rather than carried alone.

·

Isla and I walk Tomas partway back to Highstone that same evening, the
night gone properly clear and cold, and I find myself, watching him
walk ahead of us a few paces, turning over how much this young man has
come to matter in the space of a single difficult season.

"Do you remember," Isla asks, "the first week, when he still called
you 'the wardwright's hearth' rather than by name?"

"I remember," I say. "I don't think he trusted me yet, that first
week. I don't blame him for it."

"He trusts you considerably now," Isla says. "I don't think either of
us properly appreciates how much he's had to grow, this winter,
alongside the rest of us."

"I've been thinking about that," I admit. "About what we owe him, once
all of this settles. Not simply gratitude. Something more concrete."

"What did you have in mind?"

"I don't know precisely," I say. "I think I'd like to make sure he
never has cause to doubt his place in this family again, the way he
apparently doubted it tonight. Whatever that requires, going forward."

"I think that's exactly right," Isla says, and we watch Tomas glance
back at us, checking, I suspect, that we're still following, and wave
when he catches us watching, entirely unselfconscious in a way that
makes something in my chest settle properly for the first time all
evening.

We walk the rest of the way in comfortable silence, three people bound
by considerably more than circumstance now, and I find myself grateful,
watching the frost catch the last of the evening light, for a family
that has grown, this winter, well beyond anything either Isla or I
could have properly imagined six weeks ago.

·

Tomas turns back once more before parting ways at Highstone's gate,
something clearly still weighing on him despite the evening's earlier
reassurance.

"One more thing," he says. "I want to properly thank you both. Not
simply for tonight. For the whole winter. I don't think I'd have
survived it half as well, arriving at whoever I'm becoming, without
both of you treating me as family rather than merely an apprentice
managed at arm's length."

"You were always going to be family," Isla says. "I don't think either
of us properly said so plainly enough, until recently."

"I know that now," Tomas says. "I wanted to say the thanks properly
too, though, rather than simply receive the reassurance and let the
gratitude go unspoken on my own end."

"Thank you for saying it," I say, "and for being exactly the kind of
person worth saying it to."

He heads through the gate at last, considerably lighter, and Isla and
I walk the final stretch home together, both of us carrying the whole
evening's accumulated warmth alongside everything else this difficult,
wonderful winter has taught us about a family built by choice rather
than simply circumstance.

·

"I keep thinking," Isla says, once we've reached the cottage's own
quiet, "about how much this family has grown, without either of us
properly deciding it should. I don't think I set out, six weeks ago,
to gain a sister and an apprentice's whole devoted heart alongside
whatever this is between the two of us."

"Neither did I," I say, "though I find I wouldn't trade any part of
it, given the choice again."

"Not even the frightening parts?"

"Especially not those," I say. "I think the frightening parts are
rather what taught all of us how much we actually mean to each other."

Isla considers this, settling against me by the fire.

"I think you're right," she says, finally, and we sit together a while
longer, grateful for a family assembled entirely by choice and crisis
in equal measure, considerably larger and considerably more precious
than either of us arrived at this winter expecting to gain.


## Chapter Thirty-Seven — What the Line Costs Now (Isla)

Feld returns for his figures a week after the mutual rite, expecting,
I suspect, to find precisely the failure his ledger has been quietly
budgeting for since our first meeting.

He finds instead a rite-record with two names on it, properly witnessed
by Ossory and sealed with the crown's own formal process for
recognizing precedent, and a season's worth of cairns still standing to
prove the method actually works, rather than merely theorizes about
working.

"This isn't sanctioned," Feld says, which is, technically, no longer
even true, though I understand he means something closer to *this
isn't what I expected to be arguing with today.*

"It's precedented," Ossory says, before I have to. "Older than the
version the crown's been funding, as it happens. I'd be glad to discuss
the historical record with whoever above you wants to argue geology
with a standing stone that's already survived the argument once."

Feld leaves with considerably less than he came for, and no date set
for abandoning anything, and no plan, any longer, for Tomas that isn't
entirely his own to choose. It is not a victory with a bow on it. It is
not, by any measure Feld's ledgers would recognize, complete. But it is,
this once, entirely enough.

"Sela's exemption stands regardless," I tell Aldric that evening,
having confirmed the specific clause with Ossory myself. "Whatever
happens to either of us going forward, her protection no longer depends
on any single hearth failing quietly to hold it up."

"That's the part I actually wanted to hear," Aldric says, and I
understand, hearing the particular quality of relief in his voice, that
this — his sister's safety, secured on a foundation that doesn't
require his own continued sacrifice — matters to him considerably more
than any formal recognition the crown's own process could offer either
of us.

·

Sela visits the cottage a few days later, her own record book in hand,
and lays out, for the first time properly to both of us together, the
full shape of what the household's finances look like now that the
exemption no longer hangs on anyone's individual binding.

"It's not comfortable," she says, "but it's survivable, which is
considerably more than I was budgeting for a month ago." She looks at
Aldric for a long moment. "You could have told me, you know. Any of it.
The exemption, the binding, how close you actually came to finishing
the old rite alone. I've spent this whole autumn worrying about numbers
in a ledger when I should have been worrying about you specifically."

"I know," Aldric says. "I'm working on that. Isla's teaching me."

"Good," Sela says, with the specific satisfaction of a sister who has
waited considerably longer than she'd like to hear her brother admit,
plainly, that he needs teaching in anything at all. "Because I intend
to keep asking, from now on, rather than waiting for you to volunteer
it. You've had entirely too much practice getting away with the old
method."

I watch the two of them settle into something considerably warmer than
the careful distance I first witnessed in the grain-house, weeks ago,
and understand that whatever this winter has cost all of us, it has
also, somewhere in the spending, handed the Marrow family something
they didn't have before: a household that no longer has to guess, in
silence, what any of its members are actually carrying.

·

Reny Ashe stops me at the Highstone gate, a few days later, with the
same directness she showed the morning after the binding, though the
particular quality of her attention has shifted considerably in the
weeks since.

"The whole village is talking about the mutual rite," she says, wrapping
a second twist of herbs and pressing it into my hand, unbidden, the way
she did the very first night. "Some of them frightened by it. Most of
them, I think, rather hopeful. It's not every season the northern line
gets handed genuinely good news."

"What do you think of it," I ask, curious, given her earlier plain
assessment of Aldric had proven so accurate.

"I think," Reny says, "that you've done what your mentor never managed
— found a way to actually share this line's weight, instead of simply
absorbing it until it finally took you the way it took him. I think
that's worth considerably more to this village than any single ward
you've ever set alone."

"I didn't do it alone," I say. "Aldric. Tomas. Sela. Ossory, in the
end, more than I expected of him."

"I know," Reny says. "That's rather my whole point, Wardwright. You
used to do everything alone, on principle, as though needing help were
some kind of professional failing. I'd say this whole winter's taught
you otherwise, and I'd say the line's considerably better for the
lesson, regardless of what it cost to learn it."

I think about her words the rest of the walk home, turning them over
alongside everything else this strange season has taught me, and find,
reaching the cottage, that I am, for the first time in longer than I
can accurately place, genuinely looking forward to whatever ordinary
evening waits for me inside it.

·

I stop at the healer's cottage on the way home, not for any urgent
reason, simply because I find I want to properly thank Cera Voss for
her part in all of it, rather than let the thanks go unsaid the way
this whole village apparently prefers to leave its gratitude
unspoken.

"You didn't need to come by," Cera says, though she waves me in anyway,
setting aside whatever ledger of her own she'd been working through.

"I wanted to," I say. "You treated him properly, the whole way through,
and you never once made either of us feel like a burden for needing
it. I don't think I said that plainly enough, in the middle of
everything."

"You said it plainly enough," Cera says. "You just said it in the
middle of a crisis, which tends to get lost underneath everything
else demanding attention at the time. I'll take it properly now,
though, since you've apparently decided to say it twice."

We sit together a while, two women who met properly for the first
time over a dying man's hand and found, somewhere in the weeks since,
something like an easy friendship neither of us had particularly gone
looking for.

"Can I ask you something," Cera says, eventually, "professionally,
rather than simply out of curiosity?"

"Of course."

"Would you be willing to let me document the whole progression,
properly — the numbness, the mutual rite's effect on halting it,
the recovery since? Not for gossip. For the crown's healing office,
so the next hearth who finds themselves in Aldric's position has
an actual medical record to draw on, rather than simply your own
account and Ossory's formal precedent."

"I'd be glad to," I say. "I think Aldric would say the same. We've
both decided, this whole winter, that the honest record matters more
than whatever privacy it might cost us to keep it."

"Good," Cera says, satisfied, already reaching for a fresh page. "I'll
want to examine him again properly, next week, once I've drafted the
preliminary account. In the meantime — tell him from me that the two
fingers are healing as well as any cold-marked hand I've treated. He
should stop expecting them to suddenly announce themselves worse
overnight. Some wounds simply finish healing at whatever pace they've
already settled into."

"I'll tell him," I say, and take my leave, considerably warmed by the
whole small exchange, understanding that whatever this winter has cost
the village at large, it has also handed Highstone a healer who has
decided, somewhere in the treating, that this family is worth
returning to for reasons well beyond the professional.

·

Aldric is waiting at the cottage door when I arrive, a small parcel in
his good hand, considerably more nervous than the size of the parcel
seems to warrant.

"What's this," I ask.

"Open it," he says, and I do, and find, inside, a small carved bird,
considerably more finely made than the crude wooden one we brought back
from Calder's Reach, clearly carved by a practiced hand rather than an
amateur's loving effort.

"I've been working on it," Aldric admits, "since we got back. Learning
to carve properly with the hand as it currently is, rather than
waiting for it to be whatever it used to be. It's not as good as
what a whole hand could manage. I wanted to give you something anyway,
rather than waiting for a version of myself I'm not certain is coming
back."

I turn the small bird over in my hands, feeling the specific care in
every uneven cut, and understand, holding it, exactly what it actually
represents — not a perfect craft, a choice, made deliberately, to keep
creating rather than let the numbness become an excuse to stop.

"It's beautiful," I say, and mean it entirely.

"It's not perfect."

"It doesn't need to be perfect," I say. "It needs to be yours, made
with whatever hands you currently have, which is precisely what it
is."

Aldric doesn't have an immediate answer to that, and I don't need one.
I simply set the small bird on the shelf beside Ossian's own copied
ledger, a small, permanent reminder of exactly what this winter has
taught both of us about making do, properly, with whatever we currently
have to work with, rather than waiting for some imagined wholeness that
was never actually the point.

·

Tomas discovers the small bird a few days later, examining it with the
particular careful attention he gives anything he suspects carries more
weight than its size suggests.

"You carved this," he says to Aldric, understanding immediately, the
way he understands most things about the two of us by now.

"I did. With the hand as it currently is."

"It's good," Tomas says, turning it over. "Properly good, not simply
good for a man managing a partial hand. I didn't know you carved."

"I didn't, particularly, before this winter," Aldric admits. "I'm
learning. Slowly. The hand insists on its own pace, regardless of what
I'd prefer."

"I'd like to learn," Tomas says, somewhat shyly, surprising both of us.
"Carving, I mean. Not for any practical reason. I think I'd simply like
to make something with my own hands that isn't a report or a
translation."

"I'd be glad to teach you," Aldric says, and I watch something settle
between the two of them — not quite the same bond Tomas shares with me,
something else entirely, a young man finding, in my own partner, a
second kind of mentor he didn't know he needed until the offer arrived.

They spend that same evening with a spare block of wood and Aldric's
own careful, patient instruction, and I sit by the fire watching the
two of them work, considerably more moved than the small domestic scene
probably warrants, by the sight of this whole found family continuing
to grow in directions none of us properly planned for.

·

Tomas's first attempt at carving is, by his own cheerful admission,
considerably worse than Aldric's, a lopsided shape that resembles
nothing in particular, and he holds it up for inspection with the
specific pride of someone entirely unbothered by the result's quality.

"It's terrible," he announces, delighted.

"It's a start," Aldric says. "Mine looked worse, the first month. I
simply didn't have anyone watching to document the evidence."

"I'm keeping this," Tomas says, "regardless of quality, so I can
compare it properly to whatever I manage a year from now."

"That's a good instinct," I say. "Keeping the early, imperfect version,
rather than only the polished one."

"I learned it from you," Tomas says, "keeping Ossian's ledger instead
of only the tidy summary. I've decided the same principle probably
applies to most things worth doing properly."

He sets the lopsided carving on the shelf beside the small bird Aldric
made me, an odd, mismatched pair that somehow looks, sitting there
together, exactly right, and I find myself, watching the two carved
birds keep each other company, grateful for a household that has
learned, this whole difficult season, to treasure the imperfect
beginnings of things alongside their eventual, hard-won mastery.

·

Aldric walks Tomas back to Highstone that evening, once the carving
lesson properly concludes, and I use the quiet hour alone to sit with
Ossian's ledger one final time, rereading the passages I've now
practically memorized, searching, out of habit more than genuine
expectation, for anything further his careful hand might still have to
teach me.

I find, instead, a small pressed flower tucked between two pages near
the very back — long faded, unlabeled, the kind of thing a person
keeps without ever explaining why, even to themselves. I turn it over
carefully, understanding that I am looking at some private grief or
private joy of Ossian's own that I will now never properly know the
shape of, a small mystery that will simply remain his, regardless of
how thoroughly I've come to know the rest of his careful, economical
hand.

I press it back exactly where I found it, deciding, closing the ledger,
that some things are better left as the private property of the person
who kept them, even after that person is gone — a small, respectful
mercy I extend him now that I understand, better than I once did, how
much a person's unexplained keepings can matter.

Aldric returns not long after, and I show him the pressed flower before
setting the ledger properly back on its shelf.

"Do you think we'll ever know what it meant to him," he asks.

"No," I say, "and I've decided that's alright. Some things are allowed
to stay his, even now. I don't think I need to understand every single
thing about a person to have loved them properly."

"That's a generous way to hold a mystery," Aldric says.

"I've had a good teacher in generous mysteries," I say, "this whole
difficult, wonderful winter," and we bank the record-room's lamp
together for the night, carrying Ossian's small, unexplained keeping
alongside everything else this season has taught us to hold with care.

·

I tell Tomas about the pressed flower the next morning, curious what
he'll make of it, and watch him consider the small mystery with real
seriousness.

"I think I'd want to know," he admits, "if it were me. I don't think
I'd be able to simply let it stay unexplained."

"I understand that impulse," I say. "I think I've simply learned,
watching this whole family this winter, that not every mystery is
mine to solve. Some belong to the person who kept them, even once
they're gone."

"That's a generous instinct," Tomas says. "I hope I learn it eventually,
though I confess I'm still rather better at chasing answers than
leaving them be."

"There's room for both instincts in this line," I say. "Yours for
chasing, mine for occasionally letting things rest. I think we balance
each other properly, this way."

Tomas considers this, nodding slowly, and goes back to his own ledger,
and I find myself, watching him work, grateful for an apprentice
curious enough to keep asking and wise enough to respect an answer
that isn't his to have.

·

Aldric mentions, later that same day, that he's decided to leave
something of his own in the ledger drawer eventually — not a mystery,
exactly, simply a private record of this whole winter, written
plainly, for whoever comes looking someday the way I went looking
through Ossian's own careful hand.

"I'd like that," I say. "I think future wardwrights deserve considerably
more honesty about what this line actually costs than either of us
were given, starting out."

"That's rather the whole point," Aldric agrees, "writing it down
properly, rather than letting each generation rediscover the same hard
lessons from nothing."

We spend a portion of the evening together, sketching out what such a
record might eventually contain, and I find myself, watching him take
the idea seriously enough to actually begin drafting it, grateful for
a partner determined to leave this line's next inheritor considerably
more than either of us was given ourselves.


## Chapter Thirty-Eight — Tomas's Choice (Isla)

Tomas asks the question carefully, the following week, in a voice
trying visibly hard not to sound like it's hoping for a particular
answer.

"Am I still needed," he says, "now that the line holds on new terms?
I've spent three years training to be a spare hearth, in case the
worst happened. I'm not certain what I'm training to be, now that the
worst didn't."

"You were never needed as a spare hearth," I say. "You're needed as the
next wardwright, on whatever timeline actually suits you, which was
always the job I meant to offer you, however badly Feld's ledgers
managed to obscure that underneath their own contingency planning."

He doesn't quite believe it right away, the specific hesitation of a
young man raised, these past months, on a countdown, who doesn't fully
trust a clock that's finally stopped its urgent ticking. I let him take
the time to believe it properly, rather than insisting, because I have
learned, this whole strange season, that some truths need room to
settle before they can be trusted.

"I'd like to learn the mutual form," Tomas says, eventually. "Properly.
From the start, this time, not stitched together out of a dead woman's
journal at the last possible hour, the way you and Aldric had to learn
it."

"That's the job," I agree, and mean, underneath it, that the whole
northern line is finally being handed forward properly, to someone
with the time to learn it right, rather than merely surviving from one
crisis to the next the way it has for considerably longer than my own
six years of practice.

"Will you teach me the whole history of it," Tomas asks. "Not just the
mechanics. The reason it was corrupted in the first place. I think I'd
understand the form better if I understood why anyone thought hiding
it was necessary."

"I'll teach you," I say. "All of it. I think you're right that the
history matters as much as the mechanics, this time."

·

I write, that same week, a proper letter to whatever surviving family
Fenhollow's own lost wardwright might have left behind, on the small
chance that some record of the second village's fate has survived
somewhere I haven't yet thought to look. It feels, this time, less like
the avoidance Aldric once gently named in me, and more like simply
continuing the work properly — one lost village at a time, one
predecessor's memory at a time, each of them deserving the same careful
attention I finally gave Ossian's own.

Tomas offers to help with the correspondence, and I let him, watching
him draft his own careful, formal inquiry with a precision that tells
me, plainly, exactly how far his own training has come since the
autumn's first uncertain months.

"Do you think we'll ever properly recover this line," he asks, sealing
the letter, "all of it. Every lost cairn, every lost village. Or is
some of it simply gone, the way Fenhollow's gone, past any recovering."

"I don't know," I say, honestly. "I think some of it's gone for good. I
think the honest work, going forward, is holding what's left properly,
rather than pretending we can undo what the Rime's already taken. But I
also think — and I didn't believe this, six months ago — that holding
what's left properly, together, in company, is worth doing regardless
of how much has already been lost."

Tomas considers that, nodding slowly, and I understand, watching him,
that he is filing the observation away the same careful way he files
everything I hand him that matters — not simply as instruction, but as
something to carry forward, rather than merely inherited by accident.

He goes back to his reports afterward, considerably lighter than he's
been in weeks, and I watch him go with the specific satisfaction of a
woman who has, for the first time in three years, been able to hand her
apprentice a future that isn't shaped like a contingency plan for
someone else's failure.

·

Aldric finds me in the record-room that evening, going through Ossian
Thale's old ledger properly for the first time since his death,
copying the essential entries into my own hand at last, the specific
grief-work I have avoided for seven years finally, quietly, underway.

"You're copying it," he says, watching me work.

"I decided it was time," I say. "I've spent seven years refusing to
copy his handwriting into my own, on the theory that copying it would
mean admitting he wasn't going to write in it again. I understand now
that the admitting doesn't erase him. It just means I'm finally, properly,
carrying what he taught me forward, instead of leaving it locked in a
dead man's hand where nobody else can properly learn from it."

"That sounds like Tomas's situation," Aldric observes. "Being handed
something to carry forward, rather than merely inherited by accident."

"It is," I agree. "I think that's rather the whole of what this winter
has actually been about, underneath everything else — learning which
costs are meant to be carried forward, properly, in company, and which
ones were only ever meant to be set down."

·

Aldric finds me still in the record-room, later that same evening,
Ossian's ledger properly copied now into my own careful hand, the old
handwriting and the new sitting side by side on the shelf where anyone
who comes after either of us can properly learn from both.

"How does it feel," he asks, "finished."

"Lighter," I say, surprised by how true the word is, saying it. "I
didn't expect that. I thought copying it would simply be a chore, a
formality I owed his memory. It's turned out to be considerably more
like actually saying goodbye — properly, this time, rather than the
version I managed at his funeral, surrounded by people thanking me for
a job I didn't want to be thanked for."

"I'm glad," Aldric says, and sits beside me, looking at the two hands
of writing together on the shelf. "I think I understand, now, why you
wouldn't copy it before. It wasn't simply avoidance. It was that
copying it meant admitting the story had an ending, and you weren't
ready, yet, to let it."

"I don't think I was," I agree. "I think I needed someone to help me
find the ending that actually fit, rather than the tidy, managed
version I'd have written alone."

"Is this the ending that fits?"

I consider the question properly, looking at the shelf, at Tomas's own
empty space beside it where his eventual ledger will one day sit, at
the whole long chain of wardwrights this line has held and lost and
will, someday, hold and lose again.

"I think it's an ending," I say, finally. "I don't think any of this
ever properly finishes. I think the line just keeps being held, by
whoever's standing at it, for as long as they're able, and the best any
of us can do is make sure the next person inherits something a little
kinder than what we were handed ourselves."

Aldric takes my hand, the marked one, and we sit together a while
longer in the record-room's particular quiet, two hearths who have
each, in their own way, finally learned to set down the weight that was
never meant to be carried entirely alone.

·

Tomas finds the two of us there, later, and looks at the shelf with
its two hands of writing, considering something for a long moment
before speaking.

"I'd like to start my own ledger," he says, "properly, rather than
waiting until I'm the one holding the line entirely alone. I think I'd
like to write down what this winter actually taught me, while it's
still fresh, rather than let it fade the way I suspect memory generally
does, given enough years to wear it smooth."

"That's a wise instinct," I say. "What would you write first?"

Tomas considers this properly, the particular gravity he gives most
questions that matter to him.

"That the line isn't held by one wardwright," he says, finally. "It's
held by a wardwright and everyone who loves her enough to notice when
she's spending herself past what's sustainable, and to say so, plainly,
before it's too late to matter. I think that's the actual lesson,
underneath the mechanics of any particular rite. I'd like that written
down properly, so whoever eventually apprentices under me starts with
that understanding already in hand, rather than having to learn it the
hard way, the way all three of us did."

I don't have anything to add to that, watching him settle in at the
record-room table with a fresh ledger of his own, beginning, in his
own careful hand, the next chapter of a history that will, I hope,
someday read considerably kinder than the one that came before it.

·

Aldric finds me still lingering in the record-room doorway, watching
Tomas write, and slips an arm around my waist, easy in a way that
still, some mornings, surprises me with how natural it's become.

"You're proud of him," he says. Not a question.

"Considerably," I say. "I keep waiting to stop being surprised by how
much he's grown into this, and I keep being surprised anyway."

"I think that's simply what it looks like, watching someone you love
become who they were always going to become," Aldric says. "I felt the
same, watching Sela finally stop treating her own exhaustion as a
private failing. I suspect I'll feel it again, watching you finally
learn to rest without needing a crisis to force the lesson."

"Is that a prediction or a request?"

"Both," Aldric admits, "though I'm prepared to be patient about the
timeline. I've learned, this whole winter, that some lessons take
longer to properly land than others."

We stand together in the doorway a while longer, watching Tomas fill
the first page of his own ledger with the same careful attention
Ossian once gave his, and I feel, watching the three generations of
this line's history sit side by side on one modest shelf, a settling
in my chest that has nothing at all to do with the day's ordinary
business and everything to do with a future I no longer have to face
entirely alone.

"Thank you," I say, "for making me want to rest, eventually, even if I
haven't quite managed it yet."

"Eventually is enough," Aldric says. "I'm not in any hurry. I intend to
be here for considerably longer than eventually takes."

·

We walk home together that evening, leaving Tomas still bent over his
new ledger's first page, and I find myself, partway down the track,
thinking about the whole strange arc of the past several months — a
rite neither of us chose, a binding that could have remained simply an
arrangement, and the whole unlikely family it's somehow assembled in
its wake instead.

"Do you ever think about what would have happened," I ask, "if the
rite had never named you at all? If Feld's original contingency had
simply gone forward, some other hearth entirely?"

Aldric considers the question seriously, the way he considers most
things I ask him properly.

"I try not to," he admits. "Not because the thought frightens me,
exactly. Because I don't think that version of events leads anywhere
worth imagining. I don't think you survive it, the way you were
managing things before I arrived. I don't think Tomas gets the kind of
apprenticeship he deserves. I don't think Sela ever properly learns to
ask for what she needs."

"That's rather a lot resting on one rite's particular choice."

"I think it is," Aldric says. "I've stopped finding that frightening,
lately. I've started finding it something closer to grace — that out
of every possible hearth the rite could have named, it happened to
name the one person capable of actually changing how the whole
arrangement worked, for everyone it touched."

"I don't think it was only you," I say. "I think it took both of us,
properly, choosing to do this differently, rather than simply
inheriting the old pattern because inheriting it was easier."

"Both of us, then," Aldric agrees, and takes my hand for the rest of
the walk home, the whole line stretching quiet and held around us in
the deepening evening dark.

·

We pass the small shrine near the boundary between Highstone and the
orchard on the way home, the same weathered marker where Aldric once
left an offering of grafting wax, and I find myself stopping there
without quite deciding to.

"Do you mind," I ask, "if I leave something too."

"Not at all," Aldric says, watching with quiet curiosity.

I leave a small sprig of the frost-hardy herb from our own garden,
the same variety I laid at Ossian's grave, and stand a moment with the
particular reverence I've come to associate with this whole winter's
accumulated griefs, properly named rather than merely endured.

"What did you leave it for," Aldric asks, gently.

"For everyone this whole line has cost," I say, "and for everyone it
didn't quite manage to take, this time. I think I wanted a place to put
both feelings at once, rather than choosing between grief and
gratitude as though they couldn't coexist."

"I don't think they're opposites," Aldric says. "I think this whole
winter's taught me that most of the important feelings sit closer
together than the tidy version of any of them suggests."

"I think you're right," I say, and we continue on toward home together,
the shrine's small offering settling into the dusk behind us, one more
quiet marker in a winter that has, somehow, taught this whole family to
properly name what it costs and what it gives in equal measure.

·

We find Sela waiting at the cottage when we finally arrive, an unusual
enough occurrence at this hour that I understand, seeing her face, that
something particular has prompted the visit.

"I wanted to tell you both something," she says, "before I lose the
nerve again."

"Go on," I say, gently.

"The person I mentioned, weeks ago," Sela says, "the one who taught me
what actually matters in choosing to build a life alongside someone.
I've decided I'd like to properly explain who that was, rather than
keep circling it in careful half-references."

"Only if you want to," I say.

"I want to," Sela says, and tells us, plainly, about a woman from a
neighboring village, met at a market some years back, a quiet
courtship that ended not from any falling-out but from circumstance
and timing that never quite aligned. "I don't regret it. I learned
considerably more from loving her, briefly, than I learned from years
of carefully avoiding the risk afterward."

"Thank you for telling us," I say, understanding exactly how much
courage the telling required.

"I wanted to," Sela says. "I've watched the two of you practice this
whole winter what happens when you finally say the true thing out
loud, instead of managing it quietly forever. I decided I wanted to
practice it too, properly, with the people I trust most."

We sit together a while longer, the three of us, and I find myself,
watching Sela finally set down a private grief she's carried
alone for years, grateful for a season that has taught this whole
family, one difficult confession at a time, that being truly known is
worth considerably more than the safety of staying carefully hidden.

·

"Do you ever think about finding her again," I ask Sela, once some of
the evening's weight has properly settled, "now that circumstance and
timing might align rather differently than they did before."

Sela considers this seriously, turning the question over.

"I've thought about it," she admits. "I don't know that I'm ready yet.
I think I wanted, first, to simply say her name aloud to people who
love me, properly, before I decided what to do with the wanting
itself."

"That's a wise order to do things in," I say.

"I've had good teachers, this winter, in wise ordering," Sela says,
with a small, genuine smile, and we sit together a while longer,
grateful for a sister brave enough to take the first difficult step
toward however the rest of that particular story eventually unfolds.


## Chapter Thirty-Nine — Grafting Days (Aldric)

Spring is not close, not truly — Coldwake does not release its hold on
the northern line simply because one cairn's crisis has passed — but
the orchard has stopped sounding like a countdown and started sounding,
again, like a place things are grafted rather than merely endured.

I notice the difference most in the quality of the quiet up here now —
no longer the held-breath silence of a man waiting for the next
crisis, simply the ordinary hush of an orchard going about its own
patient business, regardless of what season presses in around it.

I graft a new branch onto the crooked tree from my childhood, the one
that survived a hard frost two winters back and stopped bearing fruit
for its trouble, working around two fingers still slow to properly
answer me rather than pretending they aren't there at all, a
compensation I have stopped hiding from Isla or from myself.

"Name this one," I say, handing her the grafting knife, an act of trust
considerably larger than the simple gesture looks — I have never, in
my whole life, handed anyone else the naming of a graft I've made
myself.

Isla considers the branch for a long moment, turning the knife over in
her own hand with the specific care of a woman who understands exactly
how much this particular offer costs me to make.

"I'll name it for the day the cairn held," she says, finally. "Not the
day it cracked. The day after — the day we walked back from it, both
of us, still standing, on our own feet, uncertain whether either of us
would survive the winter and finding, regardless, that we had."

"That's a good name," I say, and mean it, watching her make the first
careful cut into the graft, precise, unhurried, the way she does most
things she's decided are worth doing properly.

Sela watches the two of us from the grain-house door, a basket of the
season's last stored apples balanced on one hip, and says nothing at
all, which is, from her, the highest praise currently available.

"You've gone soft," she calls over, eventually, unable to resist for
long. "Both of you. It's disgusting. I approve entirely."

"You're welcome to disapprove instead," I call back, "if the approval's
costing you too much dignity to maintain."

"I'll manage," Sela says, and goes back inside, and I catch Isla
smiling at the exchange in the specific unguarded way she's stopped, in
these past weeks, trying to hide from either of us.

We work the rest of the afternoon on smaller grafts, ordinary orchard
business, nothing urgent, nothing costed against any ledger either of
us is currently keeping, and I find myself, watching her hands learn
the unfamiliar work of grafting alongside my own more practiced ones,
considerably happier than I have any memory of being before this
particular winter arrived and rearranged the whole of my life around a
woman I did not choose and would not, now, given the choice again,
choose any differently.

"Tell me something," Isla says, later, as the light starts to fail and
neither of us has made any move to head back down toward Highstone
yet. "If the rite had bound you to someone else entirely — some other
wardwright, sight unseen, the way it very nearly could have — do you
think you'd have found a way to love them too? Or was this always,
somehow, specifically about the two of us?"

I think about it seriously for a long moment, because the question is
too large for a quick, comfortable answer.

"I don't know," I say, honestly. "I think I'd have found a way to be
decent to whoever the rite named. I don't think decency and this" — I
gesture, vaguely, at the whole of the orchard, the graft, the two of
us standing in it together — "are the same thing at all. I think this
specific version of it required you, specifically. Your particular
stubbornness. Your particular way of running cold on purpose and
warming, slowly, anyway, once you'd decided someone was worth the
warming."

"That's a very generous answer," Isla says.

"It's an honest one," I say, and watch her decide, this once, simply to
accept the honesty rather than examining it for a seam that isn't
actually there.

·

We walk down from the orchard as the light finally fails, Sela's lamp
already lit in the grain-house window behind us, and stop at the exact
spot on the road where I first found Isla on the road below Highstone,
weeks ago, both of us still strangers then, still running our own
private, cold arithmetic about exactly how much the other one might
cost.

"Do you remember this spot," I ask.

"I remember," Isla says. "You had grafting wax in your satchel and
entirely too much patience for a woman who'd already decided, before
you'd said a single word, that she was going to be difficult about the
whole binding."

"You weren't difficult. You were careful. I understood the difference
even then, though I don't think I'd have said so at the time."

"I was frightened," Isla says, plainly, in the way she says most
things now, without the careful management she once gave every
admission. "Of you, a little. Of what letting anyone matter that much
again might cost me, after six years of making very certain nobody
had the chance."

"And now?"

"Now I think the cost was worth considerably more than the fear
suggested it would be," she says, and takes my hand, standing on the
exact spot where this whole improbable arrangement first properly
began, and I understand, holding it, that whatever this winter has
taken from either of us, it has handed back something I would not
trade for any version of my life that didn't include this particular
woman, on this particular road, choosing, every single day since, to
keep choosing me back.

·

We stop by the orchard on the walk home, mostly out of habit now
rather than any specific errand, and find Sela in the grain-house,
finally, properly at rest for once, a cup of tea going cold beside her
untouched ledger.

"You're not working," I observe.

"I'm allowed not to be working," Sela says, without looking up, though
I catch the small smile she's not quite managing to hide. "I've decided,
this week, to practice the same thing you two keep insisting is so
important. Resting properly, before exhaustion makes the decision for
me."

"That's remarkably wise, for someone who spent this whole autumn
insisting she didn't need anyone's help managing the household's own
arithmetic."

"I contain multitudes," Sela says, echoing, I suspect without knowing
it, some turn of phrase she's picked up secondhand from one of us over
these past months, and goes back to her cold tea, entirely unbothered.

We sit with her a while, the three of us, in the grain-house's quiet
evening warmth, and I find myself, watching Isla and my sister trade
easy, comfortable observations about nothing in particular, considerably
more at peace than I have any memory of being in the whole of my adult
life — not because anything's been fixed, precisely, but because the
whole shape of what family and partnership and honest, shared burden
actually look like has finally, properly, settled into something I
recognize as home.

·

I ask Sela, before we leave that evening, what she'd meant, weeks ago,
by her small private smile about wisdom gained from experience rather
than mere theory.

She considers the question for a long moment, and I watch her decide,
finally, to answer it properly.

"There was someone," she says, "a few years back. Before all of this.
It didn't last, for reasons that don't matter anymore, but it taught me
something about what actually matters in choosing to build a life
alongside another person, rather than simply beside them. I've been
quietly grateful, watching the two of you this winter, to see that
lesson practiced properly, by people who seem rather better suited to
each other than my own attempt turned out to be."

"I'm sorry it didn't last," I say, gently.

"Don't be," Sela says. "I learned what I needed to learn from it. I'm
not in any hurry for another attempt, but I'm not opposed to one
either, whenever it happens to arrive. I've simply decided, watching
you two, that I'd rather wait for something that fits properly than
settle for something that merely fills the space."

"That's a wise policy," Isla says.

"I learned it from watching you refuse to settle for a managed version
of anything," Sela says, "which I suppose makes you responsible for it,
in your own roundabout way."

We leave the orchard that evening with considerably more warmth in our
chests than the cold night accounts for, grateful, all of us, for the
particular found family this winter has assembled, imperfectly,
determinedly, out of a rite none of us chose and a season none of us
would trade away now that it's properly, finally, behind us.

·

I stop at Ossian Thale's old grave the next morning, on my way back to
the orchard, a visit I have never once made in six years of living at
Highstone, mostly because Isla never invited me and I never presumed to
go without her.

She finds me there, unexpectedly, having apparently decided the same
morning to make the same visit, and neither of us says anything for a
moment, simply standing together at the plain, unmarked stone that
serves this line's wardwrights in place of anything grander.

"I've never brought anyone here before," she says, finally. "Not once,
in six years. I think I was afraid that showing anyone this particular
grief would mean admitting how much of it I was still carrying,
unresolved, underneath everything else I managed to hold together."

"I'm glad you're showing me now," I say.

"So am I," Isla says. "I think I finally understand something about
grief I didn't understand before this winter — that carrying it alone
doesn't actually make it lighter. It just makes it lonelier."

We stand together at the grave a long while, saying nothing further,
and I find myself, watching her lay a small sprig of frost-hardy herb
against the stone — the same herb we salvaged together in her own
garden, weeks ago — considerably moved by the specific quiet ceremony
of it, a private grief finally given a proper, shared witness.

"Thank you for showing me this," I say, as we finally turn to leave.

"Thank you for asking to see it," Isla says, "rather than waiting for
me to offer, the way I might have waited indefinitely if left to my
own old habits."

We walk back to the orchard together, hand in hand, the whole morning
considerably lighter for the small, deliberate act of finally sharing
a grief that has, for six long years, been hers to carry entirely
alone.

·

Sela is waiting for us at the orchard gate, an expression on her face
that suggests she's been turning something over since early morning.

"I want to try something," she announces, without preamble. "I've
decided, watching the two of you finally learn to ask each other for
things properly, that I'd like to practice the same skill. Small
scale. Nothing dramatic."

"Go on," Isla says, clearly intrigued.

"I'd like help with the spring planting," Sela says, "properly, rather
than managing it entirely myself the way I have every year since
Father died. I don't need it, strictly. I'd simply like it, and I've
decided wanting help isn't the same as needing rescue, however long
it's taken me to separate the two ideas."

"We'd be glad to help," I say, and mean it, watching my sister ask for
something plainly, without the usual armor of self-sufficiency she's
worn for as long as I can remember.

"Good," Sela says, satisfied, already moving on to practical matters.
"I'll want the northern beds turned first, and someone competent with
the seed-drill, since neither of you has shown any particular aptitude
for that specific tool yet."

"High praise," Isla says, dryly.

"I don't give praise I don't mean," Sela says, echoing her own earlier
words, and the three of us set to discussing the spring's practical
business together, a small, ordinary planning session that feels,
underneath its plainness, like one more piece of evidence that this
family has finally learned to build things in company rather than
alone.

·

Isla lingers at the grain-house door once Sela's gone in to fetch the
seed inventory, and I find her watching the orchard's northern rows
with a particular thoughtful stillness.

"What is it," I ask.

"I was thinking about the crooked tree," she says. "About what it
might mean, come spring, if you tried grafting it properly this time,
rather than simply letting it stand as a monument to what the frost
took."

"I hadn't considered that," I admit, surprised by the suggestion. "I
think I'd assumed it was past grafting, permanently."

"Maybe it is," Isla says. "Maybe it isn't. I don't know enough about
orchards to say for certain. I only know that a great many things I
assumed were permanently past recovering, this winter, turned out not
to be, given proper attention and enough patience."

I consider the crooked tree properly, turning her suggestion over with
more seriousness than my first reaction offered.

"I'd like to try," I say, finally. "Even if it fails. I think I'd
rather attempt it and fail than simply assume, forever, that it
couldn't have worked."

"That seems to be the whole lesson of this entire winter," Isla says,
"stated plainly, about a tree instead of a rite."

"Perhaps it's the same lesson, regardless of what it's about," I say,
and we stand together a while longer at the grain-house door, watching
the orchard's bare winter rows, both of us already looking forward,
somehow, to whatever spring might still prove possible.

·

Tomas arrives that same afternoon with news of his own, considerably
excited for a young man who usually delivers his reports with careful
professional restraint.

"The waystones are holding better than projected," he announces. "All
seven, properly within tolerance, no signs of the strain we were
bracing for after the Long Reach cairn's own crisis."

"That's excellent news," I say, genuinely relieved.

"I think it's the mutual form," Tomas says. "Not directly — the
waystones don't draw on either of you specifically. But I think the
whole line's charge has stabilized, somehow, now that the northern
stretch isn't quietly bleeding one hearth dry. I don't have the proper
theory worked out yet for why that would be true. I intend to develop
one."

"I'd like to see that theory, once you have it," I say, genuinely
curious.

"I'll write it up properly," Tomas says, with the particular pride of
a young man who has found, this winter, a genuine intellectual passion
alongside his practical duties. "I think there's real scholarship in
understanding exactly how the mutual form's effects ripple outward,
beyond the specific cairns it's performed at."

"You might be the one who finally writes that scholarship down," Isla
says, joining us, "properly, for whoever comes after you."

"I'd like that," Tomas says, considering the prospect with visible
pleasure, and the three of us spend a portion of that afternoon
speculating together about a whole future of careful study that none
of us could have properly imagined attempting, six weeks ago, in the
middle of a crisis none of us yet knew how to survive.

·

Sela joins us partway through the afternoon's speculation, listening
with visible interest to Tomas's own excited theorizing about the
mutual form's wider effects.

"You've found your calling," she observes, once he's finished a
particularly animated point.

"Maybe," Tomas admits, somewhat shyly. "I hadn't expected to find real
scholarship interesting, before this winter. I assumed I'd simply
learn the practical mechanics and leave the theorizing to whoever
came before me."

"There's no reason you can't do both," Sela says. "Practice and
theory aren't opposites, any more than duty and desire are, apparently,
this family's whole running theme this winter."

"I like that framing," Tomas says, pleased, and the four of us
continue the afternoon's conversation together, considerably more
hopeful, all of us, about a future built on genuine curiosity rather
than merely careful survival.


## Chapter Forty — Cost Shared Openly (Isla)

The line has crept another mile since first frost, because the Rime
has never once, in eighty-eight days, given any of us reason to believe
it would stop advancing simply because we found a better way to bear
its cost. Forty-one cairns still stand anyway — the same number as the
morning of the binding, though I understand now, considerably more
plainly than I did that morning, exactly how much has changed
underneath a number that has, on its surface, stayed exactly the same
for the whole of this long, difficult winter.

Eighty-eight days. I have counted them, this time, properly, the way
I once counted only cairns and charges — not out of fear, the way I
counted in the beginning, but out of a specific, deliberate gratitude,
each day another one neither of us was fully certain, back at the
Long Reach cairn's cracked stone, that we would actually get to have.

Aldric and I set the season's last ward together at the Long Reach
cairn, the crack in the stone still visible under a fresh dusting of
frost, the cost between us ordinary now, in the way a shared thing
becomes ordinary once it has finally stopped being a secret either of
us feels the need to manage alone.

I trace the crack with one bare hand before we begin, the way I've
taken to doing at every visit since the day it very nearly took him
entirely, a small private ritual that has nothing to do with the
rite's own requirements and everything to do with properly remembering
what this particular stone has already cost, and spared, in equal
measure.

Tomas and Ossory both come out to witness it — not because the rite
requires witnesses for an ordinary seasonal ward, but because Ossory
has decided, since the mutual form's sanctioning, that every ward set
under it should be properly recorded, for whichever wardwright inherits
this line after both of us are done holding it.

Sela comes too, though nothing about a seasonal ward strictly requires
her presence, standing a little apart with the particular quiet pride
of a woman who has watched her brother survive a season she once
feared would simply take him the way it took their father, and I catch
her eye across the small gathered company, and she smiles, and I
understand, watching her, exactly how far this whole found family has
traveled since the morning of the binding.

"For everyone who's been someone else's warmth," Aldric says, at the
stone, an old blessing he alters as he speaks it, something I recognize
from a fragment of the Calder's Reach record, reworked into his own
plainer cadence, "and wanted, once, to be kept."

"Kept," I agree, and lay my hand over his marked one — the numb
fingers, the cold that never fully thawed, a permanent record of
exactly what this winter cost him — and mean the whole of what the word
costs and offers both, the way I have learned, this whole strange
season, that the truest vows are the ones taken apart, word by word,
and found to hold anyway.

The stone takes its ordinary measure from each of us, small and
unremarkable against everything the same granite has already
witnessed this winter, and I find myself grateful for precisely that
unremarkableness — a ward like any other, on a season like any other,
the specific, hard-won gift of an arrangement finally allowed to be
simply ordinary.

There is no proposal in this moment, no crown's formal blessing, no
ending any ledger I've ever kept would call properly complete. I find
I do not mind the absence of any of it, standing here at a stone that
has already given us considerably more than any formal ceremony could
properly account for. Ossory records the ward in his own careful hand,
for the historical account.
Tomas asks, immediately afterward, whether he can begin his own formal
training in the mutual form next week, and I tell him yes, and watch
him practically run back down the track toward Highstone with the
news, considerably lighter than the young man who first told me,
months ago, that he was simply making sure he was ready.

"Do you think it'll hold," Aldric asks, meaning the line, meaning the
whole arrangement we've built to hold it, meaning, I suspect,
considerably more than either of those things specifically.

"I don't know," I say, honestly, because I have decided, this whole
winter, that honest uncertainty is worth considerably more between us
than false comfort ever managed to be. "I know the Rime doesn't stop
advancing simply because we've found a better way to bear what it
costs. I know we're going to lose cairns, eventually, the way every
wardwright before me has lost cairns, regardless of how carefully
either of us manages the cost."

"That's not much comfort," Aldric says, though he's smiling, slightly,
hearing it.

"It isn't meant to be comfort," I say. "It's meant to be true. I've
found, this whole winter, that the true things hold up considerably
better than the comfortable ones, over time."

We walk back from the Long Reach cairn together, in no particular
hurry, past the village that has quietly rearranged itself around two
hearths instead of one, past Sela's orchard and Tomas's eager, ongoing
apprenticeship and Ossory's careful new record of a rite finally
practiced the way it was always meant to be practiced, and I find
myself thinking, for the first time in longer than I can accurately
place, that I have stopped running the cold arithmetic that has
governed my entire adult life, and started, instead, simply living
inside the answer it eventually arrived at.

Tomas catches up with us on the walk back from the Long Reach cairn,
having finished his own first solo inspection of the smaller waystones
closer to Highstone, and falls into step beside us with the particular
easy confidence of a young man who has, somewhere in these past weeks,
properly grown into the wardwright he is going to become.

I watch him walk that last stretch toward us, unhurried, entirely
himself, and think of the anxious sixteen-year-old who first arrived
at Highstone's gate, uncertain of his own place in a household still
shaping itself around a single grieving wardwright, and marvel, not
for the first time, at how completely that uncertain boy has grown
into someone this line can properly trust.

"The waystones held," he reports. "All seven, within tolerance. I
didn't need to feed any of them beyond the standard measure."

"Good work," I say, and mean it, watching him absorb the plain praise
with rather less flustered pleasure than he once showed, simply
accepting it now as an accurate accounting of a job properly done.

"I've been thinking," Tomas says, as we walk, "about what happens after
I've finished my own training. Whether I'll hold this line alone, the
way you did, or whether the crown might eventually sanction a proper
second practitioner, permanently, rather than treating the arrangement
as a one-time exception."

"I don't know," I say, honestly. "I know Ossory's argued for
precedent, not simply for us specifically. I'd like to think that
means the door's properly open for whoever comes after, to hold this
line however suits them best, rather than being handed the same
solitary arithmetic I inherited."

"I'd like that," Tomas says. "Not just for myself. For whoever comes
after me, too."

We walk the rest of the way to Highstone together, the three of us, in
the particular easy quiet of people who have survived a difficult
season and found, on the other side of it, something worth calling
family rather than merely circumstance, and I think, watching Tomas
peel off toward his own quarters and Sela's lamp glowing warm in the
distance above the orchard, that this is precisely the shape Ossian
never let himself have — a whole small company, properly sharing the
line's weight, rather than one wardwright, alone, holding it until it
finally held nothing back.

I think, too, about Corwin Vane, and Petra Ash, and Iven Hallow, and
every other name the old rite claimed before anyone thought to question
its shape properly. I do not know if word of what happened here will
ever properly reach whatever families they left behind, wherever the
crown's own scattered records might still hold some trace of them. I
resolve, walking the last stretch toward Highstone's gate, to write to
whatever offices might know, properly, so that their names, too, join
the small, growing record I keep of everyone this rite has cost —
not simply statistics, but people, properly mourned, properly
remembered, by the two hearths whose survival their own losses helped,
however unknowingly, to eventually purchase.

We stop at the record-room before heading home, because I have decided,
this evening, that Ossian's ledger and my own newer one belong beside
each other permanently rather than merely borrowed side by side for a
single night's copying, and Aldric waits while I set the two books
properly on the shelf, spines aligned, the old hand and the new one
keeping each other company for however many years the line requires
either of them to be consulted.

"Room for a third, eventually," Aldric observes, nodding at the space
beside them where Tomas's own ledger will someday sit.

"Room for a great many more than a third," I say. "That's rather the
whole point of finally writing it all down properly, instead of
letting each wardwright start over from nothing, the way I very nearly
did."

Ossory finds us there, on his way out for the evening, and pauses at
the doorway a moment before speaking.

"I wanted to tell you both something," he says, "now that the formal
business is finished and I'm no longer required to speak to you as a
crown official rather than simply as an old man who's watched this
line for considerably longer than either of you have been alive." He
gestures at the shelf. "I knew Ossian Thale. Not well. Well enough to
recognize, watching the two of you this winter, that he'd have been
considerably prouder of what you've built than he ever let himself
believe was possible, in his own time."

"You knew him," I say, surprised, something in my chest going
unexpectedly tight at the plain fact of it.

"Only a little," Ossory says. "Enough to know he carried more than he
ever let show. I think he'd have liked knowing his own student found
the nerve to finish what he couldn't." He nods once, formally, the old
gesture returning even as everything else about him, this evening,
feels considerably less formal than usual. "Goodnight, Wardwrights.
Both of you."

He leaves us there, in the record-room's quiet lamplight, and I find
myself, watching the door close behind him, holding Aldric's hand a
little tighter than the moment strictly requires.

"He called us both Wardwrights," I say. "Plural."

"I noticed," Aldric says. "I think I could get used to that, given
enough practice."

"So could I," I say, and we bank the record-room's lamp together
before heading home, out into the last of the evening's frost, the
whole line stretching north behind us in the dark, held now by
considerably more than one pair of hands.

·

Aldric takes my hand as we near the gate, marked fingers and unmarked
ones twined together the way they have been most evenings since the
rite was rewritten, and I feel, walking the last stretch home beside
him, the whole weight of everything this winter has cost and given,
settling, finally, into something I can properly carry rather than
merely survive.

No proposal. No crown's blessing. No ending that would satisfy anyone
keeping score on a ledger built for tidier stories than this one. Just
two hearths, sharing one winter, openly, on purpose, for as long as the
line needs holding — which, I understand now, watching Aldric's marked
hand rest easily in my own, is very likely to be considerably longer
than either one of us, alone, could ever have managed.

We pass Reny Ashe's stall on the way, shuttered for the night, and I
find myself smiling at the small, closed shopfront regardless, thinking
of every twist of herbs she's pressed into my hand, unbidden, since the
night of the binding.

"What is it," Aldric asks, catching the smile.

"I was thinking about how many people in this village decided, quietly,
to look out for me before I ever properly noticed they were doing it,"
I say. "Reny. Ida. Ossory himself, in his own careful way. I spent six
years believing I was managing this line entirely alone. I understand
now that I was never quite as alone as I believed."

"None of us are, I think," Aldric says, "once we finally let ourselves
notice."

"I'm glad I finally noticed," I say, and we continue on toward the
cottage together, considerably warmed by the whole quiet village's
accumulated, unspoken care.

·

We reach the cottage as full dark properly settles, the fire banked
low from the morning but easily coaxed back to life, and Aldric builds
it up while I set water for tea, the whole small domestic ritual
considerably more precious, tonight, for how ordinary it's finally
allowed to become.

"I keep thinking about the boundary marker at Calder's Reach," I say,
watching the fire catch. "The three rings. Your grandmother's lintel.
I wonder how many other small, unexamined fragments of this line's
older history are sitting in plain sight, waiting for someone to
finally ask what they mean."

"We could spend the rest of our lives chasing that question," Aldric
says, "and I suspect we still wouldn't run out of fragments to find."

"I'd like that, actually," I say. "Spending the rest of our lives
chasing it. I've spent six years believing the whole of my work was
simply holding the line steady against an ending. I like the idea of
spending the years after this one actually discovering something,
instead."

"Then that's what we'll do," Aldric says, settling beside me with the
tea properly steeped, "alongside everything else — the wards, the
ordinary evenings, whatever Tomas eventually needs from either of us.
Room enough, I think, for discovery too."

We drink the tea slowly, in no particular hurry, the cottage settling
around us in the specific comfortable quiet of two people who have,
somewhere in this long, difficult winter, stopped merely surviving
inside their own lives and started, instead, properly building one,
together, on purpose, for as long as either of them is given to keep
choosing it.

"Goodnight, Wardwright," Aldric says, echoing Ossory's earlier
farewell, with a small smile that tells me exactly how much he's
enjoying the new title's particular weight.

"Goodnight, Wardwright," I say back, and we bank the fire low for the
night, the whole line stretching north into the dark beyond our
windows, held now, and for as long as it needs holding, by
considerably more than one pair of hands.

·

I wake once, deep in the night, to find Aldric already awake beside me,
watching the last of the banked fire's glow with the particular quiet
stillness of a man turning something over privately.

"You're not sleeping," I observe.

"I'm simply grateful," he says. "I keep waking, some nights, and
needing to properly confirm that all of this actually happened — that
I'm still here, that you're still here, that the whole terrible
arithmetic of this winter somehow resolved into something worth waking
up for."

"I do the same," I admit. "I don't think either of us is going to
stop, entirely, checking for a while yet."

"I don't mind the checking," Aldric says. "I think I'd rather spend the
rest of my life occasionally waking to confirm this is real than take
any part of it for granted."

I settle back against him, and we lie together in the banked fire's
low glow a while longer, neither of us in any hurry to properly sleep,
both of us simply grateful, in the particular quiet of the deepest part
of the night, for a future neither of us quite believed, six weeks ago,
either one of us would get to keep.
